When I came down from Oxford, my head was still full of lectures and long corridors of talk, but my hands—unused hands for a man suddenly responsible for acres and tenants—ached for something simpler than argument: a thing you could weigh, smell, and set in order. My father had died when I was a child, my mother not long after; the estate passed to me like a loaf pulled from an oven while the baker’s back is turned—still steaming, but not made by my own labor. I knew next to nothing of the people whose name I carried. Only this: many of them had been readers, hoarders of thought, the way some families hoard seed.

If I inherited anything, it was the habit of searching for connections. I had been trained toward physical science and its neat instruments, but I could never stop myself from tasting for metaphors the way a cook tastes a broth—lifting a spoon, letting it cool, looking for what you can’t name but know when it’s missing. A theory, I learned, can be as easily over-salted as a stew: one handful too many of certainty, and the whole pot is spoiled. Still, I kept reaching for more.

The house was old enough to have learned, over generations, the domestic trick of swallowing its own additions. Nowhere was that more evident than in the library. It had begun before printing, when words were copied by hand on skin and vellum, and it had gone on accumulating in every age, taking on whatever the living believed was worth preserving. It had done what old kitchens do: expanded sideways into pantries, larders, sculleries; only here the shelves were the cupboards, and the books were the jars—some plainly labeled, some so dusted and darkened that you had to scrape a finger across the spine to raise their names.

It had absorbed room after room until most of the ground floor belonged to it. The chief room was large, book-lined nearly to the ceiling; from it the library overflowed into odd chambers of different shapes, linked by doors, arches, short passages, and steps that went up and down as if the house itself were a mind wandering from topic to topic. The place had its own sound: a continual hush stitched together from the soft sigh of paper, the faint creak of old boards, the little settling clicks of wood as the weather changed. Even the rain, when it came, had to lower its voice to speak through such thick walls.

I spent most of my time there. Not because I disliked the fields or the stables—indeed, there is a clean satisfaction in the dull thud of a fork into a heap of hay, in the blunt honesty of soil—but because the library was the estate’s true granary of inheritance. I read the sciences, old and new, interested less in what men called knowledge than in how they had arrived at it, what they had been hungry for, what had been fed to them, and what had poisoned them. Ptolemy and Dante, the two Bacons, Boyle—these were to me like old recipes written in a dead hand: imperfect, sometimes dangerous, but smelling faintly of the first fires.

On a gloomy day in August, toward evening, I sat in my usual place with my back to one of the windows and a book open before me. It had rained most of the morning and afternoon. The rain had its own music—good rain, steady and unhurried, not the metallic rattle of a storm whipped by wind. Yet even good rain, drumming long enough, can make the mind drowsy. When the sun at last dropped toward the horizon, the clouds parted in front of it and let in a sudden blade of light.

I rose and looked out. In the middle of the great lawn, the fountain’s feathering column caught the sunset and filled with red glory, as if the water itself had been warmed to wine. The air smelled rinsed, the way a scoured wooden table smells when the cloth is lifted: clean grain, damp earth, and a faint green from the bruised grass.

I turned back to my chair, and the same ruddy light caught the only picture in the room—a portrait set oddly alone in a kind of shallow shrine sunk into the shelves. I knew it was the likeness of one of my ancestors, but I had never paused to ask why it hung there rather than in the gallery with the other faces that belonged, in the usual way, to family pride. Now, in that direct sunlight, the painting woke. For the first time I seemed truly to see it: the dry, attentive look of the eyes; the suggestion of a mouth that had known both restraint and appetite. For the first time it seemed to answer my gaze, not like canvas, but like a person who has been waited on too long.

With the light from the portrait still bright in my eyes, something—no clear thought, more like a prickling along the scalp—made me glance toward the farther end of the room. There, in that comparative dusk, I saw, or thought I saw, a tall figure reaching up a hand to a shelf.

The next instant the impression collapsed like a mirage when you blink. There was no one. I scolded my own nerves for their tricks; after sudden brightness, the eye invents shadows. I sat down again and bent over my reading, determined not to become a superstitious householder frightened by his own furniture.

But a moment later I had occasion to consult a certain volume. I rose, walked to its place, and found only a gap in the row where it ought to have stood. At once the image returned: the tall figure, the lifted hand, precisely at that spot. I searched about, behind other books and along the shelves, half laughing at myself. The volume was nowhere.

The next morning, however, there it was—exactly where it belonged, as if it had never moved. No servant in the house had any reason to want it; the title was not the sort of thing one borrowed for amusement. I tried to dismiss the matter as some muddle of my own doing, but the clean absence and clean return made that hard. It felt like setting a loaf to cool and turning your back for a breath, only to find it sliced when you return, the knife put away, no crumbs on the board.

Three days later, something stranger happened.

In one of the walls was a low, narrow door to a closet where some of the oldest and rarest books were kept. The door was thick and heavy, framed so it projected slightly, and one of my long-ago predecessors had played a harmless trick with it: he had crossed it with shallow shelves filled with nothing but book-backs, so that at a glance the door disappeared into the surrounding wall of learning. The deception was made more amusing by the titles painted there—some comically invented, some the names of books long lost, as if the family would rather keep the idea of them than admit the emptiness.

I had always liked that masked door. It was the sort of whimsy an old house carries the way a kitchen carries a notch in the table from a careless cleaver—domestic history made visible. To complete the illusion, some ingenious workman—or perhaps the same fanciful ancestor—had slid into the top of one row a portion of a thin volume, so thin it could lie between the shelf and the one above. A considerable part of it had been cut away diagonally, and the remnant fixed there with one open corner projecting beyond the false backs. The binding was limp vellum, with that faint animal scent old parchment keeps, like clean leather warmed by hands. If you lifted the corner, you could see that it was manuscript upon skin—ink browned with age, the strokes made by a human wrist that had been dust for centuries.

That evening, as I read, I happened to raise my eyes, and my glance fell upon the masked door. At once I saw that the mutilated volume—if volume it could be called—was gone.

I felt an unreasonable anger, as if someone had taken a spoon from a set I used daily, though I had never once read a line of that fragment. The indignation was not for the object itself but for what it represented: an intrusion into the order of my household, into the quiet consent between the dead and the living that keeps old places sane. I rang the bell.

The butler came—an older man whose whole life seemed to have been trained into discretion, as if he had been brined in family service. I asked him if he knew what had become of it. His face, which was usually steady, lost its color. He assured me he did not.

I trusted him more than I trusted my own eyes. He had been in the family since my father was eight years old; faithfulness had become his second nature. Yet even as he denied knowledge, something in his hesitation suggested he knew where the question led, and would rather not walk there.

In the afternoon I was again in the library. The rain had stopped; the house held a damp-cool silence that made the slightest sound—page turned, chair shifted—seem too loud. I came upon a passage that demanded thought, lowered the book, and let my gaze wander as one lets a simmering pot be while the flavors marry.

In that same moment I saw, as plainly as I have ever seen a man’s back, a slender old figure in a long dark coat, shiny with wear at the shoulders and elbows, moving away from me. He was in the act of slipping through the masked door into the closet beyond.

I crossed the room in a rush. The boards gave a few sharp complaints under my feet—too loud in that careful hush. The door was shut. I pulled it open at once and looked into the closet. It was small, lined with shelves, crowded close with volumes bound in skins and cloths of every hue time can dim. There was no other exit. There was no one.

A chill moved through me—not the theatrical fear of a tale told by the fire, but a practical uneasiness, like finding the pantry unlatched in the night and not knowing whether wind or thief has been there. I told myself my eyes had played their earlier trick again. I went back to my chair and opened my book, trying to recover the thread of thought.

But I could not. A mind that listens for a footstep cannot hear a paragraph.

Presently I glanced up, needing to assure myself that I was indeed alone. I started to my feet again—for there, in its place on the sham shelf, was the mutilated vellum fragment.

I went to it and laid hold of the projecting corner. I pulled. It was firm, fixed as it had always been, as if it had never been removed. There was no looseness, no sign of tampering, not even a scuff on the false backs around it. The thing was as immovable as the notion of it had become.

Bewilderment, when it is complete, feels strangely physical. My mouth was dry; my skin prickled. I rang for the butler again.

When he came, I told him everything: the gap on the shelf, the book’s return, the missing vellum, the figure disappearing through the door, the fragment restored as if welded in place. He listened without interrupting, and when I had finished, he answered with the air of a man setting down at last a covered dish he has been carrying too long.

He said he had hoped the old gentleman would be forgotten. It was well no one but myself had seen him. He had heard a good deal of the matter when first he came into service, but by degrees it had ceased to be mentioned, and he had been careful never to allude to it.

“The place was haunted by an old gentleman, then?” I said, hearing my own voice sound thin in that large room.

At one time, he replied, everybody believed it, but the fact that I had never heard of it seemed to show the business had died away into silence, as household talk does when it threatens the peace. My grandfather, he said, would never hear a word on the subject. If any servant mentioned it, dismissal would come without warning. Old Sir Ralph, he added, believed in nothing he could not see or lay hold of; he called it a pretext of the maids for running into the arms of the men.

No maid, the butler said, had ever claimed to see the apparition; but a footman once left the place because of it. An ancient woman in the village—one of those who remember other people’s grandfathers by the sound of their steps—had told him a legend concerning a Mr. Raven, long ago librarian to “that Sir Upward whose portrait hangs there among the books.”

Sir Upward, the old woman said, had been a great reader—not only of wholesome books, but of strange, forbidden, and evil ones. In those readings Mr. Raven—who, she hinted, was likely the devil himself—had encouraged him. Then, suddenly, both Sir Upward and Mr. Raven disappeared. Sir Upward was never again seen or heard of, but Mr. Raven continued to show himself, at uncertain intervals, in the library.

Some believed he was not dead. Others, the old woman among them, found it easier to believe a dead man might revisit the world he had left than that a man might go on living for hundreds of years and still be a man. The butler had never heard that Mr. Raven meddled with anything in the house, but he might consider himself privileged where books were concerned.

How the village woman had learned so much, the butler could not tell. Yet the description she gave—slender, tall, old, dark coat worn glossy with time—matched exactly what I had seen.

“I hope it was but a friendly call on the part of the old gentleman,” he concluded, attempting a troubled smile that did not quite reach his eyes.

I told him I had no objection to any number of visits from Mr. Raven. If the library had its own customs, I could live with them. But I said it would be well he kept to his resolution of telling nothing to the servants; a household has enough noises of its own without adding the echo of fear.

Then I asked him whether he had ever seen the mutilated vellum out of its place. He answered that he never had and had always thought it a fixture.

With that, as if to reassure both of us by the honest test of touch, he went to the fragment and gave it a pull. It did not budge. It lay there as it always had, the corner inviting the curious finger, the old ink hidden just beyond the reach of certainty, as if the house kept its secrets not with locks and keys but with a finer, older power: the power to make what is real sound like a story, and what is a story press back upon the senses as real.

For some days nothing more disturbed the ordinary order of my life. The week went by with its small homely repetitions—tea that grew cold beside my elbow while I read, the soft complaint of a settling board in the panelled wall, the hush that lived in the library as if it were a creature that fed on dust and paper. Yet the thought of that torn scrap of manuscript kept returning, like a remembered flavour that will not leave the palate. More than once I went back to it, trying with thumb-nail and patience to discover how it had been caught, what invisible tack or glue of time held it so obstinately; but it would not yield to any gentleness, and I would not tear it.

Meanwhile the closet of books began to oppress me. The air in there had the sour, shut-up smell of things long unopened—dry rot and old leather, a faint mouldiness like bread forgotten in a drawer, and that bitter, powdery tang which rises when you disturb paper that has slept too many seasons. I told myself, as one does, that it was mere fastidiousness; but it was a bodily uneasiness, and it grew until, one morning, intention hardened into resolve.

I had pushed my chair back and was in the act of rising to begin the overhauling when movement caught at the edge of my sight. Across the room, coming from the door of the closet, went the old librarian.

Or rather—so little did he declare himself to the full eye—that I can only say: a shadow crossed the light, and in that shadow I received the impression of a slight, stooping man in a shabby dress-coat that fell almost to his heels. The long tails parted a little as he went, and I saw thin legs in black stockings and large feet in wide shoes like slippers, made for silent pacing among shelves. There was something in his going that reminded me of a moth moving along a wall, close to it, never quite alighting, never quite flying.

At once I followed him. I might have been following nothing but my own uneasiness, but I never doubted I was following something.

He slipped out of the library into the hall. The soundscape of the house changed with the threshold: the library held its own deadened hush, as though the books drank sound; the hall returned every noise with a faint hollowness—my footfall in particular, which seemed too loud, too modern, too much an intrusion. Ahead of me the shadow moved without any audible answer, as if he belonged to an older acoustics.

He went straight across to the foot of the great staircase and began to ascend. I went after him, listening against my will to the little protests of the house: the first stair gave a short groan, the next a sharper creak, and then another, in different pitches, like an ill-tuned instrument remembering its age. Yet from the steps before me—where I was sure he must be—there came no sound at all. The contrast made my skin tighten, as if the silence of his passage were a kind of power.

On the first floor, where the chief rooms lay, he continued past them without pause. I, following close, had a sense of being drawn along the same old route the house itself must have known for generations: the corridor’s draught carried a cold smell from unused chambers, and somewhere, far off, a window-frame ticked with a minute, restless rhythm. We came to the foot of a narrower stair that led to the second storey, and up that also he went.

When I reached the top, a strange thing happened: I found myself in a part of the house almost unknown to me.

I had never had brother or sister to drive me, in childhood, into those rollicking explorations that teach the body every turning and nook. I had been taken away while I was still small; and until a month ago—when I returned to take possession—I had not set eyes on the place again. Now, following a figure that seemed less and less to belong to my time, I moved through passage after passage as if through a family memory whose language I had half forgotten. Doors stood with their handles dulled by disuse; the air tasted thinner here, like flour sifted too long. The house spoke in small noises—wood contracting, a distant drip in a pipe, the occasional sigh of a draught—but it spoke without words I could translate into comfort.

At last we came to a door at the bottom of a winding wooden stair. The stair rose tightly, as if it had been threaded into the house like a twist of rope. We ascended. Every step cried out under my weight—one with a long rasp, another with a sharp chirp—each board with its own complaint, as if the staircase had its own dialect of pain. Still I heard nothing from the footfall of my guide, and this, more than the darkness, made the climb uncanny: the house acknowledged me, named me loudly with every creak; it did not acknowledge him at all.

Somewhere about the middle of the winding ascent I lost sight of him. One moment the shadow was ahead, the next it had been swallowed. I hurried the last steps, and when I reached the top, the shadowy shape was nowhere visible. I could not even persuade myself that I saw him. The place was full of shadows—but he was not one of them.

I stood in the main garret, under huge beams and rafters, with great spaces around me and doors set here and there like mouths in a dim face. Long vistas opened into gloom, thinned only by a few cobwebbed windows crouching low and small skylights stained with dust. The air had the dry, sweet staleness of old straw and stored timber; it held the faintest scent of mice and the powder of years. I breathed it and felt, with a strange mingling of awe and pleasure, that this wide expanse—unvisited, unmeasured—was mine.

In the middle of the garret stood an unpainted enclosure of rough planks, its door ajar. The boards were raw and pale, as if they had been put up in haste and never meant to be seen. Thinking Mr. Raven might be there—thinking anything that would give the pursuit a human explanation—I pushed the door and entered.

The small chamber inside was full of light; but it was a light like that which dwells in deserted rooms, having no work to do. It lay upon things without warming them, as if it regretted having come. Dust swam thickly; when I moved, it rose in a soft cloud, and a few rather dim sunrays drew visible paths through the motes, making them look like drifting meal in a shaft of bakery light. That old powdery smell deepened—dry wood, stale cloth, and the faint metallic tang of cold iron.

Against one wall stood a tall mirror with a face dulled by dust, old-fashioned and rather narrow, such as might have reflected many morning faces and candle-flames before it was banished to this upper wilderness. Its frame was ebony, black as char. On the top sat a black eagle with outstretched wings; in his beak he held a golden chain, and from the chain’s end hung a black ball that looked heavier than it ought, like a fruit of darkness.

At first I had been looking at the mirror rather than into it, noting the grime on its surface and the delicate cracks in the varnish of the frame. Then, with the suddenness of a changed note in music, I became aware that it reflected neither the chamber nor my own person.

For an instant I had the impression of the wall melting away. The room’s pale light seemed to withdraw, as if ashamed, and the dust in the sunbeam stopped being dust and became something else in the eye. Could I have mistaken for a mirror the glass that protected a wonderful picture?

Before me lay a wild country—broken, heathy, and open to wind. Desolate hills of no great height rose in the middle distance, yet they looked strange, as if their very shapes belonged to a land with different names for stone. Along the far horizon stretched the tops of a mountain range, blue with distance. Nearest to me lay a tract of moorland, flat and melancholy, the ground coloured like ash and old heather, with scattered stones that seemed to have been waiting a long time.

Being short-sighted, I stepped closer to examine the texture of a stone in the immediate foreground. It was speckled and rough, and the urge to see it clearly took me as naturally as hunger. In that act I espied, hopping toward me with a solemn, measured air, a large and ancient raven. His purply black was here and there softened with gray, as if age had salted him. He came as though looking for worms, pausing and tilting his head, and I felt no astonishment at the appearance of a live creature in a picture—only a sharpening curiosity, like catching a scent you cannot name but must follow.

I took another step forward to see him better, stumbled over something—doubtless the frame of the mirror—and in the same moment found myself nose to beak with the bird.

The air struck my face with the clean chill of open space.

I was no longer in the garret chamber.

I was out of doors, on a houseless heath.

I turned and looked behind me. Everything wavered in a half-light that would not settle into named things: not fog and not field, not cloud and not hillside—only a vast uncertainty that refused the comfort of edges. One fact alone held: I saw nothing I knew.

As if sight had betrayed me and touch might be the honest sense, I stretched out both hands and walked a little way this way and that, fingers combing the air like a cook testing steam for heat. My palms found nothing—no wall, no trunk, no latch, no familiar grain of wood. Only the ground underfoot, and the faint stir of low growth brushing my boots with the dry whisper of leaves.

Instinct, desperate for any living witness, pulled me toward the raven.

He stood a little off, glossy as spilled ink, watching me with an expression that was at once respectful and mocking—like an old servant who has seen too many masters panic at the first crack in their certainties. The very notion of asking counsel from a raven struck me as absurd; I turned away again, the bewilderment thickening, fear threaded through it like a bitter herb.

Had I walked into a region where the relations of mind and matter no longer kept their bargain? Could a man step, by chance, beyond order—beyond the grammar that binds cause to effect—and become a toy for the lawless?

Yet there was the raven. There was the firm press of earth beneath my soles. And there was sound: a wind-sound in the small plants around me, not loud, but present—an acoustic proof that I was not entirely unmade. It was the kind of sound that, in my own world, would have meant hedges and garden-beds and the safe hush around a house at dusk: a soundscape that steadies the mind because it belongs to living things, not to engines.

“How did I get here?” I said—apparently aloud, for my question was answered at once.

“You came through the door,” replied an odd voice, rather harsh, like a throat made for croaking forced into human consonants.

I looked behind me, then all about, but saw no human shape. The thought that madness had come—quiet-footed, without announcement—caught me hard. Must I now distrust every sense, and even the witness of my own awareness?

In the same instant I knew it was the raven that had spoken, for he stood looking up at me as if waiting to see whether I would accept the plainness of his reply. The sun was not shining, yet the bird seemed to cast a shadow, and the shadow seemed not something he made but something he wore—part of his body, like a second pelt.

I beg you—who read—to lend me your patience, and more, your imagination, for I must try to speak of a place where words do not sit properly on things. I was in a world—call it a state, an economy of conditions—so unlike our customary one that every phrase I choose is only a sketch, a smudged charcoal outline of a form that refuses to be held. I begin to suspect the impossibility of what I attempt: to tell what I cannot truly tell, because no speech I command is shaped to these impressions. When I try to refine what I write, the reality slips away like a scent you almost place—smoke, resin, rain on stone—until it changes and changes, and at last is no longer recognizable even to memory.

Still, a bird who can speak to a man deserves a civil answer. Perhaps, being a bird, he deserved more than a man. His voice carried the roughness of a croak pressed through language, but it was not disagreeable; and though what he said lit no lantern for me, it did not sound rude.

“I did not come through any door,” I said.

“I saw you come through it—saw you with my own ancient eyes!” the raven returned, positive without insolence.

“I never saw any door!”

“Of course not!” said he. “All the doors you have seen—and you haven’t seen many—were doors in. Here you came upon a door out. The strange thing to you will be that the more doors you go out of, the farther you get in.”

“Oblige me,” I said, clinging to manners as to a rail over a drop, “by telling me where I am.”

“That is impossible. You know nothing about whereness. The only way to come to know where you are is to begin to make yourself at home.”

“How am I to begin,” I demanded, “when everything is so strange?”

“By doing something.”

“What?”

“Anything; and the sooner you begin the better. Until you are at home, you will find it as difficult to get out as it is to get in.”

“I have, unfortunately, found it too easy to get in. Once out I shall not try again.”

“You have stumbled in,” he replied, “and may, possibly, stumble out again. Whether you have got in unfortunately remains to be seen.”

“Do you never go out, sir?” I asked, for his tone had the air of someone who visited my world from choice.

“When I please I do, but not often, or for long. Your world is such a half-baked sort of place—at once so childish and so self-satisfied. In fact, it is not sufficiently developed for an old raven—at your service!”

“Am I wrong, then,” I said, “in presuming that a man is superior to a bird?”

“That is as it may be. We do not waste our intellects in generalising, but take man or bird as we find him. I think it is now my turn to ask you a question!”

“You have the best of rights,” I returned, “in the fact that you can do so.”

“Well answered!” he said. “Tell me, then, who you are—if you happen to know.”

“How should I help knowing? I am myself, and must know!”

“If you know you are yourself,” said he, “you know you are not somebody else. But do you know that you are yourself? Are you sure you are not your own father?—or, excuse me, your own fool?—Who are you, pray?”

At that, a blankness opened in me. I perceived, with sudden shame, that I could not give him even the outline of an answer. Indeed—who was I? It would not help to say I was who. I understood that I did not know myself: did not know what I was; had no ground firm enough to set my heel on and say, This is me and not another. My name from my own world—what I was called, the sound by which others summoned me—was gone from me as if it had been a word in a dying dialect, fading because nothing here required it. And oddly, I did not even care to recover it; it meant nothing in this place, and it seemed of no consequence. I had nearly forgotten that, where I came from, it was customary for everybody to carry a name like a label tied to a jar.

So I held my peace—and it was wiser than any speech I might have forced. What could I say to a creature like this raven, who looked through accident into essence?

“Look at me,” he said, “and tell me who I am.”

As he spoke, he turned his back to me—and instantly I knew him.

He was no longer a raven, but a man: above middle height, stooping, very thin, wearing a long black tail-coat. Then he turned again, and I saw a raven once more, black and bright, balanced on his feet as if the ground were his familiar shelf.

“I have seen you before, sir,” I said, feeling more foolish than astonished.

“How can you say so from seeing me behind?” he retorted. “Did you ever see yourself behind? You have never seen yourself at all. Tell me now, then—who am I?”

“I humbly beg your pardon,” I answered. “I believe you were once the librarian of our house; but more who I do not know.”

“Why do you beg my pardon?”

“Because I took you for a raven,” I said—though he stood before me as plainly raven as raven could be.

“You did me no wrong,” he replied. “Calling me a raven, or thinking me one, you allowed me existence, which is the sum of what one can demand of his fellow-beings. Therefore, in return, I will give you a lesson: No one can say he is himself until first he knows that he is, and then what himself is. In fact, nobody is himself, and himself is nobody. There is more in it than you can see now, but not more than you need to see.

“You have, I fear, got into this region too soon; but none the less you must get to be at home in it. For home—whether you know it or not—is the only place where you can go out and in. There are places you can go into, and places you can go out of; but the one place, if you do but find it, where you may go out and in both, is home.”

He turned to walk away, and again I saw the librarian. He did not appear to have changed; rather, it seemed as if he had taken up his shadow and put it on. I know how that sounds; I can only tell what I saw, and even in the telling it comes out crooked.

I watched him until I could see him no more; whether distance hid him, or whether he vanished among the heather, I cannot say.

Could it be, I thought, that I was dead and did not know it? Was this what we used to call the world beyond the grave, and must I wander seeking my place in it, as one seeks a house whose address has been rubbed off the envelope?

How was I to find myself at home? The raven had said I must do something. But what could I do here? And would doing make me somebody?—for now, alas, I was nobody.

I set myself to follow the way Mr. Raven had gone, and went slowly after him. Presently I saw a wood of tall, slender pines and turned toward it. Its odour met me on the way: resin and dry needles, a clean sharpness like a knife on a cutting-board, and underneath it a sweetness like sap warming in the sun. The smell pulled me as bread-smell pulls a hungry child; I hastened, eager to bury myself in that familiar twilight, where scent at least had a language I understood.

Plunged at length into its dimness, I spied ahead something that shone between two stems. It had no colour. It was like the translucent trembling of hot air rising from sun-baked ground at noon, vibrant like the struck chord of a musical instrument—visible the way sound is sometimes visible, if you stare long enough at a humming wire. As I went nearer, it grew no plainer; and when I came close up, I ceased to see it at all. Only the forms and colours of the trees beyond seemed suddenly unsure, as if their outlines had forgotten the words that held them.

I would have passed between the stems.

A slight shock met me—no blow, rather a refusal—and I stumbled and fell as if I had walked into a pane of invisible glass. When I rose, I stood before the wooden wall of the garret chamber.

I turned. There was the mirror, and on its top the black eagle seemed to have perched but that moment, as if it had never moved since I first beheld it.

Terror seized me. I fled.

Outside the chamber, the wide garret spaces had an uncanny look. They seemed to have long been waiting for something; it had come, and now they waited again. The air there had its own silence—not the living hush of pine-woods, filled with fine rustlings, but the deadened pause of a room where sound has been swallowed and refused a natural echo. My mind recoiled from it. A shudder went through me on the winding stair: the house itself had grown strange to me, and I felt as if something might leap upon me from behind—not merely a body, but a thought with claws.

I darted down the spiral, struck against the wall and fell, rose and ran. On the next floor I lost my way, and went through several passages twice before I found the head of the stair. At the top of the great stair I had come a little to myself, and in a few moments I sat in the library, recovering breath.

Nothing should ever again make me go up that last terrible stair. The garret at the top of it seemed to pervade the whole house. It sat upon it, threatening to crush me out of it. The brooding brain of the building, it felt full of mysterious dwellers, one or other of whom might at any moment appear in the library where I sat. I was nowhere safe. I would let the place; I would sell the dreadful place, in which an aerial portal stood ever open to creatures whose life was other than human. I would purchase a crag in Switzerland and build upon it a one-storey wooden nest with never a garret above it—guarded by some grand old peak that would send down nothing worse than a few tons of whelming rock.

All the while I knew my thinking was foolish. I heard, beneath it, an undertone of contemptuous humour at my own expense, as if another part of me were listening and smiling at the panic. But suddenly even that humour was checked, and I seemed again to hear the raven’s croak, like a phrase in a tongue I half remembered and could not translate without losing its taste.

If I know nothing of my own garret, I thought, what secures me against my own brain? Can I tell what it is generating—even now—what thought it may present me the next moment, the next month, or a year away? What is at the heart of my brain? What is behind my think? Am I there at all? Who—what—am I?

I could no more answer now than when the raven put it to me in—at—where? “Where in? where at?” I said aloud, and gave myself up as knowing anything of myself or the universe.

Then, as if surrender itself were an action—something at last done—I started to my feet and hurried across the room to the masked door. The mutilated volume, sticking out from the flat of soulless, bodiless, non-existent books, seemed to beckon me like the handle of a tool half-hidden in a drawer.

I went down on my knees and opened it as far as its position would permit, but I could see nothing. I rose, lit a taper, and peeped in as into the reluctant jaws of some patient beast. The manuscript was verse.

Further I could not carry discovery. Beginnings of lines showed on the left-hand page, ends of lines on the other; but I could not reach the beginning and end of a single line. What I could read was too broken to yield a sense.

Yet the mere words—those torn fragments—woke feelings in me that I cannot properly describe, because they were unlike any I had known. There are dreams, poems, musical phrases, pictures, that rouse sensations new in colour and shape—spiritual, yet as unmistakable as flavour. So here: some phrases, some senseless half-lines, some even single words, affected me with something like the aroma of an idea—like catching, for an instant, the scent of a dish you ate in childhood in a house that no longer exists, and feeling hunger not for food but for the vanished life around it. They stirred in me a great longing to know what the poem—or poems—might hold, even in their mutilation.

I copied out a few of the larger shreds I could reach, and tried hard to complete some of the lines, but without the least success. The only thing I gained was weariness—so much that, when I went to bed, I fell asleep at once and slept soundly.

In the morning, all that horror of the empty garret spaces had left me.

The morning came in too hard and white, as if the sun had been scrubbed with lye and set to glare. I did not trust it. Weather, like men, can smile with all its teeth and still mean a blow. At the breakfast-table I turned my hand this way and that, letting the light crawl over the milky sapphire on my finger, hunting for the little star that sometimes held steady like a seed in a pear. It should have been crisp in such brightness. Instead it swam—blurred, as if seen through watered milk.

I pushed back my chair and went to the window. In the night there had been heavy rain; the lawn shone as if someone had glazed it, and from the soil rose that iron-sweet smell of earth rinsed clean. A thrush on the grass was at his work like a careful cook at a stubborn shellfish, tapping and worrying a snail until the hinge gave. Everything sounded close: drip, peck, the soft pull of the bird’s beak. I lifted the ring to the pane and rolled it between thumb and forefinger, trying to coax the star to answer the sun.

That was when a keen black eye looked back at me from the stone.

It was so alive, so intent, that my hand jerked; the ring slipped from my grasp and struck the floor. When I snatched it up, the eye was gone—gone from the sapphire as if it had never been there. At the same instant the light failed. The sun was muffled, swallowed by a dark vapour, and in a breath the whole sky clouded as though a lid had been set over it.

The air thickened, warm and sour, as if the world had begun to sweat. A gust shouldered the house. Then came a flash of lightning and one sharp clap of thunder—clean and immediate, like the crack of a cleaver on a block. The rain followed, not as rain but as poured water, a thin cataract beating the windows and flattening the grass.

I had the window open and stood there looking out at the slanting sheets when I saw him: a raven walking across the lawn with the solemn pace of a man who knows exactly where the kitchen is. The deluge struck his feathers and ran off them; he behaved as if it were no more than a mist from a cooling pot.

I congratulated myself that I was on the ground floor. Even as the thought formed, another thought slid beneath it: if I was not careful, something would happen.

He came on, nearer, nearer, and then, as if the world were a stage and he had his cue, made a profound bow. With a sudden winged leap he landed on the window-sill, stepped over the ledge, dropped into the room, and walked toward the door.

I followed, certain he meant the library, and equally certain that if he once turned toward the stair I would not put one foot after him. But he neither sought books nor ascent. He went instead to a small door opening upon a grass-patch tucked in a nook between two wings of the rambling old house. I hurried and opened it.

He stepped into the creeper-covered porch and stood looking at the rain as one might look at a well-boiling stock. I stood behind him in the doorway. The second flash came, followed by a long roll of thunder retreating into distance.

He turned his head over his shoulder and looked at me as plainly as if he had said, You hear that? Then he swivelled it forward again, and resumed his contemplation with what I could only call approval.

So human were his stance and the restless precision of his head-turning that I found myself speaking almost without will: “Fine weather for the worms, Mr. Raven!”

“Yes,” he answered in the croaky voice I had come to know, “the ground will be nice for them to get out and in!—It must be a grand time on the steppes of Uranus!” He glanced upward. “I believe it is raining there too; it was, all the last week!”

“Why should that make it grand?” I asked.

“Because the animals there are all burrowers,” he said, “like the field-mice and the moles here. They will be, for ages to come.”

“How do you know that—if I may be so bold?” I said, though boldness sat ill with the way the air kept pricking my skin.

“As any one would who had been there to see,” he returned. “It is a great sight, until you get used to it, when the earth gives a heave, and out comes a beast. You might think it a hairy elephant or a deinotherium—but none of the animals are the same as we have ever had here. I was almost frightened myself the first time I saw the dry-bog-serpent come wallowing out—such a head and mane! and SUCH eyes!—but the shower is nearly over. It will stop directly after the next thunder-clap. There it is!”

A flash came with the words, and in about half a minute the thunder arrived, as promised—delayed, like an order called to the table. The rain ceased abruptly, leaving the world steaming. Water hung on every blade of grass, and the whole garden seemed to breathe.

“Now we should be going!” said the raven, stepping to the front of the porch.

“Going where?” I asked.

“Going where we have to go,” he answered. “You did not surely think you had got home? I told you there was no going out and in at pleasure until you were at home!”

“I do not want to go,” I said.

“That does not make any difference—at least not much,” he replied. “This is the way!”

“I am quite content where I am.”

“You think so, but you are not. Come along.”

He hopped from the porch onto the wet grass and turned, waiting. The sun thrust through the cloud like a blade slipped from a sheath; raindrops flashed, each one a tiny mirror.

“I will not leave the house to-day,” I said with obstinacy.

“You will come into the garden!” returned the raven.

“I give in so far,” I said, and stepped down.

The raven walked on the soaked turf as if it were dry. “You will wet your feet!” I cried.

“And mire my beak,” he answered, and immediately plunged it deep into the sod, drawing out a great red worm, thick and shining with the black wet of soil. It wriggled like a live ribbon.

He threw back his head and tossed it into the air.

It spread great wings—gorgeous in red and black—and soared aloft, beating the washed air as if it had always belonged there.

“Tut! tut!” I exclaimed, more startled by the certainty of the act than by the impossibility of it. “You mistake, Mr. Raven: worms are not the larvæ of butterflies!”

“Never mind,” he croaked. “It will do for once! I’m not a reading man at present, but sexton at the—at a certain graveyard—cemetery, more properly—in—at—no matter where!”

“I see! You can’t keep your spade still: and when you have nothing to bury, you must dig something up! Only you should mind what it is before you make it fly! No creature should be allowed to forget what and where it came from!”

“Why?” said the raven, with the coolness of a man turning a roast that is browning too fast.

“Because it will grow proud, and cease to recognise its superiors.”

No man knows it when he is making an idiot of himself. I spoke like one repeating a lesson in a dead language, sure of my grammar and empty of the taste of what I said.

“Where DO the worms come from?” asked the raven, as if curiosity had suddenly opened in him like a cupboard.

“Why, from the earth, as you have just seen!” I answered.

“Yes, last!” he replied. “But they can’t have come from it first—for that will never go back to it!” He looked up.

I looked up also and saw nothing but a little dark cloud with edges red as if with sunset.

“Surely the sun is not going down!” I cried. The light had changed in a way I could not account for; it was as if the day had been seasoned with something bitter.

“Oh, no!” returned the raven. “That red belongs to the worm.”

“You see what comes of making creatures forget their origin!” I said, with warmth enough to cook my own words.

“It is well, surely, if it be to rise higher and grow larger!” he returned. “But indeed I only teach them to find it!”

“Would you have the air full of worms?”

“That is the business of a sexton. If only the rest of the clergy understood it as well!”

In went his beak again through the soft turf, and out came another worm, slick with loam. He tossed it, and away it flew. The air for a moment was full of a faint, clean rustle, like silk brushed the wrong way.

I turned—only to feel my stomach drop. Behind me there was no creeper-covered porch, no familiar door, no nook of house-wall with its domestic angles. There was forest: tall pines crowding close, their trunks dark from rain, their needles combing the wind with a hiss. The smell was resin and wet bark, and beneath it the cold mineral scent of stone.

I cried out, dismay breaking through my throat. I had said, not a minute ago, that I would not leave the house—and already I was a stranger, not even in a garden gone wrong, but in a world that did not bother to pretend it knew me.

“What right have you to treat me so, Mr. Raven?” I demanded. “Am I, or am I not, a free agent?”

“A man is as free as he chooses to make himself, never an atom freer,” answered the raven.

“You have no right to make me do things against my will!”

“When you have a will, you will find that no one can.”

“You wrong me in the very essence of my individuality!” I persisted, clinging to my phrases like a man clutching a recipe he cannot cook by.

“If you were an individual I could not, therefore now I do not,” he said. “You are but beginning to become an individual.”

My eyes were already searching the pines for some unaccountable glimmer—some thread of the old place—some way back. But what was “back” now? How could I call that house HOME, where every door and window opened into OUT, and even the garden refused to stay inside its fences?

I suppose my face betrayed me.

“Perhaps it may comfort you,” said the raven, “to be told that you have not yet left your house, neither has your house left you. At the same time it cannot contain you, or you inhabit it!”

“I do not understand you,” I replied, angry because my words, my inherited words, failed to hold their meaning. “Where am I?”

“In the region of the seven dimensions,” he answered, making a curious noise in his throat and fluttering his tail, as if adjusting his coat. “You had better follow me carefully now for a moment, lest you should hurt some one!”

“There is nobody to hurt but yourself, Mr. Raven! I confess I should rather like to hurt you!”

“That you see nobody is where the danger lies,” he said. “But you see that large tree to your left, about thirty yards away?”

“Of course I do: why should I not?” I answered, too sharply.

“Ten minutes ago you did not see it, and now you do not know where it stands!”

“I do.”

“Where do you think it stands?”

“Why THERE, where you know it is!”

“Where is THERE?”

“You bother me with your silly questions!” I cried. “I am growing tired of you!”

“That tree stands on the hearth of your kitchen, and grows nearly straight up its chimney,” he said.

“Now I KNOW you are making game of me!” I returned, with a laugh of scorn that sounded thin in the pine hush.

“Was I making game of you when you discovered me looking out of your star-sapphire yesterday?”

“That was this morning—not an hour ago!”

“I have been widening your horizon longer than that, Mr. Vane; but never mind!”

“You mean you have been making a fool of me!”

“Excuse me: no one can do that but yourself!”

“And I decline to do it.”

“You mistake.”

“How?”

“In declining to acknowledge yourself one already. You make yourself such by refusing what is true, and for that you will sorely punish yourself.”

“How, again?”

“By believing what is not true.”

The forest listened. In the needles there was a thin, continual whisper, a kind of green static. It was not the honest sound of a hedgerow full of birds; it was a larger hush, an acoustical ceiling pressing down. I found myself craving the homely noises of the house—the clink of crockery, the soft scold of kettle-lid, the steady human rhythm that keeps a mind from roaming into dark places. Here, the silence had teeth.

“Then, if I walk to the other side of that tree,” I said, “I shall walk through the kitchen fire?”

“Certainly,” replied the raven. “You would first, however, walk through the lady at the piano in the breakfast-room. That rosebush is close by her. You would give her a terrible start!”

“There is no lady in the house!”

“Indeed! Is not your housekeeper a lady? She is counted such in a certain country where all are servants, and the liveries one and multitudinous!”

“She cannot use the piano, anyhow!”

“Her niece can: she is there—a well-educated girl and a capital musician.”

“Excuse me; I cannot help it: you seem to me to be talking sheer nonsense!”

“If you could but hear the music!” he said, and his voice softened oddly, as if he were remembering a sound that had fed him. “Those great long heads of wild hyacinth are inside the piano, among the strings of it, and give that peculiar sweetness to her playing!—Pardon me: I forgot your deafness!”

The word struck me. Deafness. Not of ear, perhaps, but of something in me that could not catch what was being offered. I thought of languages I had heard in markets abroad—full of consonants like stones in a bowl—beautiful, alive, and meaningless to me, because I had never learned the mouth-shape of their sense. Here was a world speaking, and I had only the dominant tongue of my own certainties. No wonder everything tasted wrong.

“Two objects,” I said, grasping at a law like a man clutching a salt cellar, “cannot exist in the same place at the same time!”

“Can they not?” said the raven, with mild surprise. “I did not know!—I remember now they do teach that with you. It is a great mistake—one of the greatest ever wiseacre made! No man of the universe, only a man of the world could have said so!”

“You a librarian, and talk such rubbish!” I cried. “Plainly, you did not read many of the books in your charge!”

“Oh, yes!” he answered. “I went through all in your library—at the time, and came out at the other side not much the wiser. I was a bookworm then, but when I came to know it, I woke among the butterflies. To be sure I have given up reading for a good many years—ever since I was made sexton.—There! I smell Grieg’s Wedding March in the quiver of those rose-petals!”

He moved as he spoke, and I found myself beside a rose-bush that, a moment before, I could have sworn was not there. Its leaves were wet and dark; the buds were heavy with rain. I leaned in and listened hard. I heard no music—no thread of melody, not even the faintest ghost of it. Only the pine-hush behind, and the near, intimate tick of droplets falling from petal to leaf.

But I smelt something I had never smelt in any rose: still rose-odour, yes—honeyed, green, with that faint spice at the back of the nose—but altered by something that was not in the blossom. It was as though a procession had passed through the scent, leaving behind an imprint of polished wood, warm cloth, and a sweetness like crushed hyacinth-stems. I could not have named it had he not named it first. His word gave my nose permission to know.

When I looked up, the bird was beside me, close enough that I could see the rain beaded along the scales of his legs.

“Mr. Raven,” I said, and meant it, “forgive me for being so rude: I was irritated. Will you kindly show me my way home? I must go, for I have an appointment with my bailiff. One must not break faith with his servants!”

“You cannot break what was broken days ago!” he answered.

“Do show me the way,” I pleaded.

“I cannot,” he returned. “To go back, you must go through yourself, and that way no man can show another.”

I argued, entreated, tried to bargain with him as if he were a stableman and I a man late for a train. It was all useless. His refusal was as final as the last scrape of a pot when the food is gone.

So I stood there, the resinous air in my mouth, and accepted what I could not alter. How was life to be lived in a world where even place would not keep its word? Yet there would be—must be—adventure. The thought steadied me like bread in the stomach. Whether I found my way home or not, I would at least have the rare knowledge of two worlds.

I had never yet done anything to justify my existence; my former world was nothing the better for my sojourn in it. Here, however, I must earn, or in some way find, my bread—bread that would have its own smell, its own crust and crumb, its own language in the mouth. The body cannot live on notions. If this land meant to keep me, it would have to feed me; and if it fed me, then it would have claim on me in return.

A strange fairness lay in that, as in the old bargains of village life: you eat, you owe; you are sheltered, you work. Only I did not know the measures here, nor the names of the grains.

“There is no hurry,” said the raven, who had been regarding me as if he could hear the clatter inside my skull. “We do not go much by the clock here. Still, the sooner one begins to do what has to be done, the better! I will take you to my wife.”

“Thank you. Let us go!” I answered; and at once he led the way under the pines, while the forest whispered overhead like a great, listening roof.

I went after him into the pine-forest, and the world narrowed to resin and shadow.

The air there had a clean bite, as if someone had split fir-logs all night and left their pale wounds to sweeten the dark. Needles carpeted the ground, giving back our footfalls softened and swallowed, while above us the boughs held their own low, chapel-like hush. Only the raven’s wings now and then made a dry, deliberate sound—paper on paper—so that I began to feel the forest not merely as a sight but as an instrument, tuned to quiet; a place where any careless noise would bruise the mind.

We went deeper. The trunks grew thicker, older, each tree more like a face that had lived long enough to forget how to pretend. Some were knotted into grotesque gestures, as if age had turned their limbs into a language of its own. Then, gradually, the pines stood farther apart; the shade thinned and the scent changed—less pitch, more open air, with a faint dryness like heather warmed in sun.

My guide halted on the edge of a heath and pointed with his beak.

“You see that hawthorn?” he said.

I looked where the wood broke and the open land began, and I answered what my eyes insisted on offering me: “I see a gnarled old man, with a great white head.”

“Look again,” he returned. “It is a hawthorn.”

“It seems indeed an ancient hawthorn,” I said, still half resisting what he required of my seeing; “but this is not the season for the hawthorn to blossom!”

“The season for the hawthorn to blossom,” he replied, “is when the hawthorn blossoms.”

The way he said it was not argument but an overturning, like tipping a pot and watching the contents obey gravity without caring what the label claims. The blossoms were there all the same—white as sifted flour, dense as foam. Their smell reached me even at a distance, not the thin prettiness I expected out of season, but a thick, living sweetness edged with something sharp, like apple peel bruised on a stone.

“That tree,” he went on, “is in the ruins of the church on your home-farm.”

The words landed with the strange certainty of a remembered taste returning to the tongue before the mind admits it is hungry.

“You were going to give some directions to the bailiff about its churchyard, were you not, the morning of the thunder?”

I found the moment in me as if I had opened a cupboard and touched a familiar bowl in the dark. “I was going to tell him I wanted it turned into a wilderness of rose-trees,” I said, “and that the plough must never come within three yards of it.”

“Listen!” said the raven, and for an instant even he seemed to hold his breath.

I listened.

At first there was only the forest’s own careful silence, the kind that has weight. Then—so faint that I could not have sworn to it in any other place—I caught something that might have been the sighing of a far-off musical wind, or else the ghost of music that had once been glad and had not yet learned to be sad. It hovered like a melody half remembered after waking, when the mind can still taste it but cannot speak it.

Or did I indeed hear anything?

“They go there still,” said the raven.

“Who goes there?” I asked. “And where do they go?”

“Some of the people who used to pray there,” he answered, “go to the ruins still.”

“But they will not go much longer, I think.”

“What makes them go now?”

“They need help from each other to get their thinking done, and their feelings hatched,” he said, as if speaking of something as ordinary as kneading dough until it learns to hold together. “So they talk and sing together; and then, they say, the big thought floats out of their hearts like a great ship out of the river at high water.”

The image would not leave me—hearts as harbours, thought as a vessel that cannot be launched alone. And behind it, like a warning I could taste as bitterness under sweetness, was the sense that such gatherings were becoming rare: that whatever words they used when they met, whatever old turns of phrase their grandparents’ mouths had carried, were thinning, being replaced by the loud, easy currency of elsewhere. Even a hymn, shouted in a borrowed tongue, can become a kind of noise.

“Do they pray as well as sing?” I asked.

“No,” said the raven. “They have found that each can best pray in his own silent heart.—Some people are always at their prayers.—Look! look! There goes one!”

He pointed right up into the air.

A snow-white pigeon was mounting, with quick and yet quicker wing-flap, the unseen spiral of an ethereal stair. The sunlight caught it and made the air around its wings tremble, flashing and quivering, as if brightness itself were beating time.

“I see a pigeon,” I said, foolishly literal, as if naming it could keep my world from being altered.

“Of course you see a pigeon,” rejoined the raven, “for there is the pigeon! I see a prayer on its way.—I wonder now what heart is that dove’s mother! Some one may have come awake in my cemetery!”

“How can a pigeon be a prayer?” I asked. “I understand, of course, how it should be a fit symbol or likeness for one; but a live pigeon to come out of a heart!”

“It MUST puzzle you!” he returned. “It cannot fail to do so!”

“A prayer is a thought,” I insisted, clinging to my definitions the way a man clings to a recipe he has never actually cooked from. “A thing spiritual!”

“Very true!” said the raven. “But if you understood any world besides your own, you would understand your own much better.—When a heart is really alive, then it is able to think live things.”

His voice had that dry certainty again, like a knife that knows the grain of wood.

“There is one heart,” he went on, “all whose thoughts are strong, happy creatures, and whose very dreams are lives. When some pray, they lift heavy thoughts from the ground, only to drop them on it again; others send up their prayers in living shapes, this or that, the nearest likeness to each. All live things were thoughts to begin with, and are fit therefore to be used by those that think. When one says to the great Thinker:—‘Here is one of thy thoughts: I am thinking it now!’ that is a prayer—a word to the big heart from one of its own little hearts.—Look, there is another!”

This time he pointed not upward but down—toward the foot of a block of granite, half sunk into the earth as if the heath had tried to swallow it.

There, in a crease of soil and stone, was a little flower.

I had never seen one like it. I felt it before I understood it, as you can feel a fragrance and be caught by a memory you cannot translate into speech. Its form was gracious and trusting, its colour not loud but certain—pale rose-hue like the inside of a shell, and in the centre a golden heart, warm as if it had stored sunlight for leaner hours. It carried an odour as of a new world that was yet the old: something like crushed strawberry leaf and young honey, with an undertone of rain on warm dust. The scent did not merely enter the nose; it seemed to enter the attention, making every other thought fall quiet so that this one could be heard.

“That is a prayer-flower,” said the raven.

“I never saw such a flower before!” I said, and the words sounded blunt beside what I felt.

“There is no other such,” he answered. “Not one prayer-flower is ever quite like another.”

“How do you know it a prayer-flower?”

“By the expression of it,” he said. “More than that I cannot tell you. If you know it, you know it; if you do not, you do not.”

His refusal was not unkind; it was like telling a man you cannot give him your tongue and ear and childhood, cannot lend him the secret weight of a word that has only ever lived in one mouth.

“Could you not teach me to know a prayer-flower when I see it?” I asked.

“I could not,” said the raven. “But if I could, what better would you be? you would not know it of YOURSELF and ITself! Why know the name of a thing when the thing itself you do not know? Whose work is it but your own to open your eyes?”

Then, as if the universe itself had a voice and it used his, he added, “But indeed the business of the universe is to make such a fool of you that you will know yourself for one, and so begin to be wise!”

I stood there, looking down at the flower tucked against the granite, and I did see—at least I saw that it was different from any flower I had ever seen before. And because I could not make it fit any remembered category, I knew I must be seeing, in its tenderness and its scent, a shadow of a prayer in it.

A great awe came over me to think of the heart listening to the flower.

We had gone long across a moor of broken stone and stiff, dead stalks, the kind of ground that gives back nothing but a dry rasp beneath the boot and a smell of old peat long since burnt out. Moss lay in patches like faded cloth. The light had not yet failed, yet the sun was already muffled—wrapped in a grey lid of cloud that kept the day from ever quite warming. The wind came with a peculiar chill, not the honest cold of height or season, but as if it had slipped through a door left ajar on a room where night never ended.

Far off, a single cottage showed itself, small and low against the heath—so distant it seemed at first a darker knot in the pallor of the horizon.

“Here we are at last!” said the raven at my side. His voice had that dry, amused edge it always carried, like a twig snapping between clever fingers. “What a long way it is! In half the time I could have gone to Paradise and seen my cousin—him, you remember, who never came back to Noah! Dear! dear! it is almost winter!”

“Winter!” I cried. The word tasted wrong against what I remembered. “It seems but half a day since we left home!”

“That is because we have travelled so fast,” he returned. “In your world you cannot pull up the plumb-line you call gravitation, and let the world spin round under your feet! But here is my wife’s house! She is very good to let me live with her, and call it the sexton’s cottage!”

The thought of a sexton set my mind searching for its usual furniture: church, stones, gate, the hush of clipped grass and the particular quiet that belongs to the dead when the living have agreed to be gentle.

“But where is your churchyard—your cemetery—where you make your graves, I mean?” I asked, looking round. There was nothing but heath, flat as a board, and wind.

He stretched his neck and held his beak straight out, then slowly swung it, point by point, through every quarter of the compass, saying nothing.

My eyes followed that beak as if it were a needle drawing a circle, and the circle widened and widened until it had no rim. Without wall, without gate, without cross or stone, the whole moor became a burial-ground. Wherever the dreary wind ran its cold fingers, there lay his cemetery. He was sexton of all he surveyed—keeper of what had been laid aside. The horizon itself, low and starless, seemed a boundary not built but endured.

I stood in the burial-ground of the universe: its compass the unenclosed heath, its wall the grey edge of seeing. It was as if I had walked out of spring and summer, out of autumn and sunshine, and into the winter waiting for me—had set out in the prime of my youth and found myself already at the last season.

But the place corrected me even as the thought formed. The day there was not a day as I had known it; it was a turning wheel that carried all seasons in its slow revolution. Winter slept there through the night in a winding-sheet of ice; with a childlike smile, Spring woke at dawn; at noon Summer blazed abroad; and with the long change of afternoon, old Autumn crept in and died at the first breath of the vapourous, ghostly night.

As we neared the cottage, the clouded sun rushed down the steepest slope of the west as if eager to be rid of the sky. He sank while we were still a few yards from the door. At the instant his light went out, cold struck me—almost with weight, as if a pale hand had closed round my chest. I staggered over the threshold like a man escaping an icy death that had already caught his sleeve. Behind me the wind swelled and flung itself at the door; I fought it shut, feeling its insistence like an animal’s shoulder. Then, abruptly, all was still.

The cottage held a silence that was not emptiness but shelter, as if thick walls had caught the outside world’s hiss and worrying moan and pressed it into harmlessness. The change was so sudden that I noticed the sound of my own breath, and the small tick of something cooling.

A candle burned on a deal table in the middle of the room. Its flame made a little golden pond of light with edges of brown shadow. The air carried the clean scent of wax and dried wood, and beneath it a faint tang as of earth that has been turned and left to settle. Against the wall stood what I took at first for the lid of a coffin set upright. Even that seemed fitting, and made my skin tighten.

But it opened.

It was a door, and a woman entered.

She was all in white—as white as new-fallen snow. Yet her face, though as pale as her dress, did not suggest cold. At once it suggested warmth: the warmth of milk just drawn, of bread before it crusts, of linen folded from sun and wind. Her features might have been perfect; but her eyes made me forget to measure them. All life in her face and person was gathered and concentrated there, and what had gathered became light.

If death was coming for her, it had made her luminous; but in her eyes there was life enough for a nation. They were large and dark—dark not like soot but like depth, ever deepening as I gazed. A whole night-heaven seemed condensed in each pupil; within that blackness stars flashed; and round it, as a horizon, lay an iris like eternal twilight coiled and held.

What any eye is, God only knows. Hers looked as if they had come straight out of his own hand—fresh made, not inherited. The still face might have been primeval perfection; the living eyes were continuous creation.

“Here is Mr. Vane, wife!” said the raven.

“He is welcome,” she answered, in a low, rich, gentle voice. It was the kind of voice that carries not merely words but their native country: its vowels had valleys, its consonants stones warmed in the mouth. I felt in it buried treasures of immortal sound—tones too old to be fashionable and too true to be sold. After the outer wind’s harshness, the sound of her speech was like stepping from a street of iron into a kitchen where the only noises are knife on board, kettle beginning to murmur, and the soft thud of dough being worked.

I stared, and could not speak.

“I knew you would be glad to see him!” added the raven.

She stood in front of the door by which she had entered, and did not come nearer, as if distance were part of her courtesy.

“Will he sleep?” she asked.

“I fear not,” he replied; “he is neither weary nor heavy laden.”

“Why then have you brought him?”

“I have my fears it may prove precipitate.”

“I do not quite understand you,” I said. Uneasy foreboding pressed against my ribs like that earlier cold, yet with it came a thin hope of escape—as if misunderstanding might be a door.

“Surely a man must do a day’s work first!” I protested.

I looked into the white face of the woman and my heart fluttered, like a bird caught between wanting to flee and wanting to trust the hand that would hold it. She returned my gaze in silence, and that silence had a weight of its own, like a loaf resting on a board, proving its readiness without argument.

“Let me first go home,” I resumed, scrambling for the habits of my own world, “and come again after I have found or made, invented, or at least discovered something!”

“He has not yet learned that the day begins with sleep!” said the woman, turning to her husband. “Tell him he must rest before he can do anything!”

“Men,” he answered, “think so much of having done, that they fall asleep upon it. They cannot empty an egg but they turn into the shell, and lie down!”

At his words my eyes shifted from the woman to him—and the shift broke something.

I saw no raven.

In his place stood the librarian—the same slender elderly man I had known, in a rusty black coat, large in the body and long in the tails. Before, I had seen only his back; now I saw his face for the first time. It was so thin the bones shaped it plainly, suggesting the skulls his last-claimed profession must have made him familiar with. Yet I had never seen a face so alive, or a look so keen and friendly as that in his pale blue eyes—eyes that wore a haze, as if they had done much weeping and could not entirely forget the salt.

“You knew I was not a raven!” he said, smiling.

“I knew you were Mr. Raven,” I replied; “but somehow I thought you a bird too!”

“What made you think me a bird?”

“You looked a raven, and I saw you dig worms out of the earth with your beak.”

“And then?”

“Toss them in the air.”

“And then?”

“They grew butterflies, and flew away.”

“Did you ever see a raven do that? I told you I was a sexton!”

“Does a sexton toss worms in the air, and turn them into butterflies?”

“Yes.”

“I never saw one do it!”

“You saw me do it!—But I am still librarian in your house, for I never was dismissed, and never gave up the office. Now I am librarian here as well.”

“But you have just told me you were sexton here!”

“So I am. It is much the same profession. Except you are a true sexton, books are but dead bodies to you, and a library nothing but a catacomb!”

“You bewilder me!”

“That’s all right!”

He stood silent for a few moments. The woman stood silent too, motionless as a statue, by the coffin-door. In the stillness I became aware of the candle’s small, steady sound—yes, sound, a faint thread of burning—and beyond it, through the thick walls, the moor-wind trying the world with its long, low breath. The cottage seemed to choose what it would admit and what it would refuse.

“Upon occasion,” said the sexton at length, “it is more convenient to put one’s bird-self in front. Every one, as you ought to know, has a beast-self—and a bird-self, and a stupid fish-self, ay, and a creeping serpent-self too—which it takes a deal of crushing to kill! In truth he has also a tree-self and a crystal-self, and I don’t know how many selves more—all to get into harmony. You can tell what sort a man is by his creature that comes oftenest to the front.”

He turned to his wife. I watched him more closely now. He was above the ordinary height, and stood more erect than when last I saw him. His face was very pale—pale like her own. The nose handsomely encased the beak that had withdrawn within it. His lips were very thin, without colour, but beautifully curved; about them quivered a shadowy smile that held humour and love and pity together, as if none could be true without the others.

“We are in want of something to eat and drink, wife,” he said; “we have come a long way!”

“You know, husband,” she answered, “we can give only to him that asks.”

She turned her unchanging face and radiant eyes upon me, and it came to me with a sudden shame how often I had taken without asking—how often I had used the very words of my tongue as if they were mine by right, not gifts that require humility. Her gaze asked for no coin but speech.

“Please give me something to eat, Mrs. Raven,” I said, “and something—what you will—to quench my thirst.”

“Your thirst must be greater before you can have what will quench it,” she replied; “but what I can give you, I will gladly.”

She went to a cupboard set into the wall. Its door opened with a quiet wooden sigh, and the sound seemed old, as if that cupboard had been opening on the same errand for generations. She brought out bread and wine and set them on the table with a care that made the simple act ceremonial. The bread’s crust was browned, not hard but decisive; it carried the smell of grain that has known sun and wind and the patient turning of soil. The wine held a dark light in it, as if it remembered summer even here.

We sat down to the perfect meal.

I ate, and the bread and wine went deeper than hunger and thirst. The bread had a sweetness that was not sugar but field; the wine warmed like a small hearth laid inside the breast. With each mouthful I felt something in me loosen—anxiety and discomfort slid away as if unclenched. In their place came expectation, a quiet that listened. I became sharply aware of the sonic cleanliness of the cottage: no clatter of needless machines, no manufactured music insisting on itself, no loudness pretending to be joy. Only the soft dealings of life—swallowing, breathing, the candle’s whisper, and far off the wind, now more like surf than threat.

I grew very sleepy. Only then did I first feel weary, as if the body, granted permission, remembered it was mortal.

“I have earned neither food nor sleep, Mrs. Raven,” I said, “but you have given me the one freely, and now I hope you will give me the other, for I sorely need it.”

“Sleep is too fine a thing ever to be earned,” said the sexton; “it must be given and accepted, for it is a necessity. But it would be perilous to use this house as a half-way hostelry—for the repose of a night, that is, merely.”

As he spoke, a wild-looking little black cat sprang up on his knee. Its fur had the dull shine of soot, and its eyes were like small green lamps. He patted it as one pats a child to make it go to sleep; and yet the motion struck me strangely—as if he were patting down sod upon a grave, smoothing it lovingly, humming an inward lullaby that needed no tune.

“Here is one of Mara’s kittens!” he said to his wife. “Will you give it something and put it out? she may want it!”

The woman took it gently from him. The cat did not struggle; it yielded as if it understood her hands. She gave it a little piece of bread—small, but offered with the same gravity as the whole loaf—and went out with it, closing the door behind her with a sound as soft as a page turned.

“How then am I to make use of your hospitality?” I asked.

“By accepting it to the full,” he answered.

“I do not understand.”

“In this house no one wakes of himself.”

“Why?”

“Because no one anywhere ever wakes of himself. You can wake yourself no more than you can make yourself.”

The words struck at something deep and stubborn in me—my old pride in doing, in beginning, in claiming the first motion as mine.

“Then perhaps you or Mrs. Raven would kindly call me!” I said, still not understanding, but feeling again that vague foreboding—stronger now, because sleep was drawing near and with it the surrender of control.

“We cannot.”

“How dare I then go to sleep?” I cried. The question came out like a child’s, and I hated that it was true.

“If you would have the rest of this house, you must not trouble yourself about waking. You must go to sleep heartily, altogether and outright.”

My soul sank within me. He sat looking me in the face. His eyes seemed to say without words, Will you not trust me? And I, staring back, heard in the quiet between us the whole moor’s wind held at bay, and the steadier, kinder rhythm of this room—a place where sound itself had been disciplined into mercy.

“I will,” I answered.

“Then come,” he said. “I will show you your couch.”

As we rose, the woman came in again. She took up the candle. The flame leaned toward her as if it loved her. She turned to the inner door and led the way. I went close behind her, and the sexton followed.

The moment I crossed the threshold, cold met me as if I had walked into the belly of an ice-house—air so clean it bit, so dry it seemed to scour the palate like crushed snow. The door fell to behind us with a hush that swallowed the last familiar sound, and in that sudden stillness the sexton leaned toward his wife and said something under his breath.

She turned.

What had happened to her in the few minutes since I last saw her was beyond any easy telling. It was as if the great splendour that used to blaze from her eyes had overflowed its bounds and filled her whole face; not a feverish flush, but a lucent quiet, like light held in milk. Her hands, too—those hands that had seemed carved for gentle labour—shone pale as pearl, every little “helmet” of nail catching the candle’s frail flame and returning it with a moonstone gleam. The sight struck me with the same pressure as a scent too strong in a close room: beautiful, overpowering, almost suffocating. I was glad when she turned that radiance away.

The candlelight reached no farther than a few cautious steps. At first the place was only blackness and cold—an immense, stored cold, like a larder cut in rock where winter is kept for summer’s need. Then the flame fell on something that glimmered just above the floor.

A bed?

Who could sleep in such mortal chill? If anything here did sleep, no wonder it did not wake of itself.

Beyond that first shine there came another, fainter, as though the darkness were sprinkled with frost. And then—uncertain at first, then undeniable—gleams on every side. We took a few paces and the candle disclosed the thing beneath the shimmer: a human form under a sheet, straight and still. I could not tell whether man or woman; the light seemed to slide away from the face as we passed, as if unwilling to touch it.

We were walking, I saw, along an aisle of couches. On almost every one, with its head toward the passage, lay something asleep—or dead—covered with a sheet white as sifted flour. My mind, that had been loud with questions, became quiet with dread. Aisle after aisle we went, couches without count. I could see only a handful at once, but they were on all sides, vanishing as if the chamber had no walls—only distance.
