Was this where my choice of bed waited? Must I lie down among the unwaking, with no one to rouse me? Was this the sexton’s library, and were these his books—human volumes closed and shelved on stone?

Truly it was no half-way house: this was a store-room of the unanswering.

“One of the cellars I am placed to watch,” remarked Mr. Raven, and his voice was low, as if even breath were noise enough to offend. “Much wine is set here to ripen!—But it is dark for a stranger!” he added.

“The moon is rising; she will soon be here,” said his wife.

Her clear voice, low and sweet, sounded like an old song hummed while hands are busy—something sorrowful, yet long since folded and put away, as one lays dried herbs between paper to keep their scent without letting them dominate a room.

Even as she spoke, the moon looked in through an opening in the wall, and a thousand little gleams answered her. The sheets took her light the way salt takes moonlight by a coast—coldly, instantly. Yet even with that added brightness I could not descry beginning or end. Couches stretched away and away, as if the whole disparted world had been given each a narrow bed, each a private silence.

At first I thought their sleep was simply death. But as the moon climbed, and the pallid faces emerged from shadow into her bluish glare, I knew it was something deeper still—something I did not know, and had no name for.

The moon rose higher and shone through other openings. Still I could never see enough at once to understand the chamber. Now it seemed a cathedral nave with its long aisles of devotion; now a huge barn repurposed as a dwelling of tombs. The moon herself looked colder than any moon I had known, and where she shone straight upon the sleepers, she cast an icy glaze upon the sheets and the features—though it might have been that the faces made the moon so cold.

Of such as I could see, all were alike in the brotherhood of this sleep; all were unlike in the histories written upon them. Here lay a man who had died—though this was not death, and I had no other word—at the height of strength: his dark beard flowed from his frozen countenance like a stream released from a glacier’s lip; his forehead was smooth as polished marble; a shadow of pain lingered around his mouth, but it was only a shadow, thinned as a remembered bitterness in a dish long since sweetened.

On the next couch lay a girl, passing lovely to behold. The sadness of parting had not yet been wholly absorbed into peace, but absolute submission possessed her placid features. There was no sign of wasting disease, no mark of “killing care or grief of heart.” If pain had visited, it had been charmed asleep as utterly as a bruise that no longer aches when the body is healed past reminding.

Many were beautiful, lying very still—some mere children; but I saw no infant. The most beautiful of all was a lady whose white hair—and that alone—suggested she was old when first she fell asleep. On her stately countenance rested not submission, but a noble acquiescence, an assurance firm as the foundations of the universe that all was as it should be.

On some faces lingered the nearly erased scars of strife, the faint marrings of hopeless loss, the last shadows of sorrows that must once have seemed unendurable. It was as if dawn had begun its work but had not yet melted every rim of frost. Those faces were few, and each seemed to plead, “Pardon me: I died only yesterday!” or, “Pardon me: I died but a century ago!” That some had lain thus for ages I knew—not merely by their unutterable repose, but by something for which I had neither word nor symbol, as untranslatable as the taste of a childhood broth remembered years later in a foreign land.

At last we came to three empty couches. Immediately beyond them lay the form of a beautiful woman a little past her prime. One arm was outside the sheet, and her hand lay palm upward. In the centre of it was a dark spot.

Next to her lay the stalwart figure of a man in middle age. His arm too was outside the sheet, the strong hand almost closed as if still gripping the hilt of a sword. I thought he must be a king who had fallen fighting for truth.

“Will you hold the candle nearer, wife?” whispered the sexton, bending to examine the woman’s hand.

The candle moved, and its little flame made the dark spot look darker.

“It heals well,” he murmured to himself. “The nail found in her nothing to hurt!”

At last I ventured to speak, though my voice felt out of place here, like a spoon clinking in a sanctuary of soundless snow.

“Are they not dead?” I asked softly.

“I cannot answer you,” he replied, still subdued. “I almost forget what they mean by DEAD in the old world. If I said a person was dead, my wife would understand one thing, and you would imagine another.—This is but one of my treasure vaults,” he went on, “and all my guests are not laid in vaults: out there on the moor they lie thick as the leaves of a forest after the first blast of your winter—thick, let me say rather, as if the great white rose of heaven had shed its petals over it. All night the moon reads their faces, and smiles.”

“But why leave them in the moonlight?” I asked. “Would it not corrupt them?”

“Our moon,” he answered, “is not like yours—the old cinder of a burnt-out world; her beams embalm the dead, not corrupt them. You observe that here the sexton lays his dead on the earth; he buries very few under it! In your world he lays huge stones on them, as if to keep them down; I watch for the hour to ring the resurrection-bell, and wake those that are still asleep. Your sexton looks at the clock to know when to ring the dead-alive to church; I hearken for the cock on the spire to crow; ‘AWAKE, THOU THAT SLEEPEST, AND ARISE FROM THE DEAD!’”

The words, in that enormous hush, sounded less like preaching than like an old refrain—one that had been carried through generations, mouthed even when its meaning was lost, and yet still held power over those who remembered the dialect of its first singing. I began to suspect the self-styled sexton was in truth an insane parson; the whole thing was too mad.

But how was I to get away from it? I was helpless. In this world of sleepers the raven and his wife were the only living I had yet seen. Whither should I turn for help? I felt myself lost in a space larger than imagination; and a thought came, swift and sickening, that if here two things could occupy the same place—if realities could interpenetrate without mingling—why not twenty, why not ten thousand? I dared not follow that thought to its end.

“You seem in your dead to see differences beyond my perception!” I ventured.

“None of those you see,” he answered, “are in truth quite dead yet, and some have but just begun to come alive and die. Others had begun to die, that is to come alive, long before they came to us; and when such are indeed dead, that instant they will wake and leave us. Almost every night some rise and go. But I will not say more, for I find my words only mislead you!”

He stopped beside the three empty couches.

“This is the couch that has been waiting for you,” he said, and pointed to one.

“Why just this?” I asked, and I began to tremble—more with the cold than fear, I told myself, though the lie tasted thin.

“For reasons which one day you will be glad to know.”

“Why not know them now?”

“That also you will know when you wake.”

“But these are all dead, and I am alive!” I objected, shuddering.

“Not much,” rejoined the sexton with a smile, “—not nearly enough! Blessed be the true life that the pauses between its throbs are not death!”

“The place is too cold to let one sleep!” I said.

“Do these find it so?” he returned. “They sleep well—or will soon. Of cold they feel not a breath: it heals their wounds.—Do not be a coward, Mr. Vane. Turn your back on fear, and your face to whatever may come. Give yourself up to the night, and you will rest indeed. Harm will not come to you, but a good you cannot foreknow.”

We stood by the couch: the sexton and I at its side; his wife at its foot with the candle. Her eyes were full of light still, but her face had gone back to a still whiteness; it no longer poured radiance.

“Would they have me make of a charnel-house my bed-chamber?” I cried aloud, and my voice broke the air like a dropped pan in a quiet kitchen. “I will not. I will lie abroad on the heath; it cannot be colder there!”

“I have just told you that the dead are there also, ‘Thick as autumnal leaves that strow the brooks in Vallombrosa,’” said the librarian.

“I will NOT,” I cried again.

In the compassing dark the two of them gleamed out like spectres that waited on the dead. Neither answered me. Each stood still and sad, and looked at the other. The silence around us did not merely lack sound; it held sound at bay, as if this place were an acoustic sanctuary where even fear ought to tread softly.

“Be of good comfort; we watch the flock of the great shepherd,” said the sexton to his wife.

Then he turned to me.

“Didst thou not find the air of the place pure and sweet when thou enteredst it?” he asked.

“Yes; but oh, so cold!” I answered.

“Then know,” he returned, and his voice grew stern, “that thou who callest thyself alive, hast brought into this chamber the odours of death, and its air will not be wholesome for the sleepers until thou art gone from it!”

They went farther into the great chamber, the candle dwindling as they withdrew, and I was left alone in the moonlight with the sleepers.

I turned to escape.

What a long way it was back through the dead—or through those not dead, those not yet awake. At first I was too angry to be afraid; my indignation burned like pepper on a raw tongue. But as I went on, and the anger cooled, the still shapes grew terrible. The moon lay on the sheets like frost. Every face was a mute record in a language I could not read, yet somehow I understood the power of it—the way a dominant word can crush a lesser one into silence, and with it a whole history of taste and tone and home.

At last, with loud offence to the gracious quiet, I ran. I fled wildly along the narrow ways, and bursting out, flung to the door behind me.

It closed with an awful silence.

I stood in pitch-darkness. Feeling about me, I found another door and opened it. A dim lamp-light met me, as familiar as the smell of old leather.

I stood in my library, with the handle of the masked door in my hand.

Had I come to myself out of a vision—or lost myself by going back to one? Which was real: what I now saw, or what I had just ceased to see? Could both be real, interpenetrating yet unmingling—like two airs in one room, each with its own smell, each insisting it was the only true atmosphere?

I threw myself on a couch and fell asleep.

In the library there was one small window to the east. At this time of year the first rays of the sun struck through it upon a mirror, whence they were reflected straight to the masked door. When I woke, there they shone—thin gold, clean and insistent—and thither they drew my eyes.

With the feeling that behind it must lie the boundless chamber I had left by that door, I sprang up and opened it.

The light, like an eager hound loosed, shot before me into the closet and pounced upon the gilded edges of a large book.

“What idiot,” I cried, “has put that book in the shelf the wrong way?”

But the gilded edges caught the sun and flung it on a nest of drawers in a dark corner. One of them was half open.

“More meddling!” I cried, and went to close it.

It was stuffed with old papers, so full it would not shut. I took out the topmost. The moment I saw the hand, my own breath changed; it was my father’s writing, and of some length. The words on which my eyes first fell made me suddenly hungry to know the whole, as if I had caught a first whiff of something long absent from the house—a spice my mother used, a name spoken in the old way, a taste that could not be translated into any other tongue.

I carried it into the library, sat down in one of the western windows, and read what follows.

Awe is a troublesome guest: it seats itself at the table unbidden and will not be sent away. The day had laid out its plain fare with a lavish hand—the sun pouring its gold as steadily as broth from a tilted ladle, the sea below taking it like a blue bowl that could never quite be filled. The wind came in clean from the water, salted and sharp as if it had passed through a cellar of seaweed and stone; gulls wrote their thin cries across it, and the steady hush of the surf kept time like a patient breathing in a sleeping house.

Yet all the while I knew the brightness for a curtain—handsome cloth, cleverly painted—ready at any moment to be whisked up, and the stage behind it to show a deeper world.

It was not long after my father’s death that this knowing began to trouble my days. One morning I sat in the library, where the smell of leather and dust had always seemed to me a kind of old family food—dry, yes, but sustaining; the books packed close like loaves cooling on a shelf. I had been staring, with a listless hunger for origins, at the portrait hung among them: a face I knew only as “an ancestor,” and therefore as a name without a taste. I wanted—absurdly, fiercely—to know what sort of man he had been, what manner of air he had breathed, what words he had spoken in his own dialect of life.

I took a volume down and began to read, not because the page drew me, but because the motion steadied my hands. When I glanced up, it was with the mild start of one who hears a footfall where no one should be. A man was coming toward me—yet not between me and the door, but between me and the portrait, as if he had stepped out from the painted past and taken the room for a corridor.

He was thin and very pale, dressed in rusty black as if his coat had been long hung in a chimney corner where smoke had browned it and time had fretted the nap. His face was sharp, eager, cut by a notable nose that brought to mind—ridiculously, inevitably—a jug my sisters had once christened Mr. Crow, because its spout curved like a beak.

“Finding myself in your vicinity, Mr. Vane,” he said, and his voice had a peculiar cadence—dry but not unpleasant, like paper rubbed between the fingers—“I have given myself the pleasure of calling. Your honoured grandfather treated me—as I may say without presumption—as a friend, having known me from childhood as his father’s librarian.”

I did not at the moment count the years that must have sat behind such a sentence. Death had lately rearranged my sense of time; it had made everything at once too short and too long.

“May I ask where you live now, Mr. Crow?” I said, and heard the foolishness as soon as it left my mouth.

He smiled, amused rather than offended, as if I had attempted his name in a tongue I did not quite possess.

“You nearly hit my name,” he returned. “Which shows the family insight. You have seen me before, but only once, and could not then have heard it.”

“Where was that?”

“In this very room. You were quite a child, however.”

The library seemed suddenly larger, as if the walls had stepped back to make room for what my memory might have forgotten. I could not be sure I remembered him; yet for an instant there stirred in me a faint recognition—like a flavour half-recalled from an old dish you ate in childhood, when you cannot name the herb but your mouth knows it.

“I beg you to set me right as to your name,” I said.

“There is such a thing as remembering without recognising the memory in it,” he remarked, as one might speak of tasting without knowing the spice. “For my name—which you have near enough—it used to be Raven.”

I had heard that name; marvellous tales had carried it to me as kitchen gossip carries a rumour from house to house, changing in the telling but keeping its heat.

“It is very kind of you to come and see me,” I said. “Will you not sit down?”

He sat at once, as if the chair had been waiting for him. His movements were quick, economical—those of a man who has lived long among shelves and secret doors.

“You knew my father, then, I presume?” I asked.

“I knew him,” he answered with a curious smile that did not quite belong to courtesy, “but he did not care about my acquaintance, and we never met.—That gentleman, however,” he added, pointing with a long finger toward the portrait, “old Sir Up’ard, his people called him—was in his day a friend of mine yet more intimate than ever your grandfather became.”

Only then did the strangeness of the interview begin to take shape. But why should it be stranger that he remembered Sir Upward, than that he had been my great-grandfather’s librarian? Both were improbable in the same breath.

“I owe him much,” he went on. “For although I had read many more books than he, yet through the special direction of his studies he was able to inform me of a certain relation of modes which I should never have discovered of myself, and could hardly have learned from any one else.”

“Would you mind telling me all about that?” I said. Grief had made me greedy: for any fact, any story, any link—anything that would not dissolve at a touch.

“By no means—as much at least as I am able: there are not such things as wilful secrets,” he answered, and began as if opening a cupboard that had long been kept locked.

“That closet held his library—a hundred manuscripts or so, for printing was not then invented. One morning I sat there, working at a catalogue of them”—and in my mind I saw his careful hand moving over parchment as a cook’s hand might move over a ledger of stores—“when he looked in at the door, and said, ‘Come.’ I laid down my pen and followed him—across the great hall, down a steep rough descent, and along an underground passage to a tower he had lately built, consisting of a stair and a room at the top of it.

“The door of this room had a tremendous lock, which he undid with the smallest key I ever saw.”

As he spoke, the library seemed to listen; even the house settled its creaks, as if it wanted to hear.

“I had scarcely crossed the threshold after him, when, to my eyes, he began to dwindle, and grew less and less. All at once my vision seemed to come right, and I saw that he was moving swiftly away from me. In a minute more he was the merest speck in the distance, with the tops of blue mountains beyond him, clear against a sky of paler blue. I recognised the country, for I had gone there and come again many a time, although I had never known this way to it.

“Many years after, when the tower had long disappeared, I taught one of his descendants what Sir Up’ard had taught me; and now and then to this day I use your house when I want to go the nearest way home. I must indeed—without your leave, for which I ask your pardon—have by this time well established a right of way through it—not from front to back, but from bottom to top!”

The words had the cool assurance of a man discussing a path across a field, a gate left unlatched. Yet they set a strange pressure in my head, as if he were asking me to accept an untranslatable experience in the language of ordinary rooms.

“You would have me then understand, Mr. Raven,” I said, “that you go through my house into another world, heedless of disparting space?”

“That I go through it is an incontrovertible acknowledgement of space,” returned the old librarian.

“Please do not quibble, Mr. Raven,” I rejoined. “Please to take my question as you know I mean it.”

“There is in your house a door, one step through which carries me into a world very much another than this.”

“A better?”

“Not throughout; but so much another that most of its physical, and many of its mental laws are different from those of this world. As for moral laws, they must everywhere be fundamentally the same.”

“You try my power of belief!” I said. The sea outside kept its immense whispering; it seemed suddenly like a great ear pressed to the window.

“You take me for a madman, probably?”

“You do not look like one.”

“A liar then?”

“You give me no ground to think you such.”

“Only you do not believe me?”

“I will go out of that door with you if you like: I believe in you enough to risk the attempt.”

“The blunder all my children make!” he murmured, more to himself than to me. “The only door out is the door in!”

His sentence fell in my mind like a proverb in a dead dialect: I could hear its weight and shape, and yet it would not quite yield me its sense. I began to think him cracked.

He sat silent for a moment, head resting on his hand, elbow on the table, eyes on the backs of books—those patient, closed mouths.

“A book,” he said louder, “is a door in, and therefore a door out.—I see old Sir Up’ard,” he went on, closing his eyes, “and my heart swells with love to him:—what world is he in?”

“The world of your heart!” I replied. “That is, the idea of him is there.”

“There is one world then at least on which your hall-door does not open?”

“I grant you so much; but the things in that world are not things to have and to hold.”

“Think a little farther,” he rejoined. “Did anything ever become yours, except by getting into that world?—The thought is beyond you, however, at present!—I tell you there are more worlds, and more doors to them, than you will think of in many years!”

He rose abruptly, as if the time for talk were a mere garnish and the real meal awaited elsewhere. He left the library, crossed the hall, and went straight up toward the garret with the confidence of one who knows every turn by the sound of his own footfall. I followed, studying his back as if it might tell me what his face would not. His hair hung long and dark, straight and glossy, like a spill of ink. His coat was wide and reached to his heels. His shoes seemed too large, as if he had stepped into them from some other life and never quite grown to fit.

In the garret, light came through at the edges of the great roofing slabs in thin blades, and dust floated in them like flour shaken in a beam over a baker’s board. There were places where no flooring lay; we must step from joist to joist, the hollow below breathing up a cool, dry scent of old wood. The house here had a different voice: the small clicks of settling timbers, the whisper of grit under our soles, the far thud of a shutter moving in the sea-wind.

In the middle of one of the open spaces rose a partition with a door. Through it I followed Mr. Raven into a small, obscure chamber whose top contracted as it rose and went slanting through the roof, as if the room itself were trying to press its head into the sky.

“That is the door I spoke of,” he said, pointing.

It was an oblong mirror, standing on the floor and leaning against the wall. I went before it and saw our figures dimly reflected in its dusty face. It looked old-fashioned and neglected; the frame had that air of an heirloom shoved aside. Yet on its top perched an eagle with outstretched wings—carved, severe, and somehow threatful, like a heraldic predator frozen at the moment of strike.

“As a mirror,” said the librarian, “it has grown dingy with age; but that is no matter: its clearness depends on the light.”

“Light!” I rejoined. “There is no light here!”

He did not answer, but began to pull at a little chain on the opposite wall. I heard a creaking, slow and complaining like a mill-wheel turned after long idleness: the top of the chamber was revolving.

He ceased pulling, looked at his watch with an exactness that felt almost culinary—timing a thing that must not be overdone—and began to pull again.

“We arrive almost to the moment!” he said. “It is on the very stroke of noon!”

The top went creaking and turning for a minute or so. Then he pulled two other chains—now this, now that—and returned to the first, as if setting unseen shutters, angling unseen mirrors, tasting the air for readiness.

A moment more and the chamber grew clearer. A patch of sunlight had fallen upon a mirror on the wall opposite that against which the other leaned, and on the dust I saw the path of the reflected rays drawn like a narrow road. The ray struck the mirror on the ground—

—and from it none were returned.

There was nowhere in the chamber a second patch of light. The sunbeam had gone as cleanly as water through a sieve, leaving no spill, no glimmer, no warmth on the boards. My skin tightened with a kind of cold wonder.

“Where are the sunrays gone?” I cried.

“That I cannot tell,” returned Mr. Raven; “back, perhaps, to where they came from first. They now belong, I fancy, to a sense not yet developed in us.”

Then he began to talk—of the relations of mind to matter, and of senses to qualities—in a manner I could follow only a little, the way one follows a recipe spoken too quickly in an unfamiliar tongue. He went on to stranger things I could not at all comprehend: dimensions beyond the three that my hands and eyes had always trusted; powers in us like dormant flavours in an uncrushed seed, present but inaccessible until some proper bruising releases them. His words slid over my understanding; they did not take hold. I thought he hardly knew what he was saying. The chamber’s acoustic seemed to shift as he spoke, as if the very air had changed its grain; the creak of the turning roof sounded farther off, the house’s small noises muffled, like a kitchen suddenly thick with steam.

Suddenly I became aware that our forms had gone from the mirror. Its face, where we should have been, seemed full of a white mist, as if the glass had turned into a shallow, breathing cloud.

I stared. Beyond the mist something began to show—first faintly, then more clear—the tops of a range of mountains. They grew distinct with a steadiness that did not feel like imagination: blue upon blue under a sky of paler blue, as Mr. Raven had described. Soon the mist vanished entirely, uncovering the face of a wide heath. At some distance on it was the figure of a man moving swiftly away.

I turned to address my companion.

He was no longer by my side.

A dread, sharp as the smell of scorched fat, rose in me. I looked again at the moving form within the mirror and recognised the wide coat flying and the long black hair lifting in a wind that did not touch me—wind that made no sound in my ears, as if it belonged to another acoustic world altogether.

And then terror, simple and bodily, took me by the throat.

I rushed from the place.

I laid the manuscript down, and for a time its paper-smell—dust, ink long dried, the faint sourness of old paste—was enough to steady me. My father had not merely brushed the edge of that other country: he had known Mr. Raven. That comfort came like broth to an empty stomach; it warmed first, and then it revealed the ache beneath.

For I remembered, with a sudden shame that tasted like iron on the tongue, that I had never been told how my father died, nor why there was always a pause—like a door gently closed—whenever his end approached in conversation. A thought pushed up, inexorable: he must at last have followed Mr. Raven, and not come back. And I—fool that I was—had fled.

What might I not have learned by now of life and death and the wide fields beyond ordinary seeing? The Ravens were good folk. A night in their house would not have harmed me. Strange, yes; but his strangeness had the clean edge of faculty, and hers the calm of a beauty that made one lower one’s voice without knowing why. And I had suspected them—treated them as if they harboured a plot against me, as if hospitality were a trap.

The more I chewed on my behaviour, the more bitter it grew. Why should I have feared such dead? Their stillness—sweet, stately—returned to me like the remembered fragrance of bread cooling under linen: you do not eat it yet, but it calls you to patience. To share their holy rest would have been an honour; I had proved myself unworthy. What could that sleeping king have done me? What could the lady with the wound in her palm have done—she whose hand had lain open, quiet as a bowl waiting?

I fell longing after their faces, and wept; and weeping, I threw myself on a couch and slid at once into sleep, as if my grief had been a tide pulling me under.

I woke as suddenly as I had fallen, with the sharp conviction of having been called—no voice I could name, but an insistence as definite as a knock. The house lay still as an empty church, its very timbers holding their breath. Outside, a blackbird sang on the lawn: clear notes dropped into the silence and spread through it, each sound cleanly bounded, as if the air itself were a cup that could be filled without spilling. The song made me think how noise—mere noise—can bruise the mind, and how this single bird, unbought and unadvertised, restored order more truly than any performed music in a bright hall with its clapping and talk.

“I will go and tell them I am ashamed,” I said aloud, “and will do whatever they would have me do.”

I rose and went straight up the stairs to the garret. The wooden chamber was as it had been when first I saw it: boards dry as old bones, the dim mirror returning everything with a film over it, like breath on glass. It was nearly noon; the sun would be a little higher than when first I came. I must raise the hood a little and adjust the mirrors accordingly. If only I had watched Mr. Raven do it—seen the small sure motions, the way one watches an elder’s hands when he makes something that cannot be learned from words alone.

I pulled the chains and let the light fall upon the first mirror. Then I turned to the other. There were the shapes of the former vision—plain enough to recognize, and yet all tremulous, like a landscape seen in a pool ruffled by the faintest breathing wind. I touched the glass: it yielded nothing, hard and sealed. Suspecting polarisation—some shifting of relation that would make the one surface speak truly to the other—I altered and altered them, tiny changes, angle after angle, as one keeps tasting a sauce and adding a grain of salt, a drop of acid, until, not by inspiration but by patient correction, the thing comes right. At last, in great degree—so far as I was concerned—by chance the relations fell into place, and the mountains stood blue, steady, and clear between the mirrors.

I stepped forward. My feet were among the heather.

The air there had the scent of crushed thyme and cold stone. Each stalk that brushed my boot released its own small bitter-sweetness, as if the ground itself were a pantry of dried herbs. I knew only that the way to the cottage had passed through a pine-forest. I pushed through thickets and small fir-woods, forever thinking I recognized a rise of ground, a twist of stream, a particular boulder—only to find my certainty crumble. I met no great forest. The day slipped; the sun sank; the air sharpened with the coming winter until it felt like drinking water from a metal cup.

Then, when the sun was near the horizon and my hope had begun to thin, I saw a little black object coming toward me. It bobbed and glided with that peculiar decision of a bird on business.

It was the raven.

Relief flooded me so strongly I nearly laughed, but my shame held me. I hastened to meet him.

“I beg your pardon, sir,” I said, “for my rudeness last night. Will you take me with you now? I heartily confess I do not deserve it.”

“Ah!” he returned, and looked up at me—one bright bead of an eye taking in more than my face. After a brief pause he said, “My wife does not expect you to-night. She regrets that we at all encouraged your staying last week.”

The words fell like cold ash. “Take me to her that I may tell her how sorry I am,” I begged, forcing humility into my voice as one forces down a mouthful that will not go.

“It is of no use,” he answered. “Your night was not come then, or you would not have left us. It is not come now, and I cannot show you the way. The dead were rejoicing under their daisies—they all lie among the roots of the flowers of heaven—at the thought of your delight when the winter should be past and the morning with its birds come: ere you left them, they shivered in their beds. When the spring of the universe arrives—but that cannot be for ages yet! how many, I do not know—and do not care to know.”

His speech had a cadence like an old dialect heard in childhood: half familiar, half beyond translating. The words were mine, yet their meanings kept slipping, as if he spoke in the deeper sense of them and I in the shallow. I could grasp only the sting: that my fear had chilled those I had wronged.

“Tell me one thing, I beg of you, Mr. Raven,” I said, hunger rising in me like a physical pain. “Is my father with you? Have you seen him since he left the world?”

“Yes; he is with us, fast asleep. That was he you saw with his arm on the coverlet, his hand half closed.”

The image returned at once—linen, a hand resting as if it had just set down a tool. My throat tightened. “Why did you not tell me? That I should have been so near him, and not know!”

“And turn your back on him!” corrected the raven.

The rebuke struck home. “I would have lain down at once had I known!”

“I doubt it. Had you been ready to lie down, you would have known him! Old Sir Up’ard, and your twice great-grandfather, both are up and away long ago. Your great-grandfather has been with us for many a year; I think he will soon begin to stir. You saw him last night, though of course you did not know him.”

“Why of course?” I demanded, smarting.

“Because he is so much nearer waking than you. No one who will not sleep can ever wake.”

“I do not at all understand you!”

“You turned away, and would not understand!”

I held my peace. His words were like a dish made in a tongue you do not speak: you can smell the richness, you can tell it nourishes, but you cannot name the ingredients. Yet if I said nothing, he would go, and I could not bear that.

“And my grandfather—is he also with you?” I asked.

“No; he is still in the Evil Wood, fighting the dead.”

“Where is the Evil Wood, that I may find him?”

“You will not find him; but you will hardly miss the wood. It is the place where those who will not sleep, wake up at night, to kill their dead and bury them.”

“I cannot understand you!”

“Naturally not. Neither do I understand you; I can read neither your heart nor your face. When my wife and I do not understand our children, it is because there is not enough of them to be understood. God alone can understand foolishness.”

At that, I felt stripped—naked and of small worth, as if my very language had been taken from me and I must stand silent in another’s market. The power in his words was not loud; it was the quiet authority of one whose meanings are rooted deeper than the listener’s.

“Then,” I said at last, “will you be so good as show me the nearest way home? There are more ways than one, I know, for I have gone by two already.”

“There are indeed many ways.”

“Tell me, please, how to recognise the nearest.”

“I cannot,” answered the raven; “you and I use the same words with different meanings. We are often unable to tell people what they need to know, because they want to know something else, and would therefore only misunderstand what we said. Home is ever so far away in the palm of your hand, and how to get there it is of no use to tell you. But you will get there; you must get there; you have to get there. Everybody who is not at home, has to go home. You thought you were at home where I found you: if that had been your home, you could not have left it. Nobody can leave home. And nobody ever was or ever will be at home without having gone there.”

“Enigma treading on enigma!” I cried, anger flaring to cover fear. “I did not come here to be asked riddles.”

“No; but you came, and found the riddles waiting for you! Indeed you are yourself the only riddle. What you call riddles are truths, and seem riddles because you are not true.”

“Worse and worse!” I exclaimed.

“And you must answer the riddles!” he continued. “They will go on asking themselves until you understand yourself. The universe is a riddle trying to get out, and you are holding your door hard against it.”

“Will you not in pity tell me what I am to do—where I must go?”

“How should I tell your to-do, or the way to it?”

“If I am not to go home, at least direct me to some of my kind.”

“I do not know of any. The beings most like you are in that direction.” He pointed with his beak.

I looked. I could see nothing but the setting sun, its glare whitening the world and making a mockery of sight. The day’s last light had the harshness of a bright shop-window: it showed, but it did not reveal.

“Well,” I said bitterly, “I cannot help feeling hardly treated—taken from my home, abandoned in a strange world, and refused instruction as to where I am to go or what I am to do!”

“You forget,” said the raven, “that, when I brought you and you declined my hospitality, you reached what you call home in safety: now you are come of yourself! Good night.”

He turned and walked slowly away, his beak toward the ground as if listening to the earth with it. I stood dazed. It was true: I had come of myself. Yet had I not come to atone? My heart ached; my brain held neither quest nor purpose—no hope with a name, no desire with a direction. I gazed after him and would have followed, but a knowledge like lead settled in me: it was useless.

All at once he pounced upon a spot, throwing the whole weight of his body on his bill, and for some moments dug vigorously, scraping and thrusting as if he were unearthing a buried seed. Then, with a flutter of wings, he flung back his head, and something shot from his bill, cast high into the air.

That moment the sun set. The air darkened at once, as if a hand had drawn a curtain. But the thing he had thrown opened into a soft radiance, and came pulsing toward me like a fire-fly—only larger, and with a yellower, fuller light, not sharp but mellow as lantern-light through skin of ripe fruit.

It flew over my head.

I turned and followed it.

Here I must break the straight thread of my telling, because what follows is forever resisting the words I have. To set it down is like trying to write a forgotten dialect with borrowed letters: the shapes may resemble the sounds, but the life of it escapes between. The things I record were, in their nature and in the nature of the creatures concerned in them, so different from any events of this economy, that I can only present them by the forms and language of this world—by the way they struck my senses and altered my heart. Even so, I write under a continual sense of failure. At best I can give one phase of a meaning that had many, like rings within rings; one circle of embodiment where there were graduated spheres.

A single thing would sometimes seem to be and to mean many things at once, with an uncertain identity at the core, shifting and changing its look. I am driven, again and again, to set down what I know is but a clumsy representation of the feeling aimed at—none of our media fit to carry the peculiar strangeness with clearness or certainty. Even to one who knew the region better than I, I could not be sure of transmitting the reality of my experience. For while I had no doubt I was regarding a scene of activity, I might at the same moment be conscious—as if with another sense—that I was perusing a metaphysical argument, sounded rather than read, like meaning conveyed through an air that had its own acoustics, its own laws of listening.

As the air thickened to ink and winter cinched its hard belt around the land, the little fluttering fire that had lured me on burned the brighter, then checked its flight as if to count my breaths. When I stepped into its radiance it moved again—slowly now, lingering above stony patches where the ground showed through like bone under thin flesh. Each time I lifted my eyes it seemed larger, nearer, as though it were not merely leading me but tasting my attention, drawing it out as steam draws scent from a hot pot.

Plainly it was a bird-butterfly: it had the swallow’s double stroke, yet the body was slight and bookish, and the wings—nearly square—flashed the whole rainbow as if every colour were a spice rubbed fresh and released at once. There were gradations too, delicate as the difference between toasted and burnt, and some hues I had no names for, as if they belonged to a dialect of light my tongue had never learned.

I watched it too greedily. My boot caught a low rock; I fell and lay stunned, cheek on cold stone, the taste of peat-dust in my mouth. When sense returned, the creature hovered above my face, pouring down its chord of light in layered notes. I rose and followed, but still I stared upward instead of minding my steps, and my foot struck a stone so sharply pain jumped to my knee.

Fearing a worse fall, I sat in the heather to steady myself and only look. A hunger woke in me—an absurd, intimate longing to have that splendour in my hand, to hold it as one holds a warm loaf just out of the oven, close, possessive, smelling of its own life. To my unspeakable delight it began to sink toward me. Slowly at first, then swiftly, it came, growing as it came, as if the treasure of the universe had decided to be mine.

I put out my hand, and had it.

The instant my fingers closed, the light went out. Darkness, thick as pitch and lidless. In my palm lay something dead-cold and heavy—a book, boards spread like wings, but without one glimmer left in it. I flung it up in horror, only to hear it thud among the heather, a dull sound swallowed immediately by the great hush.

I bent forward and buried my face in my hands. Misery sat on me as solid as frost. Yet the cold went on sharpening, drawing heat from my bones as salt draws water from meat; and fear of being frozen forced me to stand.

The moment I rose, a faint sense of light stirred—not in the air so much as in me. “Is it coming to life?” I cried, and hope stabbed quick as hunger. But no: it was the edge of a moon peering up—keen, sharp—over a level horizon.

She brought me light, but no guidance.

She would not hover; she would not wait while my steps faltered; she offered only an indifferent seeing, as if I must choose my way from whatever her cold eye happened to show. When she rose to a full face, I began to make out the land: westward of her, not far, a range of low hills broke the horizon like the rim of a great dish. I set out for them.

What a night lay between.

The moon did not behave like the one I had known on earth. Her face was strange, her light stranger still—thin, aloof, as though it came from an unknown sun and had lost something in translation on the way. Worst of all was her staring. Every time I looked up, she was looking down as if with all her might, as if my being there in her night was a question she could not stop asking. At first I was nettled, as at the rude attention of a fellow creature; then I fancied in her gaze a sort of wondering pity. Why was I out, awake, moving, when everything around me seemed made for burrowing and hiding?

Then the thought struck me with a chill deeper than the frost: what an awful thing it is to be awake in the universe. I WAS, and could not help it.

Underfoot, the heather thinned. My boots sank into a bare spongy soil like dry, powdery peat—crumbly, scenting faintly of old rot and rain long gone. It gave a little heave beneath me, not the steady give of turf but a living lift, as if I walked on the skin of something vast. Ahead, in the low moon, I saw a shadowy ripple—like an earthquake-wave—running away from me across the plain.

It passed into the distance; while I stared after it, a single wave rose up and came back toward me. A yard or two away it burst, and out of it scrambled an animal like a tiger. Clots of mould hung about its mouth and ears, as if it had been kneading itself through compost; its eyes winked and flamed, and it rushed at me showing white teeth in a soundless snarl.

I stood rooted, not brave, not afraid—merely caught, as one is caught by the sight of a knife lifted without any sound. The creature turned its head to the ground and plunged into it as into water.

“That moon is affecting my brain,” I said aloud when my feet could move again. “What life can be here but the phantasmic—the stuff of which dreams are made? I am walking in a vain show!”

So I tried to keep my heart above the waters of fear, not knowing that the very light I distrusted was my defence from the realities I took for phantoms. The moon, with her cold stare, was binding the monsters as salt binds flesh; without her, I would scarcely have taken a second step on that hideous, heaving earth.

“I will not be appalled by that which only seems!” I told myself—and yet it is a terrible thing to walk on a sea when you know there are fishes beneath that might rise at any moment.

Near my feet, the head of a worm began to push up from the soil—slowly, insistently—as big as a polar bear’s head, and much resembling it, with a white mane set on a red neck. The writhing draws by which its immense length extricated itself were horribly methodical, like a rope being pulled through mud. I dared not look away. When at last the tail came free, it lay as if exhausted, wallowing and making feeble effort to burrow again.

“Does it live on the dead,” I wondered, “and is it unable to hurt the living? If they scent their prey and come out, why do they leave me unharmed?”

I know now it was because the moon paralysed them.

All that night as I walked, hideous creatures—no two alike—threatened me from the restless ground. In some of them, beauty of colour enhanced the loathsomeness of shape: one large serpent was covered, head to far tail, with feathers of glorious hues, as if it had rolled in a spilled casket of dyes and come up preening. The air remained unnaturally hushed. Even when something lunged, there was no proper sound of it—no honest scuff, no cry, no breaking of twigs—only the faintest suggestion of movement, as though the world had been robbed of its own language and could only mouth at me in silence.

That silence itself began to press upon my mind. On earth a night has a score of small noises—a wind in grass, a bird shifting, water talking to stone. Here, the lack of such homely speech made every shape more dreadful, as if the land were under a rule forbidding it to testify. I found myself craving, absurdly, the simplest domestic sound: the clink of a spoon against a bowl, the sigh of a kettle, the respectful crackle of a hearth. Without them, the soul feels unmoored, and the body listens too hard.

By degrees I grew so accustomed to the hurtless menaces that I began to beguile the way by inventing monstrosities of my own, as if naming horrors—making a private lexicon—could give me mastery. I never suspected that each moment of my life was held on the thin edge of that moonlight.

For light is yet light, though it be only the last of a countless series of reflections.

How differently would my feet have carried me over that moving soil had I understood that, if I were still within their range when her lamp ceased to shine on that cursed spot, I should in that instant be at the mercy of such as have no mercy—swallowed into a writhing heap of hideousness, each thing as terrible as before it had seemed, only now no longer “seeming.”

Fool of ignorance, I watched the descent of the weary, solemn, anxious moon down the widening vault above me with no worse uneasiness than dread of losing my way—where as yet I had, indeed, no way to lose.

The low hills I had chosen for my goal were near. The moon was sinking toward their skyline when the soundless wallowing ceased, and the burrow—if burrow it was—lay motionless and bare, as if the land had drawn in its breath.

Then I saw, walking slowly over the pale soil, the form of a woman.

A white mist floated about her, now gathering to the shape of a garment, now losing it again as a wind—always the same wind, like a servant that hates its master—dogged her steps, stealing the covering as soon as it formed. She was beautiful; yet on her countenance lay such pride and misery together that I could hardly trust my sight. Up and down she walked, striving with hungry fingers to catch the mist and wrap it round her. The eyes in the beautiful face were dead, as if whatever language they once spoke had been erased. On her left side was a dark spot, and now and then she pressed her hand there, as if to stifle pain or sickness.

Her hair hung nearly to her feet. Sometimes the wind mixed it with the mist so I could not distinguish one from the other; then it would fall and gather itself again, and in the moonlight it shone pale gold—thin as old straw kept too long in a loft, yet still catching light.

Suddenly she pressed both hands upon her heart and fell. The mist rose from her body and melted away as if relieved to be free.

I ran to her.

But she began to writhe in such torture that I stopped, helpless, my throat tight. A moment more and her legs hurried from her body—sped away as serpents. From her shoulders fled her arms, serpents also, as though they could not endure to belong to her. Then something flew up from where her head had been, like a bat tearing itself out of a net; and when I looked again, she was gone.

The ground rose like the sea in a storm. Terror seized me—not the clean fear of a shouted warning, but the sickness of silence breaking at last. I turned and ran for the hills.

I was already on the lower slope when the moon sank behind one of their summits and I stepped into shadow as into a cellar. Behind me rose a waste and sickening cry, the only sound I had heard since the dead butterfly fell—a cry of frustrate desire. It shook my heart like cloth snapping on a line in a hard wind. The plain, which had been mute as a stopped mouth, had found one awful syllable and flung it after me.

I looked back. Many dark objects bounded in pursuit.

I made for the crest of a ridge where the moon still shone. She seemed to linger there, as if she would at least grant me enough sight to defend myself. I climbed faster. Crossing the shadow of a rock, I heard them panting at my heels—at last a natural sound, and because it was natural it was worse: breath is the oldest language of hunger.

Just as the foremost threw itself upon me with a snarl of greedy hate, we rushed together into the moonlight.

She flashed out an angry light, and the creature fell from me a bodiless blotch, as if the light had stripped it of all substance. Strength came into my limbs. I turned on the rest. One by one, as they darted into the radiance, they dropped with a howl and became nothing but stains of darkness on the pale ground. And I saw—or fancied—a strange smile on the round face above me, as if she approved the small justice of it, or found it curious.

I climbed to the top of the ridge. Far away the moon shone, sinking toward a low horizon. The air here was pure and strong, like water from a deep well, and in it the silence felt less like a gag and more like a held pause between two honest sounds.

I descended a little way, found it warmer, and sat down to wait the dawn.

The moon went below, and the world again was dark.

Sleep took me like a hand over the eyes. When it let go, the sun was climbing, slow and clear, as if it had all the time in the world to expose how empty mine was.

I went again to the top of the height and looked back. The hollow I had crossed by moonlight lay as smooth and still as a cooled pan, not a tremor of leaf or fur, not a smudge of smoke to say a hearth had ever tried to live there. Yet I could not rid myself of the thought that the calmness might be only a lid—below it, ravenous things in crowds, moving with the blind appetite of mouths. I turned the other way.

Before me stretched a wide waste, the colour of old iron, and far off a stain of deeper tone—green, or something pretending to be green—that might be a wood. No shadow of plough, no scab of roof, no line of beasts, no dust-thread of travel. The sky was a clean bowl with nothing floating in it; even the air looked rinsed.

So I went toward that distant blot of life, because on this side of it there could hardly be anything at all.

When I reached the plain I found I had been walking on the bed of a river that had forgotten how to be wet. Rock everywhere: here flattened and scored like a butcher’s board after long use, there reared in humps and little pinnacles, as if the water, leaving, had pulled shapes up after it and then dropped them. Innumerable channels ran and re-ran, braided and broken, but not one held the least dampness—not even the cool, honest smell of buried moisture that clings to stone after rain. In some grooves lay a dry moss, grey-green and brittle as old herb tied for winter and then forgotten. On certain faces clung lichens, pale and stubborn, almost as hard as the rock itself—poor seasoning, ground too fine and still refusing to melt.

The air that should have been filled with the pleasant clatter and hush of waters was silent—worse than silence, a kind of stopped ear. Yet through the noon, while the sun glared as if polishing everything to a glare, I was haunted by sound: an aural mirage, as palpable as thirst. I heard—distinct as if my head were bent over a pot at full simmer—the voice of many streams. There were little lively runs that chuckled as they went, and broad heavy thrusts of current that made the deep, steady note a river makes when it is at work. My eyes insisted on dryness; my hearing argued back with such confidence that I almost believed the stones were lying.

All day it took me to reach the patch. It was a forest indeed, and when, near sunset, I climbed out of the last rock-worn trench and entered between trees, it was like stepping from an oven into a larder built in shadow. The sun, already below the crowns, sent his rays in at slants between pillar-like boles, laying long bright bars on the ground as if he were showing me where to put my feet.

I had expected pines—some uniform, disciplined stand—but here were many sorts together. Some bore a strong family likeness to trees I knew, though with a turn of branch or thickness of leaf that made them feel like words pronounced in a neighbouring dialect: recognizable, yet carrying a different mouth’s history. Others were marvels, as if an older botany had kept its own names and refused mine.

I threw myself down under what seemed a eucalyptus in blossom. Its scent hit me at once—sharp and medicinal, like crushed leaves rubbed between fingers, then sweetened by something airy and faintly honeyed. But the flowers were wrong in the way a familiar dish can be wrong when the cook has changed one essential spice. Each bloom sat in a hard calyx like a little skull, the top of it lifting like a lid to let the froth-like flower-brain overflow its cup. The sight made my stomach tighten as if I had tasted bitterness where I expected sugar.

From under those falchion leaves my gaze wandered into deep after deep of the forest. At first it seemed spacious, aisles and corridors and glades with room enough to lay out a long table and feed a village. But as the light failed, doors and windows closed. The forest drew in upon itself. The warmth ran away. Cold came, instant and sharp, as if someone had opened a cellar door inside my bones.

Night there was not merely absence of light; it was a thing with texture, like damp wool laid over the world. The tree I leaned against rose straight for a long way before it branched; yet its boughs bent so low that, in the dimming, they seemed eager to fold over me and shut me in like a pot-lid. I sat half drowsing, half staring, letting my eyes drift among the clumps of foliage that, in that uncertain light, began to suggest other shapes than their own.

A light wind began to move—first a cautious stirring, like a hand testing the surface of a fermenting vat. It set a neighbouring tree rocking. Every branch took up the motion; every twig, every leaf contributed its little sound and sway, all of them blending into the larger rhythm. In that moving mass my fancy found a pack of wolves, straining as if against a wizard’s leash. The leaves snapped and hissed like teeth. The whole clump surged and recoiled, savage with effort. I watched, and the more the wind gathered, the more the shapes acquired a dreadful life.

Another heavier mass presented a group of horses’ heads and forequarters thrust out from stalls. Their necks went up and down with impatience, a rhythm that should have been almost comical—like farm horses at feeding time, smelling the bucket—had the heads not been so gaunt, so strange. Several were bare skulls. One had skin drawn tight over bone, like meat dried too close to the fire. One had lost its lower jaw and hung low, unutterably weary; then suddenly it would heave high, as if trying to ease a bit in a mouth that was no longer there. Above them, perched at the end of a branch, floated upright the form of a woman, arms waving in imperious gesture, as if conducting with no music but rage.

The definiteness of these leaf-bodies first surprised me and then began to discompose me. It was as though the forest had a vocabulary of shapes and, in the twilight, was choosing the most violent nouns it knew. What if it should overpower my brain with seeming reality? What if, by looking, I consented?

Darkness thickened. The wind dropped. The shapes folded back into leaf and branch and shadow. The forest shut its mouth. I fell asleep.

It was still dark when I began to be aware of noise far off—a confused rushing, mingled with faint cries, as if a distant market had broken into a riot. It grew. The sound swelled through the trees until the whole wood seemed filled with gathering multitudes. From all sides at once the tumult drew nearer. The place where I lay became, impossibly, the centre of a commotion that extended everywhere.

I scarcely moved hand or foot. I lay as one lies when a wild creature is near—still, listening, trying to make one’s breath small.

At length the moon approached the forest and came slowly into it. With her first gleam the noises increased to a deafening uproar, and dim shapes rose out of the dark around me. As she climbed and brightened, the shapes sharpened, and the sound became more than sound: it pressed on the skin, it rattled the teeth. It was like being inside a vast iron kitchen where a thousand pans were being struck, dragged, dropped—only the clanging had voices in it.

A battle raged around me.

Wild cries and roars of rage, the shock of onset, the long grind of struggle—everything mingled with words, articulated and yet made monstrous by the mouths that uttered them. Curses and credos, snarls and sneers, laughter and mockery, sacred names and howls of hate came huddling together, each trying to drown the other, a chaos of tongues insisting on their own righteousness. I heard phrases that might have been prayers if they had not been flung like stones.

Skeletons and phantoms fought in maddest confusion.

Swords swept through the phantoms; they only shivered, like mist cut by a knife, and surged back. Maces crashed on the skeletons, shattering them hideously; yet not one fell or ceased so long as a single joint held two bones together. Bones of men and horses lay scattered and heaped; and grinding and crunching them under foot, the skeletons fought on. Everywhere charged white steeds, gaunt as famine, their hooves striking sparks in moonlight. Everywhere, on foot or astride battle-horses made of wind-blown vapour, raged and ravened the indestructible spectres. Weapons and hoofs clashed and crushed; jaws that had no flesh and throats that were only suggestion swelled the tumult with the war-cry of every opinion—bad or good—that had ever bred strife and injustice and cruelty in any world.

The holiest words went with the most hating blow. Truths, bent out of shape by lies, flew hurtling like javelins. Every moment some one would turn against his comrades and fight more wildly than before, “THE TRUTH! THE TRUTH!” still his cry—like a cook screaming “purity” while poisoning the pot.

One I noted who wheeled ever in a circle and smote on all sides, unable to stop, unable to choose a single enemy because everything offended him. Now and then, worn out, a pair would sit for a minute side by side—as if taking breath between courses—then rise and renew the fierce combat. None stooped to comfort a fallen one. None stepped wide to spare him. The ground was a chopping-block, and they made mince of one another with endless appetite.

The moon shone till the sun was near, and all the night long I had glimpses of a woman moving above the strife-tormented multitude, as if she floated on a current only she could feel. Now she was on this front, now on that, one outstretched arm urging the fight onward, the other pressed against her side. And always her voice cut through the din with the cruel clarity of a command shouted in a crowded hall:

“Ye are men: slay one another!”

When I saw her dead eyes and the dark spot upon her, I remembered the leaf-woman of the evening before and felt the same sickness rise. She was as much part of the wood’s language as the wolves and horses had been: an old word for domination, spoken without mercy.

Such was the battle of the dead which I saw and heard as I lay under the tree.

Just before sunrise a breeze went through the forest, not like the earlier wind that had played at making shapes, but like a clean draught through a room long shut. A voice cried—not screamed, not raged, but uttered as if it were a proverb older than any quarrel:

“Let the dead bury their dead!”

At the word, the contending thousands dropped noiseless. The uproar ceased as if a hand had turned it off. When the sun looked in, he saw never a bone, never a phantom—only here and there a withered branch that might have been broken by wind or age.

I rose and resumed my journey through as quiet a wood as ever grew out of quiet earth. The morning wind had ceased when the sun appeared, and the trees were silent. Not a bird sang. Not a squirrel, mouse, or weasel showed itself. Not even a belated moth crossed my path. The forest held its breath, and the hush was not peace but a kind of enforced muteness, like a song whose language has been forbidden until the singers forget the words.

As I went, I kept watch over myself and did not dare let my eyes rest on any forest-shape. For all the time I seemed to hear, faint under the silence, sounds of mattock and spade and hurtling bones—as if somewhere, behind the bark of the world, labourers were digging and piling and throwing, busy with the debris of unended wars. Any moment my eyes might open on things I would not see. Daylight prudence muttered that perhaps, to appear, ten thousand phantoms awaited only my consenting fancy—only my mind lending them the names that would make them real.

In the middle of the afternoon I came out of the wood and found before me another net of dry water-courses. At first I thought I had wandered from my line and turned back, but I soon saw I had not; it was another branch of the same vanished river-bed, or a second channel of the same old life, split and lost.

I began at once to cross it. I was in the bottom of a wide channel when the sun set. I sat down to await the moon, and sleepiness came on me like hunger. I stretched myself on the moss.

The moment my head lay down I heard the sound of rushing streams—suddenly, intimately, as if I had put my ear to a cask of fermenting cider and heard it working, or to a shell that carries a remembered sea. All sorts of sweet watery noises came at once: little trickles stitching over stones, broader glides, occasional chuckles where water would have caught at roots. The melody was veiled, molten, as if poured from a ladle in the dark. It sang me into a dreamless sleep.

When I woke, the sun was already up, and the wrinkled country lay widely visible. Covered with shadows, it was striped and mottled like the skin of some wild animal. As the sun rose, the shadows diminished, and it seemed as if the rocks were re-absorbing the darkness that had oozed out of them during the night, drinking it back as dry earth drinks spilled broth.

Hitherto I had loved my Arab mare and my books more, I fear, than live man or woman. Now at length my soul was athirst for a human presence. I longed even for those inhabitants of this alien world whom the raven had so vaguely described as nearest my sort—anyone with a hearth-sound in their speech, anyone whose day was measured by hunger and giving and the small domestic ceremonies that keep memory alive.

With heavy yet hoping heart, and mind haunted by a doubt whether I was going in any direction at all, I kept wearily travelling north-west and by south.

In one of the dry channels I came upon what I took, at first glance, for a low shrub standing guard—one of those humble outposts that tells you there is a greater growth gathered behind it. I went down on one knee and drew close, as I would to a strange loaf whose crust I could not read. It carried a small fruit, bead-bright, with skin too taut to be harmlessly guessed. Hunger is not wisdom; it is only insistence. I did not pluck it.

Had I known then how the air itself was being listened to, how the faint rasp of my sleeve on stone and the soft knock of my breath in my own throat had been taken as evidence, I might have moved differently. From behind the rocks hundreds of eyes were waiting on the hinge of my fingers: would I take it, would I eat, would I prove myself the sort of creature that puts anything into his mouth so long as it is sweet?

A few yards on, I found another plant, a little taller; then another; and presently I understood that they were not shrubs at all but dwarf-trees, a whole modest orchard crouched to the ground as if the wind had taught it thrift. By the time I neared the bank of a second branch of the river-bed, the channels were thick with them, their roots lacing the sand and their branches closing the gaps so that I crossed only with difficulty, leaping what I could and threading through what I could not.

In one hollowed trench there was a sudden rush—like a whole wall of ivy letting go of birds at once—but I saw nothing. The sound was there and gone, leaving a brief cleanliness in the silence, like the after-scent when a pot has boiled over and you’ve wiped the stove before it burns.

Then the land opened into a wide depression that must once have held a lake. On its rim stood larger fruit trees, heavy with something coarse-looking—thick-skinned, dull-coloured globes, as if bred for storage and not for joy. To the left, a forest seemed to pour down and fill the basin; above, the canopy was mixed, but in the hollow the trees were almost all bearers. It had the feel of an old pantry: not decorative, but intent on feeding.

I went a few yards down the slope where grass grew through moss like fine green threads through a loaf, and I laid myself out upon it, tired in bone and mind. A little lower stood a tiny tree, its top close enough to my hand that it felt as if it had leaned toward me. It was full of apples the colour of dawn in a red clay bowl—rosy, polished, no bigger than small cherries. I plucked one.

The skin broke clean under my teeth. The flesh was crisp and wet, sharp as a clear note in an empty room, then melting into a sweetness that seemed to carry its own memory of sun and leaf. I swallowed, and my tongue went looking for the taste again as if it were a word in a half-forgotten dialect.

I was lifting my hand for another when a shout burst out—children’s voices, laughter braided through it, clear and sweet as a brook running over rounded stones.

“He likes our apples! He likes our apples! He’s a good giant! He’s a good giant!”

“He’s a giant!” objected one voice, more severe than the others.

“He IS rather big,” said another, briskly conceding what could not be denied, “but littleness isn’t everything! It won’t keep you from growing big and stupid except you take care!”

I raised myself on an elbow and stared.

Above and about and below me stood a multitude of children, seeming of all ages: some hardly steadied on their own legs, some twelve or thirteen, and three or four that looked older still. The older ones kept together a little apart, less fevered than the rest. The many chattered in knots, declaiming and contradicting one another with the bustle of a city crowd—only the air around them was cleaner, their noise made of bright edges and quick pauses, not the grinding, ceaseless industrial roar that turns thought into fatigue. Their sound belonged to the place the way birdsong belongs to a hedge; it did not drown the world, it revealed it.

I did not speak. I feared the bluntness of my adult voice might frighten them, and besides, I was learning more with my ears than I could with questions. I understood nearly everything they said, though I could not have told you why. But to understand a people is not more wonderful than to love them; and for all my bewilderment, I found myself warmed, as by a kitchen where many hands have been at work and the air still holds the ghost of fruit and bread.

There was a shifting among them, a small opening made, and a little fellow came forward—innocent-faced, with a loving roguery about the eyes. He held out to me a huge green apple.

At once silence fell, as when a spoon stops clinking against a pot and every ear waits for what comes next.

“Eat, good giant,” he said.

I sat up, took the apple, and smiled my thanks. Its weight was wrong in my hand—too heavy for its size, as if it had been swollen with water rather than grown with sun. I brought it to my mouth and bit.

The taste struck like a slap: bitterness under a false, harsh sweetness, and a rot-sourness that seemed to bloom in the back of the throat. My stomach turned with a cook’s instinctive revulsion—the body’s own historian of what will harm the household. Before I could think, I flung it far away.

A shout of delight went up so sudden and high it startled me—delight, not anger. The children hurled themselves upon me as if I had passed some test of theirs. They nearly smothered me with their light bodies and their quick hands. They kissed my face and fingers; they clung to my legs; they swarmed my arms and shoulders, hugging my neck, my head, as if I were a tree they meant to climb and claim.

I went down under them at last, overwhelmed by the lovely little goblins.

“Good, good giant!” they cried. “We knew you would come! Oh you dear, good, strong giant!”

Their babble sprang up again, and every few breaths the jubilant shout rose anew from hundreds of clear little throats. It was a kind of music, communal, ancient in its pattern though made by the newest of beings; a sound that would have been sold and bottled in my own world and played back through tinny devices until it meant nothing.

Then—silence again, so abrupt it felt like a hand over the mouth of the valley.

Those around me drew back. Those who had been on me slid off and began tugging at my arms, trying to set me upright. Concern had drained their faces, as if the air had changed.

“Get up, good giant!” said a little girl, urgent, her voice small but sharp as a knife tapping a board. “Make haste! much haste! He saw you throw his apple away!”

Before she finished, I was on my feet. She stood pointing up the slope.

On the brow of it was a clownish, bad-looking fellow, a few inches taller than I, broad in the shoulders, slack in the mouth. He had no weapon, yet hostility hung from him like a smell. The children—my bright little chorus—vanished as if the earth had opened and swallowed them, leaving only the orchard’s hush and the faint rustle of leaves.

The fellow began to descend. I, wanting better footing, started up. He made a growling noise like an animal that has learned only one syllable of speech and uses it for every grievance.

We met on a more level patch, and I stood waiting. As he came near, he held out his hand.

I would have taken it, foolishly ready for any human-seeming custom of greeting, but he snatched it back, threatened a blow, and thrust it out again, demanding rather than offering. At last I understood: he claimed the apple I had thrown away as his property, his right, his tribute.

I pulled my mouth into a grimace and made a rejecting gesture. His face contorted, and he howled—a sound that seemed to say, with all the wounded pride of a bully, Do you dare tell me my apple was not fit to eat?

“One bad apple may grow on the best tree,” I said.

Whether he took my meaning or only heard contradiction, I cannot tell. He strode nearer, and I set myself. He did not strike at once; his eyes flicked aside, waiting. In that moment I heard, behind me, a footfall that had tried to be silent and failed—another presence parting the grass.

A second giant, much like the first, had been stealing up from my rear. The first rushed me.

I met him with a solid blow in the face—felt the soft shock of flesh, the sting in my knuckles. But the second struck me hard on the back of the head. The world rang for an instant, as if my skull were a bell and their fist the clapper. Between them I was soon overpowered.

They dragged me up into the wood above the valley where their tribe lived. There were huts there—wretched things, thrown together from fallen branches and a few stones, like nests built by creatures that hated their own shelter. Inside one they shoved me down and began to kick.

A woman stood by, watching with indifference. During all my captivity I hardly learned to distinguish the women from the men; they differed so little, as if the same dull mould had been used for both and only the accidents of growth had varied them. More than once I wondered if I had stumbled upon a kind of fungoid people—just enough mind to move, and to show anger and greed, but no more. Their speech, if it was speech, was mostly grunt and harshness; it had the feel of a language whose tender words had died out, leaving only commands and complaints. And without the gentler words, the gentler perceptions seemed to have died also.

Their food was tubers, bulbs, fruits—things that might have been nourishing in skilled hands, roasted patiently, sliced thin, softened with heat, sweetened with time. But they ate them as they were or badly prepared, and to me they were inexpressibly disagreeable: bitter, earthy in the wrong way, raw with a sting like sap. The worst of it was not the taste but the rule: nothing offended them so much as to show dislike. When I hesitated, when I could not force my throat to accept what my senses warned against, the women cuffed me and the men kicked me harder, as if they meant to beat my palate into obedience.

That night I lay on the hut floor hardly able to move. The air smelled of damp wood and bruised roots. Somewhere outside, a wind worried the branches; inside, their breathing was thick and irregular. I slept a good deal despite the pain and woke a little refreshed, the way one does after a fever—alive, but not grateful for it.

In the morning they dragged me down to the valley. They tied my feet with a long rope to a tree, not tight enough to break but tight enough to teach helplessness. Then they pressed a flat stone into my left hand. Its edge was toothed like a saw, a crude tool meant for scraping.

I shifted it to my right. A kick corrected me; they forced it back into my left, making plain that even the choice of hand was not mine. With gestures and snarls they gave me to understand my task: scrape the bark off every branch that bore no fruit. Another kick sealed the instruction, and they left me there.

I set to the dreary labour, not because I revered their command, but because I hoped that, if I satisfied them, they would leave me mostly alone—alone to watch, to listen, to measure the pattern of their comings and goings, and choose my time for escape. The stone rasped; strips of bark came away in reluctant curls. Sap bled pale along the wounds, smelling green and bruised.

Happily, one of the dwarf-trees grew close by. Every other minute I could lean, pluck one of its small fruits, and eat. Each bite was a small mercy: crispness, clean juice, a quick bright flavour that seemed to put strength back into my limbs as surely as broth restores a sick man. I worked and ate, worked and ate, and let the orchard’s living sounds—the whisper of leaves, the distant hush where children had been—remind me there were friends somewhere in this valley, and foes who thought hunger was a weapon.

I had scarcely set my tool again to the bark—scrape, lift, scrape, lift, with the sap’s faint sourness rising like a bruised herb—when I heard, close at hand, a scatter of tiny voices. They were not loud; they were quick, threaded through leaves, and their laughter chimed like a flock of sheep-bells shaken in play. The sound made the wood feel inhabited in a different way: not by weight and thud, but by breath, by the small consonants of feet on moss and the clean punctuation of whistles.

They came out from among the little trees that grew like brushwood between the great trunks—creeping, peering, then suddenly, as if a curtain had been pulled aside, pouring forth until there were scores and scores of them around my knees and up the roots, bright-eyed and brown-limbed, with hair like seed-down and bracken.

I signed to them—clumsy signals with my hands—that the giants had only just gone, that they were near. They answered with a ripple of mirth.

“The wind is quite clean,” said one, and another echoed it, and the phrase ran through them like a tune. “They are too blind to see us!”

A little fellow cocked his head at the rope about my ankles, where it bit and rubbed.

“Do you like that rope?” he asked.

“I want them to think I cannot take it off,” I whispered back, not knowing why I too lowered my voice, as if the air itself could betray us.

“They can scarcely see their own feet!” he cried, and danced where he stood, a tiny heel-drumming on a root. “Walk with short steps and they will think the rope is all right.”

As he capered, one of the bigger girls—still small to me, but among them tall, with a steadier face—dropped to her knees. Her fingers went to the knot. I smiled at the futility of those pretty hands against the giants’ clumsy tying; but she touched the cord as if it were dough she had kneaded a hundred times, felt where it crossed, found the strain, and in a breath the thing lay loose. She had unmade it the way one unthreads a string from a stitched seam—no tugging, no waste.

They made me sit down as if I were another child and not a captive. Then they fed me.

It was not the giants’ coarse fare, flung like fodder, but little fruits—plums no bigger than the end of my thumb, berries with skins tight as lacquer, and something like a quince in miniature whose scent rose before it touched my tongue, all honeyed and floral, with a faint sting like ginger at the back of the throat. They placed them with care, climbing the tree’s roots, reaching up to my mouth, their fingertips cool and quick, leaving on my lips the dust of bloom and crushed leaf. The first bite burst with cold juice, and the world, which had seemed built for bruising, suddenly remembered sweetness.

Afterward the smaller ones began to play with me—tugging at my sleeves, clambering onto my shoulders, sliding down my back, hiding behind my elbows, darting in and out like wrens. Their bodies were light, but their merriment had weight enough to topple any intention of work. When the first group tired, others took their place, and the hours went by in a round of their inventions, each game rising out of the last, as if play were a craft passed down in a lineage older than my own habits of seriousness.
