Toward sunset the acoustic world changed. The high tinkling voices drew back; the wood seemed to hold its breath. From far off came the heavy approach: not one sound but a cadence—thud, drag, thud—each step a dull blow to earth that made the roots under me tremble.

The little people sprang away as if the ground had grown hot. I made haste to wind the rope again about my ankles and knot it as I had seen the giants do, though my fingers were slow and sore.

“We must have a care,” said the girl who had freed me. Her voice was grave now, the way a careful cook’s voice changes when a child reaches toward a pot. “A crush of one of their horrid stumpy feet might kill a very little one.”

“Can they not perceive you at all then?” I murmured.

“They might see something move,” she said. “And if the children were in a heap on the top of you, as they were a moment ago, it would be terrible; for they hate every live thing but themselves—Not that they are much alive either!”

She lifted her face and whistled like a bird—so exact an imitation that the note seemed not made by her throat but borrowed from the branches. The instant the whistle ended, not one of them was to be seen or heard. The girl herself vanished as if the wood had swallowed her whole. Only the quiet remained—quiet with a listening edge.

My master, as doubtless he counted himself, came and loomed. He bent, untied my ankles, and dragged me to the door of his hut. There he threw me down, tied my feet again, gave me a kick—his foot like a blunt log—and left me.

I might then have escaped. The rope was nothing to me now; the knot was no longer a mystery. But I had—at last—friends. Their small hands had put fruit to my mouth; their small voices had made the brutal forest sound like a home. I could not think of leaving them.

“To-morrow,” I told myself, with a delight that was almost hunger, “I shall see them again!”

And indeed, from the moment silence settled in the huts until sleep took me, I heard them whispering all about me—soft sibilants in the grass, tiny breathy calls like insects speaking. I knew, without seeing, that I was watched; and the watch was loving.

After that I think they hardly ever left me quite alone.

The giants I did not come to know at all; I suspect there was scarcely anything in them to know. They never grew friendly, but they were too stupid to invent cruelties beyond what their clumsiness did by accident and habit. More than once I avoided a bad kick by catching a descending foot and giving its owner a fall; when he sprawled, amazed at his own gravity betrayed, he never on that occasion renewed his attempt. Their world was a world of impacts.

The Little Ones’ world was a world of attentions.

While I scraped at bark and laboured in the monotony the giants required, the small folk kept coming and going—amusing and delighting me, taking the misery, and much of the weariness, out of my toil. Every day I grew more loath to leave them. Very soon I loved them more than I can tell.

They did not know much in the giants’ sense—no counting, no measuring, no hoarding of facts like stones in a pocket. Yet they were wise, and seemed capable of learning anything; and their wisdom was woven into their care for one another as surely as a good broth is made from bones and patience.

I had no bed save the bare ground, but almost as often as I woke, it was in a nest of children—one or other of them in my arms, though which I seldom could tell until the light came, for they ordered the succession among themselves. When one crept into my bosom, unconsciously I clasped him there; the rest lay close around me, the smaller nearer, as if warmth were a thing to be distributed with justice. Cold could not find me through that living quilt.

The first thing they did in the morning, and the last before sunset, was to bring the good giant plenty to eat. They fed him as they fed me: not with submission, but with the household rhythm of those who keep someone alive because life matters. Their offerings had the scent of crushed green, of fruit opened by fingernail, of the wood’s own pantry.

One morning I woke and found myself alone.

For a moment the loneliness startled me—an empty plate after one has grown used to company. Then I heard subdued sounds of approach: not the giants’ thud, but the careful, gathered hush of many little steps constrained by love. Presently the girl I had first spoken with appeared from the wood, tallest and gravest of the community, regarded by all as their mother. Behind her came the multitude in jubilation manifest—but silent, lest they should rouse the sleeping giant at whose door I lay.

She carried a boy-baby in her arms.

Hitherto a girl-baby, apparently about a year old, had been the youngest. Three of the bigger girls were her nurses, but they shared their treasure with all the rest. Among the Little Ones, dolls were unknown; the bigger had the smaller, and the smaller the still less, to tend and play with. Care was their play; play was their schooling.

“Lona,” I heard someone breathe—a name passed between them like a word for bread.

Lona came to me and laid the infant in my arms. He opened his eyes, looked at me as if tasting me with sight, closed them again, and fell asleep with his cheek against my wrist.

“He loves you already,” Lona said, her face alight with a pride that was not possessive, only glad.

“Where did you find him?” I asked.

“In the wood, of course,” she answered, eyes beaming as if she had brought in a harvest. “Where we always find them. Isn’t he a beauty? We’ve been out all night looking for him. Sometimes it is not easy to find!”

“How do you know when there is one to find?”

“I cannot tell,” she replied. “Every one makes haste to tell the other, but we never find out who told first. Sometimes I think one must have said it asleep, and another heard it half-awake. When there is a baby in the wood, no one can stop to ask questions; and when we have found it, then it is too late.”

“Do more boy or girl babies come to the wood?” I persisted.

“They don’t come to the wood; we go to the wood and find them.”

“Are there more boys or girls of you now?” I tried again, changing the question as if that might unlock it.

She hesitated, brows drawing together—their sign of a place where language snagged. “I do not know.”

“You can count them, surely.”

“We never do that,” she said, as if I had suggested weighing sunlight. “We shouldn’t like to be counted.”

“Why?”

“It wouldn’t be smooth,” she replied simply. “We would rather not know.”

The answer made me think of the way names can bruise what they claim to describe. In my world we count and label until the living thing becomes a number that can be traded; here even the notion felt like grit between teeth.

“Where do the babies come from first?” I asked, and heard my own voice take on the greedy tone of a historian prying into an archive.

“From the wood—always. There is no other place they can come from.” She knew where they came from last, and thought nothing else was to be known about their advent.

“How often do you find one?”

She smiled at me over the baby’s head. “Such a happy thing takes all the glad we’ve got, and we forget the last time. You too are glad to have him—are you not, good giant?”

“Yes, indeed, I am,” I answered, and it was true: the weight of that sleeping warmth in my arms made my heart ache like hunger.

“But how do you feed him?”

“I will show you,” she said, and went away—returning almost at once with two or three ripe little plums.

She put one to the baby’s lips. “He would open his mouth if he were awake,” she said, and took him back into her arms as naturally as breathing. She squeezed the fruit until a drop rose to the surface—dark gold, fragrant—and touched it again to his mouth. Without waking, he began at once to suck it, small mouth working with a seriousness that made every onlooker quiet. Lona went on, slowly squeezing, careful as if she were pressing oil from olives, until nothing but skin and stone were left.

“There!” she cried in a tone of gentle triumph. “A big-apple world it would be with nothing for the babies! We wouldn’t stop in it—would we, darling? We would leave it to the bad giants!”

“But what if you let the stone into the baby’s mouth when you were feeding him?” I said, thinking of choking hazards and all the crude accidents of bodies.

“No mother would do that,” she answered, almost offended—not by me, but by the imagined carelessness. “I shouldn’t be fit to have a baby!”

As she spoke I saw what she might become: a woman strong in tenderness, a keeper of lives and flavours. Yet the question rose in me like smoke: what became of them when they grew up? Where did they go? And again, behind that: where did they come from first?

“Will you tell me where you lived before?” I said.

“Here,” she replied.

“Have you never lived anywhere else?”

“Never. We all came from the wood. Some think we dropped out of the trees.”

“How is it there are so many of you quite little?” I asked, and my words felt blunt beside their smooth not-knowing.

“I don’t understand,” she said. “Some are less and some are bigger. I am very big.”

“Baby will grow bigger, won’t he?”

“Of course he will!”

“And will you grow bigger?”

“I don’t think so. I hope not. I am the biggest.” Her gaze flicked away, as if the thought made a shadow on the air. “It frightens me sometimes.”

“Why should it frighten you?”

She did not answer.

“How old are you?” I tried, reaching for time as one reaches for a common tongue.

“I do not know what you mean,” she said. “We are all just that.”

“How big will the baby grow?”

“I cannot tell.” Then, with trouble in her voice, she added, “Some begin to grow after we think they have stopped. That is a frightful thing. We don’t talk about it!”

“What makes it frightful?”

She was silent a moment, then said low, “We fear they may be beginning to grow giants.”

“Why should you fear that?”

“Because it is so terrible. I don’t want to talk about it!” She pressed the baby to her bosom with such anxious protectiveness that I dared not press her further. In that embrace was a whole unspoken genealogy of dread.

Before long I began to perceive in two or three of the smaller children traces of greed and selfishness—little grabs, little refusals, a berry held back while others watched. I saw too that the bigger girls cast on these children not infrequent glances of anxiety, like elders noticing blight in a row of beans.

None of them put a hand to my work; they would do nothing for the giants. But they never relaxed their loving ministrations to me. They would sing to me, one after another, for hours; sometimes the song was no more than a chain of sounds that fitted their mouths like a dialect I could not enter—bright syllables, soft clicks, breathy vowels that seemed made to travel through leaves. Sometimes they climbed the tree to reach my mouth and popped fruit into it with their dainty fingers, leaving smears of juice that smelled of summer and earth. Always they kept constant watch for the approach of a giant, listening to the ground more than the air.

Sometimes they would sit and tell me stories—childish stories that often seemed to mean hardly anything, and yet the cadence of them carried a kind of memory, a taste I could not name. It troubled me: I could sense significance the way one smells bread before seeing the oven, but the words would not translate themselves into my mind. Their experience was untranslatable, and the gap in me had the ache of a language dying.

Now and then they would call a general assembly to amuse me. On one such occasion a moody little fellow sang a strange crooning song, with a refrain so pathetic that, although unintelligible to me, it made the tears run down my face.

The sight of water startled them.

Those who saw my tears stared as if some impossible fruit had appeared on my cheeks. Then first I bethought myself that I had not once, in that world, looked on water, falling or lying or running. Plenty there had been in some long-vanished age—that was plain enough from the old channels and the worn stones—but the Little Ones had never seen any before they saw my tears.

And yet it seemed they had some dim, instinctive perception of their origin; for a very small child went up to the singer, shook his clenched pud in his face, and cried in the blunt baby-speech of their kind something like, “’Ou skeeze ze juice out of ze good giant’s seeberries! Bad giant!”

The accusation, ridiculous and tender, made my throat hurt more than the song had done.

One day, as Lona sat with the baby in her arms at the foot of my tree, the question that had been circling in me returned.

“How is it,” I said, “that I never see any children among the giants?”

She stared, searching my words for a handle. Then she answered as if it were self-evident: “They are giants; there are no little ones.”

“Have they never any children?”

“No; there are never any in the wood for them. They do not love them. If they saw ours, they would stamp them.”

“Is there always the same number of the giants then?” I asked. “I thought—before I had time to know better—that they were your fathers and mothers.”

She burst into the merriest laughter; it rang like struck glasses. “No, good giant; we are their firsters.”

But as she said it, the merriment died out of her, and her face went white with fear, as if she had spoken a word that did not like being spoken.

I stopped working. I stared at her, bewildered. “How can that be?”

“I do not say; I do not understand,” she replied quickly, as if she wished to fold the thought up and hide it away. “But we were here and they not. They go from us. I am sorry, but we cannot help it. They could have helped it.”

“How long have you been here?” I asked, hoping time might throw side-light on the matter.

“Always, I think,” she said. “I think somebody made us always.”

I turned back to my scraping. The bark came away in thin curls like peelings; the sound was dry, oddly domestic. She saw I did not understand.

“The giants were not made always,” she went on. “If a Little One doesn’t care, he grows greedy, and then lazy, and then big, and then stupid, and then bad. The dull creatures don’t know that they come from us. Very few of them believe we are anywhere. They say, NONSENSE!—Look at little Blunty: he is eating one of their apples! He will be the next! Oh! oh! he will soon be big and bad and ugly, and not know it!”

She pointed. A child stood a little way off by himself, eating an apple nearly as big as his head. The fruit was pale green, thick-skinned, with a harsh shine like wax. He bit into it greedily, the crunch loud in the hush, as if he wanted the sound of his eating to be heard. I had often thought he did not look so good as the rest; now, with the apple juice shining around his mouth, he looked—yes—disgusting, as if the fruit were changing him from the inside out.

“I will take the horrid thing from him!” I cried, and made to rise.

“It is no use,” Lona said sadly. “We have done all we can, and it is too late. We were afraid he was growing, for he would not believe anything told him; but when he refused to share his berries, and said he had gathered them for himself, then we knew it! He is a glutton, and there is no hope of him. It makes me sick to see him eat!”

“Could not some of the boys watch him,” I said, “and not let him touch the poisonous things?”

“He may have them if he will: it is all one—to eat the apples, and to be a boy that would eat them if he could.” Her voice held the weary authority of an elder who has seen the same mistake ripen over seasons. “No; he must go to the giants! He belongs to them. You can see how much bigger he is than when first you came! He is bigger since yesterday.”

“He is as like that hideous green lump in his hand as boy could look,” I muttered.

“It suits what he is making himself,” she returned.

“His head and it might change places!”

“Perhaps they do!” she said, and though she smiled, her eyes did not.

“Does he want to be a giant?” I asked.

“He hates the giants,” Lona answered, “but he is making himself one all the same: he likes their apples!” She hugged the baby closer, as if appetite were an infection. “Oh baby, baby, he was just such a darling as you when we found him!”

“He will be very miserable when he finds himself a giant.”

“Oh, no; he will like it well enough. That is the worst of it.”

“Will he hate the Little Ones?”

“He will be like the rest; he will not remember us—most likely will not believe there are Little Ones. He will not care; he will eat his apples.”

“Do tell me how it will come about,” I pleaded. “I understand your world so little! I come from a world where everything is different.”

“I do not know about world. What is it? What more but a word in your beautiful big mouth?—That makes it something!” She said it with wonder, and I felt the power of a dominant tongue—how naming pretends to mastery, how a word can be used like a fence. Yet in her mouth “world” was just a strange seed that might or might not take root.

“Never mind the word,” I said. “Tell me what next will happen to Blunty.”

“He will wake one morning and find himself a giant—not like you, good giant, but like any other bad giant.” Lona spoke as if reciting a known recipe: if you mix greed and laziness and let it sit, this is what it becomes. “You will hardly know him, but I will tell you which. He will think he has been a giant always, and will not know you, or any of us.”

Then, as if repeating something she had heard from an older voice, she added, “The giants have lost themselves, Peony says, and that is why they never smile. I wonder whether they are not glad because they are bad, or bad because they are not glad. But they can’t be glad when they have no babies!” She looked down at the infant’s sleeping face. “I wonder what bad means, good giant!”

“I wish I knew no more about it than you,” I said. “But I try to be good, and mean to keep on trying.”

“So do I,” she answered softly, “and that is how I know you are good.”

A long pause followed. Around us the wood’s small sounds returned—the tick of a beetle, the far creak of a bough. Somewhere a giant snored like a distant saw.

“Then you do not know where the babies come from into the wood?” I asked, making one attempt more, though I felt the question itself was a kind of violence, a prying at a mystery that resisted my language.

“There is nothing to know there,” she said, almost impatient now. “They are in the wood; they grow there.”

“Then how is it you never find one before it is quite grown?” I persisted.

She knitted her brows. “They’re not there till they’re finished,” she said at last.

“It is a pity the little sillies can’t speak till they’ve forgotten everything they had to tell!” I remarked, half in jest, half in grief for all the lost stories that never make it into words.

“Little Tolma,” Lona said, “the last before this baby, looked as if she had something to tell, when I found her under a beech-tree, sucking her thumb, but she hadn’t. She only looked up at me—oh, so sweetly! She will never go bad and grow big! When they begin to grow big they care for nothing but bigness; and when they cannot grow any bigger, they try to grow fatter.” Disgust curled her lip. “The bad giants are very proud of being fat.”

“So they are in my world,” I said under my breath; “only they do not say fat there, they say rich.”

“In one of their houses,” Lona continued, “sits the biggest and fattest of them—so proud that nobody can see him; and the giants go to his house at certain times, and call out to him, and tell him how fat he is, and beg him to make them strong to eat more and grow fat like him.”

As she spoke, I thought of temples built around emptiness, of languages bent to praise what cannot even answer. The wood around us, for all its strangeness, suddenly felt cleaner than that unseen throne.

In time the rumour reached my ears that Blunty had vanished. I saw a few grave faces among the bigger ones, but he did not seem much missed; the community closed around the loss as a hearth closes after a spark has flown.

The next morning Lona came to me and whispered, “Look! look there—by that quince-tree: that is the giant that was Blunty! Would you have known him?”

I looked where she pointed. A giant stood by the quince-tree, staring with a dull, fogged expression, an apple in his hand. His cheeks were slack; his eyes seemed set too far apart, as if thought had retreated inward and left only appetite at the door.

“Never,” I answered. “But now you tell me, I could fancy it might be Blunty staring through a fog. He does look stupid!”

“He is for ever eating those apples now,” she said.

“That is what comes of Little Ones that won’t be little,” I murmured.

“They call it growing-up in my world,” I said to myself, and the phrase tasted bitter, like peel left too long on the tongue. If only she would teach me to grow the other way, and become a Little One—shall I ever be able to laugh like them? I had had the chance, and had flung it from me.

Blunty and I were alike.

He did not know his loss, and I had to be taught mine.

For a span of days—if indeed days were passing in any measure that could be trusted in that valley—I wanted nothing but to remain among the Little Ones, as a kettle set aside where the hearth is warm and the air is sweet. Their life had the rhythm of a household: waking, gathering, sharing, dozing; small hands always busy with some quiet craft of survival, as unshowy and constant as kneading. They fed me as naturally as they laughed. They mended me with fruit and touch and the nearness of their uncorrupted joy.

Yet even in the richest kitchen, a cook begins to listen for what is missing.

It came first as a vague shame, like the aftertaste of smoke when one has stood too long in the wrong wind: I ought to be doing something. I was not made for the fattening of boors. Then, sharper, the sense of where I was took hold of me with the grip of hunger. This world was marvellous, and its laws were not a spectacle to be admired from a bench: they were a recipe to be learned. If I meant to give the children anything in return for their goodness, I must discover what they could not tell me. And to learn, I must be free to move.

For there was a fact that would not soften, however I turned it, like a bone in the mouth: the growth of the Little Ones was arrested. Something interfered with it. Lona seemed the eldest, and yet she was no more than fifteen in semblance—long in charge of many who were, in look and mostly in behaviour, mere children, and who regarded her as their mother. Were they growing at all? I doubted it. Of time they had scarcely an idea; of their own age they knew nothing. Lona herself spoke and carried herself as though she had lived always. She was full of wisdom and empty of knowledge—if indeed what I called ignorance was not rather my own failure of perception. She was at once their Love and their Law.

Her one anxiety, plain as salt on the tongue, was that her Little Ones should not grow and change into bad giants.

Their “good giant,” therefore, was bound to do his best for them; but without more knowledge of their nature, and some knowledge of their history, I could do nothing. To stay was to remain a kindly animal in a pen—loved, perhaps, but useless. Worse: I brought danger. They had not become dependent on me; they were still my protectors. My presence was like a bright lamp in the bad giants’ dark: it drew the heavy feet.

I longed to teach them many things. Their voices were little streams of melody—phrases murmured and forgotten as quickly as a scent on the air. Might they not be taught to set down those tunes, to keep them from dying on the tongue? Might they not learn numbers and measures, the careful kind of knowledge that does not trample feeling but can preserve it—like a food historian writing a grandmother’s method for bread so it does not vanish when her dialect vanishes? Yet to teach, I must first understand those I would teach. Knowledge makes bad people worse, no doubt; but it must make good people better.

So my conclusion formed itself: I must rise and go on, in the hope of coming upon some elucidation of the fortunes and destiny of the bewitching little creatures. Still, my design would not have passed so quickly into action, but for what now occurred.

I was at work one day—busy with some simple labour they delighted to share, the sort that makes a body feel useful and calms the mind with repetition—talking as we worked. I told them, to prepare them for my temporary absence, that I would long ago have left the bad giants, but that I loved the Little Ones so much—

As if I had dropped a handful of grain and startled a whole yard of sparrows, they came rushing upon me at once. They scrambled over each other and up the tree and dropped on my head until I was nearly smothered. Three very little ones were in my arms; one clung to each shoulder, their warm fingers hooked about my neck; one stood straight up on my head as if I were a stool; four or five held my legs; others grappled my body and arms, and a multitude climbed and descended upon these, a tumbling, laughing heap. Their light bodies had a surprising density in their number. I was helpless—overwhelmed as by lava, only this lava squealed and giggled and smelled of leaves and fruit.

Absorbed in that merry struggle, not one of them saw my tyrant coming until he was almost upon me.

There was just one cry—thin with terror, sharp as a bird’s alarm—“Take care, good giant!” and at once they ran from me like mice. They dropped from me like hedgehogs. They flew up the tree like squirrels. In the same moment, sharp round the stem came the bad giant, and with a stick he dealt me such a blow upon the head that the world went out, as a candle goes out when a wet thumb pinches it. I fell to the ground.

Afterward, they told me they sent him “such a many bumps of big apples and stones” that he was frightened and ran blundering home. I could imagine it: their small arms, quick as kitchen hands passing tools, flinging what came to them—fruit and stone alike—without malice, only with the fierce instinct to defend what they loved.

When I came to myself it was night. Above me were a few pale stars waiting for the moon. I thought I was alone. My head ached badly. My mouth was so dry it felt lined with dust and bark. I was terribly athirst.

I turned wearily on my side, and the moment my ear touched the ground I heard it: the gushing and gurgling of water. The sound was not loud, but it was near, and it moved through the earth with a tenderness that made my throat ache worse. Those soft noises made me groan with longing, as one who has smelled broth simmering after a fast.

At once I was amid a multitude of silent children. Delicious little fruits began to visit my lips. They came and came—cool skins, burst sweetness, a tang like rain held in flesh—until my thirst was gone, and my tongue remembered itself. Then, as my hearing cleared, I became aware of sounds I had never heard there before: the air was full of little sobs. Not cries, not wails, but the small brokenness of creatures trying to keep quiet and failing.

I tried to sit up. Instantly a pile of small bodies heaped itself at my back, bracing me like sacks of grain stacked for a fall. I struggled to my feet with much pushing and pulling from the Little Ones—wonderfully strong for their size, as if their strength had gone inward when their height would not.

“You must go away, good giant,” they said.

“When the bad giants see you hurt, they will all trample on you.”

“I think I must,” I answered. The admission had the bitter clarity of medicine.

“Go and grow strong, and come again,” they said.

“I will,” I replied—and sat down, for the world still swayed.

“Indeed you must go at once!” whispered Lona, who had been supporting me, and now knelt beside me. Her voice had that quality hers often held: not loud, not commanding, but carrying weight, as if it belonged to the place.

One of the bigger boys stepped close. “I listened at his door,” he said, “and heard the bad giant say to his wife that he had found you idle, talking to a lot of moles and squirrels, and when he beat you, they tried to kill him. He said you were a wizard, and they must knock you, or they would have no peace.”

“I will go at once,” I said, and the decision steadied me. “And come back as soon as I have found out what is wanted to make you bigger and stronger.”

“We don’t want to be bigger,” they answered, looking very serious, as if I had offered them some fashionable food that tasted of nothing and cost too much. “We WON’T grow bad giants!—We are strong now; you don’t know how much strong!”

There was no use holding before them a prospect that had no attraction. Their refusal was not obstinacy; it was identity. They spoke as if bigness were not a gift but a language that would replace their own, and with it would come a whole lexicon of dominance—heavy words, heavy hands. In their world, to “grow” did not mean simply to become more: it meant to become other, perhaps enemy.

So I said nothing more. I rose and moved slowly up the slope of the valley.

At once they formed themselves into a long procession. Some led the way, feeling the ground with their feet as if reading it. Some walked beside me helping me, hands under my arms, palms against my back. The rest followed, a small river of bodies and whispers. They kept feeding me as we went, not with solemnity, but with the habitual care of those who have always known that life is maintained by passing what one has to the one who needs it.

“You are broken,” they said. “And much red juice has run out of you: put some in.” And they pressed more fruit to my mouth, as if fruit were a kind of blood that could be shared. It had the taste of sun and sap. The smell of it rose in my nostrils and made me think—absurdly, painfully—of my own world’s kitchens, where everything is measured and named, and yet the naming sometimes kills the thing it tries to keep. Here, their care had no labels, only practice.

When we reached the edge of the valley, there was the moon, just lifting her forehead over the rim of the horizon.

“She has come to take care of you, and show you the way,” said Lona.

We were taking the second branch of the river-bed, its stones pale under the moonlight. The night had its own acoustic ecology: water speaking to itself in the dark, insects making their thin instruments, leaves shivering together like paper. Even the children’s feet had a sound—soft taps and scuffs, never the brutal stamp I had learned to dread from the bad giants. I found myself thinking how a land’s noises train its hearts. Where everything roars, people must shout to exist; where the world murmurs, tenderness can be heard.

As we walked, I questioned those about me, and learned there was a great place with a giant-girl for queen. When I asked if it was a city, they said they did not know. Neither could they tell how far off, or in what direction it was, or what the giant-girl’s name; all they knew was that she hated the Little Ones, and would like to kill them—only she could not find them.

“How do you know that?” I asked.

Lona answered simply, “I have always known it.”

If the giant-girl came to look for them, they must hide hard, she said, as if speaking of a storm that never ceased to exist, only sometimes moved nearer. When I told them I should go and ask her why she hated them, they cried out at once, voices overlapping like frightened birds:

“No, no! she will kill you, good giant; she will kill you! She is an awful bad-giant witch!”

“Where am I to go then?” I asked, for the world was suddenly wide again, and I had no map but the moon.

They told me that beyond the baby-forest, away where the moon came from, lay a smooth green country, pleasant to the feet, without rocks or trees. The idea of ground that did not bruise, of walking without always looking down, came to me with a bodily gratitude.

“But how am I to set out for it?” I asked.

“The moon will tell you, we think,” they answered, as if the moon were an elder with a sure memory, one who kept the old routes in her pale face when the living forgot.

When they saw that the moon had reached her height, they stopped to return.

“We have never gone so far from our trees before,” they said, and there was a tremor in it—not fear of danger so much as the discomfort of leaving the only vocabulary of place they possessed. Their land was not merely where they lived; it was what they were. I remembered how once, when I suggested they should leave the country of the bad giants and go with me to find another, they answered, “But that would be to NOT ourselves!” Their love of place was so strong that their country seemed essential to their very being—like a mother-tongue: not a tool, but a home. To tear them from it would be to force upon them a dominant speech in which their finest feelings might not fit.

“Now mind you watch how you go,” they said, “that you may see inside your eyes how to come back to us.”

“I will,” I promised, though the promise sat heavy; for I knew how easily the eye forgets what the body cannot repeat.

“And beware of the giant-woman that lives in the desert,” said one of the bigger girls as they were turning, with the eager relish of passing on lore.

“I suppose you have heard of her!”

“No,” I answered.

“Then take care not to go near her. She is called the Cat-woman. She is awfully ugly—AND SCRATCHES.”

As soon as the bigger ones stopped, the smaller had begun to run back, as if the nearer darkness behind them were suddenly full of something calling. The others looked at me gravely for a moment, then walked slowly away.

Last to leave me, Lona held up the baby to be kissed. The little face, half in moonlight, had the soft seriousness of bread before it rises—promise contained in stillness. I kissed it, and its skin smelled faintly of milk-fruit and leaf.

Lona gazed into my eyes, and her gaze was as steady as the moon’s path.

“The Cat-woman will not hurt YOU,” she whispered, and went without another word.

I stood a while, watching them through the moonlight until their bodies were only motions and then only absence. Then I turned and, with a heavy heart, began my solitary journey.

Soon their laughter overtook me. It came behind like sheep-bells innumerable, rippling the air, echoing among the rocks. It was a music without commerce—unbottled, unperformed, unadvertised—only life sounding itself. I turned again and gazed after them: they went gamboling along with never a care in their sweet souls. But Lona walked apart with her baby, as if carrying not merely a child but the whole fragile archive of their people.

As I went, I pondered many traits of my little friends. Without ambition or fear, discomfort or greed, they had no motive to desire change. They knew of nothing amiss. Except their babies, they had never had a chance of helping any one but myself. How were they to grow—if growth meant accumulation of knowledge, or widening of want? But again—why should they grow? In seeking to improve their conditions, might I not do them harm, and only harm? To enlarge their minds after the notions of my world—might it not be to distort and weaken them, like translating a song into a language that has no vowels for its sorrow? Their fear of growth as the possible start of gianthood might be instinctive, a wisdom their mouths could not define.

The part of philanthropist is indeed a dangerous one. The man who would do his neighbour good must first study how not to do him evil—and must begin by pulling the beam out of his own eye.

With the moon ahead and their laughter dying behind, I walked on, tasting still the fruit they had pressed on my lips, and feeling in my bruised head not only pain, but the pressure of an unfinished duty—like dough that has been mixed and must now be tended, or it will sour and be wasted.

I went on with the moon for company.

She was always full—so full she seemed less a lamp than a presence—and that night, as she sank, I caught on her bright face a curve that might have been a smile. While her lower rim still touched the edge of the world, a cottage appeared in the very middle of her disc, as if a careful hand had painted it there: a low house with its door and window open to the moonlight, so that her beam lay on its floor like poured milk. With the sight came a certainty, quiet as hunger, that I was expected inside.

Then the moon slipped under, and the cottage went with her. Darkness thickened quickly. My path lay across a fretwork of little ravines and dry channels, and I did not fancy breaking my neck in the unseen. I chose a sandy hollow, as one chooses a hearth when there is no roof—made my supper of the fruit the children had pressed into my hands, and let their sweetness and the faint rasp of sand between my teeth lull me down.

I woke as if called, and above me were constellations I had never seen from my former sky—new patterns, new distances, and a strange silence behind them, like a held breath. I lay and listened: even the night seemed to have its own acoustics here, a wide, soft bowl of air in which every minute sound—my own swallow, the sift of sand settling, a far insect’s rasp—stood out clean. It was a kind of mercy after the grinding, mindless noise of machines in the world I had left, where men fill their days with din and call it progress.

Then I became aware of another presence.

A figure sat on the ground a little way from me, above the lip of my hollow, so that her outline cut the sky. From where I lay, she looked larger than human, as if the stars had chosen her for their foreground. She moved her head, and I saw her back was toward me.

“Will you not come with me?” said a voice—sweet, mellow, like warm bread broken open, the steam of it rising.

“I thank you,” I answered, startled into politeness, “but I am not uncomfortable here. Where would you have me go? I like sleeping in the open air.”

“There is no hurt in the air,” she returned; “but the creatures that roam the night in these parts are not such as a man would willingly have about him while he sleeps.”

“I have not been disturbed.”

“No; I have been sitting by you ever since you lay down.”

“That is very kind of you! How came you to know I was here? Why do you show me such favour?”

“I saw you,” she said, still giving me only the set of her shoulders, “in the light of the moon, just as she went down. I see badly in the day, but at night perfectly. The shadow of my house would have hidden you, but both its doors were open. I was out on the waste, and saw you go into this hollow. You were asleep, however, before I could reach you, and I was not willing to disturb you. People are frightened if I come on them suddenly. They call me the Cat-woman. It is not my name.”

I remembered what the children had said—ugly, and scratches. Yet her voice held no cruelty; it had the careful gentleness of someone who knows what a sudden noise can do to a living thing.

“You shall not hear it from me,” I said. “Please tell me what I may call you.”

“When you know me,” she answered, “call me by the name that seems to you to fit me. That will tell me what sort you are. People do not often give me the right one. It is well when they do.”

“I suppose,” I ventured, trying to steady my own thoughts with talk, “you live in the cottage I saw in the heart of the moon?”

“I do. I live there alone, except when I have visitors. It is a poor place, but I do what I can for my guests, and sometimes their sleep is sweet to them.”

Her voice settled on me like a cloth laid over a restless dish—stilling the steam, quieting the tremor. I rose.

“I will go with you, madam.”

She rose at once, and without a glance behind her began to walk. I could see her just well enough to follow: a tall woman, taller than I, but not the giant my first frightened fancy had made. That she never once turned her face toward me pricked my curiosity. It did not, at first, wake dread—her tone was too true for that. And yet, how was I to find her name, when she would not let me see her?

More than once I tried to come abreast; each time, when I quickened, she quickened as easily, keeping always a little ahead, as if the air itself carried her. The dry world around us gave back our footfalls thinly. Every now and then some small nocturnal sound—an unseen wing, a pebble turned—rang with unnatural clearness in the hollows. I began to feel the pressure of the silence between her words, and in it the old human fear that grows where there is no familiar noise: what cannot be heard begins to be imagined.

At last my apprehension rose into something like horror. Why was she so careful not to be seen? If she was grotesque, she might be sparing me; but the mind, when it cannot translate what it meets, invents monstrosities. I was following, through a black land, a being who would not show her face. The thought made my mouth dry.

Neither of us spoke, and the quiet became unbearable. I forced words into it.

“I want to find my way to a place I have heard of,” I said, “but whose name I have not yet learned. Perhaps you can tell it me.”

“Describe it, then, and I will direct you. The stupid Bags know nothing, and the careless little Lovers forget almost everything.”

“Where do those live?”

“You are just come from them.”

“I never heard those names before!”

“You would not hear them. Neither people knows its own name.”

“Strange!”

“Perhaps so! but hardly any one anywhere knows his own name. It would make many a fine gentleman stare to hear himself addressed by what is really his name!”

Her words went through me with a cold edge. I found myself wondering, in spite of myself, what my own name would sound like if spoken by someone who knew it better than I did.

“What now do you fancy yours?” she went on, as if she heard my thought the way one hears a shift in another’s breathing. “But, pardon me, it is a matter of no consequence.”

I had opened my mouth to answer—then discovered, with a shock like a cupboard found empty, that my name was gone. Not merely hidden: absent. I could not even grasp its first letter. This was the second time the question had come, and the same blank had swallowed the answer.

“Never mind,” she said. “It is not wanted. Your real name, indeed, is written on your forehead, but at present it whirls about so irregularly that nobody can read it. I will do my part to steady it. Soon it will go slower, and, I hope, settle at last.”

We walked on. The channels fell away behind us; the sand spread out in long, soundless slopes. Still no cottage.

“The Little Ones told me,” I said at length, clinging to the children’s world because it had had fruit and laughter in it, “of a smooth green country, pleasant to the feet.”

“Yes?” Her voice was a small lamp in the dark.

“They told me too of a girl giantess that was queen somewhere. Is that her country?”

“There is a city in that grassy land,” she replied, “where a woman is princess. The city is called Bulika. But certainly the princess is not a girl! She is older than this world, and came to it from yours—with a terrible history, which is not over yet. She is an evil person, and prevails much with the Prince of the Power of the Air.

“The people of Bulika were formerly simple folk, tilling the ground and pasturing sheep. She came among them, and they received her hospitably. She taught them to dig for diamonds and opals and sell them to strangers, and made them give up tillage and pasturage and build a city. One day they found a huge snake and killed it; which so enraged her that she declared herself their princess, and became terrible to them.

“The name of the country at that time was the Land of Waters; for the dry channels, of which you have crossed so many, were then overflowing with live torrents; and the valley, where now the Bags and the Lovers have their fruit-trees, was a lake that received a great part of them. But the wicked princess gathered up in her lap what she could of the water over the whole country, closed it in an egg, and carried it away. Her lap, however, would not hold more than half of it; and the instant she was gone, what she had not yet taken fled away underground, leaving the country as dry and dusty as her own heart.

“Were it not for the waters under it, every living thing would long ago have perished from it. For where no water is, no rain falls; and where no rain falls, no springs rise.

“Ever since then, the princess has lived in Bulika, holding the inhabitants in constant terror, and doing what she can to keep them from multiplying. Yet they boast and believe themselves a prosperous, and certainly are a self-satisfied people—good at bargaining and buying, good at selling and cheating; holding well together for a common interest, and utterly treacherous where interests clash; proud of their princess and her power, and despising every one they get the better of; never doubting themselves the most honourable of all the nations, and each man counting himself better than any other. The depth of their worthlessness and height of their vainglory no one can understand who has not been there to see, who has not learned to know the miserable misgoverned and self-deceived creatures.”

Her words painted the city without showing it: a place that had traded fields for stones that glitter, sheep-bells for the ring of coin. I could almost hear the soundscape of such a change—the steady, sane noises of husbandry drowned by the clatter of commerce and the harsh scrape of excavation. The thought made me ache for the children’s valley, where the loudest thing had been laughter and falling fruit.

“I thank you,” I said. “And now, if you please, will you tell me something about the Little Ones—the Lovers? I long heartily to serve them. Who and what are they? and how do they come to be there? Those children are the greatest wonder I have found in this world of wonders.”

“In Bulika you may, perhaps, get some light on those matters. There is an ancient poem in the library of the palace, I am told, which of course no one there can read, but in which it is plainly written that after the Lovers have gone through great troubles and learned their own name, they will fill the land, and make the giants their slaves.”

“By that time they will have grown a little, will they not?”

“Yes, they will have grown; yet I think too they will not have grown. It is possible to grow and not to grow, to grow less and to grow bigger, both at once—yes, even to grow by means of not growing!”

“Your words are strange, madam.”

“But I have heard it said,” she returned, “that some words, because they mean more, appear to mean less.”

I thought of how languages die—not with trumpet-blasts, but with small everyday surrenders. A word that once carried a whole climate of meaning gets replaced by a cheap, general token; soon no one remembers what is missing, because the loss makes no noise. Even here, in a land of wonders, names mattered. Names were not labels but histories, recipes of identity no stranger could truly taste unless he had grown up with the tongue that made them.

“That is true,” she said, “and such words have to be understood. It were well for the princess of Bulika if she heard what the very silence of the land is shouting in her ears all day long! But she is far too clever to understand anything.”

“Then I suppose,” I said, “when the little Lovers are grown, their land will have water again?”

“Not exactly so: when they are thirsty enough, they will have water, and when they have water, they will grow. To grow, they must have water. And, beneath, it is flowing still.”

“I have heard that water twice,” I said, and as I spoke I could almost taste it, cold and mineral on the tongue. “Once when I lay down to wait for the moon—and when I woke the sun was shining. And once when I fell, all but killed by the bad giant. Both times came the voices of the water, and healed me.”

She did not turn, and always kept that little lead; yet every syllable she uttered came to me as if set in a bowl and offered. Her voice reminded me of another woman’s voice—heard in a house where death lay orderly as a made bed.

Much of what she said afterward I did not understand, and so cannot keep; but the dread I had felt slid away, as if her very speaking had changed the air I breathed.

At last, after we had crossed yet another long tract of sand, the cottage stood before us. Its foundation sank deep in sand, but I could see the base was rock. In shape it resembled the sexton’s, though the walls were thicker, and the door looked made to bar more than wind.

She opened it, and we entered a large bare room. Two little windows faced each other, without glass, so that the night looked in freely. She went straight to the farthest corner, lifted a long white cloth from the floor, and wound it about her head and face. Then she closed the other door—the one the moon had looked through before—trimmed a small horn lantern on the hearth, and at last turned toward me.

“You are very welcome, Mr. Vane!” she said, speaking the name I had been unable to find inside my own mind. “Your entertainment will be scanty, but, as the night is not far spent, and the day not at hand, it is better you should be indoors. Here you will be safe, and a little lack is not a great misery.”

“I thank you heartily, madam,” I replied. “But, seeing you know the name I could not tell you, may I not now know yours?”

“My name is Mara,” she answered.

At the word, memory stirred—of the sexton, and the little black cat.

“Some people,” she went on, “take me for Lot’s wife, lamenting over Sodom; and some think I am Rachel, weeping for her children; but I am neither of those.”

“I thank you again, Mara,” I said. “May I lie here on your floor till the morning?”

“At the top of that stair,” she answered, “you will find a bed—on which some have slept better than they expected, and some have waked all the night and slept all the next day. It is not a very soft one, but it is better than the sand—and there are no hyenas sniffing about it!”

The stair was narrow and steep, rising straight from the room into an unceiled, unpartitioned garret with one broad, low dormer-window. Under the slant of the roof stood a narrow bed. Its white coverlet made me shiver—not from cold only, but from remembrance: the chamber of death, the still figures laid out as if waiting for a feast they could not yet taste.

On a table stood a dry loaf, and beside it a cup of cold water. After months of fruit—sweetness without salt, pleasure without depth—the plain smell of bread, even stale, struck me like a promise. The water had the clean, dull scent of a covered well.

“I must leave you in the dark,” my hostess called from the foot of the stair. “This lantern is all the light I have, and there are things to do to-night.”

“It is of no consequence,” I returned. “To eat and drink, to lie down and sleep, are things that can be done in the dark.”

“Rest in peace,” she said.

I ate the loaf, crumb by crumb, the dry crust rasping pleasantly against my teeth; I drank the water to the last drop, and felt it go through me as if my body remembered springs. Then I lay down. The bed was hard; the covering thin; the night sharp with cold. I dreamed I was again in that chamber, between the warrior and the lady with the wound that healed.

I woke in the middle of the night, thinking I heard the low noises of wild animals.

“Creatures of the desert scenting after me, I suppose,” I told myself; and because the walls were thick and Mara had promised safety, I would have slept again.

But at that instant a rough purring rose under my window, swelling into a howl that seemed to scrape the night itself. I sprang from the bed and looked out.

Before the cottage door, in full moon-radiance, a tall woman stood clothed in white, her back toward me—Mara again, face hidden as ever from my sight. At her feet was a great white animal like a panther, its body a pale muscle in the moonlight. She stroked it with one hand—slow, sure passes, as a cook smooths down dough to feel where it resists—while with the other she pointed to the moon halfway up the sky, then drew a straight line down to the horizon.

Instantly the creature darted away with astonishing speed, as if shot from a bow, straight along the line she had drawn.

My eyes followed it, then swung back to the woman; she was gone. Still I had not seen her face. I peered after the animal again; whether I truly saw a fading white speck or only imagined it, I cannot tell.

What did it mean? What errand could such a beast be sent upon? The sound it had made left my nerves raw. I went back to the bed trembling.

Then another thought struck me: when I had lain down in the sandy hollow, the moon had been setting; and yet here she was, only hours later, high and bright.

“Everything is uncertain here,” I whispered to myself, “even the motions of the heavenly bodies!”

I learned afterward that several moons served this world, but the laws of their comings and goings, their different paths and times, I never discovered. The sky itself here seemed to have more dialects than my understanding.

At last I slept again, and was not disturbed.

In the morning, when I went down, bread and water waited me—another loaf, so large I ate only half. My hostess sat muffled beside me while I broke my fast. The room held the clean, dry smell of flour and old stone. Outside, the air carried the faint whistle of wind over sand; inside, her stillness made a quieter silence, one that did not threaten.

Except to greet me when I entered, she did not speak until I asked her how to reach Bulika.

She told me to go up the bank of the river-bed until it disappeared; then verge to the right until I came to a forest—in which I might spend a night, but which I must leave with my face to the rising moon. Keeping in the same direction, she said, until I reached a running stream, I must cross it at right angles, and go straight on until I saw the city on the horizon.

I thanked her, and ventured a remark, for my mind still clung to what I had witnessed.

“Looking out of the window in the night,” I said, “I was astonished to see your messenger understand you so well, and go so straight and so fast in the direction you had indicated. If I had but that animal of yours to guide me—”

“It was to Bulika she went—the shortest way,” Mara interrupted, as if she would not have me follow the thought farther than she chose.

“How wonderfully intelligent she looked!”

“Astarte knows her work well enough to be sent to do it,” she answered.

“Have you many messengers like her?”

“As many as I require.”

“Are they hard to teach?”

“They need no teaching. They are all of a certain breed, but not one of the breed is like another. Their origin is so natural it would seem to you incredible.”

“May I not know it?”

“A new one came to me last night—from your head while you slept.”

I could not help laughing. In this world, even a man’s stray thought might take on fur and teeth and run errands. Mystery here was not a curtain drawn for show; it was the very air’s way of being, as if meaning itself had a native tongue I did not speak.

“Then the creature is mine!” I cried.

“Not at all,” she answered. “That only can be ours in whose existence our will is a factor.”

I had no answer ready. The bread sat heavy and honest in me; the words did not.

After a time I asked, “May I take what is left of the loaf?”

“You will want no more to-day,” she replied.

“To-morrow I may!”

“It has nothing to do with to-morrow,” she said, rising. “But you may take it if you will.”

She went to the door and opened it, holding it for me. I stood, took up the remaining bread, and lingered, shamefacedly hungry for one thing more than food: to see her face, to fit her at last with the right name.

“Must I go, then?” I asked.

“No one sleeps in my house two nights together!” she answered.

“I thank you, then, for your hospitality, and bid you farewell!” I said, and turned to go.

“The time will come when you must house with me many days and many nights,” she murmured sadly through her wrappings.

“Willingly,” I replied, for gratitude made me bold.

“Nay, not willingly!” she answered.

At once I thought she was right. I would not willingly be her guest again—not with that hidden face, not with that beast under the moon. But my heart rebuked me as soon as the thought formed, and before I had crossed the threshold I turned back.

She stood in the middle of the room. Her white garments lay about her feet like foamy waves, and among them lay the swathings she had removed. Her face—at last—was lovely as a night of stars. Great grey eyes looked upward as if listening to something far above the roof; tears ran down pale cheeks. She reminded me of the sexton’s wife, and yet not: the one had looked as if she had not wept for thousands of years; this one looked as if she wept continually behind the wrappings of her beautiful head. And still, in the very eyes that wept, there was a promise—as if sorrow here had its own old proverb: night does not erase morning.

I bowed my head, about to kneel and beg her forgiveness for my fear. When I lifted it in the motion, I found myself outside a house without a door.

I went around and around it, hands brushing the rough wall, searching for hinge or latch, for any seam in the stone. There was none. I stopped beneath one of the windows, ready to call out my confession.

Then a sudden wailing, howling scream invaded my ears—so sharp it seemed to strike the breath out of my chest.

Something sprang from the window above my head and landed beyond me. I turned and saw a large grey cat, its hair bristling, shooting toward the river-bed. The sound of it—wild and tearing—filled the air like a ripped cloth.

I fell with my face in the sand, and through it all I seemed to hear, from within the sealed house, the gentle sobbing of one who suffered, but did not repent.

I rose to go on, and my feet took up again the long, dry miles as if they had been trained for nothing else. The land lay spread like a board that had once been a table, and had been left uncovered until every crumb of comfort had been carried off by wind: level, pale, and fasting. More than hunger for food, I hungered for a height—some ridge, some boulder, any upthrust of stone from which the eye might take in a shape of hope, the way a cook lifts a lid for the first smell that tells him the broth has begun to speak. But what would sight have given me? In this country it was not what lay ahead that governed what would meet me; it was what lay inside me, working in the dark like yeast—silent, unseen, yet making the whole mass rise or collapse.

The sun sank toward the rim, and at last I saw, before me, a rough climb of rock—broken steps and splintered ledges, as if the world had once tried to build a stairway and then forgotten why. Yet when I neared it, my desire to climb was already spent, and I wanted only to lie down. The air thickened with dusk. At my feet spread a carpet of moss—soft, deep, springing under the palm like well-proofed dough. I dropped upon it, and at once the weariness began to run out of me, because the moment my head lay low, I heard it again—the sound I had heard twice before: many waters, far below, moving where no water showed itself.

They played in buried channels, and the stones were their instruments. It was not one tune but a tangle: a gurgle returning like a refrain, a bell-like drip that answered and contradicted, little rushes that broke and rejoined, as if some hidden hand were pouring from one vessel to another in a rhythm too ancient to be learned and too intimate to be written. In my other life there had been machines that screamed as they did their work, and men who called it progress; here the world laboured in secret, and the labour made music. The sound was the opposite of that modern din that bruises the mind—this was an acoustic mercy, a hush that did not silence but soothed. Had any master of sound been near—any Händel of waters—what might he not have woven from that chaos of pure material? Yet it was already complete, because it was alive.

Lying there, I let my eyes wander the cliff above me. Its face held the record of a cataract that had once flung itself down, age after age, filling the very channels I had followed. I could almost taste the old spray on my tongue—mineral and cold—and smell the green wetness that must have clung to every rock when the fall was young. My heart swelled at the thought of that splendid tumult: waves dancing because they could not help but fall, gathering all their scattered noises into one vast organ-roar below. Then the hidden brooks, faithful as a lullaby sung in a mother’s own dialect, drew me under, and their water-speech flowed into my dreaming.

Before the sun, I woke and climbed eagerly, as if height might finally give me a name for what I was doing. But beyond the ridge there was nothing—nothing but a desert of finest sand, sifted and level as flour, dead-white where the first light struck it. Not a trace remained of the river that had plunged here. The powdery drift had filled its course until the old bed was indistinguishable from the blank expanse.

Looking back, I saw how the river had once split as it fell—two branches, one whose bank I had followed to this scaur, the other I had first crossed on my way toward the Evil Wood. Between the two, far off, I could just make out the wood itself, a dark band on the horizon like a remembered word that refuses translation. Before me and to the left, sand ran out beyond my vision; but far to the right the sky-line lifted a little, and the faint rise gave me the only sort of direction this land granted—a hint, not a promise—toward the forest my strange hostess had named.

I sat down and reached for the half-loaf I had brought. Only then did I understand what she had meant. The bread was not for tomorrow. It had shrunk and hardened to a stone, as if time here baked without heat and with no pity. I turned it in my hands—its crust no longer a crust but a shell—and the smell of it was gone. There was no comfort in it, no memory of grain-fields or ovens. I flung it away and went on.

Toward noon the land offered a few tamarisk and juniper, then stunted firs with needles thin as old broom-straw. A little farther and the thickets thickened, and the firs rose taller, and soon I was within a forest of pines and other trees like that in which the Little Ones had found their babies. For a moment I thought I had circled back into the same place, only farther along its edge; but in this country WHERE meant little when EVERYWHERE tasted like NOWHERE. I had not yet turned any patch of ground into a place by doing something in it. I was still not alive; I was only watching myself exist, as a food-historian might catalogue a dish he never eats, describing texture without letting it touch his tongue.

I told myself that if I caught even the faint gleam of the mirror again, I would turn aside at once. I would not be trapped back into my former life, where I had lived among words as among tins on a pantry shelf—labelled, orderly, and unopened. Here, perhaps, I might learn to be something by making something, by serving, by sharing—by any act that left a taste behind it.

I went deeper into the wood, weary enough to rest. The trees were large now, set in a regular fashion, almost geometric, with wide aisles between. There was little undergrowth; the sight ran far in every direction. The forest felt like a great church—solemn, silent, and empty. Nothing on two feet or four came near me that day. Once or twice a swift thing crossed a distant space, and again a slow thing, but always far off, as if even the animals kept their own counsel and would not spend breath on company. I heard a few birds. Butterflies drifted—some gaudy as spilled spice, others so white they startled the eye like a flash of salt.

Then I reached a spot where the pines stood farther apart and flowering shrubs had room, and—because hunger is not only of the stomach—I took the direction where more roses grew. I was hungry for the voice and face of my kind, for any living soul, human or not, whose being I might in some measure understand. What a hell it would be, I thought, to wander alone forever: a bare existence never stepping out of itself, never widening by entering another life, but bound in the cords of its own peculiarities like a prisoner with his own chains for companions. Only among others can one live for oneself at all—and only there can one become so fearfully selfish. Evil, I began to see, can only feed on good. Selfishness is a parasite; it cannot root where there is no tree of life.

In my own world I had sung when I was alone, not thinking about it—little tunes under my breath. Here no crooning murmur ever left my lips. There I had sung without thinking; here I thought without singing. There I had never had a bosom-friend; here the affection of an idiot would have been a sacrament. “If only I had a dog to love!” I sighed, and wondered at the man I had been, who preferred book or pen to a breathing creature; who, if the author of a tale he enjoyed had entered the room, would have wished him gone so the story could go on without the intrusion of its maker. I had chosen the dead over the living; the thing thought over the thing thinking. “Any man,” I said now, “is more than the greatest of books.”

The wood grew richer. Pines thickened into pillars, huge stems rising as if eager to hold up the sky. Other trees appeared. The roses were no longer bushes but trees, and their blossoms had a splendour that seemed too deliberate to be mere accident—like a recipe perfected over generations, passed down in a language that only the hands fully understand. Suddenly I spied what looked like a great house or castle; yet its outline was strangely indistinct, as if made of hesitating air. From a distance its forms held together; nearer, they did not grow more definite, as though the closer I came, the more the thing refused to be translated into certainty. When at last I stood before it, I was no less doubtful than at first. Habitable it certainly was not; perhaps a ruin overgrown with ivy and roses. Yet of stone or timber, not the poorest remnant of wall could I find. Again and again I thought I saw a corner, a lintel, a buttress; always, at the moment of inspection, it vanished into leaf and vine.

It came to me that the ivy might have embraced some vast building and consumed it, and that its interlaced branches had kept, like a mould, the memory of the walls it had devoured. Memory in this world was a strange thing—kept not in words, but in shapes, in scents, in sounds. Before me yawned a rectangular vacancy: the ghost of a doorway without a door. I stepped through, and found myself in an open space like a great hall. Its floor was grass and flowers; its walls and roof were ivy and vine mingled with roses. If one were to sleep anywhere, this was a place made for it: a green room that had eaten its own architecture.

I gathered withered leaves and laid them in a corner for a bed. The air held the warmth of a day that had not yet learned to be cold. I lay down, and a red sunset filled the hall as if poured in like wine. Above me the living ceiling made tracery against the fading sky—branches, twigs, clouds of foliage, and peeping patches where the higher roof opened. My eyes wandered in it as if caught and gently held. When the sun was gone and the dark began to thicken, the red roses turned black; then only yellow and white showed, pale as butter and cream in dim light. When those too vanished, the stars came instead, hanging among the leaves like jewels—topazes, opals, sparks of colour. It was as if I lay under a tree from Aladdin’s cave, except the treasure was light and breath.

Then I saw the roof was full of nests. Tiny heads, almost indistinguishable, popped out with a chirp or two, and vanished again. There were rustlings and small stirrings—little domestic noises, the soft economy of a nursery settling. I listened as one listens to a kitchen at night when the last embers shift: not loud, but full of life. Gradually each feathered mother had her brood tucked under her wings; the small talk in the little beds ceased; and God’s bird-nursery lay quiet beneath the waves of sleep.

Once more a flutter made me look up. An owl sailed across. I caught only a glimpse, but I felt the cool waft of his silent wings several times, like a hand passing over a candle without putting it out. The mother birds did not move. They knew he hunted mice, not children.

About midnight I woke wide awake, roused by revelry. The noise was not loud, yet it was close—thin, attenuated, like music heard through layers of cloth. For a moment my eyes were dazzled, as if the dark had been replaced by a brightness my sight could not at once receive. Then they steadied, and I found I was no longer looking at ivy walls and nests, but at a splendid hall, and my heap of withered leaves lay in a corner of it as if it had always belonged there.

Before me thronged a crowd—men in gorgeous dress, women in robes that fell and floated with an elegance belonging to another age. None of them appeared to see me. Dance followed dance, each vaguely embodying the story of life—meeting and courting, passion and pride, parting and regret—made visible by steps and turns. In the world I had left, I had studied Shakespeare enough to have learned something of the dances he names; so I recognised, or half-recognised, the minuet, the pavin, the hey, the coranto, the lavolta. Their fashions matched their measures—antique as memory, formal as ritual.

Moonlight had risen while I slept, shining down through a roof that seemed filled with countless windows. Yet the light was broken by so many shadows that at first I could scarcely make out faces. Still, something was odd. I sat up, peering harder—then the truth struck like a cold mouthful swallowed unawares.

Faces? They were skull fronts.

Hard bone gleamed where flesh should have softened. Bare jaws worked without lips. Noses were truncated; teeth stood exposed, incapable of smile. Some skulls flashed set and white and murderous; others were clouded with decay, broken, gapped, stained with the earth in which they seemed to have lain. Worse than all, the eye-sockets were not empty. In each socket lived a lidless eye.

In those wrecks of faces eyes glowed, flashed, sparkled—eyes of every colour and expression. A proud dark eye, lustrous and condescending, was more terrible than a dull one. A lovely languishing eye was more repulsive than any glare. Yet the dim sad eyes—less at variance with their setting—were sad beyond telling, and drew my heart toward them in spite of the horror framing their gaze, as if grief itself spoke a dialect the dead still remembered.

I rose and walked among them, eager to understand what I was seeing. Were these souls? Or were they only phantasms—motions left behind, repeating like a tune whose words have been forgotten? By no look, no gesture, not the slightest break in measure did they show awareness of me. I was not present to them. But were they present to one another? Surely they must see their companions as I saw them: death dancing with living eyes. Or did each only dream itself and the rest?

Had they once used their faces not to communicate, not to utter thought and feeling, not to share existence with their neighbours, but to appear what they wished and hide what they were—turning face into mask? And having made masks of their faces, were they now deprived of them, condemned to go without faces until they repented? How long must such punishment endure—this flaunting of facelessness in eyes that cannot close? Had shame begun at last to do its quiet work? Had any of them yielded?

No word was spoken. No naked mouth moved. Perhaps they were bereft of speech because they had lied. Yet with their eyes they seemed to speak, longing to be understood. Was it truth or falsehood that shone there? They appeared to know one another. Did one skull seem beautiful to them, another plain? Difference must remain, and they had had long study of skulls.

When a dancer came close to me, the body passed through mine as through a thin curtain. I could tell, without seeing, whether a man or a woman had gone through my house. On many skulls the hair still clung, and however lovely in itself it might have been, it looked frightful on the bare forehead and temples. Sometimes an outer ear remained also, and from its tip hung a jewel—pearl, opal, diamond—glimmering under brown or raven locks, under golden ripples, under pale fluffy cirri like cloud-threads. The hair lay lichenous upon ivory-white or damp-yellow bone.

I looked down and saw daintily domed insteps. I looked up and saw plump shoulders carrying the spring of a round full neck—only for that neck to wither halfway into the fluted shaft of a gibbous cranium. The sensual grace of the body went on performing its old art, while the head proclaimed what the body would not admit.

The music grew wilder. The dance sped faster and faster. Eyes flared, jewels twinkled, throwing colour and fire upon pallid grins that glided through the hall. The crowd wove a ghastly rhythmic cloth of motion—intricate, multitudinous. Then, sudden as a knife laid down, came a pause. Every eye turned to one spot.

In the doorway stood a woman.

She was perfect in form and bearing and hue—alive in a way that made the others seem not merely dead but forbidden. She regarded the company as from the pedestal of a goddess, while the dancers stood frozen, “like one forbid,” held still by the vision of life that killed. Her scornful glance said without words, Dead things, I live.

Then, like leaves when a wind wakes in them, they turned each to another and broke again into motion—melodious, consorted—yet changed. Their eyes, late solitary, now filled with a common triumph, spoke as plainly as shouting. Thou also, they seemed to say, wilt soon become weak as we. Thou wilt soon become like unto us.

I turned back to the woman and saw, upon her side, a small dark shadow—as if something had touched her that did not belong to the living. She had seen the change in the dead stare; she looked down; she understood the talking eyes. She pressed both her lovely hands upon the shadow, gave a smothered cry, and fled.

The birds rustled in their nests. A flash of joy lit the eyes of the dancers. Then a warm wind swept through the place, growing in strength as it went, and it blew out every light. The low moon still glimmered on the horizon with sick attempt to shine, and a turbid radiance still leaked from so many eyes that I could see what followed.

As if each shape had been a snow-image warmed too quickly, it began to fall to pieces. Flesh peeled from bone in papery flakes, dropping like soiled snow from under garments. Rags and strips fluttered down, littering the floor. The white skeleton emerged from garment and flesh together, bare and lank amid the decay.

A faint rattling shiver ran through the naked company. Pair after pair, the lamping eyes went out. Darkness thickened around me with a loneliness that had weight.

For a moment the leaves—or what looked like leaves—still swept fluttering all one way. Then the wind ceased. The owl floated silent through the silent night.

Not for a moment had I been afraid. Whoever would cross the threshold of any world must leave fear behind; but I could claim no virtue in its absence. No conscious courage worked in me. Simply, I was not afraid. I did not know why. I did not know what fear would have been for. I feared not even fear—which of all dangers is the most dangerous.

I went out into the wood at once to resume my journey. Another moon was rising, and I turned my face toward it.

I had not gone ten paces through that hush of trees before something in the dim ahead took shape—an object too square and deliberate to be a boulder, too upright to be a stump. I stepped nearer, and the air changed, as if I had come upon an old pantry long sealed: a dry, sweetish mould, the ghost of leather, and the faint metallic taint of rusted iron. The forest’s own sound—leaf-breath and distant drip—seemed to draw back out of respect for what waited there.

It was a carriage, ancient in cut and heavy in build, set as if it had simply halted and been forgotten. The wheels still held, though the wood was grey and furred with rot; the pole lay straight between two heaps that were once horses. Their skulls, bleached clean as table-bones, grinned up at nothing. From those rigid heads the reins climbed, shrivelled to corded threads, to the hand of a coachman who was himself only a framework on a tattered hammer-cloth. Both doors had dropped away. Inside, in the corners where satin and perfume must once have gathered, sat two skeletons leaning back as if asleep after a long road and a long supper.

Even as I looked, they started awake.

The sound was like a larder shelf collapsing: a cracking, a brittle rattle, a chatter of joints. Each sprang toward the doorless opening on its side. One went down in a clatter and lay sprawled; the other stood a moment, shivering through every hinge as if its very structure were trying to remember how to be. Then, stiffly—like a cook with cramped fingers trying to tie a truss after winter—it crept along, one hand gripping the carriage-back, the thin leg-bones trembling under the burden of its own emptiness. It reached the fallen one and dropped to its knees beside it, fumbling to lift it, nearly toppling itself again.

At last the prostrate skeleton rose, not smoothly, but with a sudden jerk into a sitting posture. For a few moments its yellowed skull turned this way and that, as if scenting for a door in the air. Then, heedless of its neighbour, it hauled itself upright by grasping the spokes of the hind wheel. Half erected, it stood with its back to the other, both hands clamped around one knee-joint as if trying to keep a loose lid on a pot that would not stay shut.

With little less difficulty and not a few grotesque contortions, the kneeling one rose and spoke.

“Have you hurt yourself, my lord?” it asked.

The voice came thin and ill-articulated, like speech heard across a draughty passage—sound blown aside, words stripped of warmth as if the forest itself had no patience for human quarrels.

“Yes, I have,” answered the other, rougher. “You would do nothing to help me, and this cursed knee is out!”

“I did my best, my lord.”

“No doubt, my lady, for it was bad! I thought I should never find my feet again!—But, bless my soul, madam! are you out in your bones?”

She cast a look down her length, a motion comical in its bareness.

“I have nothing else to be out in,” she returned; “—and YOU at least cannot complain! But what on earth does it mean? Am I dreaming?”

“YOU may be dreaming, madam—I cannot tell; but this knee of mine forbids me the grateful illusion.—Ha! I too, I perceive, have nothing to walk in but bones!—Not so unbecoming to a man, however! I trust to goodness they are not MY bones! every one aches worse than another, and this loose knee worst of all! The bed must have been damp—and I too drunk to know it!”

“Probably, my lord of Cokayne!”

“What! what!—You make me think I too am dreaming—aches and all! How do YOU know the title my roistering bullies give me? I don’t remember you!—Anyhow, you have no right to take liberties!
