My name is—I am lord—tut, tut! What do you call me when I’m—I mean when you are sober? I cannot—at the moment,—Why, what IS my name?—I must have been VERY drunk when I went to bed! I often am!”

“You come so seldom to mine, that I do not know, my lord; but I may take your word for THAT!”

“I hope so!”

“—if for nothing else!”

“Hoity toity! I never told you a lie in my life!”

“You never told me anything but lies.”

“Upon my honour!—Why, I never saw the woman before!”

“You knew me well enough to lie to, my lord!”

“I do seem to begin to dream I have met you before, but, upon my oath, there is nothing to know you by! Out of your clothes, who is to tell who you may not be?—One thing I MAY swear—that I never saw you so much undressed before!—By heaven, I have no recollection of you!”

“I am glad to hear it: my recollections of you are the less distasteful!—Good morning, my lord!”

She turned away and hobbled a few paces, the clack and chime of her bones sharp in the soft moss-muffled world, like cutlery dropped on stone. Then she stopped and stood again, as if memory—like a half-remembered recipe—had caught her and would not let her go until it was finished.

“You are just as heartless as—as—any other woman, madam!—Where in this hell of a place shall I find my valet?—What was the cursed name I used to call the fool?”

He swung his bare noddle this way and that on its creaking pivot, still gripping his knee with both hands, as if nursing a joint of meat that refused to bind.

“I will be your valet for once, my lord,” said the lady, turning back to him. “—What can I do for you? It is not easy to tell!”

“Tie my leg on, of course, you fool! Can’t you see it is all but off? Heigho, my dancing days!”

She stared about with her eyeless sockets and found a strip of fibrous grass—tough as old twine, pale and sinewy. With the resigned deftness of someone who has spent years mending what another has broken, she knelt and bound the adjoining parts where the knee had been. The motions were domestic, almost homely: loop, draw tight, tuck, as if trussing a bird for roasting—only there was no flesh to hold the knot, no skin to give.

When she had finished, he stamped once or twice, carefully tentative.

“You used to stamp rather differently, my lord!” she said as she rose.

“Eh? what!—Now I look at you again, it seems to me I used to hate you!—Eh?”

“Naturally, my lord! You hated a good many people!—your wife, of course, among the rest!”

“Ah, I begin, I be-gin— But—I must have been a long time somewhere!—I really forget!—There! your damned, miserable bit of grass is breaking!—We used to get on PRETTY well together—eh?”

“Not that I remember, my lord. The only happy moments I had in your company were scattered over the first week of our marriage.”

“Was that the way of it? Ha! ha!—Well, it’s over now, thank goodness!”

“I wish I could believe it! Why were we sitting there in that carriage together? It wakes apprehension!”

“I think we were divorced, my lady!”

“Hardly enough: we are still together!”

“A sad truth, but capable of remedy: the forest seems of some extent!”

“I doubt! I doubt!”

“I am sorry I cannot think of a compliment to pay you—without lying, that is. To judge by your figure and complexion you have lived hard since I saw you last! I cannot surely be QUITE so naked as your ladyship!—I beg your pardon, madam! I trust you will take it I am but jesting in a dream! It is of no consequence, however; dreaming or waking, all’s one—all merest appearance! You can’t be certain of anything, and that’s as good as knowing there is nothing! Life may teach any fool that!”

“It has taught me the fool I was to love you!”

“You were not the only fool to do that! Women had a trick of falling in love with me:—I had forgotten that you were one of them!”

“I did love you, my lord—a little—at one time!”

“Ah, there was your mistake, my lady! You should have loved me much, loved me devotedly, loved me savagely—loved me eternally! Then I should have tired of you the sooner, and not hated you so much afterward!—But let bygones be bygones!—WHERE are we? Locality is the question! To be or not to be, is NOT the question!”

“We are in the other world, I presume!”

“Granted!—but in which or what sort of other world? This can’t be hell!”

“It must: there’s marriage in it! You and I are damned in each other.”

“Then I’m not like Othello, damned in a fair wife!—Oh, I remember my Shakspeare, madam!”

She stooped and picked up a broken branch that had fallen into a bush—dry wood with a faint, dusty scent when it scraped the moss. Steadying herself with it, she walked away, tossing her little skull as if she could still toss a coiffure.

“Give that stick to me,” cried her late husband; “I want it more than you.”

She returned him no answer.

“You mean to make me beg for it?”

“Not at all, my lord. I mean to keep it,” she replied, continuing her slow departure.

“Give it me at once; I mean to have it! I require it.”

“Unfortunately, I think I require it myself!” returned the lady, walking a little quicker, her joints cracking sharper, her bones clinking like a cupboard of china in an earthquake.

He started to follow her and nearly fell. The grass binding had burst; with an oath he stopped and clutched his leg again. He would have thundered, but he only piped and whistled, a ridiculous reed of rage in a forest that had better music.

She turned and looked at him.

“Come and tie it up instantly!” he repeated.

She walked a step or two farther away.

“I swear I will not touch you!” he cried.

“Swear on, my lord! there is no one here to believe you. But, pray, do not lose your temper, or you will shake yourself to pieces, and where to find string enough to tie up all your crazy joints, is more than I can tell.”

She came back and knelt once more at his side—first, however, laying the stick in dispute beyond his reach and within her own, like a careful housekeeper setting the good knife where the drunk cannot snatch it.

The instant she had finished retying the joint, he made a grab at her, as if to seize her by the hair; but his hard fingers slipped on the smooth poll.

“Disgusting!” he muttered, and laid hold of her upper arm-bone.

“You will break it!” she said, looking up from her knees.

“I will, then!” he answered, and began to strain at it.

“I shall not tie your leg again the next time it comes loose!” she threatened.

He gave her arm a vicious twist; but happily her bones were in better condition than his, as if somewhere, long ago, she had been fed more honestly than he deserved. She stretched her other hand toward the broken branch.

“That’s right: reach me the stick!” he grinned.

She brought it round with such a swing that one of the bones of his sounder leg snapped with a dry report—like a brittle spar breaking in a gale, or a roasted bone cracking under a careless knife. He fell, choking with curses.

The lady laughed; in that laugh there was something that might once have been tears, only dried out of all softness.

“Now you will have to wear splints always!” she said; “such dry bones never mend!”

“You devil!” he cried.

“At your service, my lord! Shall I fetch you a couple of wheel-spokes? Neat—but heavy, I fear!”

He turned his bone-face aside and did not answer, but lay and groaned. I marvelled he had not gone to pieces when he fell, as an old crate does when dropped. The lady rose and walked away—not all ungracefully, I thought, and the click of her departing steps dwindled until the forest’s own small sounds flowed back in, like broth returning to a pot when the lid is lifted.

What could come of it? I asked myself. They were too wretched for any world; and this could not be hell, for the Little Ones were in it, and the sleepers too. What could it all mean? Could things ever come right for skeletons—could there be any slow cooking of the soul, any long simmer that draws bitterness out and leaves something fit to be eaten?

“There are words too big for you and me: ALL is one of them, and EVER is another,” said a voice near me—quiet, close, as if spoken over my shoulder in a library aisle.

I looked about, but could not see the speaker.

“You are not in hell,” it resumed. “Neither am I in hell. But those skeletons are in hell!”

Before the last word had quite settled into the air, I caught sight of the raven on the bough of a beech right over my head. The same moment it left the branch and dropped to the ground, and there stood the thin old man of the library—long nose, long coat—like a figure stepped out of a narrow folio and made flesh enough to cast a shadow.

“The male was never a gentleman,” he went on, “and in the bony stage of retrogression, with his skeleton through his skin, and his character outside his manners, does not look like one. The female is less vulgar, and has a little heart. But, the restraints of society removed, you see them now just as they are and always were!”

“Tell me, Mr. Raven, what will become of them,” I said.

“We shall see,” he replied. “In their day they were the handsomest couple at court; and now, even in their dry bones, they seem to regard their former repute as an inalienable possession; to see their faces, however, may yet do something for them! They felt themselves rich too while they had pockets, but they have already begun to feel rather pinched! My lord used to regard my lady as a worthless encumbrance, for he was tired of her beauty and had spent her money; now he needs her to cobble his joints for him! These changes have roots of hope in them. Besides, they cannot now get far away from each other, and they see none else of their own kind: they must at last grow weary of their mutual repugnance, and begin to love one another! for love, not hate, is deepest in what Love ‘loved into being.’”

“I saw many more of their kind an hour ago, in the hall close by!” I said.

“Of their kind, but not of their sort,” he answered. “For many years these will see none such as you saw last night. Those are centuries in advance of these. You saw that those could even dress themselves a little! It is true they cannot yet retain their clothes so long as they would—only, at present, for a part of the night; but they are pretty steadily growing more capable, and will by and by develop faces; for every grain of truthfulness adds a fibre to the show of their humanity. Nothing but truth can appear; and whatever is must seem.”

“Are they upheld by this hope?” I asked.

“They are upheld by hope, but they do not in the least know their hope; to understand it, is yet immeasurably beyond them,” answered Mr. Raven.

His unexpected presence brought me no astonishment. In that world I was like a child always wondering, and therefore surprised at nothing: the strange arrived with the simplicity of bread set on the table.

“Did you come to find me, sir?” I asked.

“Not at all,” he replied. “I have no anxiety about you. Such as you always come back to us.”

“Tell me, please, who am I such as?” I said.

“I cannot make my friend the subject of conversation,” he answered, with a smile.

“But when that friend is present!” I urged.

“I decline the more strongly,” he rejoined.

“But when that friend asks you!” I persisted.

“Then most positively I refuse,” he returned.

“Why?”

“Because he and I would be talking of two persons as if they were one and the same. Your consciousness of yourself and my knowledge of you are far apart!”

The lapels of his coat flew out and the lappets lifted, and I thought the change from HOMO to CORVUS was about to happen before my eyes; as if a word were being stripped of its borrowed meaning to reveal the older tongue beneath. But the coat closed again in front of him, and he added, with seeming inconsequence:

“In this world never trust a person who has once deceived you. Above all, never do anything such a one may ask you to do.”

“I will try to remember,” I answered; “—but I may forget!”

“Then some evil that is good for you will follow.”

“And if I remember?”

“Some evil that is not good for you, will not follow.”

As he spoke, he seemed to sink to the ground, as if the earth had opened like a silent book and taken him between its leaves. Immediately I saw the raven several yards from me, flying low and fast through the trees, its wings making a clean, living sound in the forest’s careful hush.

I went on, keeping my face to the moon as if she were a lantern I had once trusted and now did not dare to turn my back upon. She was not yet high. She stared straight into the forest with a look that made me think of old kitchen copper left too long in the rain—dented, darkened, and tired of shining. No cloud came near her; the stars were too sharp and bright, as if they belonged to some newer coinage. She seemed to travel one road while I took another, yet for a long while we kept pace through the trees, she the dulled disc above, I the moving shadow below. The forest had its own speech—needles whispering, trunks creaking in slow syllables, night insects ticking like tiny spoons against glass—and over it all, the silence carried a weight. No wheels, no engines, no iron clatter; the absence of modern noise felt like a clean, strict diet after a lifetime of greasy din.

My eyes were mostly on the ground. It is the way of anyone who has ever foraged: you learn to read the floor for what it offers, what it hides, what it has dropped. Under a spreading tree something pale caught the moon’s meagre shine. At first it was only a whiteness, an irregular patch among needles and shadow, but as I drew nearer it took the terrible shape the mind recognizes before it admits.

“Another skeleton!” I said, and knelt.

My hand found not bone, but body—body so wasted it might have been only a skin stretched over a frame. It was cold; not the honest cold of stone, but the cold of something that had once held warmth as naturally as bread holds steam, and now would not. The more I looked, the more I touched, the more my senses insisted on death. For a bewildered instant the fancy came that it was one of the wild dancers—some ghostly Cinderella of that strange revel—lost on the way home and spent at last in the open night.

She was quite naked. Even in the shadow under the boughs I could have counted each rib without laying a finger between them. The skin clung like tight parchment. The lips had drawn back from the teeth, and those teeth—beautiful in shape, terrible in their bareness—gleamed through the dark. Her hair was longer than her body, thick, fine as sifted flour to the touch, and black as the space between stars. She must have been tall; she might once have been graceful. How had she come here? Not of herself, surely, and already so far gone. Had she staggered until her strength failed, fallen, and lain until hunger finished the work? But even hunger… how could it carve a person so clean? And naked—how? No decent thought fitted. If savages had stripped her, why leave her? If beasts had taken cloth, why not flesh? Yet there she was, abandoned to any irreverent eye, any brutal claw, to be worried and tossed until the friendly rains, year upon year, washed her into the soil.

I rose, stood over her, and tried to think like a man who still belonged to men. I must not—could not—leave her like that. Even the garment of a woman claims a kind of reverence; her uncovered body demanded it with a sharpness that made my throat tighten. But the ground was hard, knotted with roots, and I had only my hands. Burial would be slow work, perhaps impossible.

Then another question, small and fierce, began to stir: What if she were not dead?

This world had already taught me that the ordinary rules did not always hold. At first I had thought she could not have been dead long—there was no sign of decay—but then what was there in her to decay? Her wasting had left her almost nothing but form. Could life, reduced to some ember, still be hiding in that pitiful hearth?

As I left the forest-hall, I had seen a bunch of ripe grapes in the doorway and had taken it, eating as I came. A few still hung on the stalk, their skins tight, their scent a dark sweetness that seemed indecently alive beside her. If anything could persuade a spark to flare, it might be that sharp, clean juice.

Her mouth was a little open. The head lay awkwardly. To move her I slid my arm under the shoulder she lay upon—and felt warmth in the pine-needles beneath her. Not much, but unmistakable: the bed under her had not yet cooled to the forest’s temperature. She could not have been long as I found her. She might still be—alive, though no breath stirred my cheek, no heart declared itself to my searching hand.

One of her hands was clenched hard around something small. I could not see what it was. I tried not to force it; the grip looked like the last stubborn habit of a will that refused to let go.

I squeezed a grape into her mouth, letting the juice wet the teeth and tongue. No swallowing followed. No flutter of the throat. Still, I could not stop. Doubt may be poor encouragement, but it is a worse excuse for doing nothing.

I made a nest as if for a loaf that must not lose its heat: a thick layer of pine-needles and dry leaves, then one of my garments, warm from my body. I lifted her onto it—so light she seemed scarcely to weigh more than her hair—and covered her with my clothes and a great heap of leaves. I wanted to keep whatever little warmth remained, to hoard it until the sun returned like an oven door opening.

I tried another grape. Nothing.

So tight was the skin upon her bones that I dared not rub her, as one might rub frozen hands. I crept into the heap of leaves and got as close to her as I could, drawing her into my arms. I had not much heat in me, but what I had I would share. Her cold seemed to breathe outward, as if death were a kind of climate. No answering warmth came back from her; only the steadiness of her stillness.

And there, under that sad, slow-setting moon, I lay with the dead—or the nearly dead—and watched for dawn.

When the darkness began to thin, the forest’s sound changed with it. A faint chorus of waking stirred in the understory: the quick shiver of small wings, the soft patter of something light-footed, the deliberate creak of a bough adjusting its load. The acoustic world felt like weather—pressure and release—and I found myself listening as if the right combination of sounds might mean her life had returned.

Instead I saw motion close to the ground: the low undulating glide of a large snake passing in an unswerving line. Then, as if pulled by the same invisible thread, a creature like a roebuck-doe went by with her calf. After that, two beasts like bear-cubs, with three or four smaller ones behind them. The light came quickly now, and a troop of horses trotted past—no larger than the smallest Shetland pony, yet full-grown in their perfection. Some had the build of cart-horses, others the carriage of chargers or racers. Their hooves made a soft, numerous drumming in the grass, not the harsh ring of iron on road, but a sound like hands on packed earth.

Dwarf cattle followed. Small elephants—ridiculous and solemn—moved with their private thunder. The procession had the feel of an old habit, a domestic routine of the wild, as if this migration were as regular as morning bread.

“Why are the children not here!” I thought, suddenly pierced with the memory of the Little Ones. “The moment I am free of this poor woman, I must go back and fetch them!”

But I could not leave her yet. I laid my hand on her. It seemed—only seemed—a trifle warmer. Perhaps she had stolen a degree from my body. She could hardly have made any of her own. Still, hope did not lessen.

The horizon flushed. The sun came up peering, innocent, as if he had never seen this world before and had risen early just to stare at it. His light struck through me like a draught of clean hot broth after days of hunger. I rose full of life, braced against death.

I lifted the handkerchief I had arranged to keep pine-needles from her mouth and eyes and looked, trying to decide whether I had found a priceless jewel or only its empty case. She lay motionless. In the new light her face was worse than I had known: the hollows were deeper, the bones sharper, every tooth insisting through the drawn lips. The human garment was worn to threads; yet perhaps the bird of heaven still nested within. Perhaps it would wake to motion and song.

But the sun shone on her face. I covered her again, light leaves over the handkerchief, and went to see where the animals were going.

Their track was well beaten, old with use. Other paths joined it like tributaries, widening it. The trees retreated. Grass thickened. Then the forest ended, and a plain of the loveliest green stretched away until it met the sky. Along the forest’s edge a small river flowed, and to it the track led.

At sight of the water a new hope leapt in me—hot, immediate. Mist, bluish and thin, rose and vanished as it rose. On the far side, in abundant grass, small animals fed as if breakfast were the one sure law. It seemed they slept in the forest and in the morning swam the river to the plain.

I knelt to drink. The water was hot.

It shocked me more than cold would have. I tasted it: it had a strange metallic savour, like water that has slept in copper, or like the lick of a knife’s edge. I sprang up. Here was warmth in plenty—warmth that ran, constant, like a fire that never needed feeding. Warmth was the first necessity of life. This might be the difference between her ember and her ash.

I ran back.

Without understanding my solitude no one will know what seemed to hang upon the redemption of this woman. “Prove what she may,” I thought, “I shall at least be lonely no more.” In that thought was a selfishness I could not clean away, as one cannot clean hunger out of a starving man. I had found myself such poor company that only now did I seem to know what hope was. This blessed hot water would expel the cold death—and drown my desolation.

I lifted her. Tall as she was, she weighed almost nothing; her bones were delicate, scarcely clothed. Yet she was not stiff. She yielded like a sleeping child. That looseness made me more hopeful than any warmth in the needles had done. I carried her on one arm, her head against my shoulder, and moved softly, as if even the wind of my passing might frighten away the last flutter of life.

At the stream I hesitated. The water was too hot to plunge her into at once. A sudden shock might startle whatever remained and drive it out, like steam escaping when you lift a lid too fast. I laid her on the bank and soaked one of my garments, then began to bathe her slowly.

As I worked, I could not even guess her age. Youth and age were both erased by starvation. Only the astonishing abundance of her black hair argued for life—hair that looked as if it drank darkness and stored it. Her eyelids were not quite shut, which made her seem more dead: a crack in the clouds of her night with no dawn behind it.

I kept bathing her continuously, as a careful cook keeps a sauce from breaking by never letting one part cool while another is heated. I would not allow any section of her poor frame to lose what the water gave it. Gradually her skin felt less like winter. At last, when the heat had soaked through as much as it could, I ventured to submerge her. I stepped into the stream and drew her in, holding her face above the surface while the swift, steady current flowed around the rest of her.

Her clenched hand did not loosen, though the heat swaddled her. I noted it and could conclude nothing.

After about ten minutes I lifted her out, laid her on the bank, dried her, covered her as well as I could, and ran to the forest for leaves. The soil and grass were dry and warm. When I returned she had scarcely cooled. I spread leaves over her and ran for more—again and again, until she lay like some strange seed buried shallow, warmed from below, waiting.

Now I could leave her briefly to seek shelter. I ran upstream toward rocky hills not far off. There the river issued full-grown from a rock at the base of one hill, as if it had travelled hidden through stone and chosen that spot to break into day. It did not fill the opening; I crept through and found a little cave.

Inside, the stream did not roar down any stair as I had imagined. It rose quietly from the ground at the back like the base of a thick column and ran along one side in a deep, narrow channel, almost filling it. The air in that cave smelled of wet rock and warm mineral—an odour like heated iron with the sting filed off—and the sound of the water was steady, a living hush that calmed the nerves, not like the punishing noise of machines. It was a place where a mind could heal simply by listening.

I saw at once what might be made: if I could find fallen boughs long enough to span the channel, stout enough not to bend, then with smaller branches and leaves I could build a couch above the warm stream—a bed kept constantly heated from beneath, like a hearth that never smoked.

I ran back to her. She lay as I had left her. Heat had not awakened her, but neither had it summoned decay. Hope remained possible.

I set boulders near her feet and along her sides to keep her from rolling. Then I went to the wood and found boughs fit for my purpose, many of beech, dry and pale, with yellow leaves still clinging as if unwilling to forget their summer language. I laid them across the channel, then crossed them with smaller branches, interlaced twigs, and buried everything deep under leaves and dry moss until the bridge became a warm, dry, soft couch.

When at last it was finished, after not a few journeys, I lifted her again and carried her to the cave. She was so light that more than once I had the irrational fear that when I set her down she would have become a skeleton after all, emptied while I walked. When I laid her gently on the leaf-deep bridge, it was a relief to let go of that fancy even more than to set down the weight.

I covered her thickly with leaves. Then I tried again with a grape. This time I could open her mouth a little farther. The grape lay there, unheeded, but I hoped the juice might creep down of its own accord, like rain into dry ground.

After an hour or two on that warmed couch, she was no longer cold. The brook’s heat had seeped into her—truly she was but a frame—and she was warm to the touch. Not, perhaps, with the warmth of life, but with a warmth that made life more possible. I remembered having read of one in a trance lying motionless for weeks.

So in that cave I stayed.

Day after day, night after night—seven long days and nights—I sat or lay, now waking, now sleeping, but always watching. Each morning I went out and bathed in the hot stream. Each morning, stepping from it, I felt as if I had eaten and drunk. That strange nourishment gave me courage to lay her in it also every day, carefully, as if curing her were a slow cooking that must not scorch. After her bath I always put a fresh grape in her mouth.

Once, lifting her, I saw a shadow of discoloration on her left side. Terror struck me like a cleaver’s blow. But the next morning it had vanished, and I continued. I too ate grapes and other berries from the forest, their skins bursting with sharp sweetness, their scents marking the hours better than any clock. Yet I believed that with my daily bath in that river I could have lived well enough without food at all.

Every time I slept I dreamed I had found a wounded angel. Unable to fly, she stayed with me until at last she loved me and would not leave. Every time I woke it was to see, instead of bright eyes, the white motionless wasted face on the couch—lips drawn, eyelids cracked open upon nothing.

But Adam himself, when first he saw her asleep, could not have watched more anxiously for Eve’s waking than I watched for this woman’s. Adam, perhaps, knew nothing of his own need; I, an alien from my fellows, had learned to love what I had lost. If this shred of womanhood vanished—if she proved only a husk—there would be nothing left in me but a consuming hunger after life.

I forgot even the Little Ones. Things were not amiss with them, I told myself; they had each other, and their own harmless customs. Here lay something else: what might wake and be a woman—might actually open eyes, and look out of them upon me. Now first I knew what solitude meant: not being without voices in the world, but gazing on one who neither saw nor heard, neither moved nor spoke, while the cave filled with the tender sound of water that spoke in a tongue I could not translate into comfort.

A man alone is not a man, but a creature that may become one—need and possibility. To be enough for himself, a being would have to be some eternal self-existent worm. Man is too superbly made, too simply intricate; he rises from and stands upon a pedestal of lower lives and higher structures, and he cannot breathe long in any atmosphere less divine than that offered by other souls. Only in the reflex of other lives can he ripen into himself.

Save for the faint prospect of dawn in the form beside me, I should have fled for fellowship to the beasts that grazed and did not speak. Better to go about with them—infinitely better—than to live alone. But with even the smallest chance that the silent mouth might yet shape a word, in whatever native dialect her spirit still remembered, I—the poorest of creatures—was yet a possible man.

I woke out of a sleep so deep it felt older than my own life, with pain already speaking in my hand before my eyes were open. The back of it had risen into a hot swell, and in the centre a wound gaped—three clean sides meeting like a little pale triangle, as if some hungry mouth had taken a measured taste. The ache was sharp as brine in a cut.

All that day the swelling sank as though the flesh remembered its proper shape. By evening the hurt was almost gone, the skin drawn tight and smooth again as kneaded dough that has taken back its spring. I turned the cave inside out—lifted stones, felt along cracks, searched the mossy edge of the hot stream where it ran through the rock with a constant hiss like a kettle never allowed to boil over. I found nothing that could have done it. No teeth, no claw-marks, no track in the damp sand; only the steady, domestic noises of the place—the water’s hurry, the faint drip from the roof, and now and then the small click of grit shifting under my own feet.

The days took on their slow, circular labour: morning light filtering into the mouth of the cave; the bath in the hot river; the return; the fruit; the watching. She lay where I had brought her, white as washed bone, and still as if the last breath had been taken and carefully folded away. Yet she did not rot. There was no sweetening stench, no thickening air. The cave stayed clean with the stream’s moving breath.

And sometimes—only sometimes, and then I doubted my own eyes until doubt sickened me—she seemed a little less terrible. The knife-edges of her bones were not so abrupt. The hollows under the cheeks held less shadow. The skin—once like old parchment stretched too far—began to look more like skin. If that change was real, then life had turned its face back again. The tide that had ebbed toward the infinite had found some hidden moon and begun to creep home.

So I watched with the hunger of a man counting down the minutes of a slow-roasting feast while knowing he has no fire but patience. Twenty times a day I leaned close to catch the smallest sign: a pulse, a warmth, a shift in the lips. Twenty times a day I told myself I had imagined it. Then I would remember what she had been when first I found her—nothing but hair and horror and the stubborn refusal to decay—and hope would rise again like yeast.

Weeks went by. One night I lay long awake, the cave close despite the stream’s freshness, and at last rose to go out to the mouth and drink the cooler air. The moon was full outside; inside, everything was washed in a pale clarity that made the white of her face seem to glow.

Naturally—how could I not?—I looked at her again before stepping out.

“Bliss eternal!” I cried aloud. “Do I see her eyes?”

Two great orbs, dark as if cut from a starless sky, seemed to shine there—luminous not with light, but with an excess of darkness that drank the moon’s pale spill and returned it as depth. My heart beat so hard it felt noisy enough to wake the stones. I crept nearer, bent over her—

Her lids were shut.

Hope and imagination, like two cooks seasoning the same broth, had overdone it and made me taste what was not there. I turned away, dropped to the cave floor, and wept into the cool grit like a child shamed by his own longing.

Then another thought came, small but steady as the stream’s sound: her eyes had been a little open before. Now that dreadful chink—like a slit cut into nothingness—was gone. Something had closed them. Sleep, perhaps; or a will, perhaps. In either case, life—more or less—must have moved. I held that thought the way a starving man holds a crust, and I slept.

That night I was bitten again.

I woke with my mouth scorched and my throat begging, as if my blood had been simmered away while I slept. In the morning, I searched again—more thoroughly than before, turning stones until my fingers were raw, poking into every crevice where damp made a dark lip. Still nothing. The wound was the same triangular taste, and again it was nearly healed by evening, as if whatever fed on me brought its own quick salve.

I began to suspect the hot stream. In its ceaseless rushing there was a sound that could hide anything: a soft slick movement against stone; a body sliding through water without splash. Some great leech-kind, perhaps, that came when the cave was darkest and my senses dull.

“If blood be its object,” I told myself, “so long as I am here, it need not touch my treasure.”

That same morning, as I did what had become my most careful, most reverent cookery—peeling a grape so the skin would not snag in her dry mouth, picking out every seed with a fingernail as one removes grit from rice—I placed the fruit between her lips.

Her mouth moved.

Not much—only the smallest closing, the faintest acceptance, like a child taking milk without waking fully. But it was unmistakable. The lips met the grape. The grape vanished.

I knew, with a certainty that made my hands shake, that she lived.

Hope made me practical. If she woke and tried to rise, she must not rise naked into the cold; she must have something between her revived skin and the rough world. I went to the forest and searched not for beauty but for material—what would bend, what would breathe, what would not chafe. There I found, in the fibrous skeletons of leaves like those of the prickly pear, a kind of netted tissue: veins without the pulp, strength without weight.

I gathered a bundle and laid them in the sun, letting heat dry them until they crackled faintly when touched. The forest had its own acoustic order—the insect-thin whirrs, the heavy drop of fruit, the wind combing the upper leaves. Compared to the harsh clank and whine of the world I had known before, it was music made for sanity. Here, nothing screamed to sell itself. Nothing demanded attention except by being alive.

Back in the cave I pulled apart the reticulated layers and began to fashion garments: one to hang from her waist, one from her shoulders. I used the stiletto-point of an aloe leaf as my awl, and for thread I twisted various filaments until they held. Three thicknesses, sewn together—because softness matters, and because I could not bear the thought of her waking to be hurt by my clumsiness.

During the week that followed she gave no clearer sign than that she took the grapes more readily, as if the tongue had remembered its work. Yet her body changed. She grew plumper; the skin took on a faint living sheen, no longer the dry pallor of something left behind. Still she did not open her eyes.

And into my hope crept a fear as cold as spoiled broth: what if this growth was wrong? What if something other than her was feeding, swelling her as fungi swell on wood? The few grapes could not account for the change. I tried to reason it away, but my body, drained by those night visitations, grew weaker, and reason had less salt in it each day.

For the biting continued. The thing—whatever it was—came now with a terrible regularity: every three days. It bit me in the neck or the arm. Always one bite. Always while I slept. Never in the daytime, even when I slept then. I lay awake for hours, listening, trying to tune my mind to the smallest change in the cave’s soundscape—the stream’s pitch, the drip’s rhythm, the shift of my own breath—hoping to catch the moment when something else joined the choir.

I never heard it.

If it moved, it moved like a thief in silk. If it drank, it drank without even the indignity of pain to warn me. And the cave’s constant water-speech swallowed every secret.

At last I grew so hopeless of catching it that I stopped trying. By day I no longer tore the cave apart. By night I no longer stared into the dark until my eyes ached. I knew, from the faintness that came too easily and the way I tired after the smallest labour, that I was losing blood at a dangerous rate.

I did not care.

In sight of my eyes, death was yielding to life. A soul was gathering strength to save me from loneliness. We would go away together, and when I could walk in the open again, eat properly again, I would recover quickly enough. A man can be bled and still live; a man can be alone and die without a wound.

When the garments were finished, I looked at them with the satisfaction of a poor cook who has managed, with wild ingredients and no proper tools, to make something that will at least not poison the one he serves. Then I set to matting layers of fibre into sandals, for I would not have her step barefoot on stone and thorn.

One night I woke suddenly, breathless, faint, craving air as if the cave had become a sealed jar. I rose to crawl toward the mouth when a slight rustle in the leaves of my couch pinned me still. The sound was tiny, but in that place of continuous water it was a distinct syllable.

“I caught the vile thing,” said a feeble voice—in my mother-tongue, the old words shaped exactly as they had been shaped in my childhood kitchen and my father’s fields, before other languages and other noises had tried to wear them down. “I caught it in the very act!”

For a moment the language itself struck me harder than the news: that she spoke in my own speech, unborrowed, unbroken, as if it had never been dragged through other mouths. I felt the fierce, almost superstitious relief of hearing a dialect live—hearing memory keep its skin.

She was alive. She spoke.

I dared not exult aloud, lest I frighten her back into silence. “What creature?” I breathed.

“The creature,” she answered, “that was biting you.”

“What was it?”

“A great white leech.”

“How big?” I forced myself to keep my voice calm, as if discussing the size of a fish at market.

“Not far from six feet long, I should think,” she said.

“You have saved my life, perhaps—But how could you touch the horrid thing! How brave of you!” My own words sounded foolish beside the stark fact of her waking.

“I did!” was all she answered, and I thought I heard a shudder in her breath.

“Where is it? What could you do with such a monster?”

“I threw it in the river.”

“Then it will come again, I fear!”

“I do not think I could have killed it, even had I known how. I heard you moaning, and got up to see what disturbed you; saw the frightful thing at your neck, and pulled it away. But I could not hold it, and was hardly able to throw it from me. I only heard it splash in the water!”

“We’ll kill it next time,” I said, and meant it; but the words were hardly out when darkness tilted. My blood, already thinned by those feedings, failed me. I turned toward the open air and fell as if the cave floor rose to meet me.

When I came to myself, sunlight was in the mouth of the cave, and the world had that early hush before birds and wind build their full day. She stood a little way off.

Even in the clumsy attire I had fashioned from leaf-skeletons, she looked grand and graceful, like a tall flame wrapped in pale smoke. I had seen those eyes. Through the night they had shone. Dark as primeval night, they now outshone the day—not by brightness, but by a depth that made everything else seem thin.

She stood erect as a column and regarded me. Her pale cheek showed no emotion—only question, like a blade held flat.

I rose, unsteady. “We must be going,” I said, thinking first of the leech and second of everything else.

“The white leech—” I began, but stopped. A strange smile flickered across her face, brief as a spark on wet wood.

“Did you find me there?” she asked, pointing to the cave.

“No; I brought you there,” I replied.

“You brought me?”

“Yes.”

“From where?”

“From the forest.”

“What have you done with my clothes—and my jewels?”

“You had none when I found you.”

“Then why did you not leave me?”

“Because I hoped you were not dead.”

“Why should you have cared?”

“Because I was very lonely, and wanted you to live.”

“You would have kept me enchanted for my beauty!” she said, proud scorn in her voice, as if the very idea were an insult that explained everything.

Her words and her look stirred my anger—hot, sudden, like oil catching. “There was no beauty left in you,” I said.

“Why, then, again, did you not let me alone?”

“Because you were of my own kind.”

“Of YOUR kind?” she cried, and contempt made the words ring hard in the cave, a note that sat ugly against the stream’s honest rush. “I thought so, but find I was mistaken!”

“Doubtless you pitied me!”

“Never had woman more claim on pity, or less on any other feeling!”

Pain, mortification, and anger crossed her face in an expression too quick and too deep for me to name. She turned from me and stood silent. The splendour went out of her eyes as if someone had snuffed a lamp. Starless night lay profound in them; whatever had made them shine seemed slain by hate—hate of the one who had dragged her back.

“Had you failed to rouse me, what would you have done?” she asked suddenly, without moving.

“I would have buried it.”

“It! What?—You would have buried THIS?” she exclaimed, flashing round upon me in a white fury. Her arms flung wide, and in her eyes a cold lightning forked, not light but judgement.

“Nay; that I saw not. That, weary weeks of watching and tending have brought back to you,” I answered. With such a woman I must be plain. “Had I seen the smallest sign of decay, I would at once have buried you.”

“Dog of a fool!” she cried. “I was but in a trance—Samoil! what a fate!—Go and fetch the she-savage from whom you borrowed this hideous disguise.”

“I made it for you. It is hideous, but I did my best.”

She drew herself up to her full height, as if height were a weapon. “How long have I been insensible?” she demanded. “A woman could not have made that dress in a day!”

“Not in twenty days,” I rejoined, “hardly in thirty.”

“Ha! How long do you pretend I have lain unconscious?—Answer me at once.”

“I cannot tell how long you had lain when I found you, but there was nothing left of you save skin and bone: that is more than three months ago. Your hair was beautiful—nothing else. I have done for it what I could.”

“My poor hair!” she said, and drew a great armful of it from behind her, letting it spill through her fingers as if it were something precious saved from fire. “It will be more than a three-months’ care to bring YOU to life again. I suppose I must thank you, although I cannot say I am grateful!”

“There is no need, madam: I would have done the same for any woman—yes, or for any man either!”

“How is it my hair is not tangled?” she said, and her voice softened only for that, for the feel of it. “It always drifted in the current.”

“How?—What do you mean?”

“I could not have brought you to life but by bathing you in the hot river every morning.”

She shuddered as if I had smeared her with something foul, and stood a while staring at the hurrying water, hearing in its sound whatever history it carried that I could not translate for her. The stream spoke one language; her pride spoke another; my words, even when true, seemed to come to her already tainted, like food served in the wrong vessel.

Then she turned back to me. “We must understand each other,” she said. “You have done me the two worst of wrongs—compelled me to live, and put me to shame: neither of them can I pardon!”

She raised her left hand and flung it out as if she would push me from the world. Something ice-cold struck my forehead—cold as a river-stone pulled from deep water. The cave spun, sound stretching thin, and I knew no more.

When I came to myself, I lay on the ground, wet and shivering, the stream’s ceaseless rush filling my ears like a verdict that would not end.

I got up and the world wavered, as if the ground itself had been lifted and set down again a little aside from where it belonged. For a heartbeat I could not find her. The place where she had been—so near that her cold seemed to have entered my skin—was empty. Loneliness rolled back over me like a fog bank returning after a clear hour, smothering the taste of air.

Gone.

She whom I had dragged—by my own will, by my own stubborn hope—back from the verge of death, had fled and left me in the same desolation I thought I had bought my way out of. I could not endure that kind of solitude even for a moment. It had a sound: not silence, but a hollow ringing, like a room after the fire has gone out and the chimney keeps talking to itself. Had I wronged her? If I had compelled her to carry again the weight of living, then my life must be spent under that weight with her, not standing aside congratulating myself for a rescue.

I caught sight of her at last, moving swiftly over the grass, angling away from the river. The water behind me gave a low continuous voice, a dark, bread-thick murmur that had steadied me earlier; I plunged once into it for a last cold restorative—like swallowing a mouthful of bitter herb to wake the body—and then went after her.

The white leech’s visit and the woman’s blow had left me thin as a scraped bone, but strength was already creeping back, warm and insistent. I kept her in sight without trouble. Still, my heart carried a miserable refrain: Is this the end? Her eyes as I had last seen them—angry, hating—kept floating up before me, as if burned onto the underside of my lids.

I could understand her resentment at being forced to breathe again; I could not understand her loathing. What had I done beyond service? Could modesty itself take offense at help freely given? Every touch of mine had been reverent. I had borne her as a father bears his motherless child—careful of her weight, careful of her dignity, careful of the thin boundary between saving and taking. Had all my labor, all my despairing hope, purchased nothing but ingratitude?

No, I answered myself, as if speaking to an elder at a hearth who would not let me indulge in self-pity. Beauty must have a heart. It might be buried deeper than any seed in winter ground, but it must be there. And the deeper it lay, the more true would be its waking—if it ever woke. To rouse that heart would be a gift beyond the happiest life: it would be a nobler life.

She climbed a gentle slope ahead, walking straight and steady as one who knew her destination. Then I realized with a start that she was drawing away from me. I called on my strength as on a sleeping dog, and it came in a rush. My veins filled as if some new, clear broth had been poured into them. My body felt suddenly lighter—almost as though the air had become a buoyant medium meant to carry me. I moved after her like an easy wind and gained on her quickly.

Not once did she glance back.

She went swiftly, but without the agitation of haste—gliding, self-possessed, like a statue that has learned to walk. I was within three yards when she turned sharply and yet with unbroken grace, and stood. She showed neither fatigue nor heat; her paleness was not sickly but pure, like milk just skimmed, like flour sifted into a white bowl. Her breathing was slow and deep. Her eyes seemed to fill the heavens and cast light down into the world.

It was nearly noon, yet the sense upon me was of a great night: an invisible dew making the stars look close and large, so that one might almost smell their cold metal.

“Why do you follow me?” she asked—quietly, sternly, with the remote tone of a stranger meeting me for the first time.

“I have lived so long,” I said, “on the mere hope of your eyes, that I must want to see them again.”

“You will not be spared,” she returned, cold as a knife laid flat against the tongue. “I command you to stop where you stand.”

“Not until I see you in a place of safety will I leave you,” I said.

“Then take the consequences.” She turned and resumed her swift, gliding walk.

But as she turned she cast me one glance, and I stopped as if run through with a spear. Scorn had failed her. She would kill me with her beauty—kill me as surely as if she fed me sweetness until I forgot to breathe.

Despair brought my will back like a snapped cord mended in haste. The spell broke. I ran, caught up with her again.

“Have pity upon me!” I cried.

She gave no sign she had heard. I followed her as a child follows a mother who pretends to abandon him, who walks on without turning, trusting the child’s terror to keep him close.

“I will be your slave,” I said, and laid my hand on her arm.

She wheeled as if I had touched her with something venomous. The blaze of her eyes drove me inward; still I could not look away.

“Pity me,” I cried again.

She turned away and went on.

All day I followed her. The sun climbed, paused as if listening to the grass breathe, then went down the other side. She never stopped. I never ceased. Her pace did not relax. She did not once turn her head. There was nothing between us but the constant soft sound of our feet in thick, yielding turf—no stone, no road, no human artifact; only the world’s old carpeting under an immense sky.

When the sun slid under, night rose up. I kept close to her, because the thought of losing her even for a moment struck me like hunger. If she passed out of my sight, I knew, it might be forever.

We had walked the whole day over that thick, soft grass when suddenly she halted and threw herself down upon it. There was still light enough to show she was utterly weary. The sight struck me with an odd domestic tenderness—as if, for a moment, she were not a curse or a queen but simply a woman whose strength had given out at last, like a cook sinking onto a bench after feeding a household through harvest and winter.

I stood behind her, staring down. Did I love her? I knew she was not good. Did I hate her? I could not leave her.

I knelt beside her.

“Begone! Do not dare touch me,” she cried. Her arms lay on the grass at her sides, as if numb.

Then suddenly they rose and closed about my neck—rigid, inhuman, like the arms of an instrument built for pain. She drew my face down to hers. Her lips clung to my cheek.

A sting of pain shot through me and pulsed, a sharp, clean agony as if some hidden needle had found a vein. I could not stir even a hair’s breadth. The pain drained away. In its place came a slumberous weariness, and with it a dreamy pleasure—like the heavy comfort that follows a draught too sweet, too strong, taken in ignorance. Then I knew nothing.

All at once I woke.

The moon sat a little above the horizon, but gave no radiance; it was only a bright thing pinned into blackness, like a coin pressed into velvet. My cheek smarted. I lifted my hand and found it wet. My neck ached; there too my fingers came away damp. I sighed and felt emptied out, as if whatever had filled me earlier had been drawn off.

I turned my eyes dully and saw what had become of the moonlight: it was gathered about the lady. She stood wrapped in a shimmering nimbus, as if the darkness itself had agreed to honor her outline.

I rose and staggered toward her.

“Down!” she cried, imperiously, as one speaks to a dog that has forgotten its place. “Follow me a step if you dare!”

“I will,” I murmured, and it cost me like lifting a stone with broken arms.

“Set foot within the gates of my city, and my people will stone you: they do not love beggars!”

Her words hit my ears without entering me. Weak as water and only half awake, I did not know I moved; still the distance lessened.

She stepped back once, lifted her left arm, clenched her hand, and seemed to strike me on the forehead.

It was as if an iron hammer had fallen. I went down.

And then—instant as a snapped string—I sprang to my feet, cold and wet, clear-headed and strong. The blow had left no wound, no pain. Had it revived me? But how was I wet? I could not have lain long, for the moon was no higher.

The lady stood some yards away, her back to me, doing something I could not make out. Then by her sudden gleam I knew she had thrown off her garments and stood naked-white in the dazed moon.

For one moment she stood.

Then she fell forward.

A streak of white shot away from where she had been, drawn out in a swift line. In the same instant the moon seemed to recover herself and flashed full, and I saw that the streak was a long-bodied thing, rushing in great low-curved bounds over the grass. Dark spots streamed down its back as it went, as if it had been running under the edge of a wood and gathering leaf-shadows without ever touching the trees.

“God of mercy!” I cried. “Is the terrible creature speeding to the night-infolded city?”

And as I stared after it, fear turning my mouth to dust, it seemed to me that from far away there came a sudden bloom of sound—an outcry bursting and spreading—terror leaping from house to house as the pale savage bounded among them, rending and slaying. The thought had its own noise inside my skull: a modern, merciless clamor, like the crash of metal and the shriek of machines, drowning the delicate, living acoustics of a place where human voices ought to carry memory instead of panic.

While I gazed, another creature shot past me from behind—swift, almost noiseless as an arrow. It was large, pure white. Its path went straight for the place where the lady had fallen.

My tongue clove to the roof of my mouth. I sprang forward after it.

But the spot I aimed for fell behind the beast almost at once. It moved with a wasteless speed—long, level, skimming leaps that seemed to spend nothing, like a dancer who never lands, or a knife thrown true.

“It was well I could not cry out,” I thought wildly; “if she had risen, the monster would have been upon her!”

When I reached the place, the lady was not there. Only the garments she had dropped lay dark in the moonlight—cloth without a body, like a language after its last speaker has gone: the familiar shapes remain, but the living meaning has vanished, and one stands staring, suddenly illiterate.

I looked after the second beast. It tore over the ground even faster than the first, following the same line. I watched it grow smaller and smaller until it disappeared into the uncertain distance.

But where was the lady?

Had the first beast surprised her—creeping noiselessly? I had heard no shriek. There had not been time to devour her. Could it have caught her up as it ran and borne her away to its den? But burdened, it could not have flown so fast—and I should have seen it carrying something.

Horrible doubts woke in me like rats in a pantry.

I searched thoroughly, circling, combing the grass with my eyes and hands as if I might find a dropped pin, a lost seed, a trace of warmth. Nothing—no body, no mark, no sign beyond the abandoned clothes and the lingering wrongness in the air.

At last, with the night’s hush pressing close and the distant imagined cries still echoing in my nerves, I set out in the track of the two animals.

The moon had been riding clear as a pale platter hung in the sky when a bank of cloud slid over it, muffling the light as a hand muffles a bell. In that gray dark a figure broke out suddenly—white upon white—running bent forward as if she would make herself smaller than fear. She clasped a child hard to her breast, the way a woman presses a warm loaf to keep it from cooling, and every motion of her was driven, not chosen.

She ran on a line almost level with mine and never once turned her head. Terror had taken her hearing and sight and left her only flight.

She is chased, I said in myself. Some prowler of this night is after her.

To run after her would have been to add my own footfall to the pursuing rhythm and make her think the worse had doubled. I stepped into her track instead, to meet whatever followed and break its course. The grass was damp underfoot; it whispered when I shifted my weight, and somewhere near, unseen water spoke in a low, steady syllable as if pouring over leaves.

I had stood scarce a moment, peering after her diminishing whiteness, when behind me came a swift, soft-footed rush—no clumsy stamp, but the soundless certainty of a hunter. Before I could turn, something sprang over my head and struck me sharply on the forehead. Stars burst in my eyes like sparks from flint; I went down into the wet grass and tasted iron in my mouth.

I was up again at once. All I caught of my assailant was a vanishing whiteness melting into the dim, and the blood that began to trickle down my brow cooled instantly in the night air, like warm broth spilled on a stone.

I ran after it, wiping blindly with my sleeve, but had gone only a few steps when a shriek of despair tore the quivering dark. It was not a cry that belonged among beasts. It snapped the night’s quiet like a bone. I ran the faster, though the fear in me said the sound had come too late and I was running only toward the remainder.

Then, through vapour-dusted moonlight that came and went as the cloud thinned and thickened, I spied a low white shape approaching. At first I thought it another beast coming to finish what the first had begun; but it came slowly, almost crawling, and its movement was a broken series of strange, floundering leaps, as of a creature in pain that cannot decide which leg to trust.

I drew aside and waited, listening. The night had its own domestic noises—grass bending, the minute click of insects, the distant hush of water—but beneath them, close now, was a low rushing sound as of a small fall, steady and disgracefully intimate, like liquid poured where it ought not to be poured.

As it neared I saw it went on three legs, carrying its left fore-paw high from the ground. Its skin shone white as if washed, and upon it were many dark oval spots. From the lifted paw something streamed and pattered softly into the grass.

It is blood, I said to myself. Some readier champion than I has wounded the beast.

Yet, strange to tell, pity seized me at the sight. Its suffering made a kind of claim, as if pain itself were a language older than my own. If an axe had been in my hand I could not have struck it. In a broken succession of hobbling leaps it went past and out of sight, and still the blood, as it seemed, issued in a small torrent, which kept flowing back with that same soft rush through the blades beside me.

If it go on bleeding like that, I thought, it will soon be hurtless.

But I had no time to follow. The woman and her child were ahead, and whatever had screamed—whether mother or beast—had screamed for them. I went on, hoping still to be of use, and perhaps to see the deliverer I had imagined.

A little way off I descried her seated on the grass, the child in her lap. Even in the thin light I could see how she bent over it, sheltering it with her whole body, as if her own ribs were a cage to keep harm out. The baby lay white and quiet, as if sleep had put its hand upon her mouth.

“Can I do anything for you?” I asked.

At the sound of my voice she started violently and would have risen. The fear in her sprang up again, quick as a hare.

I threw myself down on the ground at her feet, letting the wet soak through my sleeve, making myself small, unthreatening.

“You need not be frightened,” I said. “I was following the beast when happily you found a nearer protector. It passed me now with its foot bleeding so much that by this time it must be all but dead.”

“There is little hope of that!” she answered, trembling, as if even hope had teeth. “Do you not know whose beast she is?”

Certain strange suspicions stirred in me—like a remembered smell from childhood that you cannot name but cannot forget—yet I said I knew nothing of the brute and asked what had become of her champion.

“What champion?” she rejoined. “I have seen no one.”

“Then how came the monster to grief?”

“I pounded her foot with a stone—as hard as I could strike. Did you not hear her cry?”

“Well, you are a brave woman!” I said. “I thought it was you gave the cry!”

“It was the leopardess.”

“I never heard such a sound from the throat of an animal,” I said. “It was like the scream of a woman in torture!”

“My voice was gone,” she answered, and the words came out thin and dry, as if she had no moisture left in her mouth. “I could not have shrieked to save my baby. When I saw the horrid mouth at my darling’s little white neck, I caught up a stone and mashed her lame foot.”

She laid her hand on the child as she spoke, not stroking so much as counting her whole and unharmed: head, throat, shoulder. A mother’s fingers have their own history; they remember bruises and fevers the way a cook remembers burns and near-spills.

“Tell me about the creature,” I said. “I am a stranger in these parts.”

“You will soon know about her if you are going to Bulika!” she answered. “Now, I must never go back there!”

“Yes,” I said, “I am going to Bulika—to see the princess.”

“Have a care; you had better not go!—But perhaps you are—!” She broke off, listening, as if the darkness itself were speaking in a tongue she had been forced to learn. “The princess is a very good, kind woman!”

There was a small movement beside her. The clouds had gathered thick over the moon; I could hardly see the line of her cheek. I feared she was rising to run from me.

“You are in no danger of any sort from me,” I said. “What oath would you like me to take?”

“I know by your speech that you are not of the people of Bulika,” she replied. “I will trust you!—I am not of them, either, else I should not be able: they never trust any one.” She leaned forward, trying to read me by hearing. “If only I could see you! But I like your voice. There—my darling is asleep! The foul beast has not hurt her!—Yes: it was my baby she was after!”

As she spoke she caressed the child, pressing her lips to the little head once, quickly, as if to take in the scent and keep it for courage. The night held many smells—wet earth, crushed grass, the sharp clean tang of my own blood—but around the baby there was only that warm, faint sweetness of milk and living skin, a smell that makes the whole world seem briefly worth saving.

“And then she would have torn her mother to pieces for carrying her off,” she went on. “Some say the princess has two white leopardesses. I know only one—with spots. Everybody knows HER! If the princess hear of a baby, she sends her immediately to suck its blood, and then it either dies or grows up an idiot.”

Her voice had the flat certainty of a thing often repeated in whispers. It was not a story told for fright alone; it was instruction—how to live under a power that listens at doors.

“I would have gone away with my baby,” she continued, “but the princess was from home, and I thought I might wait until I was a little stronger. But she must have taken the beast with her, and been on her way home when I left, and come across my track. I heard the SNIFF-SNUFF of the leopardess behind me, and ran;—oh, how I ran!—But my darling will not die! There is no mark on her!”

Even as she said it, she seemed to taste the words, as if daring to speak them might sour them. Somewhere in the dark the water went on murmuring, indifferent, and the grass answered the smallest wind with its dry hush. It struck me how the sounds of a place can either heal the mind or keep it perpetually braced for terror; here every soft noise could be mistaken for a paw.

“Where are you taking her?” I asked.

“Where no one ever tells!”

“Why is the princess so cruel?”

“There is an old prophecy that a child will be the death of her. That is why she will listen to no offer of marriage, they say.”

“But what will become of her country if she kill all the babies?”

“She does not care about her country.” The bitterness in her was like burnt crust scraped from a pan—black, hard, and earned. “She sends witches around to teach the women spells that keep babies away, and give them horrible things to eat.”

The phrase struck me with an odd domestic horror: food turned against the table, the very act of nourishment corrupted into a tool of rule. A people can lose more than children that way; they can lose recipes, songs, the names that go with hearth-things—lose the language of the mouth as well as the language of the tongue.

“Some say she is in league with the Shadows to put an end to the race,” the woman went on. “At night we hear the questing beast, and lie awake and shiver. She can tell at once the house where a baby is coming, and lies down at the door, watching to get in. There are words that have power to shoo her away, only they do not always work—”

She broke, as if the very mention of those words were dangerous in the air.

“But here I sit talking,” she ended suddenly, “and the beast may by this time have got home, and her mistress be sending the other after us!”

She rose in haste, shifting the child with practiced care so the little head would not loll. I rose also.

“I do not think she will ever get home,” I said, thinking of the blood pouring into the grass like a spilt jug. “Let me carry the baby for you!”

She gave me no answer. When I moved my arms toward the child she only clasped it closer, the way a woman clutches the last seedcorn in a famine.

“I cannot think,” I said, walking beside her as she began to go on, “how the brute could be bleeding so much!”

“Take my advice, and don’t go near the palace,” she answered, and her voice lowered as if the very walls might hear through distance. “There are sounds in it at night as if the dead were trying to shriek, but could not open their mouths!”

The thought of such muffled agony—the acoustic of a place where cries cannot properly be cries—made my skin tighten. A land can be ruled by silence as surely as by proclamation; even pain can be censored.

She stopped, turned her face from me into the dark, and bade me an abrupt farewell. Plainly she did not want more of my company, however kindly meant. So I stood still and listened as her footsteps went away through the grass, growing fainter, swallowed at last by the night’s damp hush, until there was only the low talk of water and the troubled quiet of a world where even lullabies must be whispered.

I had lost all reckoning of where I was. The dark had no corners, no landmarks—only a thickness to it, like soot stirred up in a closed kitchen. I walked in it the way one paces when a pot refuses to come to the boil: helpless, listening, counting heartbeats, trying to borrow patience from sound.

Then, without warning, I stepped straight into the leopardess’s track.

Something ran about my ankles with the push and hurry of a small brook—warm at first in my mind, because my mind leapt to the most dreadful ingredient it could name. Blood, it whispered: somebody’s life poured out and wasted. I sprang aside as if scalded, yet the very next instant I found myself keeping close to the noise of it, as a cook will follow the hiss of a pan in a darkened room. The stream’s voice would lead me to Bulika.

Only, as I hurried on beside it, reason began to stir—slowly, like embers remembered under ash. No leopardess, no elephant, no ancient brute out of the world’s first bestiary could bleed so much and still walk. To keep such a torrent running, its heart would have to be a pump fed by lakes; its veins would have to drink whole meadows and refill themselves as fast as they emptied. It could not be blood.

I knelt, dipped a finger, and brought it to my tongue before fear could stop me. It was water. Not cold—soft, as if it had been warmed in the sun and then set to singing; clean, with a faint sweetness like crushed green stems. However it had come there, it ran without bed or channel over the grass, a thin shining skin upon the earth, murmuring the way a kettle murmurs when it is content and not yet urgent.

Sweet as its music was, I dared not drink. In that world I had learned to mistrust appetite, to distrust even thirst; some draughts promised comfort and gave fever. But the mere wetting of my feet refreshed me as a basin of cool water refreshes a baker’s wrists after hours at the oven. The sound of it—so unlike the harsh, hot rush of that other stream—was like a remembered lullaby in a language one spoke before learning the harsher dialects of fear. I went on without fatigue, guided by its gentle acoustics, until the darkness began to thin, and I knew the sun was drawing near.

A few minutes more and, against a pale aurora like flour sifted into the air, I made out the wall-towers of a city—old as if time itself had been kneaded into its stones and baked hard.

I looked down, expecting to see the brook. It was gone.

I had noticed its song growing fainter for a long while, as a cook notices a simmer falling silent when the fire dies; but at last, in my thoughts, I had ceased to attend. Now there was only grass, bent and laid over where the water had gone, and here and there a small pool glimmering like spilled broth. Toward the city there was no trace at all. Near where I stood, its fountain—if fountain there had been—must at least have paused, as if some unseen hand had clapped a lid over it.

Around the city, gardens lay close to the walls: long beds of vegetables, many of them so strange that I could not name them, though they had the look of things meant for a pot and a knife. Some had leaves like thick tongues; some thrust pale knobs above the soil; some stood in stiff ranks with seed-heads like little maces. Yet there were no flowers, no visible watercourses, no animals moving among the beds. The earth looked worked, but the work had no tenderness in it—no mulched paths, no border herbs, no bees. Between the gardens and the walls were huge heaps of gravel and refuse flung down from the battlements: broken stone, ash, and the grey leavings of a place that consumed and did not compost.

I went to the nearest gate. It stood half-closed, not barred, not watched. Its hinges looked as if they had forgotten the very idea of change—so worn, so settled in their position that it seemed the gate could neither open wider nor shut tighter.

I passed through.

A long ancient street stretched ahead, and it was silent—not the good silence of dawn over fields, where birds hold their breath before the first song, but the dead silence of a kitchen after a feast when the fire is out and the plates are licked clean. There was scarcely an indication of life. The stones underfoot were flat and cold; my own footsteps sounded too loud, as if I were making noise in a place where noise was forbidden.

Had I come upon a dead city?

I turned and went out again. I toiled a long way over the dust-heaps, crossed several roads that each led up to a gate, and lingered outside, refusing to enter until the inhabitants should begin to stir. I tried to ask myself, as one asks oneself before returning to a hostile table: What am I here for? What do I expect? What do I mean to do?

One answer kept rising, bitter and unavoidable: I must see—if only once more—the woman I had brought to life.

I did not desire her company. She had waked in me suspicions like the smell of meat kept too long in heat: not a certainty, but enough to turn the stomach. Friendship—love—between us was wildly impossible. And yet her presence had had a strange influence upon me, a seasoning in the air that changed the taste of my own thoughts. In her presence I must resist and at the same time try to understand what was being done to me. The inscrutable in her drew me like a dish whose aroma promises one thing and delivers another. If I could learn her mode of being, I might look into marvels no imagination would have dared cook up.

In that I was too bold. A man must not, for the sake of knowledge, walk of his own will into temptation.

And yet there was guilt in my caution. I had reinstated an evil force that was about to perish; to the extent of my opposing will, I was answerable for whatever mischief might follow. I had learned she was the enemy of children. The Little Ones might be in her danger. I had left them to seek some understanding of their history, and I had found a little light—but not enough. I must have more. I must learn how to protect them.

At last I heard a faint stir within the walls: not birdsong, not market-call, but the scraping and shuffling of waking bodies. I chose another gate and went in, along a narrow street between tall houses. The air had a stale, shut-up smell, as of rooms that never let their smoke out. No friendly domestic sounds came—no clink of crockery, no bubbling, no laughter, no chopping on a board. Sound itself seemed policed, as if life had been trained to move without making music.

The narrow street led to a small square where a pillar stood, and atop it a hideous creature like a bat in stone, wings folded as if it had never known flight but only the idea of menace. I sat down on the base of the pillar and waited.

Ere long, several inhabitants sauntered past—men with the vacant gait of those who are neither hurrying to work nor lingering with purpose. I spoke to one. He gave me a rude stare and a ruder word, and went on as though my voice were merely an unpleasant noise to step around.

I rose and went through one narrow street after another. The city gradually filled with idlers, but still the soundscape remained thin and sour: no children’s cries, no games, no bright shrieks that stitch the hours together. I was not surprised to see no children.

By and by, near one of the gates, I encountered a group of young men who reminded me of the bad giants—something in the set of their shoulders and the way their attention fixed on weakness as a dog fixes on a dropped scrap. They came about me, staring, then began to push and hustle me, then to throw things. Pebbles first, then whatever their hands found, not from hunger, not from sport, but from a practiced contempt that had the rhythm of a city’s custom.

I bore it as well as I could, unwilling to provoke enmity where I meant to remain a while. More than once I appealed to passers-by whom I fancied more benevolent-looking. None halted even a moment to listen. I looked poor, and that was enough. To the citizens of Bulika—as to house-dogs—poverty was an offence.

I learned in those few moments something of their law without hearing it proclaimed: deformity and sickness were taxed; and no legislation of their princess was more heartily approved than what made poverty serve wealth. It was a city where the dominant tongue of power named need a crime, and the name itself did the work of a lash. The words they flung were not merely insults; they were labels meant to strip a man of any claim to a hearing. I understood the sound of them—hard consonants, clipped endings—but their meaning slid in a way my own speech could not quite catch, as if their dialect had evolved to make cruelty efficient.

At last I took to my heels, and no one followed me beyond the gate. Outside the walls their courage collapsed like over-whipped cream—showy inside, air outside. One lumbering fellow sat by the gate eating a hunch of bread. He picked up a stone to throw after me, and in his stupid eagerness threw, not the stone but the bread.

It struck the ground and rolled. I snatched it up. He did not dare follow to reclaim it.

A few hundred yards away I threw myself down in the grass and ate it. It was coarse, heavy, a bread made without care: too much bran left sharp, the crumb tight, the crust pale as if it had been baked in a reluctant oven. Yet it filled my mouth with warmth and reminded my body that it still belonged to earth and grain and the old honest chemistry of hunger. I fell asleep as one falls asleep after eating—sudden, unresisting—and slept soundly. The hot sunlight later renewed my strength the way a hearthstone holds and gives back heat long after the flames are gone.

It was night when I woke.

The moon looked down in friendly fashion, seeming to claim with me old acquaintance. She was very bright—the same moon, I thought, that had watched me through the terrors of my first night in that strange world, as if my life here moved in circles and she marked the turning like a patient timekeeper. A cold wind blew from the gate, bringing with it an evil odour: not merely the smell of refuse, but a taint like rancid fat—sweet at first sniff, then sickening, clinging in the nose and on the back of the tongue. It did not chill me; the sun had plenished me with warmth.

I crept again into the city.

The few still in the open air were crouched in corners to escape the shivering blast. Their huddling made a low, uneasy rustle, the way dry leaves scrape when something passes that they do not trust. The wind worried the streets, seeking gaps between stones, making thin whistles that suggested the place itself was always listening for weakness.

I walked slowly through a long narrow street when, just before me, a huge white thing bounded across it—one single flash in the moonlight—and disappeared. The brightness was so sudden it felt like the flare of a knife catching light.

I turned down the next opening, eager to get sight of it again.

The lane was almost too narrow to pass through, walls crowding like close cupboards, and it led me into a wider street. The moment I entered, I saw on the opposite side, in the shadow, the creature I had followed. It was following, like a dog, what I took at first for a man.

Over his shoulder, every other moment, he glanced at the animal behind him; yet he neither spoke to it nor attempted to drive it away, as if the creature were not a pursuer but a necessary accompaniment—like the smell of smoke on a man who lives by a fire he did not choose.

At a place where he had to cross a patch of moonlight, I saw he cast no shadow.

More dreadful still: he himself was but a flat superficial shadow—two dimensions walking upright. And yet he was no mere absence of light, for he was opaque: he not only darkened what lay behind him, he made it invisible, as if he carried with him a strip of night that ate whatever it touched. In the deeper shadow he was blacker than the shadow; in the moonlight he looked like one who had drawn his own shadow up about him and wrapped it close, for not a suspicion of it moved beside or under him.

The leopardess followed so close at his heels that she seemed the white shadow of his blackness. She drew her own gliding shadow over the stones, black and natural, while he had none at all, as if the world refused to admit him as a body. When they passed together from shadow into moonlight, the Shadow deepened in blackness, and the animal flashed into radiance, her coat catching the light like poured milk turning suddenly to steel.
