At that moment I was walking abreast of them on the opposite side. My bare feet sounded on the flat stones—small, human sounds, too honest for that street. The leopardess never turned her head or twitched an ear. The shadow seemed once to look at me, for I lost his profile and saw for a second only a sharp upright line.

That instant the wind found me and blew through me.

I shuddered from head to foot, and my heart went from wall to wall of my bosom like a pebble in a child’s rattle.

I slipped out of the main street as one might draw a hand from a hot pan—quickly, without thinking—and turned into a narrow alley where the moon lay like spilled milk from wall to wall. The stones underfoot held the day’s chill; the air smelt of damp lime, old soot, and a faint metallic tang as if the city itself had been scraping at buried treasure for too many lifetimes. Wind worried the roofs overhead and combed the alley with a sound like dry reeds shaken in a hollow gourd—an unsteady, rasping hush that made the silence behind it feel watched.

Halfway down I found a small archway set back from the moonlight, no bigger than a pantry door, and stepped into its mouth for shelter. The shadow there was close and cool. I had barely stopped and looked out again when a woman came gliding in after me as if she had been poured from the same darkness. She turned, trembling, and stared into the alley as I did—both of us holding our breath, listening.

In that listening the city showed its true nature. Not the proud front of tall houses, but the hollow between them: the wind’s fretful scrape, the distant clink of something hard on stone far below, as if men were still down in cellars tapping at their wealth. No birds. No dogs. No common household noise that makes a place human—no pot-lids, no children’s shouts, no broom’s whisk on a threshold. Only the wind, and then—soft, padded speed.

A huge leopard flashed across the opening of the archway, white as bone in the moonlight and dappled with dark blots. It did not so much run as cut the air, a living knife. The woman pressed close to me with the instinct of one who has been hunted often, and my heart filled with pity at the smallness of her against such a creature.

I put my arm round her shoulders—not as a gallant, but as one steadies a person who is about to faint. “If the brute comes here,” I said, “I will lay hold of it, and you must run.”

“Thank you,” she murmured, her voice thin with fear.

“Have you ever seen it before?” I asked, keeping my eyes on the slit of moonlit alley.

“Several times,” she answered, still shaking. “She is a pet of the princess’s. You are a stranger, or you would know her!”

“I am a stranger,” I said. Even the word sounded loud in that place, as if the stones themselves disliked it. “But is she, then, allowed to run loose?”

“She is kept in a cage,” the woman said, her words rushing out as if speech were the only thing between her and the leopard’s teeth, “her mouth muzzled, and her feet in gloves of crocodile leather. Chained she is too; but she gets out often—and sucks the blood of any child she can lay hold of.” Her breath hitched. “Happily there are not many mothers in Bulika!”

On that she broke into tears—silent at first, then a sob that seemed to have been stored up and salted like meat against famine. “I wish I were at home,” she wept. “The princess returned only last night, and there is the leopardess out already! How am I to get into the house? It is me she is after, I know! She will be lying at my own door, watching for me!” Her hands fluttered, then clenched. “But I am a fool to talk to a stranger!”

“All strangers are not bad,” I said. The wind shoved at the alley again, making the archway sing faintly like a pipe with a cracked lip. “The beast shall not touch you till she has done with me, and by that time you will be in. You are happy to have a house to go to! What a terrible wind it is!”

“Take me home safe,” she said, swallowing her sobs as if she had learned in this city to swallow everything, “and I will give you shelter from it. But we must wait a little.”

So we waited in that cramped hollow, shoulder against shoulder, and while the moonlight lay in a bright strip beyond us like a tablecloth laid out for a feast no one meant to share, I asked her questions. Talking kept our minds from the padding in the dark; it also filled the empty air with human sound, which in Bulika felt like a kind of defiance.

She told me her people did nothing—nothing—except dig in their cellars for precious stones. The image came to me vividly: families not gathered around bread and stew, but bent under the earth, fingers black with grit, nails split, lanterns smoking in the stale air as they chipped and sifted, listening for the small, satisfying click when a gem struck metal. And above them, kitchens kept cold, hearths barely used, the domestic rituals that carry a language and a people from one generation to the next allowed to starve.

“They are rich,” she said, “and have everything made for them in other towns.”

“Why?” I asked.

“Because it is a disgrace to work,” she replied, as if reciting a proverb every child in Bulika learned before he learned his mother’s lullaby. “Everybody in Bulika knows that!”

I tried to fit such a belief into my mind as one might try to fit a stone into a loaf—hard, wrong-shaped, splitting what should hold together. “How are you rich if none of you earn money?”

“Our ancestors saved for us,” she said. “We never spend. When we want money we sell a few of our gems.”

“But there must be some poor,” I persisted.

“I suppose there must be,” she answered with a shrug that made her shawl rasp softly, “but we never think of such people. When one goes poor, we forget him. That is how we keep rich. We mean to be rich always.”

The wind hissed down the alley, catching on corners like water on stones. There are places where sound carries memory—where the songs and the daily clatter of living preserve names and meanings. Here the very air seemed trained to erase. Forget him, she had said, as simply as one says, throw out the peel.

“But when you have dug up all your precious stones and sold them,” I said, “you will have to spend your money, and one day you will have none left!”

“We have so many,” she returned, “and there are so many still in the ground, that that day will never come.”

“And suppose a strange people were to fall upon you,” I said, “and take everything you have?”

“No strange people will dare,” she answered quickly. “They are all horribly afraid of our princess. She it is who keeps us safe and free and rich!”

As she spoke she would break off, listening, and peer behind her into the alley as if expecting the white shape to pour itself out of the moonlight again. Each pause sharpened my hearing too; the city’s soundscape was all edges—wind, far-off knocks, and now and then the ghost of a soft tread that might have been only my imagination answering her fear.

I asked why her people hated strangers so. The question itself seemed to make the air tighter.

“The presence of a stranger defiles the city,” she said, and the word defiles fell from her lips like ash.

“How is that?” I asked.

“Because we are more ancient and noble than any other nation,” she replied, with the brittle pride of someone repeating a lesson drilled in by those who profit from it. “Therefore we always turn strangers out before night.” Then, as if remembering herself, she added in a lower voice, “We must. We do.”

“How, then, can you take me into your house?” I asked.

“I will make an exception of you,” she said, and there was a strange mingling in her tone—fear, rebellion, and the habit of obedience like a string tied around the heart.

“Is there no place in the city for the taking in of strangers?”

“Such a place would be pulled down,” she answered, “and its owner burned.” She said it without drama, as if speaking of a recipe that everyone knew by heart. “How is purity to be preserved except by keeping low people at a proper distance? Dignity is such a delicate thing!”

Delicate—like a language that dies when it is not spoken at the table, like a song that cannot survive the replacement of voices with decrees. In Bulika even the words for kindness and welcome seemed to have been driven underground with the gems.

She went on to tell me of their princess—how she had reigned for thousands of years; how she had power over the air and the water as well as the earth, and, she believed, over the fire too; how she could do what she pleased and was answerable to nobody. The city around us, with its narrow lanes and closed doors, felt shaped by that thought: a long tyranny made ordinary, like smoke that has so long filled a room the inhabitants no longer smell it.

At length she judged the moment fit—whether because she heard nothing, or because courage had finally boiled up in her despite fear—and we set out. We moved through lanes and narrow passages that twisted like the corridors of a cellar. The wind followed, sometimes funneling into a shriek, sometimes dropping into a deep, uneasy moan. Not once did we meet a living creature. No footfall echoed ours. No voice called. The emptiness itself had a sound: the soft absence where life should have been.

Her door was in a wider street between two tall houses that rose like cliffs. A narrow, steep stair climbed to it, and she began to mount, slowly at first, her hand on the wall as if she could feel, through stone, whether danger lurked. I followed close.

Before we reached the top, she seemed suddenly to take fright, as if some scent had reached her—cold, animal, familiar. She darted up the remaining steps with the desperate speed of someone snatching a pot from a flame.

I reached the landing just in time to have the door closed in my face.

For a moment I stood there, confounded, the wind pushing at my coat, the moonlight whitening the street below. The landing was barely long enough, between the opposite doors of the two houses, for a man to lie down. Weary—too weary to care that Bulika counted my presence a stain—I sank into what shelter there was, poor as it was, and let the city’s thin, scraping wind sing over me while behind the shut door her hurried breathing vanished into the hush of a house that would not admit a stranger.

At the foot of the stair the street lay like a strip of hammered silver, moonlight bruised into every cobble. Wind prowled there—thin, sour, and inhospitable—whistling between doorways like a blade drawn slowly from a sheath. Yet not a thread of it reached the nook where I had made my poor lodging. The air above the steps held its own temper: stale stone, old dust, and the faint, teasing memory of bread that had once risen somewhere in this quarter, before Bulika forgot how to feed itself with anything but fear.

I had set my thoughts in order as one sets a table with no guests expected, and was lowering myself to rest when my eyes opened of their own accord—wide, as if some sound too small for the ear had touched them.

There—lifting above the uppermost step—was the head of the shining creature I had seen at the Shadow’s heel. White as milk under moon, but not soft-looking: a sheen like clean fat on chilled broth, beautiful and dangerous both. The moment her gaze met mine, she froze. Then, as if the stair were a narrow pantry passage and she feared to turn and clatter a pot, she began to withdraw—tail foremost, careful, almost polite.

I sprang up. With no room to pivot, she flung herself backward, head over tail, scrambled upright, and in a heartbeat was down the stair and gone. I followed to the bottom and looked up and down the street. Nothing moved but that unwholesome wind, combing the empty way with cold fingers.

I went back to my hard couch.

So there were two prowlers in this city—one with spots, one without. The thought had the sourness of a stew turned in the pot. I was not inclined to risk much for man or woman in Bulika; here even kindness seemed bartered and measured. But the life of a child—warm, breathing, still capable of learning the taste of its mother’s tongue—might be worth a poor life like mine. I resolved to keep watch at the door the rest of the night.

Presently I heard the latch move—slow, slow—like a careful hand easing a knife from a block. I looked up; the door stood half open. I rose, slid softly in, and found behind it not the woman I had befriended but the muffled woman of the desert, wrapped and soundless, as if she had swallowed her own language and would not risk spilling it.

Without a word she led me a few steps to an empty, stone-paved chamber and pointed to a rug on the floor. The rug smelled of old wool, smoke, and something that might once have been cumin ground between callused palms. I wrapped myself in it and lay down again, like dough set aside to rise in a cold room.

She shut the door. Then I heard the outer door open and close once more.

There was no light save what drifted in from the moonlit air, pale as skimmed milk. I lay sleepless, listening to the city’s silence—not the clean silence of fields at night where you can hear the separate insistence of cricket and owl and wind through grasses, but the muffled hush of stone that has been long denied its proper living noises: the scrape of bowls, the laughter around a hearth, the creak of a cart bearing vegetables in from morning ground. This silence had been engineered, tended, like a cruel art; it pressed on the ear until the mind itself began to ring.

In that ringing, a stifled moaning began. It went on a good while, squeezed through walls as if through cloth. Then came the cry of a child—sharp and new, like the first crackle when fire takes—and immediately after it a terrible shriek.

I sprang up and darted into the passage.

From another door in it came the white leopardess, a new-born baby in her mouth, carrying it as she would a cub of her own. The infant’s skin shone damply in the moonlight; its small life beat and trembled in the careless grip of teeth meant for tearing.

I threw myself upon her. In the narrow passage we struggled—my hands in her fur, my weight forcing her jaw to open. I compelled her to drop the infant. It fell upon the stone slabs with a piteous wail that struck me like a pan dropped on flagstones—sudden, ringing pain.

At the cry appeared the muffled woman. She stepped over us—beast and man tangled together—without a pause, as if stepping over a spilled sack, lifted the child with swift certainty, and carried it away. Returning, she seized me by the shoulders, hauled me off the animal, opened the door, and pushed me gently out, as one pushes a troublesome dog from the kitchen before it upsets the pail.

At my heels followed the leopardess.

“She too has failed me!” I thought. “Given me up to the beast to be settled with at her leisure! But we shall have a tussle for it!”

I ran down the stair, expecting the weight of her spring on my back, the hot iron of her claws. But she followed quietly, pads soundless on stone, a silence with a body.

At the foot I turned to lay hold of her. She sprang over my head. When again I faced her, she was crouching at my feet.

I stooped and stroked her lovely white skin. It was cool where moon had touched it, warm beneath where life kept moving—like the surface of a loaf that seems cold until you break it and the steam tells the truth. She responded by licking my bare feet with her hard, dry tongue—rough as a rasp, yet oddly tender in its insistence.

Then I patted and fondled her, and a well of tenderness overflowed in me. She might be treacherous too; this city trained everything living to betray. Yet if I turned from every show of love lest it be feigned, how was I ever to find the real love which must be somewhere in every world—even one whose common speech seemed to have lost the words for mercy, or to have had them stolen and repurposed for commands?

I stood up. She rose and stood beside me, close enough that I heard her breathing: not a snarl now, but a steady, domestic sound, like bellows when the fire is kept at a low, useful heat.

A bulky object fell with a heavy squelch in the middle of the street a few yards from us.

I ran to it. It was a pulpy mass, with just form enough left to show it had been the body of a woman. Something in me tried to name what I saw, to fit it into known categories—flesh, bone, death—but the naming failed. There are horrors no word will carry whole; the mouth refuses, as if to speak them would be to season them and make them fit for consumption.

It must have been thrown from some neighboring window.

I looked around.

On the other side of the way, the Shadow was walking along—calm, deliberate—and the white leopardess was again at his heel.

I followed, gaining upon them. In my heart I pleaded for the leopardess: perhaps she was not a free agent. Perhaps her will had been taken and tied to his like a tethered beast’s rope.

When I drew near, however, she turned and flew at me with a hideous snarl. The sound tore at the street’s enforced quiet—an ugly tear, like ripping cloth. Instinctively I drew back. Instantly she resumed her place behind the Shadow.

Again I drew near. Again she sprang, eyes flaming like live emeralds, her mouth a bright, wet threat.

Once more I made the experiment. She snapped at me like a dog and bit me.

My heart gave way and I cried out. At the sound she looked round with a glance that plainly meant, Why would you make me do it?

I turned away, angry with myself, as if I had kept trying to taste from the same bowl knowing it had been poisoned. I had been losing my time ever since I entered this place. Night as it was, I would go straight to the palace.

From the square I had seen it—high above the heart of the city, compassed with many defenses, more fortress than palace. Yet when I reached it, I found the fortifications, like those of the city, much neglected and partly ruinous. For centuries, clearly, they had been of no account. Even stone forgets its purpose if no living hand remembers how to mend it.

Great gates stood there, strong enough once to have denied an army. There was something like a drawbridge over a rocky chasm, but the gates stood open, and it was hard to believe water had ever occupied the hollow before them. The place had the drained look of a cistern long dry: built for one element, abandoned to another.

All was so still that sleep seemed to have seeped into the masonry, making the very moonlight look discordantly awake—as if light itself had become an intruder, too loud for such quiet. I must either enter like a thief or break a silence that made the mere thought of sound frightful. In a world where noise had been stripped away, a footfall felt like an act of violence.

Like an outcast dog I walked about the walls until I came to a little recess with a stone bench. I took refuge there from the wind. The bench held the day’s cold as a platter holds chill, but I lay down, and in spite of it fell fast asleep.

Something leapt upon me. A rough tongue licked my face.

“It is the white leopardess,” I thought, half-dreaming. “She has come to suck my blood—and why should she not have it? It would cost me more to defend than to yield it.”

So I lay still, waiting for the shoot of pain.

But the pang did not come. Instead a pleasant warmth began to spread through me, slow as broth warming on a low flame. Stretched at my back she lay as close as she could, the heat of her body penetrating mine. Her breath—strangely clean, without the rank wildness I expected—wrapped my head and face in a genial atmosphere. It smelled not of slaughter but of wind over snow, and of something faintly animal-sweet, like milk on a warm lip.

A full conviction took hold of me that her intention toward me was good.

I turned like a sleepy boy, threw my arm over her, and sank into profound unconsciousness.

When I began to come to myself, I fancied I lay warm and soft in my own bed. For an instant the well-known scents of the garden seemed to crowd in—earth after rain, leaf-mould, the green bite of crushed herbs, the ghost of apples stored for winter. They were so vivid they might have been spoken into my nostrils in my mother tongue, those aromas that need no translation because they are older than words.

“Is it possible I am at home?” I thought.

I rubbed my eyes and looked out.

I lay on bare stone, in the heart of a hateful city.

I sprang from the bench. Had I indeed had a leopardess for my bedfellow, or had I only dreamed it? She had but just left; the warmth of her body lingered on my back like the memory of a hearth.

I left the recess with a new hope—strong as it was shapeless. One thing only was clear: I must find the princess. Surely I had some power with her, if not over her. Had I not saved her life, and had she not prolonged it at the expense of my vitality? The thought, bitter as unsalted porridge, still gave me courage to go to her, be she what she might.

I went the long way round the castle, letting my steps complete the circuit as if my feet needed to learn the place by repetition. The air in the outer passages had a hollow hush, the kind that holds your own breathing up to you like a cup: every bootfall sounded too loud, then died as though swallowed by stone. At last I came again to the open gates and crossed the moat—no water in it now, only a chasm of shadow, like a drained channel that remembered rivers. Beyond lay a paved court, its stones set close as a well-laid hearth, and planted at even intervals with tall, straight trees, poplar-like in their patience. One in the centre rose above the rest; near its crown its branches spread, not much, but enough to make it seem a palm trying to remember a warmer country.

Between the trunks I caught glimpses of the palace itself—long and low, with lofty corner towers and a huge dome in the middle that swelled up from the roof to half their height. Something in its curves and weight of line suggested an older southern craft, a style that belonged to heat and spice and slow evenings. The main entrance gaped in the centre: a low arch, half an ellipse, like a mouth that had forgotten how to close.

No sentries. No voices. No clink of metal, no domestic clatter, no kitchen-rattle of pans, none of the friendly chaos that tells you a house is inhabited by hunger and its satisfactions. The doors stood wide, and I went in as though expected.

The first hall was a long ellipse, dim and cool. Toward one side stood a cage. In it lay a leopardess—huge, white, patterned with dark oval spots as if someone had pressed ripe olives into snow. She was chained by a steel collar; her mouth was muzzled, her paws muffled, and yet she looked less restrained than the whole palace. Her green eyes were wide open, the pupils narrow as canoes; her gaze fixed, unblinking. She watched me, I think, but not a whisker moved, not an eyeball shifted, not the tip of her tail stirred. That tail lay stretched behind her, rigid as an iron bar. For a breath I could not tell whether she was alive or carved and set there as ornament—a monstrous trophy of elegance.

Two low passages led away. I took one. It branched into many, narrow and irregular, turning like a warren, the air changing by degrees: here faintly sour as of damp cloth, there dusty as old rushes, then suddenly a thread of warm scent that vanished before I could name it.

At a place where the walls pressed close, a page ran into me. He sprang back as if he had struck a ghost, then looked me over, found courage in my unarmed state, puffed himself out, and demanded my business.

“To see the princess,” I said.

“A likely thing!” he scoffed. “I have not seen her highness this morning myself!”

I caught him by the back of the neck, shook him—hard enough to rattle the impudence out of his bones—and said, “Take me to her at once, or I will drag you with me until I find her. She shall know how her servants receive her visitors.”

He darted a glance at my face, measuring whether I meant it. Then he began to pull and lead as a blind man’s dog might—resentful, yet obedient to necessity.

He brought me to a large kitchen.

It ought to have been the beating heart of the palace, full of knife-song and fire-breath, full of talk and the plain music of work. Instead it lay under a kind of languor. Many servants moved about feebly, as if each had been roused too early from a heavy sleep. The room held a faint warmth, but no true cheer. I smelled stale ash and lukewarm bread, a milkiness that had stood too long, and something else—something clean and distant, like crushed leaves carried on a draft from an unseen garden.

I expected them to shout, to seize me, to drive me out. They did none of it. They stared—wide-eyed—not at me but at something behind me. Their faces blanched in the same direction, as if a cold wind had slid into the room.

I turned my head.

The white leopardess stood there in the doorway, and in that instant I knew she had been no statue. She regarded the kitchen as if it were prey and she had leisure. Even muzzled, even chained in my memory, she carried the authority of claw and sinew. The servants’ fear thickened the air more than the heat ever could.

One of them, perhaps because he could not bear the stillness, began to recover himself. I let the page go.

“Take me to the princess,” I said to the man.

“She has not yet left her room, your lordship,” he answered, with the flat caution of one who speaks under threat, not from me but from what stood behind.

“Let her know that I am here, waiting audience of her.”

He hesitated. “Will your lordship please to give me your name?”

“Tell her,” I said, “that one who knows the white leech desires to see her.”

He shrank as if I had offered him poison. “She will kill me if I take such a message. I must not. I dare not.”

“You refuse?” I asked.

His eyes flicked again toward my attendant—the white terror with green eyes—then he turned and went without another word.

The others kept staring, too frightened to look away from the leopardess, as though any glance aside might invite her to spring. I went to her. She stood with her muzzle close to my heel, the whiteness of her coat carrying a warm splendour into that gloomy room like a bowl of milk set down among ashes. I stooped and patted her head.

She looked up at me. The simple movement scattered the servants as if a broom had swept them; they fled to corners, behind tables, through side doors, wherever their fear found shelter.

The leopardess rose on her hind legs and put her great paws on my shoulders. The muffling made the touch oddly gentle—like hands in thick gloves—yet the weight of her was enormous. I threw my arms around her neck, and for a moment I felt the heat of her body through her fur and the leather of the muzzle; the smell of her was like sun-warmed hide and clean dust, with a faint wild tang that made the mouth water and the skin tighten at once.

Her ears pricked. She broke from me and was out of sight in a moment, moving with that noiseless speed that belongs to creatures who do not waste sound.

The man I had sent returned, pale but compliant. “Please to come this way, my lord,” he said.

My heart throbbed once, as if it had braced itself against a blow.

He led me through passage after passage. The palace was built like a secret—corners within corners, turns that made you doubt you could ever find your way back. At last he drew aside a hanging and showed me into a room so large and so dark that its walls were invisible. The darkness had weight; it lay on the skin like cool silk.

A single spot on the floor reflected a little light. All around that spot the blackness was complete. I looked up and saw, far overhead, an oval aperture in the roof. Around its edge I could just make out the joints between blocks of black marble. The floor beneath my feet was slabbed in the same stone, fitted so close the seams were scarcely visible. Later I would understand that the walls too were black marble, drinking in what little light fell, as if the room fed on brightness and gave back nothing.

The roof was the long half of an ellipsoid. The opening sat over one focus of the floor’s ellipse, so that the little light gathered and fell in that lone patch as if the room were a lens for darkness.

I thought I caught sight of reddish lines—brief, like a memory of embers in a hearth. When I tried to look at them, they were gone.

Then, without warning, a radiant form stood in the centre of the dark.

Her robe was soft white. Her hair poured down like a cataract, black as the marble on which it fell. Her eyes were a luminous blackness—dark that shone. Her arms and feet were warm ivory. She smiled the innocent smile of a girl, yet her bearing had the poised assurance of one who had already commanded rooms far stranger than this. In face and figure and motion she seemed as if she had only just crossed the threshold of womanhood.

And I thought, with a sick turn inside me, Ill did I reckon my danger. Can this be the woman I rescued—she who struck me, scorned me, left me?

I stood, half swallowed by the darkness. She stood in her own light, gazing into the black as if searching for me. Then she vanished, as though the dark had shut over her.

She will not acknowledge me, I thought.

The next instant her eyes flashed out of the gloom straight into mine. She had found me and come to me—silent, swift, as if the darkness were her element.

“You have found me at last!” she said, and laid her hand upon my shoulder. The touch was light, but it carried heat through cloth and skin. “I knew you would!”

My body quivered with sensations I could not separate. Attraction and repulsion ran together until each seemed to be the other’s mask.

“You shiver,” she said. “This place is cold for you. Come.”

I could not speak. Her beauty had struck me dumb; her sweetness held me there as if I were a boy again, caught between reverence and anger.

She took my hand and drew me into the spot of light. There she flashed upon me anew.

An instant she studied my face. “You have grown brown since last I saw you,” she said, as if remarking on a loaf darkened in the oven.

“This is almost the first roof I have been under since you left me,” I answered.

“Whose was the other?” she asked quickly.

“I do not know the woman’s name.”

“I would gladly learn it,” she said, with a faint curl in her voice. “The instinct of hospitality is not strong in my people.”

Again she took my hand and led me through many steps of darkness to a curtain of black. Beyond it lay a white stair, its stone pale as flour. She brought me up, and at the top opened into a beautiful chamber.

“How you must miss the hot flowing river!” she said.

Her words brought back the smell of that stream—mineral and steamy, the air above it heavy as boiled roots. I did not answer.

“But there is a bath in the corner,” she went on, “with no white leeches in it. At the foot of your couch you will find a garment. When you come down, I shall be in the room to your left at the foot of the stair.”

She left me, and I stood for a moment accusing myself of presumption. How was I to treat this lovely woman as a thing of evil when she behaved to me like a sister—almost like a hostess who had been waiting with water and bread?

Whence the marvellous change in her? She left me with a blow; she received me with something like an embrace. She had reviled me; now she spoke as if my pursuit were not only expected but desired. Did she know my doubts—how much I needed explained? Could she explain it? And if she could, could I believe her?

Hospitality, at least, I had earned. I might accept it until I came to a judgment that would not shift like smoke.

Clear as crystal, the water in the great white bath sent a sparkling flash from its corner where it lay sunk in marble. The sight of it pierced me with longing. Except for the hot stream, two draughts in the cottage of the veiled woman, and pools along the track of the wounded leopardess, I had not seen water since leaving home. Here it looked celestial—like something preserved from a better world.

I plunged in.

At once my brain filled with an odour strange and delicate—herbal, floral, and faintly bitter, like bruised petals steeped too long. It was beautiful and yet it displeased me, as if it were meant to. My suspicion sprang up again: had she medicated it? enchanted it? was she working upon me unlawfully?

And how was there water in the palace when the city held not a drop? The question bit harder than the scent.

I remembered the crushed paw of the leopardess. I saw again the limping beast, heard again the howl that seemed to tear the air. I sprang from the bath, water streaming from me cold and bright.

What had I been bathing in?

For a moment I argued with myself as a thirsty man argues with scruple. What matter whence it flowed, if it was sweet? Water comes from heaven, does it not? What mattered the well where it gathered, the channel it had borrowed, the vessel that held it? Even the pitcher-plant secretes water and stores it in its heart for the weary traveller.

Yet I did not re-enter. I could not.

At the foot of the couch lay a robe of white wool, embroidered at neck and hem. It smelled faintly of sheep and smoke and some clean resin, as if it had been dried where pinewood burned. I put it on, and the warmth of it steadied me.

Then I went down the white stair to the room she had named.

It was round, all alabaster, without a single window. Light came through everywhere—not as beams, but as a soft pearly shimmer, as if the walls themselves had learned to glow. Over the low dome and along the walls drifted vague shadowy forms, like loose rain-clouds crossing a grey-blue sky.

The princess stood waiting.

She wore a robe embroidered with rings and discs, rectangles and lozenges, close together—argent shapes like a silver mail. It fell unbroken from her neck and hid her feet, but its long open sleeves left her arms bare, pale and alive against the metallic pattern.

An ivory table stood in the room. Upon it were cakes and fruit, an ivory jug of milk, a crystal jug of wine pale as rose-water, and a white loaf.

The arrangement struck me with the odd comfort of the domestic: food set out, drink ready, the old ritual of invitation. Yet even here the palace’s silence pressed in. There was no hearth-crackle, no kettle-song, no distant chopping. The quiet was so complete I could hear the faintest shift of cloth when she moved, and my own swallowing had the intimacy of a confession.

“Here we do not kill to eat,” she said. “But I think you will like what I can give you.”

I looked at the bread—white, fine-crumbed, the crust scarcely browned, as if baked in a gentle oven. The cakes had a sheen like honey set; the fruit gave off clean perfumes: citrus oil, bruised peach, something dark and winey like figs. The milk smelled sweet and animal, not long kept. For all my distrust, hunger rose in me with its plain truth.

“I could desire nothing better,” I said, and meant it.

She seated herself on a couch by the table and signed to me to sit beside her. She poured milk into a bowl and handed it to me, then offered the loaf.

“Break from it such a piece as you like,” she said.

The bread tore with a soft sigh. The crumb was tender; it tasted of grain and quiet warmth, and the milk cooled my mouth like a clean cloud.

Then she filled two silver goblets from the crystal jug. The workmanship was grotesquely graceful, as if vines and beasts had been twisted together into handles.

“You have never drunk wine like this,” she said.

I drank.

It was like flowers made liquid without becoming perfume—every bloom that bees had ever visited distilled into a single draught. It tasted of sun and slow hills, of thyme and wild honey, of something faintly saline that made the sweetness deeper. It did not strike like common wine; it unfolded, and in unfolding it made the air around my head feel thicker with scent, as though my own breath carried gardens.

“And now that you will be able to listen,” she said, “I must do what I can to make myself intelligible to you. Our natures are so different that this may not be easy.”

She spoke on, telling me that men and women live but to die, while she—she and a few like her—live to live on. Old age, she said, was horror to us, desire to her; that their perfection was a ceaseless ripening, while ours came soon and passed. She had lived thousands of my years, she said, and had not troubled to count them. The everlasting, she told me, will not be measured.

Her voice flowed like the wine—sweet, persuasive, meant to be taken in without chewing. Yet beneath it I heard something else: a way of choosing words that placed her above me, a lexicon of dominance wrapped in velvet. When she said “progress,” it was as if she had bought the word and stamped it with her seal. When she spoke of “perfection,” the meaning was hers alone.

“Many lovers have sought me,” she continued. “I have loved none of them. They sought but to enslave me; they sought me as the men of my city seek gems of price.”

Then she turned the story toward me.

“When you found me,” she said, “I found a man. I put you to the test; you stood it; your love was genuine. It was, however, far from ideal—far from such love as I would have.”

Her eyes held mine, dark and bright at once.

“You loved me truly,” she went on, “but not with true love. Pity has, but is not love. What woman of any world would return love for pity? Such love as yours was then is hateful to me.”

I felt heat rise in my face. The bread in my stomach seemed suddenly heavy, as if it had been kneaded with accusation.

“I knew,” she said, “that if you saw me as I am, you would love me—like the rest of them—to have and to hold. I would none of that. I would be otherwise loved. I would have a love that outlived hopelessness, outmeasured indifference, hate, scorn. Therefore did I put on cruelty, despite, ingratitude.

“When I left you, I had shown myself such as you could at least no longer follow from pity: I was no longer in need of you. But you must satisfy my desire or set me free—prove yourself priceless or worthless. To satisfy the hunger of my love, you must follow me, looking for nothing—not gratitude, not even pity in return—follow and find me, and be content with merest presence, with scantest forbearance.

“I, not you, have failed,” she said, and her voice softened. “I yield the contest.”

She looked at me tenderly and hid her face in her hands.

But I had caught a flash behind the tenderness—a sparkle like a blade briefly shown then put away. I did not believe her. It seemed to me she laid herself out to secure and enslave me; she fascinated rather than confessed.

“Beautiful princess,” I said, forcing steadiness into my voice, “let me understand how you came to be found in such evil plight.”

“There are things I cannot explain,” she replied, lowering her hands, “until you have become capable of understanding them—which can only be when love is grown perfect. There are many things so hidden from you that you cannot even wish to know them. But any question you can put, I can in some measure answer.”

She spoke then of her journey.

“I had set out to visit a part of my dominions occupied by a savage dwarf-people—strong and fierce, enemies to law and order, opposed to every kind of progress—an evil race.” The words came out as if they had been taught to her by a court. I heard again that ownership of language: “law,” “order,” “progress,” each a banner she waved to make conquest seem like virtue.

“I went alone,” she said, “fearing nothing, unaware of the least necessity for precaution. I did not know that upon the hot stream beside which you found me, a certain woman—not so powerful as myself, not being immortal—had cast what you call a spell.”

“Spell,” she said, and her mouth shaped the mortal word with a faint disdain, as if it were a crude utensil.

“It is merely the setting in motion of a force as natural as any other,” she continued, “but operating primarily in a region beyond the ken of the mortal who makes use of the force.

“I set out on my journey, reached the stream, bounded across it—”

A shadow of embarrassment rose in her cheek—quick as blush on fruit. I understood why and gave no sign.

“—you know what a step it is in parts,” she added, and hurried on. “But in the very act an indescribable cold invaded me. I recognised at once the nature of the assault and knew it could affect me but temporarily. By sheer force of will I dragged myself to the wood—nor knew anything more until I saw you asleep, and the horrible worm at your neck.

“I crept out, dragged the monster from you, and laid my lips to the wound. You began to wake; I buried myself among the leaves.”

As she spoke of laying her lips to my wound, the room’s scent seemed to thicken—milk, bread, wine, and that delicate bitterness I had smelled in the bath, all braided into one. I could not tell whether it came from the food, from her, or from my own suspicion.

She rose suddenly. Her eyes flashed as never human eyes flashed, and she threw her arms high over her head, the silver patterns on her robe catching the alabaster light and scattering it like scales.

“What you have made me is yours!” she cried. “I will repay you as never yet did woman. My power, my beauty, my love are your own: take them.”

She dropped kneeling beside me, laid her arms across my knees, and looked up into my face.

Then I saw her left hand.

On it was a large clumsy glove. Instantly my mind filled it with hair and claws. Yet I knew it was a hand shut hard—perhaps badly bruised, perhaps worse. I glanced at her other hand: it was lovely as a hand could be, long-fingered, capable of gentleness and command. I felt that if I did less than loathe her, I should love her.

Not to dally with emotions that would usurp my judgment, I turned my eyes aside.

She started to her feet.

I sat motionless, looking down.

To me she may be true, whispered vanity. For a moment I was tempted to love a lie because it offered itself warmed and perfumed, like bread handed out in a famine.

An odour—more than a breath, less than a wind—fanned my face. I glanced up.

She stood erect before me, waving her lovely arms in a fashion that looked mystic, as if she were shaping the air with her wrists.

A frightful roar tore through the palace.

My heart rebounded against my ribs as if trying to escape. The alabaster trembled; the very light seemed to quiver. The roar was not merely loud—it had the weight of a living thing, the kind of sound that belongs to muscle and rage, not to stone and echo.

The princess shuddered visibly.

“My wine was too strong for you,” she said, and her voice quavered. “I ought not to have let you take a full draught. Go and sleep now, and when you wake ask me what you please. I will go with you. Come.”

She led the way up the white stair. Her tone tried for ease, but the palace’s silence had been wounded; every step we took rang sharper in the renewed hush.

“I do not wonder that roar startled you,” she said as we climbed. “It startled me, I confess: for a moment I feared she had escaped. But that is impossible.”

Yet to me the roar seemed—how I could not tell—like the voice of the white leopardess. And it seemed meant for me, not for the princess: a warning sounded in an animal language older than courts and their polished words.

At my door she turned and smiled, and the smile was perfect as the crust of a cake glazed to deceive the eye into hunger. But as she turned away, I read anxiety on her beautiful face, as plain as salt on the tongue.

I flung myself upon the bed as a man flings down a load he has carried too far, and set my mind to worrying, like fingers at a knot, the tale she had poured into me. One loose word of hers—no more than a grain dropped from the lip—had suddenly seasoned the whole with meaning. I saw again the bank of the stream, the princess stretched prone, the leap of the leopardess—how the water, running with that clean, kitchen-knife insistence of a mountain rill, had melted her self-wrought glamour as surely as warm broth loosens congealed fat. The stream had undone her. And her errand—told as if it were some necessary errand of household and realm—had shown me, plain as salt on the tongue, what peril hung over the Little Ones. I—blind meddler—had saved the one dread enemy they had.

I had scarce reached the end of that bitter mouthful when sleep took me. Perhaps the wine she had given was not all grape and sun; perhaps some old craft had been steeped in it, like herbs left too long in spirits until they turn the head and soften the will.

When I woke, night lay thick outside, but a lamp hung from the ceiling and shed a clear, gentle light, as if it were careful not to startle the shadows. A delicious languor wrapped me. I felt as though I floated far off shore, rocking on a twilight sea whose swell was made of breath. Mere existing was pleasure. No ache anywhere, no stitch, no throb. I thought, with a calm that frightened me, Surely I am dying.

No pain—ah—

A dart went through me, clean and vile, like the puncture of some hidden fang. It struck straight through the centre of me, and left behind it a sickness as if something rancid had been poured into the heart. Again—sharper, more exact, as if the point had learned its way. I could not lift my hand to my breast. Something held it down. My body would not answer me: not dead, but numbed—paralysed like a limb slept upon, only it was my whole self. My will raged and beat at the bars and could not move so much as a finger. Some hateful thing lay upon me.

I ceased striving and let myself lie, because struggle was only a mockery. Then I became aware—first by touch, then by the faintest change in the air—of a soft hand upon my face, pressing my head into the pillow, and of a heavy weight across me. My breath, that had been pinned, came freer; the weight lifted from my chest. I opened my eyes.

The princess stood above me on the bed, steady as if she had risen there like a dream made solid. She was looking out into the room, not at me, with the still, remote attention of one walking in sleep. Her great eyes were clear and calm. Her mouth had that look—not of tenderness, but of satisfied passion, as if she had drunk deep. She drew a hand across her lips and wiped away a streak of red.

She caught my gaze. In one swift motion she bent, and with the handkerchief in her hand struck across my eyes. It was like the edge of a knife drawn over them—cold, keen, blinding. For a moment the world went white and swimming.

In that blindness I heard a dull, heavy sound: the soft landing of some great, padded thing that had taken a little leap. My sight came back in a rush of pain and watering, and I saw the long swing of a tail disappearing through the half-open door.

I sprang after it.

The creature was gone as if it had never been. I shot down the stair into the hall of alabaster. The moon was high, and the place looked like the inside of a pale shell, all washed with cold light—a faint, sun-blanched moon within the moon. The princess was not there.

I must find her. In her presence I might protect myself; out of it I could not. It was as if she had made me a tame beast to be bled at leisure—set me, living, like a fountain in her garden, for a thirst that was not human. While my eyes were open I did not fear her. But my eyes would close. Sleep would come—soft as flour sifted—then she would come. Not seeing her, I felt her everywhere, as one feels a smell in the dark and cannot tell from what corner it creeps. She might be anywhere—might even now be waiting me in some secret cavern of sleep. Only with my eyes upon her could I feel safe from her.

Beyond the alabaster hall the darkness was thick as soot. I had to grope with hands and feet, feeling my way like a man in a smoke-filled kitchen. At last my fingers found a curtain. I pushed it aside and entered the black hall.

There was a great silent assembly.

How it was visible I cannot tell; the walls, the floor, the roof were smothered in a blackness deeper than any moonless night I have ever known—black as a pot left too long on the fire until the bottom is char and the air itself tastes of it. Yet my eyes could separate—vaguely, uncertainly—individuals in that mass, forms half-swallowed and half-born of dark. It was as if sight itself had been thinned, like broth stretched too far with water.

Silence and undefined motion possessed the wide space. All was a dim, confused dance, stirred with recurrent glimpses of shapes that struck some memory in me like a forgotten word on the tongue. Now a woman appeared, but with glorious eyes looking out from a skull. Now an armed figure upon a skeleton horse. Now one, now another of the hideous burrowing phantasms I had seen before. No order held; currents and eddies crossed and broke; if I thought I caught the rhythm of a dance, it shattered and confusion poured back in.

With the shifting colours of the more solid shapes mingled a multitude of shadows, as if cut free from their owners—each moving after its own will, each a counterfeit that had forgotten what it counterfeited. There was little sound—no laughter, no cries, only a hush that pressed on the ear until I became aware of my own blood moving, the small internal surf of a body trying to be alive. The silence had a kind of tyranny in it, like a dominant language imposed: it did not merely lack speech, it forbade it; it made every unuttered thought feel suspect, every private name unpronounceable.

I searched everywhere for the princess. Through the shifting, kaleidoscopic scene I could not see her, nor any sign that she was near. Where was she? What might she not be doing? No one took the least notice of me as I wandered, as if I were only another shadow strayed from its source.

At length, losing hope, I turned away. Finding the wall—though even then I could not see it, only feel its cold smoothness—I kept to it with my hand and groped along to a curtained opening into the vestibule.

There, dimly moonlighted, the cage of the leopardess was become an arena.

What I saw was a desperate struggle, and silent as the rest. Two forms—one human, one bestial—were so entangled that limb and flank and arm seemed one writhing knot. It lasted but an instant before, with the sick certainty of nightmare, the leopardess was out of the cage and walking quietly to the open door, as if nothing had happened at all.

I hurried after her. Yet even as I moved I glanced behind me, and there—impossible—was the leopardess again in the cage, couching motionless as when I had first seen her, like a beast carved of night and patience. My mind reeled as if some familiar word had been spoken in a dialect I did not know: the sound was near meaning, but meaning slipped away.

The moon, half-way up the sky, shone round and clear. The bodiless shadow I had seen the night before was walking through the trees toward the gate, and after him went the leopardess, tail swinging with that easy, domestic assurance of a creature that belongs where it goes. I followed a little way off, as silent as they. Neither once looked round.

Through the open gate we went down to the city, lying quiet under the moonshine as a covered loaf cools on a board—still, pale, waiting. The face of the moon was very still, and its stillness looked like expectation held in the breath.

The Shadow went straight to the stair at the top of which I had lain the night before. Without pause he went up, and the leopardess followed. I quickened my pace.

A cry of horror rang out—so sudden in that muffled world that it tore the silence like cloth.

Then something fell between me and the stair. It struck with a soft, heavy thud, like a sack dropped. At my feet lay a body—frightfully blackened and crushed—yet still recognisable as the woman who had led me home and shut me out. The moon showed her as it shows spilled ink: a darkness that drinks light.

I stood, petrified, while the meaning tried and failed to enter me.

Then the spotted leopardess came bounding down the stair with a baby in her mouth.

The sight turned my stomach as if I had bitten on bitterness. I darted to seize her before she could turn at the foot—but that instant, from behind me, the white leopardess shot through the moonlight like a great bar of glowing silver and took the spotted one by the neck.

The spotted leopardess dropped the child. I caught it up—warm, startlingly heavy with its small life, its skin smelling of milk and fear and the close air of human rooms. Its tiny mouth opened soundlessly; in that hush even its breathing seemed a stolen thing.

I stood and watched the battle.

Now the one was uppermost, now the other, both too intent for any loud noise—only a low growl, a whimper, a snarl of hate, and then the quicker scrambling of claws as each, worrying and pushing and dragging, fought for foothold on the pavement. The city remained moon-quiet around them; the sounds of the fight were the only breaks in that imposed silence, like a nearly forgotten dialect forcing itself back through a throat trained to other words.

The spotted leopardess was larger than the white, and anxiety tightened in me for my friend; but soon I saw that, though neither stronger nor more active, the white leopardess had the greater endurance. Not once did she lose her hold upon the other’s neck.

At length from the spotted throat issued a howl of agony—and it changed, by swift-crowded degrees, into the long-drawn crescendo of a woman’s uttermost wail, as if beast-speech had failed and human speech, too, had only that one raw syllable left.

The white leopardess relaxed her jaws.

The spotted one drew herself away and rose on her hind legs.

Erect in the moonlight stood the princess.

A confused rush of shadows careered over her whiteness—the leopard’s spots crowding, hurrying, fleeing to the refuge of her eyes, where, merging, they vanished. The last few, outsped and belated, tangled in the cloud of her streamy hair and were gone, leaving her radiant as the moon when a legion of little vapours has flown off her disc—save that down the white column of her throat a thread of blood still trickled from every wound made by her adversary’s terrible teeth.

She turned away, took a few steps with the gait of a Hecate—ancient, ruthless, at home in night—then fell, covered afresh with her spots, and fled at a long, stretching gallop.

The white leopardess turned also.

She sprang upon me, and with a force that was not savage but absolute, pulled my arms asunder. The baby slipped from my grasp. She caught it as it fell—caught it as neatly as a cook catches a falling knife by the handle—and flew with it along the street toward the gate, white as driven moonlight, swift as hunger.

I wheeled and went after the spotted leopardess, catching only the streak of her flank as she skimmed up the hill and vanished through the palace gate like a thrown shadow.

When I reached the entrance-hall, the princess was just lifting the robe she had dropped in her haste and drawing it round her. The blood on her neck had stopped running; it had stiffened and dulled in the night wind, as if the air itself had baked it dry. She saw me, and anger flashed—quick as a knife catching moonlight—then she turned her face away. A moment later she looked back with a smile she tried to make easy, as if she were laying a cloth over a cracked table.

“I have met with a small accident!” she said lightly. “Happening to hear the cat-woman was again in the city, I went down to send her away. But she had one of her horrid creatures with her: it sprang upon me, and had its claws in my neck before I could strike it!”

She shivered, and the movement pulled the torn skin so that my own throat tightened in sympathy. I knew she lied—her voice had the smoothness of something over-stirred, all surface and no body—but the wounds were real. And the set of her mouth, the strained brightness of her eyes, dragged up the memory of her face as I had seen it in the cave: not merely frightened, but hungry in some inward way that made fear look like appetite.

My heart accused me that I had let her be alone with her enemy. Perhaps the pity showed in my face.

“Child of folly!” she said, with a little, broken laugh that tried to be mocking and almost became tender. “—not crying, surely! Wait for me here; I am going into the black hall for a moment. I want you to get me something for my scratches.”

But I followed her close. Out of my sight I feared her.

The instant she crossed the threshold, the air altered—as if I had stepped from an orchard into a mill-house. A buzzing began, not loud, but thick: a swarm of low voices with no single tongue I could catch. Then, one portion after another, the vast dimness inside started to kindle, as though a narrow beam travelled restlessly from place to place, touching now a cluster, now a corner, then slipping away.

Group after group would flare into clear sight for a breath, then sink back into the general dusk, while elsewhere something else brightened. The sound never resolved into words; it stayed at that uneasy pitch where listening becomes labour. It made me think of a kitchen full of talk that has been muffled by a closed door—only here the door was the skull.

In those wandering illuminations I saw actions I knew. I had been in them, or watched them. So had the princess. And now—present in every scene as it brightened—I saw her.

The skull-headed dancers stamped the grass in the forest-hall, their feet keeping time with a rhythm that was all bone and no warmth: there, at the door, was the princess looking in. The fight raged in the Evil Wood: there was the princess urging it, her posture eager as a cook leaning over a pot that has begun to boil over. Yet I was close behind her all the while, and she stood motionless, head bowed on her breast, as if she were only listening.

The confused murmur continued; colours and shapes churned and shifted; the ray kept hunting.

At last it settled on the hollow in the heath. There was the princess, walking up and down, gathering at the air with desperate hands, trying to wrap the vapour round her like a shawl, and failing, and trying again. The sight made the hair prickle along my arms—not because it was strange, but because it was familiar, as if a thing long unspoken had suddenly been named.

Then I saw what startled me into a colder kind of attention: the old librarian came up to her. He stood a moment looking at her as one who knows the true weight of a word in its own dying dialect. She fell.

Her limbs forsook her and fled, as if her body were only a story told badly and could no longer hold its parts together. Her shape collapsed into nothing. And with the fall of that likeness—a mere showing, an eidolon—there came a wild shriek that rang round the place, and the princess herself, who had stood like a statue before me, fell heavily and lay still.

I turned at once and went out. Not again would I seek to restore her.

I stood trembling beside the cage. The silence outside seemed to have its own sound after that buzzing chamber, as if the palace courtyards were an old landscape where the birds had been driven off by some distant industry. Then I knew what I had been inside: the black ellipsoid was in the brain of the princess. The thought sat in me like a foreign seed.

I saw the tail of the leopardess quiver once.

While I was still trying to steady my breathing, I heard the princess’s voice beside me, as if she had never fallen at all.

“Come now,” she said. “I will show you what I want you to do for me.”

She led the way into the court. I followed, dazed, in a compliance I could not account for—like a man who keeps walking because his feet remember the path even when his mind has forgotten why.

The moon stood near the zenith. Her silver seemed brighter than the gold of the absent sun, and it laid a clean glaze over stone, leaf, and skin, as if everything had been rinsed and set out to cool.

The princess brought me through the trees to the tallest—standing in the centre like a mast in the middle of a dark sea. It was not quite like the rest: its branches drew their ends together at the top, making a clump that from beneath looked like a fir-cone, tightly closed, keeping something hidden.

She stood close under it, gazing up, and spoke as if talking to herself, as one might mutter over a recipe that will not come right.

“On the summit of that tree grows a tiny blossom which would at once heal my scratches! I might be a dove for a moment and fetch it, but I see a little snake in the leaves whose bite would be worse to a dove than the bite of a tiger to me!—How I hate that cat-woman!”

The hatred in her voice sharpened the air. It had a hiss of its own, a sibilant edge that made me imagine the snake whether it was there or not.

She turned quickly to me, and for an instant her face wore one of her sweetest smiles, as if she were offering bread. “Can you climb?”

The smile vanished with the brief question, and her features fell into sadness and pain so practiced it might have been a garment she knew how to drape. I ought to have left her to suffer; yet the way her hand rose to her wounded neck—careful, as if seasoning a dish too hot to touch—went straight through my resolve.

I considered the tree. All the way up to the branches were projections on the stem like the remnants on a palm where fallen leaves have left their scars—steps for a barefoot man, if the bark did not bite.

“I can climb that tree,” I answered.

“Not with bare feet!” she returned at once, and for once her concern sounded like something more than strategy. “A fall from the top would kill you!”

“So would a bite from the snake,” I said—though I confess I did not truly believe there was any snake. It seemed too neat a story, too well placed, like a garnish meant to distract from a thin broth.

“It would not hurt YOU!” she replied. “—Wait a moment.”

In my haste to follow the leopardess I had left my sandals in my room. I was used to going barefoot; the soles of my feet had learned the language of stone and earth. Still, she would not allow it.

She tore from her garment the two wide borders that met in front—thick, embroidered strips that looked as if they had once been meant for ceremony, not for binding. She knelt on one knee, and made me put first my left foot, then my right, upon the cloth. She wrapped and tied them about my feet and ankles with the quick precision of someone who knows how to truss a bird so it will roast evenly.

“You have left the ends hanging, princess,” I said, watching the dangling lengths.

“I have nothing to cut them off with; but they are not long enough to get entangled,” she replied.

I turned to the tree and began to climb.

At first the bark felt warm from the day’s stored heat, as if the tree were a great oven cooling. But after sundown in Bulika, though the cold is gentler than in other parts—especially about the sexton’s cottage—the chill comes in layers, like thin sheets laid one after another. As I climbed I began to feel very cold; colder as I rose, and coldest of all when I got among the branches. My shivering made my grip uncertain, and my hands and feet seemed to lose their own sense, as if nerve and muscle had been numbed by some unseen frost.

There was hardly any wind; the branches did not sway. Yet as I neared the summit I became aware of a peculiar unsteadiness: every branch I stepped on, every limb I grasped, felt as if it were on the point of giving way—not bending like wood, but failing like an intention.

The moon laid patches of pale shine—silvery foam—on the rugged bole. High above, a little rush went through the top with a murmurous sound as of water falling softly into water. That sound drew me on with a thirst I had not expected. In a world where the ear is battered by false clamour and the gaudy selling of noises for music, the promise of honest water—water that does not advertise itself—has a power like bread on a fasting day.

When my head rose above the upper branches and I came into open moonlight, I began to look about for the tiny blossom—some pale star of a flower that could close flesh like a stitch.

At that instant I was drenched from head to foot.

The next, as if I had been plunged into stormy water, I was flung about wildly and felt myself sinking. There was no splash I could name, no clean roar of a river: only a smothered, swallowing rush, like being inside a great pot when the lid is clapped down and the boil turns violent.

Tossed up and down, hurled this way and that, rolled over and over, checked, rolled the other way and tossed up again, I sank lower and lower. I gasped, gurgled, choked—my mouth filling with cold, tasteless water that nonetheless carried a strange absence, as if it had forgotten the mineral words of earth.

At last I struck a solid bottom.

“I told you so!” croaked a voice in my ear.

I wiped the water from my lashes, and the first thing the dawn gave back to me was the raven.

He perched on the rim of the great stone basin as if it had been made for his feet alone, his feathers taking the cold morning light the way a well-oiled pan takes fire—steel-dark, glossy, untroubled. He looked down at me with a steadiness that made the fountain’s uproar seem like mere background.

I lay on my back in the water, buoyed as if the basin were a broad bowl and I the unconsidered broth within it. Propping myself on my elbows, I lifted my face to breathe. The air was sharp with wet stone and lawn-grass, with that clean metallic tang fountains always bring—water striking water, water striking carved rock. High above, the thick stalk of the jet rose like a polished column, hard and shining, shouldering its torrent a hundred feet into the sky before it burst into a white blossom of foam. The sound was not a simple roar but a layered life: the continuous rush, the scatter-hiss of spray, the slap of falling sheets, the thin singing of droplets when the wind caught them. It was a whole acoustic garden, and it steadied me even while it drowned thought.

Nettled—more by the raven’s tone than by the cold soaking through me—I said, “You told me nothing!”

“I told you to do nothing any one you distrusted asked you.”

His voice cut cleanly through the fountain’s noise, as if it knew where to place itself in my hearing.

“Tt—how was mortal to remember that?” I snapped, half shivering, half angry, tasting stone-cold water on my tongue.

“You will not forget the consequences of having forgotten it,” he returned.

He leaned over the margin. A hand—his hand now, not a claw—stretched across the basin. I took it, and in the same instant I was no longer in the water at all but standing beside him on the lawn, my clothes clinging and streaming as if the fountain had spat me out.

“You must change your clothes at once!” he said. “A wetting does not signify where you come from—though at present such an accident is unusual; here it has its inconveniences!”

Even as he spoke he was again a raven, walking toward the house with something measured and almost ceremonial in his step. The door stood open.

“I have not much to change!” I laughed, though my teeth clicked with the cold; for I had flung aside my robe to climb the tree, and what I wore was scarcely worth calling a suit.

“It is a long time since I moulted a feather!” said the raven, as if that settled the matter between us.

Inside, the house held the hushed heaviness of early morning—no footsteps, no voices, only the distant, persistent water outside, threading its way through corridors and floors like a reminder you could not shut out. I went to my room, found a dressing-gown, and exchanged dripping for damp. Cloth against skin was suddenly loud to me: the soft rasp of dry fabric, the faint suction where wet had been. I pulled the gown close as one draws a blanket over a child.

Then I descended to the library. As I entered, the librarian came out from the closet—so quietly that I might have thought him a shadow detaching itself from the wall. I threw myself on a couch as if my body had been carrying a sack of stones and had at last found a place to drop it.

Mr. Raven—still raven, and yet not—drew a chair to my side and sat down.

For a minute or two neither of us spoke. The silence was not empty; it was full of the fountain’s far-off insistence, and of the house’s breathing—timbers settling, a faint draft worrying a curtain. I listened as if my mind could eat sound the way the body eats bread, hoping for some nourishment in it.

At last I broke it. “What does it all mean?”

“A good question!” he rejoined. “Nobody knows what anything is; a man can learn only what a thing means! Whether he do, depends on the use he is making of it.”

“I have made no use of anything yet!”

“Not much; but you know the fact, and that is something! Most people take more than a lifetime to learn that they have learned nothing, and done less! At least you have not been without the desire to be of use!”

“I did want to do something for the children—the precious Little Ones, I mean.” The very thought of them brought back their small hands and grave eyes, their careful ways of tending what they had, as if every crumb were a relic.

“I know you did—and started the wrong way!”

“I did not know the right way.”

“That is true also—but you are to blame that you did not.”

“I am ready to believe whatever you tell me—as soon as I understand what it means.”

“Had you accepted our invitation, you would have known the right way. When a man will not act where he is, he must go far to find his work.”

“Indeed I have gone far, and got nowhere, for I have not found my work! I left the children to learn how to serve them, and have only learned the danger they are in.”

“When you were with them, you were where you could help them: you left your work to look for it! It takes a wise man to know when to go away; a fool may learn to go back at once!”

His words landed with a thud, like a loaf dropped too early from a peel—heavy, misshapen, undeniable.

“Do you mean, sir, I could have done something for the Little Ones by staying with them?”

“Could you teach them anything by leaving them?”

“No; but how could I teach them? I did not know how to begin. Besides, they were far ahead of me!”

“That is true. But you were not a rod to measure them with! Certainly, if they knew what you know, not to say what you might have known, they would be ahead of you—out of sight ahead! but you saw they were not growing—or growing so slowly that they had not yet developed the idea of growing! they were even afraid of growing!—You had never seen children remain children!”

The fountain’s steady thunder seemed, for a moment, like a rebuke too: water endlessly rising and falling, refusing stagnation.

“But surely I had no power to make them grow!”

“You might have removed some of the hindrances to their growing!”

“What are they? I do not know them. I did think perhaps it was the want of water!”

“Of course it is! they have none to cry with!”

The sentence struck me with a strange, bitter clarity, as if he had named an ingredient I’d been missing without knowing it—something common and essential, something you cannot substitute with good intentions.

“I would gladly have kept them from requiring any for that purpose!”

“No doubt you would—the aim of all stupid philanthropists! Why, Mr. Vane, but for the weeping in it, your world would never have become worth saving!”

I heard again, as if my ears were opening to a remembered landscape, the faint subterranean murmur I had once noticed near them—a watery sound under earth, like a hidden brook speaking its own language to roots and stones. It was the sort of sound a farmer knows, an elder with soil in his nails: the promise of a spring, the secret pantry beneath the field. And I had let it be only a passing curiosity, a noise among other noises.

“You confess you thought it might be water they wanted: why did not you dig them a well or two?”

“That never entered my mind!”

“Not when the sounds of the waters under the earth entered your ears?”

“I believe it did once. But I was afraid of the giants for them. That was what made me bear so much from the brutes myself!”

“Indeed you almost taught the noble little creatures to be afraid of the stupid Bags!” His voice sharpened on the word, as if the very syllable were coarse in his beak. “While they fed and comforted and worshipped you, all the time you submitted to be the slave of bestial men! You gave the darlings a seeming coward for their hero! A worse wrong you could hardly have done them. They gave you their hearts; you owed them your soul!—You might by this time have made the Bags hewers of wood and drawers of water to the Little Ones!”

Water again—drawers of water. The phrase made a domestic picture despite its sting: the simple, daily labor that keeps a household alive; the carrying, the pouring, the careful sharing. In my world, so much of that had been pushed into pipes and hidden machinery until we forgot the dignity of the sound of a bucket filling, the hush when a thirsty mouth meets a cup. Here, water was not convenience; it was voice. It could be the difference between a people remaining forever child-silent and a people learning to weep—learning, perhaps, to speak.

“I fear what you say is true, Mr. Raven! But indeed I was afraid that more knowledge might prove an injury to them—render them less innocent, less lovely.”

“They had given you no reason to harbour such a fear!”

“Is not a little knowledge a dangerous thing?”

“That is one of the pet falsehoods of your world! Is man’s greatest knowledge more than a little? or is it therefore dangerous? The fancy that knowledge is in itself a great thing, would make any degree of knowledge more dangerous than any amount of ignorance. To know all things would not be greatness.”

“At least it was for love of them, not from cowardice that I served the giants!”

“Granted. But you ought to have served the Little Ones, not the giants! You ought to have given the Little Ones water; then they would soon have taught the giants their true position. In the meantime you could yourself have made the giants cut down two-thirds of their coarse fruit-trees to give room to the little delicate ones! You lost your chance with the Lovers, Mr. Vane! You speculated about them instead of helping them!”

Speculated. The word tasted dusty—like flour left too long in a sack, the life gone out of it. And in the library, with the fountain’s sound still pressing in from the world beyond the walls, I could not deny the charge: I had treated living souls like a subject for talk, as if naming their sweetness were the same as feeding it, as if admiring their table were the same as setting it with what they needed.

I sat there, damp and chastened, listening to water I had not given.

I sat with my hands fallen useless in my lap, as if shame had weight enough to pin my wrists to my knees. The room was still, yet not quiet: the house had its own breath, a low timber-creak, the faint chime of cooling coals, and beyond the walls the wind worrying at leaves like fingers testing old paper. Silence, I found, is not the absence of sound, but a kind of listening that admits everything.

Mr. Raven sat opposite—no, the librarian sat opposite, lean and dark-eyed, with that severe patience of one who has watched too many creatures make the same error and call it new. When he spoke, his voice did not trample the air; it went through it as a well-tuned bow through string.

“What I said is true,” he resumed. “You have not been a wise neighbour to the Little Ones.”

It was exactly the sort of rebuke that does not flare and die like anger, but stays, like salt in a cut, and will not let you forget the shape of the wound. I could not deny it.

“You wronged,” he went on, “at the same time the stupid creatures themselves. For them slavery would have been progress. To them a few such lessons as you could have given them—with a stick from one of their own trees—would have been invaluable.”

A gust breathed down the chimney. The fire answered in soft clicks and a brief, irritated hiss, like fat meeting a hot pan. I thought of the Little Ones—how they had cowered and giggled and run, and how I had taken their fear for harmlessness, their laughter for safety.

“I did not know they were cowards,” I muttered.

“What difference does that make? The man who grounds his action on another’s cowardice, is essentially a coward himself. I fear worse will come of it. By this time the Little Ones might have been able to protect themselves from the princess—not to say the giants; they were always fit enough for that. As it was they laughed at them. But now, through your relations with her—”

“I hate her!” The words broke out of me, raw as a bitten tongue.

“Did you let her know you hated her?”

The question found the exact place I had tried to mortar over with excuses. I made no answer.

“Not even to her have you been faithful.” He lifted a hand as if to close a book on the matter, then stopped, head tilted. “But hush! We were followed from the fountain, I fear!”

“No living creature did I see—except a disreputable-looking cat that bolted into the shrubbery.”

“It was a magnificent Persian—so wet and draggled, though, as to look what she was—worse than disreputable!”

His words caught my mind as a hook catches cloth. A new horror tightened, not in the chest alone but in the throat, as if my own breath were suddenly a stranger.

“What do you mean, Mr. Raven?” I whispered. “There was a beautiful blue Persian about the house, but she fled at the very sound of water. Could she have been after the goldfish?”

“We shall see,” he returned. “I know a little about cats of several sorts, and there is that in the room which will unmask this one, or I am mistaken in her.”

He rose with the deliberate economy of an old man who has learned to conserve strength for the necessary moments. From the closet he brought the mutilated volume I had seen before—yet as he turned and came back to his chair, it was no mutilation he carried, but a whole book, entire and sound, the vellum cover unbroken, the pages thick as dried leaves.

I stared until my eyes ached.

“Where was the other half of it?” I managed.

“Sticking through into my library,” he answered, as if the passing of a book through worlds were no stranger than a ladle through steam. He sat down beside me again. I had questions—an ocean of them—but the edge of something nearer than curiosity pressed against my skin, and I held my peace. One more question might have been a step into a dark place with no time left to climb out.

“Listen,” he said. “I am going to read a stanza or two. There is one present who, I imagine, will hardly enjoy the reading!”

He opened the vellum, turned leaves with a care that reminded me of handling saffron threads—touch too roughly and you lose what you meant to use. The parchment smelled faintly of animal skin and dust, but also of something stranger, like rain caught in wool and kept too long in a chest. One leaf showed a dark stain, old as dried blood in a seam. He turned it and searched as if following a remembered path through a forest.

He began to read.

What follows is not what he read—how could it be?—but what it did to my mind. The sounds of the words were unlike any tongue I had known, and yet their meaning slid into me with the certainty of hunger recognizing bread. It was as if some buried dialect of the soul, long outlawed by the dominant speech of waking life, was suddenly spoken aloud again—untranslatable, and therefore true.

“But if I found a man that could believe
