In what he saw not, felt not, and yet knew,
From him I should take substance, and receive
Firmness and form relate to touch and view;
Then should I clothe me in the likeness true
Of that idea where his soul did cleave!”

He turned a leaf. The house creaked softly, a settling of beams like an old throat swallowing.

“In me was every woman. I had power
Over the soul of every living man,
Such as no woman ever had in dower—
Could what no woman ever could, or can;
All women, I, the woman, still outran,
Outsoared, outsank, outreigned, in hall or bower.

“For I, though me he neither saw nor heard,
Nor with his hand could touch finger of mine,
Although not once my breath had ever stirred
A hair of him, could trammel brain and spine
With rooted bonds which Death could not untwine—
Or life, though hope were evermore deferred.”

The air grew tight. I became aware, painfully, of the soundscape of the room: the minute rush in my own ears, the fire’s cautious crackle, the far-off drip of water somewhere in the house as if a tap were not fully shut. In that listening, a new sound rose—thin, wrong, and bestial: a feline wail, not the healthy complaint of a cat shut out from the dairy, but the cry of something flayed by its own skin.

I started up on my elbow and stared about me. Nothing moved. No tail flicked, no shadow crossed the floor.

Mr. Raven turned several leaves and went on as if the book were a bell and he were ringing it for a creature that could not bear its note.

“For by his side I lay, a bodiless thing;
I breathed not, saw not, felt not, only thought,
And made him love me—with a hungering
After he knew not what—if it was aught
Or but a nameless something that was wrought
By him out of himself; for I did sing

“A song that had no sound into his soul;
I lay a heartless thing against his heart,
Giving him nothing where he gave his whole
Being to clothe me human, every part:
That I at last into his sense might dart,
Thus first into his living mind I stole.

“Ah, who was ever conquering Love but I!
Who else did ever throne in heart of man!
To visible being, with a gladsome cry
Waking, life’s tremor through me throbbing ran!”

Again the room answered with that repulsive cry, nearer now, as if it had been under the very chair. The sound made my teeth ache. I searched the carpet, the curtains, the shadow beneath the table where a cat might crouch—nothing. Yet my skin knew it was not alone.

Mr. Raven paused, listened for the space of two heartbeats, then turned pages and read on:

“Sudden I woke, nor knew the ghastly fear
That held me—not like serpent coiled about,
But like a vapour moist, corrupt, and drear,
Filling heart, soul, and breast and brain throughout;
My being lay motionless in sickening doubt,
Nor dared to ask how came the horror here.

“My past entire I knew, but not my now;
I understood nor what I was, nor where;
I knew what I had been: still on my brow
I felt the touch of what no more was there!
I was a fainting, dead, yet live Despair;
A life that flouted life with mop and mow!

“That I was a queen I knew right well,
And sometimes wore a splendour on my head
Whose flashing even dead darkness could not quell—
The like on neck and arms and girdle-stead;
And men declared a light my closed eyes shed
That killed the diamond in its silver cell.”

The feline cry came again—uglier, as if the throat that made it were clogged with water and mud. It slid through the room’s ordinary noises like oil on broth, spoiling everything.

Mr. Raven’s gaze swept once about the room, quick and assessing; then he turned several pages and resumed.

“Hideously wet, my hair of golden hue
Fouled my fair hands: to have it swiftly shorn
I had given my rubies, all for me dug new—
No eyes had seen, and such no waist had worn!
For a draught of water from a drinking horn,
For one blue breath, I had given my sapphires blue!

“Nay, I had given my opals for a smock,
A peasant-maiden’s garment, coarse and clean:
My shroud was rotting! Once I heard a cock
Lustily crow upon the hillock green
Over my coffin. Dulled by space between,
Came back an answer like a ghostly mock.”

The cry rose again, and with it I smelled something—wet fur, rank as a cloth left in a pail overnight, and underneath that a metallic taint, like the bottom of a copper pot scoured too hard. My stomach turned, and not from fear alone: the senses do not like being lied to, and the room was full of a lie that had begun to stink.

“I thought some foul thing was in the room!” said the librarian, and there was an almost weary disgust in him, like one who has heard too many imitations of music blasted from cheap horns in the street. But he did not stop. He turned a leaf or two and read again:

“For I had bathed in milk and honey-dew,
In rain from roses shook, that ne’er touched earth,
And ointed me with nard of amber hue;
Never had spot me spotted from my birth,
Or mole, or scar of hurt, or fret of dearth;
Never one hair superfluous on me grew.

“Fleeing cold whiteness, I would sit alone—
Not in the sun—I feared his bronzing light,
But in his radiance back around me thrown
By fulgent mirrors tempering his might;
Thus bathing in a moon-bath not too bright,
My skin I tinted slow to ivory tone.

“But now, all round was dark, dark all within!
My eyes not even gave out a phantom-flash;
My fingers sank in pulp through pulpy skin;
My body lay death-weltered in a mash
Of slimy horrors—”

With a fearsome yell, she burst into sight as if spat out by the room itself. Her fur clung in clammy clumps; water streamed from her like tears from a rag; her tail was thick as a cable, and her eyes flared green as chrysoprase in firelight. Her claws, distended and entangled, made her flounder across the carpet as though the very tools of her violence hindered her. Huge and white, she rushed for the chimney.

Quick as thought Mr. Raven flung the manuscript between her and the hearth.

She stopped as if she had struck an invisible wall. She crouched, her eyes fastened on the book with a hatred that was almost hunger—yet not hunger for food, but the hunger that wants to unmake what it cannot digest.

His voice went on as if he still read, and his eyes, too, seemed fixed on the pages:

“Ah, the two worlds! so strangely are they one,
And yet so measurelessly wide apart!
Oh, had I lived the bodiless alone
And from defiling sense held safe my heart,
Then had I scaped the canker and the smart,
Scaped life-in-death, scaped misery’s endless moan!”

At those words the cat loosed such a howl—so prolonged, so loaded with agony—that the sound battered the room. It was not merely loud; it was corrupting, like a factory’s unending shriek drowning out birdsong, until you forget there were ever birds. We both clapped our hands to our ears.

When the howl broke at last, Mr. Raven walked to the fireplace, took up the book, and stood between the creature and the chimney. He pointed one finger at her, and for a moment everything held—fire, air, beast, man—as if the world waited for a verdict.

She lay perfectly still.

He took a half-burnt stick from the hearth and drew with it a sign upon the floor. The charcoal whispered against the boards. He set the manuscript back in its place with a look that said plainly, Now we have her, I think! Then he returned to the cat, stood over her, and spoke in a voice so still and solemn it seemed to quiet even the ticking of ash falling.

“Lilith, when you came here on the way to your evil will, you little thought into whose hands you were delivering yourself! Mr. Vane, when God created me—not out of Nothing, as say the unwise, but out of His own endless glory—He brought me an angelic splendour to be my wife: there she lies!”

The cat’s beryl eyes burned up at him; his gaze held hers as a hand holds a lid down on a boiling pot.

“For her first thought was POWER,” he continued. “She counted it slavery to be one with me, and bear children for Him who gave her being. One child, indeed, she bore; then, puffed with the fancy that she had created her, would have me fall down and worship her! Finding, however, that I would but love and honour, never obey and worship her, she poured out her blood to escape me, fled to the army of the aliens, and soon had so ensnared the heart of the great Shadow, that he became her slave, wrought her will, and made her queen of Hell.

“How it is with her now, she best knows, but I know also. The one child of her body she fears and hates, and would kill, asserting a right, which is a lie, over what God sent through her into His new world. Of creating, she knows no more than the crystal that takes its allotted shape, or the worm that makes two worms when it is cloven asunder. Vilest of God’s creatures, she lives by the blood and lives and souls of men. She consumes and slays, but is powerless to destroy as to create.”

The words had the force of an old field-curse spoken in the dialect that names every tool and furrow exactly. I felt, with a shudder of disgust, how power that cannot make bread will always try to drink blood instead.

“Then God gave me another wife,” he said, and his voice altered—there came into it the note of a remembered hearth, the sound of a song hummed while hands knead dough: “not an angel but a woman—who is to this as light is to darkness.”

The cat gave a screech that seemed to tear at the boards under her. Then she began to grow.

She went on growing and growing, as if the room itself lent her substance. Her body swelled; the white fur mottled; the spots came as if ink were spilled beneath skin. At last, the spotted leopardess uttered a roar that made the house tremble. The very air shook, and in the far distance I thought I heard, beyond this unnatural din, the faintest echo of a bird startled from sleep—an innocent sound driven off by terror.

I sprang to my feet. Mr. Raven did not so much as blink.

“It is but her jealousy that speaks,” he said, “jealousy self-kindled, foiled and fruitless; for here I am, her master now whom she would not have for her husband! while my beautiful Eve yet lives, hoping immortally! Her hated daughter lives also, but beyond her evil ken, one day to be what she counts her destruction—for even Lilith shall be saved by her childbearing.

“Meanwhile she exults that my human wife plunged herself and me in despair, and has borne me a countless race of miserables; but my Eve repented, and is now beautiful as never was woman or angel, while her groaning, travailing world is the nursery of our Father’s children. I too have repented, and am blessed.—Thou, Lilith, hast not yet repented; but thou must.—Tell me, is the great Shadow beautiful? Knowest thou how long thou wilt thyself remain beautiful? Answer me, if thou knowest.”

As he spoke, something in my mind clicked into place with dreadful simplicity: this was no mere librarian with sharp speech and stranger books. The steadiness, the authority that did not need to shout, the sorrow that did not curdle—these were older than any library. I understood then that Mr. Raven was Adam, the old and the new man; and that his wife, ministering in the house of the dead, was Eve—the mother of us all, the lady of the New Jerusalem.

The leopardess reared. Her spots flickered and fled like a mask losing its hold. In their place stood the princess at last, radiant in her perfect shape, as if beauty were a weapon she could draw and brandish.

“I AM beautiful—and immortal!” she said.

“As a bush that burns, and is consumed,” answered he who had been her husband. “What is that under thy right hand?”

Her arm lay across her bosom; her hand was pressed to her side, as if to keep something in place. A swift pang contorted her face—so quick it might have been denied, had it not been written in the muscles like truth in a scar.

“It is but a leopard-spot that lingers! It will quickly follow those I have dismissed,” she answered, and tried to make the words ring careless.

“Thou art beautiful because God created thee,” Adam said, “but thou art the slave of sin: take thy hand from thy side.”

Her hand sank away. She looked him in the eyes with a fierceness that quailed even as it threatened—no surrender in it, only the panic of a tyrant asked to lay down her crown. He gazed a moment at the place she hid.

“It is not on the leopard; it is in the woman!” he said. “Nor will it leave thee until it hath eaten to thy heart, and thy beauty hath flowed from thee through the open wound!”

She glanced downward and shivered, as if she had smelled rot under perfume.

“Lilith,” Adam said, and his tone changed. The iron in it softened into a beseeching that was more terrible than wrath, as when an elder speaks in the old tongue to a child about to forget it forever. “Hear me, and repent, and He who made thee will cleanse thee!”

Her hand returned, quivering, to her side. Her face grew dark, as if a lamp were smoked over from within. She gave the cry of one from whom hope is vanishing; the cry passed into a howl. She fell writhing on the floor—a leopardess again, covered with spots, as if her own corruption broke through every disguise.

“The evil thou meditatest,” Adam resumed, “thou shalt never compass, Lilith, for Good and not Evil is the Universe. The battle between them may last for countless ages, but it must end: how will it fare with thee when Time hath vanished in the dawn of the eternal morn? Repent, I beseech thee; repent, and be again an angel of God!”

She rose. She stood upright, a woman once more, and said, with the cold relish of one naming a dish she intends to devour, “I will not repent. I will drink the blood of thy child.”

My eyes had been fastened on her as one watches a knife-hand. But when Adam spoke again, I turned—because the air changed. The sound changed. The room, which had endured her howls, seemed suddenly to brace itself against something greater.

He stood towering above her. The form of his visage was altered; his voice was terrible, not with loudness but with law.

“Down!” he cried; “or by the power given me I will melt thy very bones.”

She flung herself on the floor. She dwindled and dwindled, the proud shape collapsing as a fire collapses when its fuel is snatched away. She was again a gray cat, small enough to be carried, hateful enough to poison a house.

Adam caught her up by the skin of her neck, bore her to the closet, and threw her in as one casts a verminous thing into a bin. He described a strange figure on the threshold—charcoal again, a sign that seemed to bite into the air as well as the wood. Then, closing the door, he locked it.

When he returned to my side he was once more the old librarian, looking sad and worn, and furtively wiping tears from his eyes—tears that fell without sound, as if even grief here had learned to walk softly, so as not to feed the wrong listener.

“We must be on our guard,” he said; and the low voice with which he spoke seemed to lay itself along the shelves like dust that will not lift. “Or she will again outwit us. She would befool the very elect.”

“How are we to be on our guard?” I asked.

“Every way,” he returned. “She fears—therefore hates—her child; and she is in this house on her way to destroy her. The birth of children is in her eyes the death of their parents, and every new generation the enemy of the last. Her daughter appears to her an open channel through which her immortality—immortality which yet she counts self-inherent—is flowing fast away: to fill it up, almost from her birth she has pursued her with an utter enmity.

“But her craft has hitherto ended in this—that within the region she claims for her own has appeared a colony of children; and that daughter is heart and head to them, and sheltering wings. My Eve longed after the child, would have been to her as a mother to her first-born; but we were then unfit to train her. She was carried into the wilderness, and for ages we knew nothing of her fate.

“She was divinely fostered. Young angels were her playmates. She never knew care until she found a baby in the wood; then the mother-heart in her awoke, as if some old, forgotten word in the blood had been spoken again in its native sound. One by one she has found many children since, and that heart is not yet full. Her family is her absorbing charge, and never children were better mothered. Her authority over them is without appeal; yet it is unknown to herself, and never rises like a banner—only shows itself in watchfulness and service. She has forgotten the time when she lived without them, and thinks she came herself from the wood, the first of the family.

“You have saved the life of her and their enemy; therefore your life belongs to her and them. The princess was on her way to destroy them; but as she crossed that stream, vengeance overtook her, and she would have died had you not come to her aid. You did; and ere now she would have been raging among the Little Ones, had she dared again cross the stream.

“But there was yet a way to the blessed little colony through the world of the three dimensions; only, from that, by the slaying of her former body, she had excluded herself; and, except in personal contact with one belonging to it, could not re-enter it. You provided the opportunity: never, in all her long years, had she had one before. Her hand, with lightest touch, was on one or other of your muffled feet, every step as you climbed. In that little chamber she is now watching to leave it as soon as ever she may.”

“She cannot know anything about the door!” I said; “she cannot at least know how to open it!” Yet my words had the thin sound of a language spoken without its proper breath; my heart did not speak them with me.

“Hush, hush!” whispered the librarian, lifting his hand as if to still not only my voice but the very air. “She can hear through anything. You must go at once, and make your way to my wife’s cottage. I will remain to keep guard over her.”

“Let me go to the Little Ones!” I cried.

“Beware of that, Mr. Vane. Go to my wife, and do as she tells you.”

His counsel struck me like a rule laid across the knuckles: right, perhaps, but hard; and my spirit, being hungry for the plain relief of action, would not take it readily. Why haste to encounter measureless delay? If not to protect the children, why go at all? Alas, even now I believed him only enough to question, not to obey.

“Tell me first, Mr. Raven,” I said, “why, of all places, you have shut her up there! The night I ran from your house, it was immediately into that closet!”

“The closet,” he answered, “is no nearer our cottage, and no farther from it, than any or every other place.”

“But,” I returned, obstinate where I could not follow, “how is it then that, when you please, you take from that same door a whole book where I saw and felt only a part of one? The other part, you have just told me, stuck through into your library: when you put it again on the shelf, will it not again stick through into that? Must not then the two places, in which parts of the same volume can at the same moment exist, lie close together? Or can one part of the book be in space—or somewhere—and the other out of space—or nowhere?”

“I am sorry I cannot explain the thing to you,” he said. “There is no provision in you for understanding it. Not merely, therefore, is the thing inexplicable to you, but the very nature of it is inapprehensible by you. Indeed, I but partially apprehend it myself.

“At the same time you are constantly experiencing things which you not only do not, but cannot understand. You think you understand them; but your understanding of them is only your being used to them, and therefore not surprised at them. You accept them not because you understand them, but because you must accept them: they are there, and have unavoidable relations with you. The fact is, no man understands anything; when he knows he does not understand, that is his first tottering step—not toward understanding, but toward the capability of one day understanding.

“To such things as these you are not used; therefore you do not fancy you understand them. Neither I nor any man can here help you to understand; but I may, perhaps, help you a little to believe.”

He rose and went to the door of the closet. The house held its breath. He gave a low whistle and stood listening.

A moment after I heard—or seemed to hear—a soft whir of wings; and looking up, saw a white dove perch for an instant on the top of the shelves over the portrait, then drop to Mr. Raven’s shoulder and lay her head against his cheek. In that library, where every sound had its own temper and distance, this was a gentleness that altered the whole air, as if a clean wind had passed through old paper. Only by the motion of their heads could I tell that they were talking together; I heard nothing. Yet the very silence had a meaning, like a dialect understood by the ear of the heart and not by the ear of the head.

Neither had I moved my eyes from them, when suddenly she was not there; and Mr. Raven came back to his seat.

“Why did you whistle?” I asked. “Surely sound here is not sound there!”

“You are right,” he answered. “I whistled that you might know I called her. Not the whistle, but what the whistle meant reached her.—There is not a minute to lose: you must go!”

“I will at once!” I replied, and moved for the door.

“You will sleep to-night at my hostelry!” he said—not as a question, but with a mild authority that did not so much command as assume obedience.

“My heart is with the children,” I answered. “But if you insist—”

“I do insist. You can otherwise effect nothing.—I will go with you as far as the mirror, and see you off.”

He rose.

There came a sudden shock in the closet—wood jarred on wood with a thick, hurt sound, as if a great body had flung itself against the heavy door. I looked at my companion.

“Come; come!” he said.

Ere we reached the door of the library, a howling yell came after us, mingled with the noise of claws scoring at hard oak—an obscene music, like some cruel instrument forced to sing against its nature. I hesitated and half turned.

“To think of her lying there alone,” I murmured, “with that terrible wound!”

“Nothing will ever close that wound,” he answered, with a sigh. “It must eat into her heart! Annihilation itself is no death to evil. Only good where evil was, is evil dead. An evil thing must live with its evil until it chooses to be good. That alone is the slaying of evil.”

I held my peace until a sound I did not understand overtook us—something in the very air, as if the house had shifted its listening.

“If she should break loose!” I cried.

“Make haste!” he rejoined. “I shall hurry down the moment you are gone, and I have disarranged the mirrors.”

We ran and reached the wooden chamber breathless. Mr. Raven seized the chains and adjusted the hood with the care of one who has often handled tools that bite—fingers sure, movements economical, no waste, no tremor. Then he set the mirrors in their proper relation and came beside me in front of the standing one. Already I saw the mountain range emerging from the mist, as if another world were being uncovered like a dish from which the steam lifts slowly, revealing form and colour.

Between us—wedging us asunder—darted, with the yell of a demon, the huge bulk of the spotted leopardess. She leaped through the mirror as through an open window and settled at once into a low, even, swift gallop—so smooth it was like a machine made of muscle and malice.

I cast a look of dismay at my companion, and sprang through to follow her. He came after me leisurely.

“You need not run,” he called. “You cannot overtake her. This is our way.”

As he spoke he turned in the opposite direction.

“She has more magic at her finger-tips than I care to know!” he added quietly.

“We must do what we can!” I said, and ran on; but, sickening as I saw her dwindle in the distance, I stopped and went back to him.

“Doubtless we must,” he answered. “But my wife has warned Mara, and she will do her part. You must sleep first: you have given me your word!”

“Nor do I mean to break it. But surely sleep is not the first thing! Surely, surely, action takes precedence of repose!”

“A man can do nothing he is not fit to do.—See! did I not tell you Mara would do her part?”

I looked whither he pointed and saw a white spot moving at an acute angle with the line taken by the leopardess—silent speed crossing silent speed, like two sentences in different tongues converging upon the same meaning.

“There she is!” he cried. “The spotted leopardess is strong, but the white is stronger!”

“I have seen them fight,” I said. “The combat did not appear decisive as to that.”

“How should such eyes tell which have never slept? The princess did not confess herself beaten—that she never does—but she fled! When she confesses her last hope gone; when she knows it is indeed hard to kick against the goad; then will her day begin to dawn!

“Come; come! He who cannot act must make haste to sleep!”

I stood where I was and watched the last pallor of the white leopardess dissolve into the night, as if the air had swallowed her. Then I turned—reluctantly—to follow my guide.

What had I to do with sleep? In any world, I told myself, reason must keep one shape: and what reason could there be in lying down with the dead, when the hour was crying out for a living man? No one would wake me; and in that house the sleepers let morning slide into noon, and noon into night, without so much as the rustle of a sheet. I muttered against it as a child mutters at being sent to bed, and yet I followed—because I knew not what else to do.

The librarian went on in silence, and I went silent as he. Time and space, like a slow-poured broth, slipped past without edge or measure. The sun sank; the light thinned; the cold began to spread—an unseasoned chill, the kind that makes the mouth taste of tin and the skin remember the touch of stone. My heart sank with it. I began to lose sight of the lean figure in the long coat, and at last I could no longer hear even his swishing stride through the heather.

But then the soundscape altered, as if the world had moved its ear nearer. I heard, instead, the slow flap of raven-wings; and at intervals, a different small noise would rise into the rayless air—the dry flick of a firefly’s wing, the papery whisper of a gleaming butterfly that had no business in such dark. By and by the moon appeared, dragging herself up the far horizon like a pale loaf lifting in a cold oven.

“You are tired, are you not, Mr. Vane?” said the raven, alighting on a stone. His voice in that deadened night was not merely a word but a note—an insistence in the air, as if the world were made partly of hearing. “You must make acquaintance with the horse that will carry you in the morning!”

He gave a strange whistle through his long black beak—thin, sharp, and clean as a knife struck on a glass rim. A spot appeared on the face of the half-risen moon.

Then to my ears came the drumming of swift, soft-galloping hoofs: not loud at first, but certain, like a steady pestle in a deep mortar. In a minute or two, out of the very disc of the moon, low-thundered the terrible horse.

His mane flowed behind him like the crest of a wave fighting wind, torn seaward in hoary spray; his tail whisked so it seemed to blind the moon itself. Nineteen hands he looked—huge of bone, tight of skin, hard of muscle—a steed the holy Death might choose to ride abroad and reap. In that scary light he had the look of a skeleton loosely roped together; yet he moved with the lightness of a winged insect. As he drew near, his speed slackened, and his mane and tail—like steam over a hot cauldron—settled about him.

Now I was not merely a lover of horses: I loved every horse I saw. I had never spent money except upon horses, and had never sold a horse. The sight of this mighty one, terrible to look upon, woke in me a longing to possess him—pure greed, rank covetousness, an evil thing in all worlds. Not that I could have stolen him; but regardless of his place, I would have bought him if I could.

I laid my hands on him. Under my palms the hide was thin and smooth and shining as satin; the protuberant bones rose like the ridges of a starved hill-country under a stretched skin. The moon’s very shape lay reflected in his coat as in black-polished stone. I stroked those sharp-pointed ears as a careful cook strokes the edge of a blade he trusts; I whispered into them—words meant to soothe, the old stable-talk that belongs to a dialect of hands and breath. Then I bent and breathed into his red nostrils the warm, salted air of a man’s life.

He answered. He breathed into mine the breath of a horse’s life—grass-sweet, iron-warm, and edged with the clean pungency of work. In that exchange our liking leapt to love, quick as fire takes to fat. What eyes he had: blue-filmy like the eyes of the dead, and behind each film a coal glowed.

The raven, wings half spread, watched with what looked like pleasure.

“That is well! be friends with him,” he said. “He will carry you all the better to-morrow!—Now we must hurry home!”

My desire to ride the horse grew sudden and passionate, like hunger that flares when the first smell of roasting reaches the door.

“May I not mount him at once, Mr. Raven?” I cried.

“By all means!” he answered. “Mount, and ride him home.”

The horse bent his head over my shoulder lovingly. I twisted my hands in his mane and scrambled onto his back—not without aid from certain jutting bones that served me as steps.

“He would outspeed any leopard in creation!” I cried.

“Not that way at night,” answered the raven; “the road is difficult.—But come; loss now will be gain then! To wait is harder than to run, and its meed is the fuller. Go on, my son—straight to the cottage. I shall be there as soon as you. It will rejoice my wife’s heart to see son of hers on that horse!”

I sat silent. The horse stood like a block of marble, patient as an old beast in harness, yet with a storm under his skin.

“Why do you linger?” asked the raven.

“I long so much to ride after the leopardess,” I answered, “that I can scarce restrain myself!”

“You have promised!”

“My debt to the Little Ones appears, I confess, a greater thing than my bond to you.”

“Yield to the temptation and you will bring mischief upon them—and on yourself also.”

“What matters it for me? I love them; and love works no evil. I will go.”

But the truth—bitter on my tongue—was that I forgot the children, infatuate with the horse. The animal’s power rose into me like strong drink, and I took it for my own strength.

Eyes flashed through the darkness, and I knew that Adam stood in his own shape beside me. I knew also, by his voice, that he pressed down an indignation almost too strong for him, as one presses a boiling pot-lid to keep it from singing.

“Mr. Vane,” he said, “do you not know why you have not yet done anything worth doing?”

“Because I have been a fool,” I answered.

“Wherein?”

“In everything.”

“Which do you count your most indiscreet action?”

“Bringing the princess to life: I ought to have left her to her just fate.”

“Nay, now you talk foolishly! You could not have done otherwise than you did, not knowing she was evil!—But you never brought any one to life! How could you, yourself dead?”

“I dead?” I cried.

“Yes,” he answered; “and you will be dead, so long as you refuse to die.”

“Back to the old riddling!” I returned, scorn thick in my mouth, as if I had bitten burnt crust.

“Be persuaded, and go home with me,” he continued gently. “The most—nearly the only foolish thing you ever did, was to run from our dead.”

I pressed the horse’s ribs, and he was off like a sudden wind.

I gave him a pat on the side of the neck, and he went about in a sharp-driven curve, close to the ground like a cat that wheels scratchingly after a mouse, leaning so far his mane swept the heather-tops and combed them. Through the dark I heard the wings of the raven: five quick flaps—and he perched upon the horse’s head.

The horse checked himself instantly, ploughing up the ground with his feet; the sound tore the night like cloth.

“Mr. Vane,” croaked the raven, “think what you are doing! Twice already has evil befallen you—once from fear, and once from heedlessness: breach of word is far worse; it is a crime.”

“The Little Ones are in frightful peril, and I brought it upon them!” I cried. “—But indeed I will not break my word to you. I will return, and spend in your house what nights—what days—what years you please.”

“I tell you once more you will do them other than good if you go to-night,” he insisted.

But a false sense of power—rootless, merely vibrated into me from the strength of the horse—had rendered me too stupid to take in the shape of his counsel. The horse’s great body under me was like a drum; it beat arrogance into my bones.

“Would you take from me my last chance of reparation?” I cried. “This time there shall be no shirking! It is my duty, and I will go—if I perish for it!”

“Go, then, foolish boy!” he returned, anger rasping in his croak. “Take the horse, and ride to failure! May it be to humility!”

He spread his wings and flew, and the air, suddenly without him, seemed emptier, as if some needed frequency had dropped out of the world’s music.

Again I pressed the lean ribs under me.

“After the spotted leopardess!” I whispered in the horse’s ear.

He turned his head this way and that, snuffing the air; then started, and went a few paces in a slow, undecided walk—like an old beast choosing between familiar paths. Suddenly he quickened; broke into a trot; began to gallop; and in a few moments his speed was tremendous.

He seemed to see in the dark. He never stumbled—not once faltered, not once hesitated. I sat as on the ridge of a wave, lifted and driven. Beneath me I felt the play of each individual muscle: his joints were so elastic, and each movement flowed into the next, that not once did he jar me. His growing swiftness bore him along until he flew rather than ran. The wind met and passed us like a tornado, stripping sound from the ground and leaving only the hammering of hoofs and the rush of air in the nostrils.

Across the evil hollow we sped like a bolt from an arblast. No monster lifted its neck; all knew the hoofs that thundered over their heads, and the night held its breath. We rushed up the hills, we shot down their farther slopes; from the rocky chasms of the river-bed he did not swerve; he held on over them his fierce, terrible gallop.

The moon, half-way up the heaven, gazed with a solemn trouble in her pale countenance. Rejoicing in the power of my steed and in the pride of my life, I sat like a king and rode.

We were near the middle of the many channels, my horse every other moment clearing one, sometimes two in his stride, and now and then gathering himself for a great bounding leap, when the moon reached the key-stone of her arch.

Then came a wonder and a terror: she began to descend, rolling like the nave of Fortune’s wheel bowled by the gods, and went faster and faster. Like our own moon, this one had a human face, and now the broad forehead, now the chin, was uppermost as she rolled. I gazed aghast.

Across the ravines came the howling of wolves. The sound ran along the ground like a knife along a plate, setting my teeth on edge. An ugly fear began to invade the hollow places of my heart; my confidence thinned, as a broth thins when water is poured where marrow should be. The horse maintained his headlong swiftness, ears pricked forward, nostrils thirsty and exulting in the wind his own career made.

But there was the moon jolting like an old chariot-wheel down the hill of heaven, with awful boding in every turn. At last she rolled over the horizon-edge and disappeared, carrying all her light with her.

The mighty steed was in the act of clearing a wide shallow channel when we were caught in the net of the darkness. His head dropped as if the string that held him up had been cut. The impetus carried his helpless bulk across, but he fell in a heap on the margin; and where he fell he lay.

I slid down and got up shaking. I kneeled beside him and felt him all over, hands travelling his limbs and ribs with the careful urgency of one who searches a stored pantry after a quake—touching, counting, hoping to find nothing spilt. Not a bone could I find broken.

But he was a horse no more.

I sat down on the body, and buried my face in my hands, while the night—without moon, without raven—closed in, and all that could be heard was the far, hungry argument of wolves and the loud silence of my own folly.

The cold thickened as night drew itself tighter about me. The dead horse beneath my hands had already turned from warmth to board and stone; even the stink of him seemed to freeze in the air, as if scent itself could be seized and held. In the dark I could hear the land at work: pebble ticking under soft pads, the quick rasp of hungry breath, and then—threaded through it all—the wolves’ voices, a chorus tightening its circle.

Their eyes came first, green sparks hung low among the rocks. The half-moon of them narrowed. I tried to rise with some show of steadiness, but my limbs were wooden, and I had not so much as a stick—no staff, no branch, not even a stone big enough to swing with any hope. They rushed.

For an instant they checked at the horse, as if weighing carrion against living flesh; then the line bent toward me and the throats opened. I stood, a foolish upright morsel, waiting for the weight of them.

A sound like swiftness itself—almost silence, yet edged with a thin, fierce cry—slashed in from their flank. Another ring of eyes, brighter and nearer, fell upon the wolves. Bodies hit bodies. Teeth snapped. Claws tore. The wolves’ howl broke into snarls that rose and failed, and through the feeble-yet-fierce caterwauling I knew the new-comers: cats. They came as if poured out of darkness, led by a great grey one I could see only in his eyes—and yet, by those eyes, knew his bulk and colour as if he stood in noon-day.

I could not see the battle; I heard it. The night became a kind of music—no melody, only the harsh ecology of hunting, every sound finding its place: the scraping rush, the short scream, the thud of bodies on stone. For a moment I thought to run—then knew the first step would likely be a fall, and in such blackness both factions could read me by scent.

All at once the wolves’ voices ceased. The cats’ uproar swelled, wild and victorious. Soft padding followed—flight, not pursuit. The cats had beaten the wolves.

My relief lasted a breath.

Teeth—needle-sharp—closed on my legs. Then another mouth. Then a dozen. A living cataract of fur and fury climbed me; claws found purchase everywhere, pincers of curved keratin hauling at cloth and skin. They hissed hot into my face without biting it—until, in despair, I flung myself down, and the instant I did, they went straight for my features. I sprang up, and they abandoned my face as if it were a game, returning to my legs with renewed zeal.

I fought them with hands made savage by pain. I tore them away, strangled at them, crushed them under heel, jabbed at eyes—yet nothing loosened; when I tried to fling one off, it clung to my fingers like a limpet. They kept finding fresh space for fresh bites, as if my body were a board continually set with new morsels. Their assault had rhythm: when I ran, they surrounded me like a torrent and tormented me no more; when I fell—and I fell often—they waited with an uncanny patience; but if I slackened from fear of falling, they flew at my legs again, driving me onward.

So I ran through that miserable night, cleaving the solid dark, herded by silent paws and occasional pain. Yet even in persecution there was a strange order: the way underfoot was comparatively smooth; I pitched into no gully. We passed the Evil Wood without my seeing it, and left it behind as one leaves a noisome kitchen—by thrusting the door to and trusting the latch.

When morning thinned the darkness to a grey that showed the world again, I found myself beyond the channels and on the edge of the orchard valley. Joy lifted me so suddenly I would have made friends with my tormentors—but not a cat was anywhere to be seen. As if the night had only lent them to the task.

I threw myself on moss and slept at once.

A kick woke me.

I opened my eyes to daylight and bondage: hands tied, feet tied—my whole self parcelled like a bag for carrying. The giants stood over me.

“What fitter?” I said in my own mind. “To whom else should I belong?” And I laughed, not merry but sick with self-disgust, triumphant in the ugliness of it.

Another kick ended that false laughter. With their help—each “help” arriving in the shape of a boot—I got upright. There were six of them. They untied the rope that bound my legs together only to fasten a rope to each ankle, like leading-strings for a reluctant animal, and dragged me away. When they pulled both ropes at once I went down; when I lay there, they kicked me up again.

They took me straight back to my old labour. They tied my leg-ropes to a tree, undid my arms, and thrust the hateful flint into my left hand. Then they flung themselves down at ease and began to pelt me with fallen fruit and stones. Most missed; a few struck with the dull, bruising honesty of thrown things.

If I could have freed my legs and seized a stick lying a couple of yards away, I might have rushed the six of them in a madness of hunger and rage. But I looked past the stick, past the giants, into the oncoming night as one looks toward the hearth when the day has been long.

“The Little Ones will come at night,” I said to myself, and that hope fed me better than the fruit I had no chance to eat.

All day I worked. The flint’s edge—always too eager—bit the rind and stem; sap and bruised sweetness rose and mingled with the iron scent of my own scraped skin. The orchard had its own soundscape: leaves whispering like many soft tongues, branches tapping together, the occasional drop of a heavy fruit landing with a thick, domestic thud. Against that, the giants’ presence was a kind of industrial noise—blunt, intrusive, without listening—kicks and grunts that drowned the finer speech of the place.

When darkness came they tied my hands again and left me fast to the tree. I slept in pieces. Each time I woke it was from a dream of lying in the heart of a heap of children—small bodies warm as loaves just drawn from an oven, the smell of milk-breath and crushed herbs, a language of laughter around me that no grown tongue could ever translate without losing its life.

With morning my enemies returned—kicks first, then their bestial company. By noon my strength was running out through hunger and labour, and I had begun to feel as if I were hollow inside, a man made of rind.

Then the brushwood burst into commotion, and over it rang the bell-like laughter that had become dearer to me than any promise.

I cried out—delight, welcome, relief in one voice.

Answering my shout came a whole storm of noises: trumpeting as of baby-elephants, neighing as of foals, bellowing as of calves—but it was the riders making the clamour, not the animals. Through the bushes poured the Little Ones: some on diminutive horses, some on small elephants, some astride little bears. Beside the mounted troop walked older boys and girls, and among them a woman carrying a baby that crowed as if the day were a cup and it had discovered sweetness in it.

The giants heaved themselves up, slow as ill machinery. But the children moved like a practiced kitchen brigade: every small body knew its place, every hand its task. A storm of sharp stones greeted the giants. The little horses charged their legs like determined rams. The bears rose and hugged at their waists, pinning them in awkward helplessness. The elephants looped trunks around thick necks, dragged them down, and trampled them with the very lesson the giants had taught—only now received instead of given.

My ropes were undone in an instant. Arms—seemingly innumerable—closed around me. I ceased to see the giants at all.

They made me sit. Lona came close, her face intent, and without a word began feeding me red and yellow fruits—lovely as if the orchard had been saving its best for this moment. The skins gave beneath her fingers; the flesh broke with a clean, wet sigh; juice ran cold and sharp-sweet over my tongue. Hunger is a brute that speaks one language; yet even while I ate I could not help listening to the orchard’s gentler sounds returning as fear withdrew—the birds tentative, the leaves resuming their whisper, the ground no longer jarred by heavy feet.

The whole colony mounted guard until I had eaten my fill.

Then they brought two of their largest elephants and placed them side by side. They hooked their trunks together and tied their tails, arranging the great bodies parallel. The elephants could have undone the knots with a shake, could have unhooked trunks by forgetting the task; but they did neither. It was plain they understood: they held stillness as one holds a bowl steady for pouring.

I rose and laid myself in the hollow between their backs. As my weight tried to press them apart, they leaned into each other and made a living litter, firm and warm. My feet projected beyond their tails, but my head lay pillowed on an ear of each—soft, leathery, faintly dusty with the day’s travel.

Some of the smaller children mounted along the back of each bearer as a bodyguard. The rest formed a train. And so the procession began to move.

Where they carried me I did not try to guess. I yielded myself to their pleasure, almost as happy as they were. At first they chattered and laughed and played a thousand glad tricks, their voices stitching the air into a fabric that wrapped me better than any blanket. But the moment they saw I was going to sleep, they fell silent—still as judges, as if they understood the ecology of rest: too much noise can wound the mind like bright light wounds the eye.

When I woke, a sudden musical uproar greeted the opening of my eyes. We were passing through the forest where they found the babies—the forest I had suspected must stretch all the way from the valley to the hot stream. The light was different here: filtered, green-gold, like sun through a bottle.

A tiny girl sat with her little feet close to my face, looking down at me coaxingly, as if she meant to draw me into a secret. For a while she only watched me; the others seemed to hang upon her waiting.

“We make a petisson to king,” she said.

“What is it, my darling?” I asked.

“Shut eyes one minute,” she answered.

“Certainly I will! Here goes!” I said, and shut my eyes tight.

“No, no! not fore I tell oo!” she cried, scandalized.

I opened them again, and we talked and laughed for quite another hour, as if time had gone circular and returned to childhood, where promises are renewed by mere being together.

At last she said suddenly, “Close eyes!”

I closed my eyes and kept them closed.

The elephants stopped.

I heard a soft scurry, a little rustle—then a silence so complete it became audible. In that world, some silences are heard: they have edges; they ring like a bowl that has just been struck and now holds its note without sound.

“Open eyes!” shouted twenty voices from a little way off.

I obeyed.

Not a creature was visible except the elephants that bore me.

I knew the children were marvellously quick at getting out of the way—no doubt the giants had taught them, by cruelty, the art of vanishing. But when I raised myself and searched the open, shrubless forest and could descry neither hand nor heel, I stared in blank astonishment.

The sun had set; dusk gathered fast. And presently a multitude of birds began to sing.

I lay down to listen, pretty sure that if I left the hiders alone they would soon come out. The singing grew until it was a little storm of bird-voices, layered and bright, as if the forest were repairing something in the spirit with sound. I found myself thinking how different such music was from the hard noise of the giants—how it did not demand attention like a shout, but offered it like food set on a table, to be taken according to hunger.

“Surely the children must have something to do with it!” I thought. “And yet how could they set the birds singing?”

Then, happening to look up into the tree under which my elephants stood, I thought I saw motion among the leaves. I looked more keenly. Sudden white spots appeared in the dark foliage. The music died down. A gale of childish laughter rippled the air. And white spots came out everywhere.

The trees were full of children.

In wild merriment they began to descend, some dropping from bough to bough so rapidly I could scarce believe they had not fallen. I slid from my litter, and instantly I was surrounded—target for all the artillery of their joy. With stately composure the elephants walked away to bed, as if the day’s work were done and their own domestic routines called them.

“But,” I said when their uproar had run far enough to empty itself a little, “how is it that I never before heard you sing like the birds? Even when I thought it must be you, I could hardly believe it!”

“Ah,” said one of the wildest, “but we were not birds then! We were run-creatures, not fly-creatures! We had our hide-places in the bushes then; but when we came to no-bushes, only trees, we had to build nests! When we built nests, we grew birds, and when we were birds, we had to do birds! We asked them to teach us their noises, and they taught us, and now we are real birds!—Come and see my nest. It’s not big enough for king, but it’s big enough for king to see me in it!”

I told him I could not get up a tree without the sun to show me the way; when it came, I would try.

“Kings seldom have wings!” I added.

“King! king!” cried one. “Oo knows none of us hasn’t no wings—foolis feddery tings! Arms and legs is better.”

“That is true,” I said. “I can get up without wings—and carry straws in my mouth too, to build my nest with!”

“Oo knows!” he answered, and went away sucking his thumb.

A moment after I heard him calling from high up in a walnut tree of enormous size, his voice small as a remembered word in a dying dialect: “Up adain, king! Dood night! I seepy!”

And I heard no more of him until he woke me in the morning.

I lay down with my back against the rough flank of a tree whose bark smelled of rain and resin, and watched the day fold itself away. One by one, and in twos and threes, the children slipped from the grass like mice from a hearth-stone and climbed to their nests. Their bare feet made soft, brief sounds—thup of heel on bole, the faint rasp of fingers on lichen—until each small body was swallowed by leaf and shadow.

They were always like that: spent to the bone at nightfall, and at dawn as if poured newly into themselves. Sleep and waking were for them two doors in one house, both opening easily. I was tired too; my limbs had the dulled ache of healing, and my mouth still held the memory of fear like bitter herb. Yet I lay wakeful. Lona had not told me good night; and I knew, with the quiet certainty a hungry man has of bread that is on its way, that she would come.

When first I saw her again, the likeness struck me as sudden as a scent carried on wind. The princess’s face was there—those lines the moon loved—yet in Lona the dazzling edge was blunted into gentleness, and the beauty deepened as water deepens where it runs slower: with childlikeness, and with a mother’s gravity. She looked young, but not untried. How such a child should have been born of such a mother was hard to take in; and yet, happily, she had two parents—say rather three. Whatever had been broken in the blood had been mended somewhere else.

“She is occupied,” I told myself, listening to the forest’s small night-work—the creak of branch, the far cough of some beast, the whispering insects—“with the child of the woman I met fleeing.” Lona had already said the woman was not half mother enough.

At last Lona came. She sat beside me on the roots, and for a little while said nothing. Her delight was in her hands: she stroked my face and the backs of my fingers as if she were calming a dough that had risen too fast, easing it down into obedience. Her touch had the same certainty a good cook has with fire: she knew where pain would flare if she pressed, and where it would quiet if she held.

Then, as the moon lifted herself and began to look through the leaves, lighting Lona’s face in quivering patches, she began to tell me all that had befallen since I left. The moon made her features now silver, now leaf-shadowed green; thought lay in her brow, and care too—yet love redeemed and glorified even that, as salt turns plain food into something that belongs to a table.

After I left the orchard valley, she said, the giants began to believe a little more in the actual existence of their neighbours. The belief did not soften them; it sharpened them. Sometimes the Little Ones would see the huge brutes trampling furiously, perceiving—or imagining—some sign of their presence, while the children stood close by, unseen and safe, and laughed at the blind rage of it. In time, though, the giants’ spite took on a more useful shape: they began to destroy the trees.

They were not clever in malice, but they were strong. A giant would go at a trunk with his clubbing hands until the bark split and the sap bled. Sometimes they tore down branches heavy with fruit and crushed them underfoot, wasting what they could not eat. The orchard’s smells—apple-sweetness, pear perfume, the faint wine-breath of figs—were trampled into mud. The children lived on that fruit; it was their pantry, their market, their morning. To have it spoiled was like having the language of their hunger erased.

This drove the mother of them all—Lona spoke of her so, with the reverence one gives to an elder who knows seed-times and weather—to meditate counteraction. She set the sharpest of the children to listening at night. They were small, but their ears were keen; they could hear the difference between a squirrel’s scuttle and a wolf’s padding, between a branch complaining and a foot intending. And what they heard was this: the giants thought I was hidden somewhere near, meaning, when my strength returned, to come in the dark and kill them as they slept.

So the mother concluded the only way to stop the destruction was to give the giants ground for thinking the Little Ones had abandoned the place. They must remove into the forest—beyond the range of the giants, but within reach of their own trees, which they could visit by night. The plan’s objection was plain: the forest had little undergrowth to hide them. But she reflected that where birds made homes, the Little Ones could learn to live.

“They have eager sympathies,” Lona said softly, as if repeating a phrase she loved. “They can learn of the wildest creatures. Why not take refuge in the tree-tops? Why not, having lain once in low brushwood, seek now the lofty foliage?”

They had watched birds build. They had climbed trees for play. The forest trees were large, and did not look unkind. They went up nearer the sky than the orchard trees of the giants, and spread their arms—some even bent them down—as if inviting habitation. The children made a story of it at once, as children do: perhaps, in the top of the tallest tree, they might find the bird that laid the baby-eggs, sat on them till they were ripe, and then tumbled them down so the little ones could come out. In their mouths, the tale tasted true; in their minds, it was a promise.

Yes, they would build sleep-houses in the branches, where no giant would see, for never by any chance did a giant throw back his dull head to look up. The giants were people of ground and glare; sky made them squint. Then the bad giants would believe the Little Ones gone, and the children would gather apples and pears and figs and mesples and peaches while the giants slept, storing the orchard’s sweetness as carefully as a thrifty household puts by winter jars.

Thus reasoned the Lovers—for so, Lona said, they called themselves, and it was the most accurate name they had—and they adopted her suggestion eagerly. The result was that they became as much at home in the tree-tops as birds, and the giants, ere long, concluded they had frightened them out of the country. Then the giants forgot their trees and almost ceased again to believe in their small neighbours.

“Did you not observe,” Lona asked, “that many of the children are grown?”

I had not. The change had crept like yeast through dough: silent, patient, and suddenly there. But I could believe it easily.

“It is so,” she said. “And their minds have grown too. That has helped me not to fear it as much as I did.”

In the last of the short twilight—and later, when the moon shone—they would go down to the valley and gather fruit enough to serve for the next day. The giants never went out in twilight: to them it was darkness. They hated the moon; had they been able, they would have extinguished her, as a crude man snuffs a candle because it shows him his own ugliness.

But the children soon found that fruit gathered in the night was not altogether good the next day. It held the night’s chill too long. The cut stems wept and the sweetness dulled; bruised peaches turned faintly bitter by morning, and figs, once opened, lost the clean, green scent that makes them feel like a secret. There is a music to fresh food—crispness is a sound as well as a taste—and by midday that music was muted.

So the question rose among them, like hunger itself: might it not be better, instead of pretending to have left, to make the bad giants leave?

By then, exploring the forest, they had made acquaintance with the animals, and with most of them personally. Lona told it with a sort of quiet amusement: how strong as well as wise and docile some were, how swift and manageable others. The children, with their love-first way of speaking, addressed them at once as Brother or Sister—Brother Elephant, Sister Horse—until before long each creature had an individual name. It took longer before they said Brother Bear, but that too came; and the other day, Lona had heard one little fellow cry, “Ah, Sister Serpent!” to a snake that bit him because he played with it too roughly.

Most of them would have nothing to do with a caterpillar except watch it through its changes; but when at last it came from its retirement with wings, all would address it as Sister Butterfly, congratulating it on its metamorphosis with a word that meant something like repentance—turning, returning, becoming otherwise. They treated it as sacred, as if the smallest creature carried in its body the whole sermon of change, though they could not translate it into any other speech than wonder.

One moonlit evening, as they went to gather fruit, they came upon a woman seated on the ground with a baby in her lap—the woman I had met on my way to Bulika. The children took her for a giantess who had stolen one of their babies; for they regarded all babies as theirs, as naturally as they regarded apples as belonging to the tree until gathered. Filled with anger, they fell upon her in a multitude, beating her in a childish manner that was more confusing than cruel. She would have fled, but a boy threw himself down and held her by the feet.

Then, recovering her wits, she recognised them—the very children whose hospitality she had sought—and at once yielded the baby. Lona appeared and carried the child away in her bosom, as one carries warm bread to keep it from cooling.

The woman noted something that changed her mind: in striking her, the children had been careful not to hurt the baby; and as she surrendered her, she hugged and kissed the child just as they themselves longed to do. That sight softened them. They concluded she must be a giantess of the same kind as the good giant. The moment Lona had the baby, they brought the mother fruit—apples that snapped when bitten, pears with the smell of honeyed grass, figs that bled syrup at the stem—and began to show her every sort of childish attention, as if feeding her would prove their pardon.

Now the woman, Lona said, had been in perplexity where to go. She dared not return to Bulika, because the princess would certainly learn who had lamed her leopardess. The friendliness of the little people delighted her; she resolved to remain with them for the present. She would have no trouble with her infant—no crying to hush alone, no milk to coax when fear had dried her—and perhaps she might find a way back to her husband, who was rich in money and gems, and very seldom unkind.

Here, from Lona’s words and my own conjecture, I understood more of the woman. In Bulika, as among many peoples, there was a tradition: the princess lived in terror of the birth of an infant destined to her destruction. The Bulikans knew the story but not the princess’s means of holding on to youth and beauty; they did not know the price she paid in secret. Yet as her body deteriorated and required more of those frightful means, they took the apparent increase of her hostility to children for a sign she felt her doom near. No one dreamed of attempting anything against her, but the rumour nourished a hope of change as famine nourishes a dream of harvest.

In the woman’s mind rose an idea: to further the fulfilment of the shadowy prediction—or at least to use the myth for her own restoration to her husband. What seemed more likely than that fate lay with these very children? They were marvellously brave; the Bulikans were cowards, in abject terror of animals. If she could rouse the Little Ones to the ambition of taking the city, then, in the confusion, she might slip away from their small army, reach her house unrecognised, and there, hidden, await the result.

And should the children first succeed in expelling the giants, she could begin at once—while they were still flushed with victory—to suggest a loftier aim.

By disposition, indeed, the children were unfit for war. They hardly ever quarrelled, never fought. They loved every live thing, and hated either to hurt or to suffer. Still, they were easily influenced, and could certainly be taught any exercise within their strength. So the woman began at once with what looked like play: she set some of the smaller ones throwing stones at a mark. Soon all were engrossed with the new game and growing skilful. The first practical result was their use of stones in my rescue.

While gathering fruit, they found me asleep. They went home, held a council, and came the next day with elephants and horses. They overwhelmed the few giants watching me and carried me off. Lona’s voice altered as she told it—tender, proud, troubled all at once. Jubilant over victory, the smaller boys were childishly boastful, the bigger boys less ostentatious; the girls’ eyes flashed more than usual, but they spoke less. The woman of Bulika, no doubt, felt encouraged.

We talked the greater part of the night, chiefly of the children’s growth and what it might mean. I had long known Lona’s power of recognising truth; now I found myself astonished at her practical wisdom. Perhaps, had I been more of a child, I should have wondered less. Wisdom in that land did not always come by age; sometimes it came by listening—truly listening—to what life said in its own dialects: the crackle of leaf, the hush that warns of an enemy, the way a frightened baby breathes.

It was far from morning when I became aware of a slight fluttering and scrambling. I rose on my elbow. Through the moon-dappled gloom I saw many Little Ones descend from their nests. Their bodies made small noises against bark, like the whisper of a broom on a kitchen floor. They disappeared. In a few minutes all was still again.

“What are they doing?” I asked.

“They think,” Lona answered, “that, stupid as they are, the giants will search the wood. They are gone to gather stones with which to receive them. Stones are not plentiful in the forest, and they must scatter far to find enough. They will carry them to their nests, and from the trees attack as the giants come within reach.”

She spoke as one describing preparations for baking: you must gather fuel, you must fetch water, you must grind. War, in her mouth, sounded like household labour undertaken unwillingly for the sake of the children’s table.

“Knowing their habits, they do not expect them before morning. If they come, it will be the opening of a war of expulsion: one people or the other must go. The result is hardly doubtful.”

“We do not mean to kill them,” she added. “Indeed, their skulls are so thick I do not think we could. Not that killing would do them much harm; they are so little alive. If one were killed, his giantess would not remember him beyond three days.”

“Do the children throw so well that it might happen?” I asked.

“Wait till you see,” she said, and there was in the phrase a touch of mother-pride, like a cook’s satisfaction in a knife well honed. “But I have not yet told you of a strange thing that happened the night before last.”

We had come home from fruit-gathering, she said, and slept in our nests, when horrid noises of beasts fighting roused us. The moon was bright. In a moment our trees glittered with staring little eyes as the children peered down. Two huge leopardesses were below: one perfectly white, the other covered in black spots. They worried and tore each other with a fury that made the night’s air itself seem shredded. To judge by her back, the spotted creature must have been climbing when the other sprang upon her. When Lona first saw them, they were just under her own tree, rolling over and over in the moonlight, their bodies slick with blood.

At first the children enjoyed the spectacle, taking sides as if it were a game, for they had never seen such beasts and thought they played. But by degrees their roaring and growling almost ceased, and Lona saw they were in deadly earnest. She found herself wishing neither would be left able to climb a tree.

Then, when the children saw blood pouring from flanks and throats, what did they do? They scurried down to comfort them. Gathering in a great crowd, they began to pat and stroke the terrible creatures, speaking to them as they spoke to all living things, as if a name could be a salve.

Lona climbed down too, calling to them, but they were too absorbed to heed. Before she could reach them, the white leopardess stopped fighting and sprang among the children with a hideous yell. The children flew up into the trees like birds. Before Lona could regain her branch, the beasts were at it again.

Then Whitey—so Lona called her—had the best of it. Spotty ran away as fast as she could. Whitey came and lay down at the foot of Lona’s tree. A minute or two later she was up again, walking about as if she suspected the spotted one lurked near. Lona woke often, and every time she looked out, she saw the white leopardess keeping watch. In the morning, she went away.

“I know both the beasts,” I said. “Spotty is a bad beast. She hates the children and would kill every one of them. But Whitey loves them. She ran at them only to frighten them away, lest Spotty should seize one. No one need be afraid of Whitey.”

By this time the Little Ones were returning with their stones. They came with much noise, for they no longer cared to keep quiet now that they counted themselves at open war with the giants. Each carried what he could: round river-stones found in some distant bed, angular fragments prised from roots, hard lumps that would fly true. They mounted to their nests with difficulty under the burdens, like small people hauling sacks of grain up to a loft. Once settled, as if all that labour had been only a kind of prayer, they slept at once.

Lona retired to her tree. I lay where I was and slept the better for thinking the white leopardess might still be somewhere in the wood, a silent guardian in the dark acoustics of branches.

I woke soon after sunrise and lay pondering. The day’s sounds came gradually: a far call, a nearer flutter, the thin crack of a twig under some small foot. Two hours passed. Then, in truth, the giants began to appear.

They came in straggling companies of three and four, until I counted over a hundred—great loutish shapes moving between trunks, making noise enough for a world that had forgotten how to listen. The children were still asleep. To call them would draw the giants’ attention; I kept quiet as long as they did not discover me.

But one came blundering upon me. He stumbled, fell, rose again, and began to search about with his dull eyes and duller mind.

I sprang to my feet and struck him in the middle of his huge body. The roar he gave tore the morning. It roused the children like a bell.

At once a storm—hail made of stone—fell from the trees. Not a single stone struck me; not one missed the giant. He fell and lay.

Others drew near, and the storm extended. Each purblind creature, entering the range of a garrisoned tree, became a target for converging stones. The air clacked and cracked with impacts; the forest took on a harsh, percussive music, and then, as giant after giant went down, the rhythm shifted: the children’s throwing became almost like drumming, a fierce defense played on the skulls of those who would not look up.

In a short time almost every giant was prostrate. Then from the tops of fifty trees rose a jubilant paean of bird-song. The children sang as birds sing after a hawk has passed—sound returning to its proper place, the soundscape cleansed of trampling.

Many elephants came hurrying up at their call. The children descended the trees like monkeys; in a moment every elephant carried three or four of them on his back. Thus loaded, the elephants began to walk over the giants, who lay and roared.

The giants’ noise went on and on—gross complaint without meaning. Losing patience with it, the elephants gave them a few blows with their trunks, not to kill but to silence, as one raps a barrel that will not stop booming. Then they left them.

Until night the bad giants remained where they had fallen, silent and motionless at last, as if the forest itself had swallowed their voices.

In the morning they were gone—every one. The children saw no more of them. They removed to the other end of the orchard valley and never after ventured into the forest.

Victory once won, the woman of Bulika let her tongue run as if it had been waiting years for air. By the fire, while Lona tore leaves for bedding and the older girls salted down berries in the little gourds, she talked of the city—how its gates were slack, its walls neglected, its streets full of fear; how the princess was wicked as a blight in seed-time, and how the men of Bulika made a show of stature only to stoop when courage was asked of them.

The words went from mouth to mouth until even the smallest, with sticky fingers and cheeks stained with fruit, chattered “Bulika, Bulika,” as if it were the name of a game. They had no more notion of a city than a chick of the far side of its shell; to them the world was wood and clearing, firelight and sleeping heaps, the smell of crushed fern and warm milk. Yet the name took root, and with it the shape of a desire.

It was then I first saw the woman’s design, though the why of it was still hidden from me like a taste you know in the steam but cannot name on the tongue. To take the place—to go in with our strange troop and lay hold of Bulika—began to seem not merely possible but right. Lona’s eyes had a steady kindling when she listened, as if every word about the city found in her an answering memory. And I, I confess it: I let myself build on it. The Little Ones were growing—daily, hourly—quickening in wit and hand; I could no longer pretend they were helpless. The terrible Lilith—woman or leopardess—had one bare place in her armour, and I knew it: her doom ran through her daughter, and the old prophecy lay on Bulika like a charm half-believed, half-dreaded. The risk, if it was risk, seemed worth the taking.

In my heart I made pictures, and not all of them pure. Successful—how could they fail?—might not this crowd of children become in a breath a young people, and their very innocence be a rod of iron to the wicked? Might they not redeem a nation by being incapable of its cowardice? And with that thought came another, more selfish, sliding in like a spice too strong: that it was just Lona should sit where her mother had sat; that it was natural she should make of me her consort and minister. I even dreamed—shame on me—of opening trade in gems between this world and my own; as if the hunger of one world could ever feed the other without poisoning both.

Adam’s appeal returned to me too—water, the need of it, the mystery of it. I suggested to Lona that if we found them water it might hasten the growth of the Little Ones, as broth strengthens a child where dry crust cannot.

She listened, then shook her head with that quiet firmness which never bruised yet never yielded.

“They are what they are without it,” she said. “If we bring water now, we do not know what it will wake in them. It will make a new need. When we have the city, we will search for water.”

So we left that door unopened, and turned to what we could do with hands and time.

We prepared as people prepare for harvest and for war in the same season: with lists and laughter, with quarrels mended before nightfall, with every small task made part of a greater one. Lona gave herself chiefly to the commissariat, and I, by her order and my own bent, to the drilling. Under the trees I had them form and re-form, break and rally, the hardest part being to get them to come to the flag at the first sound—because each of them, by nature, was a whole little kingdom and did not understand at first the beauty of moving as one body. Most had slings, braided from fibres and softened by long handling; some of the bigger boys had bows and arrows that they had learned to make and mend with the obsession of young craftsmen. The bigger girls carried aloe-spikes—needles of living steel hardened by the sun, fixed into shafts—wickedly sharp, honest weapons that did not lie about what they could do. Their one command was to charge themselves with the too-small-to-fight, and to keep them alive by any means, fierce or tender.

Lona herself had grown, though she seemed not to notice it—she had always been, and remained, the tallest. Her hair fell longer now, heavy as ripened grain; she had become almost a woman, yet not one beauty of childhood had she lost, only gathered them into a new unity. When first we met again after the long separation, she set down her infant, put her arms around my neck, and clung to me without speech, her face lit from within as if the heart were a lamp. The child whimpered; she sprang to him instantly, and he was in her bosom before the sound had fully formed.

To see her with a little one who was obstinate or thoughtless, quick-tempered or sulky from hunger, was to see what a tender grandmother might be if she had the strength of youth too—patient, practical, never sentimental in a way that abandoned the body. I hardly remembered my own mother, yet in my mind’s eye she began to look like Lona; and if I imagined sister or child, it was Lona’s face that would come. My thoughts flew to her as birds to a familiar hedge. She hardly ever sought me, yet she was almost always within sound of my voice; and everything I did or planned I measured by her, not as a judge but as the hearth measures the whole house. She worked in an independence so complete it did not separate her from me; it only made being near her feel like being near a clear stream that does not ask permission to run.

She never neglected the smallest child for my sake; and my love, instead of resenting it, sharpened my duty. To love her and to do what must be done were not one thing, but they could not be pulled apart.

She did not overflow upon me with caresses; it was a closeness of recognition, like the devotion of a faithful animal—divine, not because it spoke great words, but because it never betrayed its nature. And because that nature was humble and true, it humbled me. I never spoke to her of her mother.

The wood in those days was full of birds. Their plumage had a splendour that seemed to pay in colour for the lack of flowers—flowers, it appeared, would not grow without water—yet their song lost nothing for all that brightness. The forest had its own acoustic law: leaf, wing, throat, and distance arranging themselves into a music that did not clatter or grind or drown the small sounds. I had known, in my own world, the hard iron shriek of wheels, the ceaseless commercial noise that pretends to be life; here the very silence had joints, and each bird note sat in its place like a well-chosen herb in broth.

Seeing their dropped feathers everywhere—caught in bark, laid on moss, lying in the little hollows where dew gathered—I began to gather them. It came into my heart, as naturally as putting a blanket on a child, to make Lona a garment. I sorted by length and strength, laid them in overlapping rows as one lays fish-scales or shingles against rain, binding them with fibres softened between my teeth and fingers. The work had the satisfaction of cookery: choosing, cleaning, arranging; the pleasure of making something that would touch the skin kindly and keep off wind.

Lona watched me, her head a little on one side, plainly pleased with my choice and the way the colours lay—never asking what it would become, as if she believed the act itself was enough until it declared its own end.

In a week or two it was finished: a long loose mantle, fastening at throat and waist, open for the arms. I rose and put it on her.

She rose, took it off, and laid it at my feet—out of a modesty that would not let her accept a splendour without first refusing it.

I put it again on her shoulders, showed her where her arms should go through. She smiled, stroked the feathers once or twice as if tasting them with her palm, then took it off again and laid it down, this time by her side.

When she left me she carried it away. For days I saw nothing of it, and thought perhaps she had hidden it as women hide things too dear to use.

Then one morning she came wearing it, and carrying another garment she had fashioned while I thought her busy only with children and stores. It was made of dried leaves of a tough evergreen—worked, pressed, and bound until it had the strength almost of leather and the look of scale-armour. It smelled of sun and sap, a clean resinous scent like a granary newly swept.

I put it on at once. From that day we always wore those garments when on horseback.

For, on the outskirts of the forest, there appeared one day a troop of full-grown horses. They were of the giant kind—great-boned, deep-chested, their breath warm and grassy. Because they were not alarmed at creatures so unlike themselves, I soon made friends with them; and two of the finest I trained for Lona and myself. Lona had been used to riding a smaller one; her delight was great when first she sat high on the back of her new horse and looked down as if the world had lowered itself in homage. The horse, too, showed himself proud of the burden he bore, stepping as though he understood her as more than a rider.

We exercised them daily until they trusted us enough to obey instantly and fear nothing; after that we rode them at parade and on the march.

The undertaking, even in the midst of our bustle, would at times loom before me as foolhardy. But the woman of Bulika’s confidence—whether genuine or worn like a mask—always pushed my hesitation back.
