The princess’s magic, she insisted, would be powerless against the children; and any force she might muster could not stand against our animal-allies. With a good stick, she said, she was ready to encounter any two men of Bulika.

Of the leopardess she confessed fear; of her I was ready. Yet I shrank from carrying all the children with us. It felt like taking a whole village’s seed-corn and putting it on one cart.

“Would it not be better,” I said to the woman, “that you remained in the forest with your baby and the smallest of the Little Ones?”

She answered that she relied greatly on the impression the sight of them would make on the women, especially the mothers.

“When they see the darlings,” she said, “their hearts will be taken by storm; and I must be there encouraging them to make a stand. If there be a remnant of hardihood in the place, it will be found among the women.”

“And you must not,” I told Lona, “encumber yourself with any of the children. You will be wanted everywhere.”

For there were two babies besides the woman’s, and even on horseback Lona had almost always one in her arms—warm, milky, breathing that soft animal breath which seems itself a kind of prayer.

“I do not remember ever being without a child to take care of,” she answered. “But when we reach the city, it shall be as you wish.”

Her confidence in me—who had failed so unworthily—shamed me. Yet neither had I begun the movement, nor had I clear ground to oppose it. If I drew back now, it would not only be fear in me; it would be moral ruin to the Little Ones, who had begun to believe that courage was simply another sort of play, only truer. I had no choice but to give the enterprise the best help I could. For myself, I was ready to live or die with Lona. Her humility as well as her trust humbled me, and I gave myself heartily to her purposes.

Our way would lie across a grassy plain, so there was no need to carry food for the horses, or for the two cows that would go with us for the infants’ sake. Their milk was not a luxury; it was the difference between peace and wailing, between sleep and a night that could break the nerves of a whole camp. But the elephants—our slow towers—had to be provided for. The grass was good enough, yet it was short; with their long one-fingered noses they could not pick enough for a single meal.

So we set the whole colony to gather grass and make hay. It was a work that made the air smell of sun-warmed green. The children cut and piled, trampling the stalks with bare feet, and the sound of it—rustle, swish, thud—was as wholesome as any hymn. There was no harsh metallic ring, no industrial scream; only the honest noise of bodies moving through plants. The elephants themselves carried loads enough to last several days, with the supplement of what we would gather fresh at every halt.

For the bears we stored nuts, and for ourselves we dried plenty of fruits—slices laid out where the air moved, turned at intervals so they would not sour. Even without salt, there is an art to drying: you must know when the flesh has given up enough water to keep, but not so much that it becomes lifeless on the tongue. The smell of the drying fruit hung about our preparations—sweet, slightly fermented, promising quick strength.

We caught and tamed several more of the big horses. When all was ready we loaded them, and the elephants, with provisions, and stood prepared at last to set out.

Then Lona and I held a general review. The troop gathered—children mounted on whatever would bear them: horses, little bulls that galloped and leaped like deer, the elephants patient as moving hills, bears shambling with a sort of grave amusement. The forest listened. The birds held their places above us, singing as if to test whether our own voices belonged to this soundscape or would tear it.

I made them a little speech.

I began by telling them I had learned a good deal about them, and knew now where they came from.

“We did not come from anywhere!” they cried, interrupting me. “We are here!”

Their certainty struck me—the way a child knows the names in his own dialect and thinks there can be no other. In that cry there was the whole power of the present moment, and also the peril: memory, if it is cut off, cannot teach.

I told them that every one of them had a mother of his own, like the mother of the last baby; that I believed they had all been brought from Bulika when they were so small they could not now remember; that the wicked princess there was so afraid of babies, and so determined to destroy them, that their mothers had to carry them away and leave them where she could not find them. And I told them that now we were going to Bulika to find their mothers and deliver them from the bad giantess.

I could feel, even as I spoke, the pressure of words that did not perfectly fit what they had lived. “Mother” meant something in them already—Lona had fed that meaning with her arms and patience—yet the mothers I spoke of were like a language their tongues had never learned. Some experiences do not translate; the mouth can repeat a word and still not taste what it stands for. But the word worked on them all the same, as smell works on hunger.

“But I must tell you,” I went on, “that there is danger before us; for, as you know, we may have to fight hard to take the city.”

“We can fight! We are ready!” cried the boys, bright-eyed, eager as if I had called for a race.

“Yes, you can,” I returned, “and I know you will. Mothers are worth fighting for. Only mind—you must all keep together.”

“Yes, yes; we’ll take care of each other,” they answered. “Nobody shall touch one of us but his own mother!”

“You must mind, every one, to do immediately what your officers tell you!”

“We will, we will! Now we’re quite ready! Let us go!”

“Another thing you must not forget,” I said, raising my voice so it carried cleanly without shouting. “When you strike, be sure you make it a downright swinging blow. When you shoot an arrow, draw it to the head. When you sling a stone, sling it strong and straight.”

“That we will!” they cried with a jubilant, fearless shout that made the birds startle and then settle again.

“Perhaps you will be hurt,” I said.

“We don’t mind that! Do we, boys?”

“Not a bit!”

“Some of you may very possibly be killed.”

A small boy—one of the finest among them—cried at once, as if he had been offered a sweet: “I don’t mind being killed!” He rode a beautiful little bull that pranced beneath him, tossing its head, proud of its rider’s wild courage.

“I don’t either! I don’t either!” came from all sides.

Then Lona—queen and mother and sister of them all—spoke from her great horse by my side. Her voice was not loud, yet it carried, because everyone wanted it.

“I would give my life,” she said, “to have my mother. She might kill me if she liked. I should just kiss her and die.”

“Come along, boys!” cried a girl, lifting her aloe-spike. “We’re going to our mothers!”

A pang went through my heart—sharp as biting a seed you thought was sweet and finding it bitter. But I could not draw back. To draw back now would be to teach the Little Ones a new word—the coward’s word—and once learned it would cling like taint to everything they did. So I held my place, and the whole living host stood ready, smelling of hay and fruit, leather-leaf and feather, milk and warm animal breath, the forest’s song pressing close around us as we prepared to go and break into the noise of Bulika.

We set out while the dew still clung to every blade, and the plain lay like a table newly laid—blue vault above, green cloth below, our little procession moving between them as if some old household had taken wings and decided the day’s work must be done elsewhere. The plan was as homely as it was warlike: travel through the morning, sleep away the afternoon, then go on again under the cold stars; rest the next day; start again in the brief twilight. Toward the end we would portion the hours more closely, so that the city should meet us at dawn, and we be within its gates before the place had properly rubbed sleep from its eyes.

It was as if the whole forest insisted on coming too, not as an army, but as a migrating pantry and larder—feathers, fur, and flutter, a moving store of life. Birds flew ahead in bright companies, calling to one another as if they meant to announce our approach like town-criers. Butterflies—spilled petals with purpose—played about our heads; wasps and dragonflies stitched the air with their thin, glinting thread. Four-footed things followed behind, padding in and out of the grass like quiet servants. At night most of the ground-creatures dropped away, each to its own den and customary darkness; but the birds and the bright insects kept with us stubbornly, all the way to the city’s very mouth.

We halted and slept through the afternoon. It was our first true march, yet none were tired—these children had bodies trained by wandering and weather, and spirits that took hardship as easily as warm bread. When night came we went faster, for the cold sharpened every step and made even the elephants eager to be done with standing. Many of the Little Ones fell asleep on the backs of their beasts and woke at morning as if they had been laid down in clean straw in some familiar loft. None tumbled. Some rode bears—shaggy, shambling fellows that looked as though they had been built for pondering, not for speed—yet they kept pace well enough, going as fast as the elephants in their long, steady way. Others were on deer, and would have turned the road into a racecourse had I not checked them; for the road was not ours alone, and a stumble in the dark would have broken more than pride.

Those perched atop the hay on the elephants—unable to see the great grey backs below, feeling only the slow sway and the warm animal-breath rising through the straw—kept talking to their mounts as long as they were awake, as if the elephants were hearth-stone kin. Once, when we had halted to feed, I heard a small voice burrowing down through the hay, and the child speaking as one speaks to dough one hopes will rise:

“Nosy dear, I am digging you out of the mountain, and shall soon get down to you: be patient; I’m a coming! Very soon now you’ll send up your nose to look for me, and then we’ll kiss like good elephants, we will!”

It was the kind of talk that makes the world feel older than any written record: the speech of people whose affection is practical, whose love is proven by feeding, brushing, keeping close.

But that same night there burst out a tumult—elephant-trumpeting, horse-neighing, and the children’s bold imitations of both—ringing far over the silent levels. The very air seemed to recoil and throw the sound back at us. It was not the wholesome chorus of a living place—the soft layers of frog, wind, leaf, and distant wing that heal the mind by their variety—but a sudden blare, a crude fanfare like metal struck in a narrow alley. I did not know how near the city might be; and if there is one thing a wicked town lives on, it is hearing. So I hurried along the line, hushed the little throats, stroked muzzles, spoke low, and at last stilled the uproar, lest we give warning of our approach.

Then, suddenly, one morning the sun and the city rose together, as if the same hand lifted them from behind the horizon. To the children the walls looked like a great mass of rock, as if a cliff had been persuaded into a square. But when I told them what waited within—nests of stone, piled close, one on another—their faces altered. Apprehension, then dislike, moved over them like a chill shadow. For the first time in their lives, I believe—many of them long little lives—they knew fear. The place looked wrong to them. How were they to find mothers in such a place? Where, among hard angles and shut doors, could the smell of milk and warm bread live?

Yet they went on bravely, because they trusted Lona—and, to my shame, me as well. We rode through the sounding archway. Never had that old pavement heard such drumming of hoof, such padding of paw and bare foot. The echo did not behave like a meadow, where sound has room to soften into wind; it came back sharp-edged, a hard copy of itself. The horses started at the noise of their own steps; some halted; some plunged and wheeled as if the stones had bitten them. But they were quieted and went on.

Some of the Little Ones shivered. All were still as death. The three girls drew the infants closer against their breasts, as if to keep out not only chill but the city’s very voice. All except the bears and the butterflies showed fear. On the countenance of the woman among us lay a dark anxiety; nor was I myself untouched by the general dread, for the whole army was on my hands and on my conscience. I had brought it up to a danger whose shadow now pressed upon us.

And still, foolishly, I was propped up by a dream: the coming kingdom of the Little Ones, the bad giants made slaves, the animals loving, obedient friends. It was an easy hope—like imagining you can sweeten rancid broth by adding honey. I, who dreamed of obedience in others, had not learned it myself. Untrusting, unfaithful obstinacy had set me at the head of that army of innocents. In the world I had left, I had seen kings who were slaves to their own appetite, doing only what pleased them; and I was not above them. But Lona rode beside me a child indeed, therefore a free woman—calm, silent, watchful, not one whit afraid.

We were near the heart of the city before any of its inhabitants truly knew we were there. Then windows began to open; sleepy heads leaned out. Every face wore at first a dull stare, the wonderless astonishment of people to whom even marvels have become a kind of inconvenience. But when they saw the animals, that stare shifted to consternation. Still, when they perceived their invaders were almost all children, the women came running into the streets, and the men followed. For a time they kept close to the houses, leaving the middle of the way open, for they dared not approach the beasts.

At last a little boy—five years old perhaps—full of the idea of his mother as a hungry child is full of the idea of food, spied in the crowd a woman whose face drew him like a remembered smell. He flung himself from his antelope, clung about her neck, and she was not slow to return his embrace and kisses.

But a man’s hand came over her shoulder and seized the child by the neck.

Instantly a girl ran her sharp spear into the fellow’s arm. He let out a savage howl, and before he could gather himself, two or three more spears found him, and he fled yelling.

“They are just bad giants!” cried Lona, her eyes flashing. She drove her horse straight at one of unusual height who stood barring her way with a club, as if his size were an argument. He did not dare abide the shock; he slunk aside, and the next moment went down, struck by several stones.

Another huge fellow, avoiding my charger, stepped suddenly—with a speech whose rudeness alone was intelligible—between me and the boy who rode behind me. The boy told him to address the king. The giant answered by striking the little horse on the head with a hammer. The poor creature fell like a loaf knocked from the board before it is baked—helpless, finished.

Before the brute could strike again, one of the elephants behind us reached him with its weight and will. It laid him prostrate and trampled on him so that he did not attempt to rise until hundreds of feet had gone over him and the army was already past.

But at sight of the women—at their faces, their mouths, their eyes—what dismay clouded Lona’s countenance. Hardly one of them was pleasant to look upon; there was something pinched and sour in them, as if their lives had been fed on bad grain and worse words. Were her darlings to find mothers among such as these?

We halted in the central square. Hardly had we settled when two girls rode up in anxious haste with tidings: two of the boys had been hurried away by some women. We turned at once, and then first discovered that the woman we had befriended—the one who had clasped the child—had disappeared with her baby.

At the same moment we saw, bounding toward us down a narrow lane that led from the square toward the palace, a white leopardess.

The Little Ones had not forgotten the fight of the two leopardesses in the forest. Some looked terrified; their ranks wavered. But they remembered the order I had just given them, and they stood fast. We stopped to see what would come, when suddenly a small boy named Odu—remarkable for speed and courage—who had heard me speak of the goodness of the white leopardess, leaped from the back of his bear. The bear went shambling after him as faithfully as a slow old dog. Odu ran to meet the leopardess.

She pulled herself up so sharply, to avoid knocking him down, that she went rolling over and over like a spilled sack. When she found her feet again, she found the child upon her back.

Who could doubt what such a sight would do to a people who lived in daily terror of that white shape? An enemy urchin bestriding what they feared like famine. Confident of the effect, we rode on.

Our guides brought us to a house. As we stopped there came a scream—thin, sudden, the kind that cuts through stone and makes the hairs along the neck listen. I sprang down and thundered at the door. My horse came and pushed me aside with his nose, turned, and began to batter the door with his heels, a patient, angry pounding like a pestle on grain.

Up came little Odu on the leopardess. At sight of her the horse stood still, trembling. But the leopardess too had heard the cry, and forgetting the child on her back, threw herself at the door. The boy was dashed against it and fell senseless.

Before I could reach him, Lona had him in her arms; and as soon as he came to himself, she set him again on the back of his bear, which had never ceased following him, as if loyalty were its natural gait.

When the leopardess threw herself the third time, the door gave way. She darted in. We followed, but she had already vanished. We sprang up a stair, searched the rooms—nothing. Then, darting down again, we spied a door under the stair and got into a labyrinth of excavations beneath the house, tunnels like cellars built not for storing apples or curing meat, but for hiding deeds that cannot bear daylight.

We had not gone far when we met the leopardess returning, the child we sought slung across her back. He told us—his words small, shaken—that the woman he had taken for his mother had thrown him into a hole, saying she would give him to the leopardess. As if the beast were the eater of children, and the woman merely the cook. But the leopardess was a good one; she had taken him out.

We followed to search for the other boy, and entered the next house more easily. There—too late. One of the savages had just killed the little captive.

Lona’s face hardened, and yet it was a strange comfort to her to learn which child it was; she had been expecting him to grow a bad giant, and from that worst of fates death had saved him. The city had murdered him, but in murdering had also barred the door on what it would have made of him. Such are the crooked economies of evil.

The leopardess sprang upon the murderer, took him by the throat, dragged him into the street, and followed Lona with him like a cat carrying a great rat in her jaws.

“Let us leave the horrible place,” said Lona. “There are no mothers here! This people is not worth delivering.”

The leopardess dropped her burden and charged into the crowd, this way and that, wherever it was thickest. The slaves cried out and ran, tumbling over each other in heaps. Their cries filled the square—raw, frantic, a noise without pattern, the opposite of the forest’s layered music. It was the sound of a place that has forgotten how to listen.

When we got back to the army we found it as we had left it, standing in order, ready. But I was far from easy. The princess gave no sign; what she might be plotting we did not know. Watch and ward must be kept through the night.

The Little Ones were such hardy creatures they could repose anywhere. We told them to lie down with their animals where they were and sleep until they were called. In one moment they were down; in another they were lapt in the music of their sleep—a sound as of water over grass, or a soft wind among leaves. It was the one pure thing the city could not counterfeit: the hush of living breath, the small, steady cadence of trust.

Their animals slept more lightly, always on the edge of waking, ears turning to the least change in the hard city air. The bigger boys and girls walked softly hither and thither among the dreaming multitude. All was still. The whole wicked place appeared at rest. But under the stones, and behind the shut mouths of houses, I felt the city listening.

Lona’s disgust had risen all morning like milk left too long by a warm hearth. The streets, the faces, worst of all the women with their hard eyes and sharper elbows—she would have shaken the place from her sandals and been gone at once. I understood her; the very air tasted of soot and old malice. Yet I could not yield.

To turn back now would be to teach the Little Ones that fear is a wisdom, and the lesson would poison them more surely than any city-brewed well. If we retreated, the princess would hunt us, certain as flies find a cut. If we went forward, we carried with us the one bright, dangerous hope bound up in the prophecy: mother and daughter must meet. I held to that line as a cook holds to a thread of scent in a market—follow it, and you find what you need, even if the crowd jostles and curses. Lona’s loveliness might, by some miracle of flesh and memory, strike through the princess’s crusted heart. And if violence came, I would stand between them. If I had no other power left, I was ready, for Lona’s sake, to strike the princess’s closed hand without pity. I felt—though I did not say it—that doom had already set its fire beneath her, and perhaps it was decreed that we should be the ones to bring the boil.

I told Lona the matter as it lay with me, but still I gave her no hint of the bond between her and the woman on the dais. She listened, jaw tight, like an elder tasting a broth for bitterness and finding too much of it. Then, suddenly, she nodded once, as if accepting a difficult duty in the household.

“Then we go,” she said.

From the top of one of the palace towers, the princess had seen us at dawn—had seen the Little Ones’ strange array moving like a living tide toward her sleeping city. It woke in her a terror that mastered her more than any army could: she had failed to destroy them, and now they came. The prophecy had found legs. When she came back to herself, she went down into the black hall and set herself in the north focus of its ellipse, directly beneath the opening in the roof.

For she must think.

But what she named thinking was only the careful tending of an image: consciousness not of what she was, but of what she chose to believe she was. To feed that false hunger, she had suspended, a little above and before her—hidden in the darkness—a mirror positioned to catch the sun reflected from her own body, so that, for a brief exact time, she could see herself as she wished: drenched in light, clothed in her beauty like a garment freshly shaken out.

Shadows moved about her in the dark—many, restless as mice behind a larder wall. When her inward eye glimpsed one, she refused it attention. Close under the mirror stood the Shadow that attended her walks, but she did not see him; self-occupation blinded her more thoroughly than night.

The city fell. Its people cowered. The Little Ones and their odd cavalry made camp in the square. And at noon the sunlight struck the princess, and for a few minutes she saw herself glorious. When the vision thinned and passed, she still sat there. Night came; darkness filled even the glass; still she did not move. The palace swarmed with gloom; servants shivered, but dared not flee for fear of the Little Ones’ beasts. All night she sat, motionless, waiting to taste again that sweet, thin draught of her own presence. But courage and will had grown tired of her and went away, like faithful dogs finally turned from a door.

In the morning we chose twelve of the tallest and bravest boys to go with us to the palace—lads with steady hands and clear ears, the sort that can hear trouble coming through stone. We rode our great horses; the boys had their small horses and their elephants. In the square the soundscape had changed: the usual city clatter, that ceaseless metallic fret of market and quarrel, was muted, as if the stones themselves were holding their breath.

We climbed. The palace rose ahead, and above it the dome of the great hall. The sun climbed with us, and as we neared, its light crept toward the roof-eye. The princess was waiting, poised like a woman waiting for the first smell of bread from an oven she believes she owns. Not a ray could enter the black hall until the sun stood at the meridian. Then, suddenly, he looked in through the opening—and with a quick radiance the princess flashed upon her own sight in the hidden mirror.

But the cry that tore from her was not triumph. It was despair.

Her whiteness—her prized blank perfection—was broken. A spot covered half her side, black as the marble around her, as if a giant hand had seared her through robe and flesh. She clutched at the fabric and fell back into her chair, breath catching like a person choking on smoke. The Shadow glided out, and this time she saw him go.

We found the gate open as it always was, as if the palace had long ago given up on guarding itself against what belonged to it. We passed through the paved grove toward the door, hooves tapping a dry, hollow rhythm that the stones threw back at us. In the vestibule the spotted leopardess lay in her cage, either asleep or dead. The Little Ones paused, staring. Then she sprang up, all teeth and brindled muscle, rearing against the bars. The horses reared and plunged; the elephants took a startled step back, their feet thudding like slow drums in a cavern.

The next instant the leopardess fell supine, writhed in quivering spasms, and went still again, like a fire suddenly starved.

We rode into the great hall. The darkness swallowed hoofbeats, chewed them, then let them out again as dull echoes under the dome. The princess still leaned back in her chair, caught in a shaft of sunlight that made her look, to us, like some splendid fruit gleaming in a cellar.

She heard us from the court stones first—the alien thunder of horses in a place used only to soft slippers and the padding of shadows. She started and listened, and the sound made her shake: never had such noise rung in her palace. She pressed her hand to her side and gasped. The trampling came nearer, entered the hall itself, and moving figures—not shadows—approached through the black.

Lona saw her and was off her horse before I could speak, springing forward with the same certainty as a girl running to the kitchen where her mother stands over the pot. I leapt down after her, heart pounding with a terrible hope.

“Mother! mother!” she cried.

Her voice went up into the dome and came back, pure and clear, a sound the hall did not deserve. For an instant the princess shivered. Her face darkened as if hatred were smoke rising under skin; her brows drew together until they nearly met. She stood.

“Mother! mother!” Lona cried again, already mounting the dais, arms flung wide. She reached her and clasped her as if she could warm her, as if she could fold her into the old domestic circle where hands pass bread and names are spoken without fear.

One step more and I would have been there.

In that single step, the princess lifted Lona high—high as a butcher lifts a lamb by the hind legs—and dashed her upon the marble floor.

The sound of her fall was the sound of a whole household dropped and broken. Lona fell at my feet and lay still.

The princess sat down, and the smile she wore belonged to something that had forgotten what tenderness tastes like.

I dropped beside Lona, gathered her up from the stones, and pressed her to my breast. My glance went to the princess with a hate so sharp it seemed to slice my sight; she answered it with her sweetest smile, like a sugared crust hiding rot. I would have sprung at her, seized her throat, and ended her there, but Lona’s weight in my arms anchored me: love of the child was stronger than hate of the mother.

Lona’s arms hung helpless. Her blood trickled over my hands and fell to the floor in soft, slow plashes, as if some careful cook were dripping sauce a drop at a time—only this was life, and it was leaving her. The scent of it spread quickly, raw and iron, and the horses caught it. Mine first: he reared, shivering, wild-eyed, then wheeled and thundered blindly down the dark hall, the little horses following in frantic, clattering imitation. Lona’s horse stood gazing down at his mistress, trembling all over.

The boys flung themselves from their horses’ backs to save them, but in the darkness they did not see the black wall. The terrified beasts dashed, with mine, straight into it and broke themselves. The shock sounded like crockery thrown against stone—too many lives shattered at once.

The elephants came on to the foot of the dais and stopped, trumpeting wildly, their voices filling the hall with an awful, living protest—as if the very ecology of sound in that place rejected what had been done. The Little Ones sprang up onto the dais and stood, horrified and rigid. The princess lay back in her seat. Her face had gone corpse-white, her eyes alone alive, wickedly flaming. She was withered and wasted again, as she had been when first I found her in the wood, and the brand on her side looked deeper, as if the darkness itself had pressed its seal upon her.

But Lona saw nothing. And I saw only Lona.

“Mother… mother…” she sighed—less a word now than a breath of habit, the last syllables of a native tongue leaving the mouth that learned it at the first breast. Then her breathing ceased.

I carried her into the court. The sun shone upon her white face, and a pitiful shadow lay on her lips like the memory of a smile. Her head hung back. She was dead as earth.

In that moment my mind tore itself free from sense, as if the world had become an intolerable noise. I forgot the Little Ones. I forgot the murderous princess. I forgot even the body in my arms. I wandered, looking for my Lona—as if the true Lona might be somewhere ahead, barefoot in some quiet garden, calling me in the language that belonged only to us.

Doors and windows were crowded with brute-faces, jeering at me, but they did not dare speak. They had seen the white leopardess behind me, head hanging close at my heel. I spurned her with my foot; she drew back a moment, then followed again, as if bound by a leash made of old guilt.

I reached the square. The little army was gone.

The emptiness struck me like hunger. Where were the Little Ones—her Little Ones? I had lost her children. I stared helplessly, then staggered to the pillar and sank upon its base with Lona still in my arms. The square’s silence was not peace; it was the kind of quiet that comes when a factory shuts down for lack of fuel and leaves only the ringing in one’s ears.

As I sat there gazing on her still countenance, it seemed—only for a living moment—to smile, not as a corpse might be arranged to smile, but as she herself smiled when tasting something sweet and familiar. I never doubted it was illusion, yet I believed what it said: I should see her alive again. It was not she who was lost. It was I. And she would find me.

I rose to go after the Little Ones and sought instinctively the gate by which we had entered. I looked about; the leopardess was gone.

The street was rapidly filling with a fierce crowd. They saw me encumbered with my dead, and for a time did not assail me—perhaps the sight of blood and stillness shook even their brutish courage. But as I neared the gate, their confidence swelled. Women began to hustle me with shoulders and hips, the way they might jostle a stranger away from a bread stall. I held on, heedless. A man pushed against my sacred burden; with a kick I sent him away howling.

The crowd pressed harder. Fearing their hands on the dead—hands too curious, too cruel—I clasped my treasure closer and freed my right arm.

That instant a commotion rose behind me, a change in the street’s sound like a wind shifting through trees. The crowd broke apart. Through it came the Little Ones I had left in the palace.

Ten of them rode upon four elephants. On two other elephants lay the princess—bound hand and foot, quite still save for the rolling of her eyes in their ghastly sockets. Two Little Ones rode behind her on Lona’s horse. Every now and then the wise creatures that bore her curled their trunks back to feel her cords, checking them with patient care, the way an old farmer checks the ties on a load before taking the road.

I walked on in front, and out of the city.

So ended the hopes with which I had entered that evil place. We had captured the bad princess—and lost our all-beloved queen. My life was bare; my heart was empty. The sounds of the city fell behind us, harsh and mechanical again as the crowd closed in upon itself, but ahead there was only the trudging rhythm of elephants, the soft creak of leather, and the silence where Lona’s voice should have been.

A low ripple of delight ran through my companions and broke against me like warm broth against a cold spoon: ahead, scattered on the long meadow, the rest of the Little Ones had sighted us. The two who rode with me on Lona’s horse pressed on first, eager as children sent to fetch water at dusk. They reached their fellows and were met by a wavering shout—then, as if someone had shut a door on the very air, the cry snapped short.

We drew nearer, and what came to us was not shouting but the sound of sobbing—small voices breaking and reforming like tiny wavelets on a pebbled shore. When I stepped among them, I understood at once that this was no common grief. Terror had baked itself into their faces, leaving them drawn and gray, as if the life in their cheeks had been smoked out by some unseen fire. No loss alone could have worked such a change.

Several came slowly around me and lifted their arms to take my burden. I yielded her up. The smile with which they received Lona’s body—tender, hopeless, and careful as hands laying pastry into a hot oven without letting it collapse—swelled my heart with pity inside my own desolation.

They tried everything the heart knows when it cannot accept a silence. Their tears fell on her as if salt might wake her. They kissed her brow and stroked her hands, coaxing as they bore her, rocking her gently, pressing their cheeks to hers. In vain. She would not wake.

Two on either side took an arm, rubbing it with slow, soothing passes as if warmth could be kneaded back into muscle the way one works life into dough. As many as could reach slid their forearms under her body, spreading the weight so no one would hurt her. Those who could not support her crowded around the bearers, touching her hair, her sleeve, the hem of her garment—each contact a plea.

They found a place where the grass grew thicker and softer, like a bed made by patient hands. There they laid her down, and all the Little Ones gathered, sobbing, a tight ring of bent heads.

Outside that ring stood the elephants, immense and still; and I stood near them, looking at my Lona over the many small crowns between. The evening held a hush in the meadow, pierced by the thin, wet sounds of grief. Even the insects seemed to keep their music low, as if the whole place understood that certain noises—metallic, grinding, loud—would be an obscenity here. Their sorrow had its own acoustic weather: breath hitching, throats breaking, palms patting grass; a domestic soundscape of loss, intimate and unbought, nothing like the staged wailing of courts.

Those nearest me caught sight of the princess. They stared, shivering, as if the air around her had turned sour.

Odu was the first to speak. He leaned toward his neighbor and whispered, “I have seen that woman before!”

“It was she who fought the white leopardess,” he went on, “the night they woke us with their yelling!”

“Silly!” said the other, though his own voice trembled. “That was a wild beast, with spots!”

“Look at her eyes!” Odu insisted. “I know she is a bad giantess, but she is a wild beast all the same. I know she is the spotted one!”

The other took a step nearer. Odu caught his sleeve and jerked him back, sharp as a cook yanking a child away from a boiling pot.

“Don’t look at her!” he cried, shrinking—yet held by the hate-filled longing in the princess’s eyes, as if her gaze were a hook. “She would eat you up in a moment! It was HER shadow! She is the wicked princess!”

“That cannot be!” someone protested in the crowd. “They said she was beautiful!”

“Indeed it is the princess,” I said, stepping between their fear and her. “Wickedness has made her ugly.”

She heard. The look she gave me was a thing with teeth, like a bitter herb chewed too long until it poisons the mouth. Even without moving, she seemed to contaminate the air with her wanting.

“It was very wrong of me to run away!” Odu said then, suddenly thoughtful, as if he were confessing to an elder over a table where the bread is already cut.

“What made you run away?” I asked. “I expected to find you where I left you!”

He did not answer at once.

“I don’t know what made me run,” said another. “I was frightened!”

“It was a man that came down the hill from the palace,” said a third.

“How did he frighten you?”

“I don’t know.”

“He wasn’t a man,” said Odu; “he was a shadow; he had no thick to him!”

“Tell me more about him,” I said, for the word shadow in that place was never an empty word, and I felt the night itself lean closer to listen.

“He came down the hill very black,” Odu said, “walking like a bad giant, but spread flat. He was nothing but blackness. We were frightened the moment we saw him, but we did not run away; we stood and watched him. He came on as if he would walk over us. But before he reached us, he began to spread and spread, and grew bigger and bigger, till at last he was so big that he went out of our sight, and we saw him no more, and then he was upon us!”

“What do you mean by that?”

“He was all black through between us,” Odu answered, “and we could not see one another; and then he was inside us.”

Even in the telling, the meadow seemed to darken by a shade, as if the memory carried its own eclipse. I thought of how a sudden industrial roar can blot out a whole living world of sound—bird, leaf, stream—until you feel alone though you stand among friends. Their terror had been like that: a violence done to hearing and seeing alike, a smothering that made each child solitary inside his own skin.

“How did you know he was inside you?”

“He did me quite different,” said Odu. “I felt like bad. I was not Odu any more—not the Odu I knew. I wanted to tear Sozo to pieces—not really, but like!”

He turned and threw his arms around Sozo, clinging hard.

“It wasn’t me, Sozo,” he sobbed. “Really, deep down, it was Odu, loving you always! And Odu came up, and knocked Naughty away. I grew sick, and thought I must kill myself to get out of the black. Then came a horrible laugh that had heard my think, and it set the air trembling about me. And then I suppose I ran away, but I did not know I had run away until I found myself running, fast as could, and all the rest running too. I would have stopped, but I never thought of it until I was out of the gate among the grass. Then I knew that I had run away from a shadow that wanted to be me and wasn’t, and that I was the Odu that loved Sozo. It was the shadow that got into me, and hated him from inside me; it was not my own self me!

“And now I know that I ought not to have run away! But indeed I did not quite know what I was doing until it was done! My legs did it, I think: they grew frightened, and forgot me, and ran away! Naughty legs! There! and there!”

And with that he kicked each leg, as if scolding disobedient tools.

“What became of the shadow?” I asked.

“I do not know,” he said. “I suppose he went home into the night where there is no moon.”

His words left a taste like cold ash on my tongue. I sat there among grass and grief and the heavy breathing of elephants, wondering where Lona was gone—Lona whose presence had been a kind of language among them, a dialect of courage and gentleness they all understood without translation. Now her body lay like a closed book whose script the living could not read.

Dropping to the ground, I drew the dead thing into my lap and bent close, as if warmth and voice might still find a way in.

“Where are you, Lona?” I whispered into her ear. “I love you!”

No answer came. Her lips, not quite cold yet, held their silence. I kissed them—one last time tasting the fading human warmth—then laid her down again. I appointed a guard over her, and rose. There were practical needs to tend even in a myth: the night would come, and with it whatever moved in darkness, and the Little Ones must be kept.

Before the sun went down I set a watch over the princess outside the camp, and placed sentinels all around. I meant to walk the circle myself through the whole night, to keep the meadow’s fragile quiet from being shattered again by any invading wrong sound or unseen footstep. I told the rest of the little army to sleep. They flung themselves on the grass and were asleep at once, as children do after a day too heavy for their small bodies.

When the moon rose, its pale light lay on the field like flour sifted thinly over a table. In that washed silver I caught a glimpse of something white: the leopardess.

She swept silently around the sleeping camp, soundless as breath, and I saw her pass three times between the princess and the Little Ones, as if she were measuring, guarding, or choosing. After the third circuit, I made the watch lie down with the others. Then I stretched myself beside the body of Lona, keeping my own vigil where love had become as mute as death, and listening to the night—its honest grasses, its distant, unbought hush—against which every cruel laugh and every spreading shadow would always be an act of war.

Morning lifted itself, grey and cool, from the desert like steam from a quenched pan, and we set out at once for the forest. I rode Lona’s horse, her body steadied before me—wrapt in her own cloak, the cloth still holding, as linen holds, the faint domestic ghosts of her: sun-warm wool, crushed leaf, a trace of the smoke that clung to us from so many small fires. I would take her to her father. He would give her a couch in the chamber of his dead—or, if he refused because she had not come of her own will, then I would keep watch over her in the waste until wind and time took what was left. Yet I believed he would receive her, for surely she had died long ago; and the thought of humbling myself before him had a taste in it like green persimmon—bitter, necessary, cleansing.

And Lilith also I must take—back to her first husband. I had no craft for penitence; I could not cook remorse in her like a slow broth, nor coax it out with sweetness. I had hardly a right to slay her, and less right to loose her on the world; and least of all a right to be made for ever her gaoler. Again and again as we went I offered her food—fruit, a crust I had saved, the last of a honeyed thing the Little Ones carried like treasure. She answered only with a look of hungering hate, as if my gifts were insults, as if even smell was a chain. Her fiery eyes rolled restlessly, taking in everything with a predator’s hunger, and I think they never once closed until we reached the far side of the hot stream. After that they shut, and did not open again until we came to the House of Bitterness.

Toward evening, when we camped, a small domestic scene rose out of the harshness as naturally as a pot set to simmer: a little girl wandered to Lilith with the solemn patience of children who have fed calves and rescued beetles, and I ran to prevent mischief. But before I could reach them, the child had already pressed something to the princess’s lips—some small fruit, soft-skinned, bursting with a sharp juice—and she gave a scream, then held up her hand, injured and indignant.

“Please, king,” she whimpered, tears standing like dew, “suck finger. Bad giantess make hole in it!”

I took her hand and sucked the tiny finger. The taste was iron and salt—blood, clean and intimate, like the first cut in a kitchen when you forget the knife is new. The pain left her face as quickly as steam leaving a lid.

“Well now!” she cried, astonished by so simple a remedy; and scarcely a minute passed before she was holding up a second fruit, offering it again with a courage that would have shamed grown people.

This time Lilith snapped for it like a starving thing, and the child snatched her hand away so swiftly the fruit dropped and rolled in the dust. The little girl’s name was Luva. I remembered it the way you remember the name of a seed you mean to plant again.

The next day we crossed the hot stream. The Little Ones, back on their own ground, grew loud with delight—not the harsh loudness of machines or marching men, but the lively, layered noise of a healthy place: feet padding, laughter bubbling, elephant-breathing like bellows, the creak of harness, the far insect-choir rising and falling. Even the river’s hiss—hot water licking stones—felt like part of a great kitchen where the world was always boiling something.

Their nests, though, were still far, and we went no farther than the ivy-hall, where I had resolved to pass the night for the grapes’ sake. The hall was green with old ivy, and the vines hung heavy with clusters—purple-black, bloom-dusted, each grape tight as a held breath. The smell struck us first: wine-promise, sun, leaf, a faint musk from the hall’s damp stones.

The Little Ones knew at once. They swarmed the clusters, ate greedily—fingers sticky, chins glossy—and within minutes were asleep where they fell: on the hall’s green floor, in the ferny fringe beyond, under the trellis where the last light sifted through.

I hoped again to see the dance. I had the children leave a wide space in the middle; then I lay down among them with Lona by my side. Sleep would not take me. Grief is a kind of hunger that refuses all offered food.

Night thickened; and suddenly the company was there—those pale dancers, those weary celebrants of a time that had already perished. Their lights glimmered like lanterns seen through kitchen steam. Their feet made no sound, yet the very air seemed to keep their rhythm; and I found myself wondering whether, night after night, they would go on thus to all eternity—whether the dance was a punishment or a ritual of remembrance, and whether one day, because of my stiff neck and my stubborn tongue, I should be drawn into their circle and made to move until I forgot my own name.

Then the children’s eyes opened. They sprang up as if the grapes had been a charm rather than a meal. Wide awake, they ran straight into the dancers. Each Little One caught a partner—hand to hand, small to spectral—and away they went, bounding and skipping. The spectres seemed to see them and welcome them. Perhaps they knew all about the Little Ones; perhaps they too had long been on their way back to childhood, as old stories go back to their first language. The children’s gambols—never rude, always merry—put little bright fractures in the solemn pattern; and if the rhythm jarred, the poor dancers, who had nothing to smile with, showed no annoyance. It was as if the living noise of the children—their breath, their footfalls, their quick cries—tuned the dead air, restoring to it some lost ecology of sound, some balance between silence and song.

Just before morning began to break, I started up.

In the doorway stood the skeleton-princess, her eyes open and glowing, and on her side the fearful spot—black, like a bruise that had learned to think. For a moment she held herself as if listening to the dance; then she glided in, as if to join it.

I sprang to my feet. A cry of repugnant fear broke from the children, and the lights vanished as if a hand had pinched them out. The low moon looked in; the children clung to each other. The ghosts were gone—or at least no longer visible. The princess too had disappeared.

I darted to where I had left Lilith. She lay with her eyes closed, as if she had never moved. Her stillness had the false calm of a pot left on the edge of the fire: quiet, but ready to boil over.

I returned to the hall. The Little Ones were already lying down, composing themselves to sleep with the same matter-of-fact ease with which they ate. A world that still remembers how to rest is a world not yet wholly conquered.

In the morning, as we started, we spied two skeletons moving in a thicket. The Little Ones broke ranks and ran to them, and I followed. I walked now with ease—no splint, no ligature—and I recognised the pair I had seen before in that neighbourhood.

The children made friends at once: they held the skeletons’ arms as if they were old relatives returned from a long field-work, stroked the bones of their long fingers with a tenderness that needed no translation. The poor creatures took their attentions kindly. They seemed on excellent terms with each other—common deprivation drawing them together; the loss of everything becoming the beginning of a new life, like a village rebuilt after a famine with stricter reverence for seed.

They had handfuls of herbs and were looking for more—presumably to rub their bones with, for how else could nourishment reach so rudimentary a system? The Little Ones examined the herbs as carefully as any cook sniffing a bundle before chopping: sharp, resinous stems; leaves that gave off bitterness when bruised; small blossoms with a honeyed breath. Then they gathered the same sorts and filled the skeletons’ outstretched hands.

They bade them goodbye, promising to come again, chattering as we went that they had not known such nice people lived in the same forest. Their language—whatever its roots—carried a whole ethic in the way they said “nice,” as if kindness were a local crop you could cultivate.

When at last we came to the nest-village, I stayed one night to see them resettled. Lona still looked like one just dead; there seemed no need to hurry. Lilith had eaten nothing; her eyes remained shut. Fearful she might die before we reached the end of our journey, I went to her in the night and laid my bare arm upon her lips.

She bit into it so fiercely that I cried out. I do not know how I got away; I came to myself lying beyond her reach, the pulse in my arm thudding like a drum in a hollow room. The wound burned with that intimate fire that tells you another being has taken something from you, not merely flesh but will.

It was morning. At once I prepared for departure.

I chose twelve Little Ones—not the biggest and strongest, but the sweetest and merriest, the ones whose laughter carried furthest without turning shrill. I mounted them on six elephants. I took also two of the wise Clumsies, as the children called them, to bear the princess. I rode again on Lona’s horse, carrying Lona’s body before me.

As nearly as I could judge, I took the direct way across the left branch of the dead river-bed to the House of Bitterness. I hoped there to learn how best to cross the broader, rougher branch beyond, and how to avoid the basin of monsters. I dreaded the former for the elephants, the latter for the children. In a world where names have power, “basin of monsters” sounded like a word from a dominant lexicon—one that flattens many kinds of danger into one saleable fear. Yet the fear was real enough.

The third night, in the desert between the two branches of the dead river, was terrible.

We stopped the elephants in a sheltered place and let Lilith slip down between them to lie on the sand until morning. She seemed quite dead, but I did not think she was. I laid myself a little way from her, with Lona’s body by my other side, to keep watch at once over the dead and the dangerous.

The moon hung half-way down the west—pale, thoughtful—mottling the desert with shadows like sifted flour. Then, of a sudden, she was eclipsed: still visible, but sending forth no light, as though a thin veil had been drawn over a lamp. A thick, diaphanous film covered her patient beauty, and she looked troubled. The film swept aside a little, and I saw against her clearness the jagged outline of something bat-like—torn and hooked.

A cold wind came with a burning sting, like air that has passed through fire and carries its memory. Lilith was upon me.

Her hands were still bound; with her teeth she pulled from my shoulder the cloak Lona had made for me—work of patient fingers, woven against the day she must have known would come—and fixed her teeth in my flesh. I lay as one paralysed. Already my very life seemed flowing from me into her, like warmth being sucked from bread into a mouth that cannot be satisfied.

Then I remembered—remembered the simplest resistance, like remembering salt when a dish goes dull—and struck her on the hand.

She raised her head with a gurgling shriek, and I felt her shiver. I flung her from me and sprang to my feet.

She was on her knees, rocking to and fro. A second blast of hot-stinging cold swept us; the moon shone out clear again, as if the sky itself were disgusted by what had crossed it. I saw her face—gaunt, ghastly, smeared with red.

“Down, devil!” I cried.

“Where are you taking me?” she asked, her voice like a dull echo from a sepulchre—sound without warmth, words without any native soil beneath them.

“To your first husband,” I answered.

“He will kill me!” she moaned.

“At least he will take you off my hands!”

“Give me my daughter!” she suddenly screamed, grinding her teeth.

“Never! Your doom is upon you at last!”

“Loose my hands—for pity’s sake!” she groaned. “I am in torture. The cords are sunk in my flesh.”

“I dare not. Lie down!”

She threw herself on the ground like a log. The rest of the night passed in peace; and in the morning she again seemed dead.

Before evening we came in sight of the House of Bitterness.

At once one of the elephants drew alongside my horse. A Little One rode on his neck, leaning forward as if to whisper into my very ear, careful not to spill fear into the whole herd.

“Please, king, you are not going to that place?” he breathed.

“Indeed I am. We are going to stay the night there.”

“Oh, please, don’t! That must be where the cat-woman lives!”

“If you had ever seen her, you would not call her by that name.”

“Nobody ever sees her: she has lost her face! Her head is back and side all round.”

“She hides her face from dull, discontented people. Who taught you to call her the cat-woman?”

“I heard the bad giants call her so.”

“What did they say about her?”

“That she had claws to her toes.”

“It is not true. I know the lady. I spent a night at her house.”

“But she MAY have claws to her toes! You might see her feet, and her claws be folded up inside their cushions!”

“Then perhaps you think I have claws to my toes?”

“Oh, no; that can’t be! You are good!”

“The giants might have told you so,” I said, and let the question hang like a kettle just beginning to sing.

“We shouldn’t believe them about you!”

“Are the giants good?”

“No; they love lying.”

“Then why do you believe them about her? I know the lady is good; she cannot have claws.”

“Please how do you know she is good?”

“How do you know I am good?”

He fell back to tell the others what I had said. They hastened after me, and when they came up, their voices layered around me like a flock’s worried rustling.

“I would not take you to her house if I did not believe her good,” I said.

“We know you would not,” they answered; and the trust in their tone was a kind of bread—plain, hard perhaps, but sustaining.

“If I were to do something that frightened you—what would you say?”

“The beasts frightened us sometimes at first,” answered one, “but they never hurt us!”

“That was before we knew them!” added another.

“Just so,” I said. “When you see the woman in that cottage, you will know she is good. You may wonder at what she does, but she will always be good. I know her better than you know me. She will not hurt you—or if she does—”

“Ah, you are not sure about it, king dear!” a voice broke in. “You think she MAY hurt us!”

“I am sure she will never be unkind to you, even if she do hurt you.”

They were silent a while, letting that distinction—hurt and unkindness—settle in them like a new word in an old dialect.

“I’m not afraid of being hurt—a little—a good deal!” cried Odu at last. “But I should not like scratches in the dark! The giants say the cat-woman has claw-feet all over her house!”

“I am taking the princess to her,” I said.

“Why?”

“Because she is her friend.”

“How can she be good then?”

“Little Tumbledown is a friend of the princess,” I answered. “So is Luva: I saw them both, more than once, trying to feed her with grapes!”

“Little Tumbledown is good! Luva is very good!”

“That is why they are her friends.”

“Will the cat-woman—I mean the woman that isn’t the cat-woman, and has no claws to her toes—give her grapes?”

“She is more likely to give her scratches.”

“Why? You say she is her friend!”

“That is just why. A friend is one who gives us what we need, and the princess is sorely in need of a terrible scratching.”

Again silence; the desert listened too. The soundscape here was spare—wind dragging grains along the ground, the elephants’ slow breathing, leather creaking, the dry click of a pebble under hoof. No industrial roar, no false music piped in to sell courage. Fear had room to be itself.

“If any of you are afraid,” I said, “you may go home; I shall not prevent you. But I cannot take one with me who believes the giants rather than me, or one who will call a good lady the cat-woman!”

“Please, king,” said one, “I’m so afraid of being afraid!”

“My boy,” I answered, “there is no harm in being afraid. The only harm is in doing what Fear tells you. Fear is not your master. Laugh in his face and he will run away.”

“There she is—in the door waiting for us!” cried one, and covered his eyes.

“How ugly she is!” cried another, doing the same.

“You do not see her,” I said; “her face is covered!”

“She has no face!” they insisted, as if repeating a phrase the giants had coined for them—language that steals what it names.

“She has a very beautiful face. I saw it once. It is indeed as beautiful as Lona’s,” I added, and grief rose again like the smell of bread when you have no hunger left for it.

“Then what makes her hide it?”

“I think I know—anyhow, she has some good reason.”

“I don’t like the cat-woman! she is frightful!”

“You cannot like, and you ought not to dislike what you have never seen. Once more, you must not call her the cat-woman!”

“What are we to call her then, please?”

“Lady Mara.”

“That is a pretty name!” said a girl, and her voice made it prettier still. “I will call her ‘lady Mara’; then perhaps she will show me her beautiful face!”

Mara, dressed and muffled in white, stood indeed in the doorway to receive us. The cottage behind her looked like a humble place that had learned, over uncounted years, how to hold grief without letting it rot the rafters. Even before she spoke, I heard the difference in its quiet: not dead silence, but a listened-for stillness, as in a dairy at dawn.

“At last!” she said. “Lilith’s hour has been long on the way, but it is come. Everything comes. Thousands of years have I waited—and not in vain!”

She came to me, took my treasure from my arms, and carried Lona into the house as if she were carrying a loaf made for a festival—careful, reverent, accustomed to weight that is both nourishment and symbol. Then she returned and took the princess. Lilith shuddered but made no resistance. The beasts lay down by the door.

We followed our hostess. The Little Ones looked very grave, as if stepping into a story older than their own words.

Mara laid Lilith on a rough settle at one side of the room, unbound her, and turned to us.

“Mr. Vane,” she said, “and you, Little Ones, I thank you. This woman would not yield to gentler measures; harder must have their turn. I must do what I can to make her repent.”

The pitiful-hearted Little Ones began to sob, their small throats catching as if they had swallowed too dry a crust.

“Will you hurt her very much, lady Mara?” asked the girl who had praised her name, slipping her warm little hand into mine as if for anchorage.

“Yes; I am afraid I must; I fear she will make me,” answered Mara. “It would be cruel to hurt her too little. It would have all to be done again, only worse.”

“May I stop with her?”

“No, my child. She loves no one; therefore she cannot be WITH any one. There is One who will be with her, but she will not be with Him.”

“Will the shadow that came down the hill be with her?”

“The great Shadow will be in her, I fear; but he cannot be WITH her, or with any one. She will know I am beside her, but that will not comfort her.”

“Will you scratch her very deep?” asked Odu, going near and putting his hand in Mara’s. “Please, don’t make the red juice come!”

Mara caught him up, turned her back to the rest of us, drew the muffling down from her face, and held him at arm’s length so he might see her.

As if his face had been a mirror, I saw what he saw.

For one moment he stared, mouth open. Then a divine wonder rose in his countenance and swiftly changed to intense delight. He gazed, entranced; and when she set him down, he stood yet a moment looking up at her, lost in contemplation—then ran to us with the face of a prophet who knows a bliss he cannot tell, because there are joys no dominant language can own without bruising them.

Mara rearranged her mufflings and turned to the other children.

“You must eat and drink before you go to sleep,” she said. “You have had a long journey!”

She set the bread of her house before them and a jug of cold water.

They had never seen bread before. It was hard and dry—brown, dense, the sort of loaf made to last through lean weather. Yet they ate without any sign of distaste, chewing as if learning the meaning of grain by mouthfuls. They had never seen water before, and they drank it without demur; and one after another looked up from the draught with faces of glad astonishment, as if the coldness and purity had spoken in a tongue older than speech.

Then Mara led away the smallest, and the rest went trooping after her. With her own gentle hands, they told me, she put them to bed on the floor of the garret, as one lays kindling carefully so it will catch when the time comes—quietly, without fuss, letting the night’s true sounds settle around them like a blanket.

Their night went wrong in the manner a loaf goes wrong when the starter has soured without anyone naming the fault: nothing visible at first, no crash of pans, no shout from the scullery—only a wrongness that keeps rising, insisting, until even waking feels like a continuation of a dream you cannot translate into daylight.

In the morning the children brought their account with them as if it were a basket of gathered things: odd, intact, and trembling with what they could not set down in proper words.

All night, they said, the house had been busy—busy in silence. They were not woken by any creak of timber, any settling of stone, any of those homely noises a cottage makes the way a hearth makes its own little speech. The dark and the quiet were welded together so tight it became terror. Once a wind had filled the whole house as steam fills a lidded pot, shaking its insides so that the walls quivered like a horse shrugging off rain—yet not a moan came through keyhole or shutter. It passed like a sob swallowed before it could become sound.

After that they slept—then woke with a start so sharp it felt, to them, as though the bed had dipped under their bodies.

The house was filling with water.

Not river-water with its rush, nor rain-water with its drip, but the same strange water they had been made to drink: cold on the tongue, too clean, too intentional, as if it had been poured from a vessel whose inside had never known dust. It rose from below without gurgle or lap, swelling up through floor and stair until, in their telling, the garret was full to the rafters. And still there was no sound—no slosh, no sigh of wave against beam. When it sank, it left them dry and warm as if the whole thing had happened only to their skins’ remembrance.

They slept again. Woke again.

Then the air—inside and out—was full of cats.

Cats as thick as grain in a bin, swarming up and down, along and across, everywhere about the room. The children felt claws questing for purchase through the nightgowns Lady Mara had put on them—felt them with the raw certainty of touch, like burrs on wool—yet in the morning not one scratch marked their arms, not one snag caught in cloth. Only once did a sound arrive, a single thread of it: far off, out of the desert, the long howl of a huge great-grandmother-cat calling as if the world were her yard and the stars her kittens. At that instant every cat stilled—an obedience so complete it was worse than noise—and the silence closed again like a mouth.

After that they fell fast asleep and did not wake until the sun was rising.

Such was their report: terror in images, but with the curious domestic detail of the untouched fabric and the unmarked skin, as if the night had tried to make itself real and been refused by the very weave of things.

But I was not with the children. I was with the veiled woman and the princess through all those hours. Something of what took place I saw; much I only felt; and there was more that no eye could seize, as if the event moved in a language older than speech, where meaning is carried by presence as flavour is carried by steam.

As soon as Mara left the room with the little ones, my gaze fell on the white leopardess. I had thought we had left her behind us, but there she was, crouched in a corner, drawn tight into herself as if any widening of her body would invite what she feared. Her eyes held that look animals get when the soundscape has altered without producing a sound—when the world’s usual chorus is gone, and the absence itself is a predator.

A lamp stood on the high chimney-piece. Its light did not behave as light should in a cottage: sometimes the room seemed crowded with lamp-shadows, thick as smoke that has no smell; sometimes with cloudy forms that were not smoke and not mist, shapes like breath you can see but cannot breathe. The princess lay on the settle by the wall and appeared never to have moved hand or foot, as if she were a dish set out to cool and left untouched—only she was no food, and the waiting was hunger in reverse.

When Mara returned she drew the settle with Lilith upon it to the middle of the room, then sat down opposite me at the other side of the hearth. Between us burned a small fire, the kind a careful housekeeper keeps in reserve, not for show but for need—coals banked, flame held low, heat husbanded like precious fat.

Something terrible was on its way. The cloudy presences flickered and shook, as if the air itself had been stirred by an invisible spoon.

A silvery creature like a slowworm came crawling out from among them, slid over the clay floor, and crept into the fire. It did not hiss. It did not sizzle. The coals did not crack. We sat motionless, the way you sit when the slightest stirring might spill what must not be spilled.

The thing came nearer. Yet the hours passed. Midnight drew nigh, and there was no change any sense could name. The night was very still—not even the modest rustle of a settling log, not even the dry tick of wood cooling. Now and again I felt a sort of heave, but whether in earth or air or the waters under the earth, whether in my own body or in my soul, I could not tell. A dread sense of judgment lay over me like a weight—but I was not afraid. I had ceased to care for aught save the thing that must be done.

Then it was midnight.

The muffled woman rose, turned toward the settle, and slowly unwound the long swathes that hid her face. They dropped to the floor with the soft, dead sound of cloth falling on clay, and she stepped over them.

The feet of the princess were toward the hearth; Mara went to her head and, turning, stood behind it.

Then I saw her face.

It was lovely beyond speech—white and sad, heart-and-soul sad, but not unhappy, as though sorrow had been baked into it the way salt is baked into bread: not to spoil, but to make it fit for keeping. Great tears ran down her cheeks. She wiped them away with her robe, and her countenance grew very still. But for the pity in every line of her expression, she would have seemed severe.

She laid her hand on the head of the princess—on the hair that grew low on the forehead—and, stooping, breathed on the sallow brow.

The body shuddered.

“Will you turn away from the wicked things you have been doing so long?” Mara asked gently.

The princess did not answer.

Mara put the question again, the same soft, inviting tone, like a mother offering warm broth to a child who insists she is not hungry.

Still there was no sign of hearing.

She spoke the words a third time.

Then the seeming corpse opened its mouth and answered, her words forming themselves of something other than sound. They were not sounds, yet they were words to me—meaning arriving without the bridge of air, as if the room itself had learned to speak without disturbing its own silence.

“I will not,” she said. “I will be myself and not another!”

“Alas, you are another now, not yourself! Will you not be your real self?”

“I will be what I mean myself now.”

“If you were restored, would you not make what amends you could for the misery you have caused?”

“I would do after my nature.”

“You do not know it: your nature is good, and you do evil!”

“I will do as my Self pleases—as my Self desires.”

“You will do as the Shadow, overshadowing your Self inclines you?”

“I will do what I will to do.”

“You have killed your daughter, Lilith!”

“I have killed thousands. She is my own!”

“She was never yours as you are another’s.”

“I am not another’s; I am my own, and my daughter is mine.”

“Then, alas, your hour is come!”

“I care not. I am what I am; no one can take from me myself!”

“You are not the Self you imagine.”

“So long as I feel myself what it pleases me to think myself, I care not. I am content to be to myself what I would be. What I choose to seem to myself makes me what I am. My own thought makes me me; my own thought of myself is me. Another shall not make me!”

“But another has made you, and can compel you to see what you have made yourself. You will not be able much longer to look to yourself anything but what he sees you! You will not much longer have satisfaction in the thought of yourself. At this moment you are aware of the coming change!”

“No one ever made me. I defy that Power to unmake me from a free woman! You are his slave, and I defy you! You may be able to torture me—I do not know, but you shall not compel me to anything against my will!”

“Such a compulsion would be without value. But there is a light that goes deeper than the will, a light that lights up the darkness behind it: that light can change your will, can make it truly yours and not another’s—not the Shadow’s. Into the created can pour itself the creating will, and so redeem it!”

“That light shall not enter me: I hate it!—Begone, slave!”

“I am no slave, for I love that light, and will with the deeper will which created mine. There is no slave but the creature that wills against its creator. Who is a slave but her who cries, ‘I am free,’ yet cannot cease to exist!”

“You speak foolishness from a cowering heart! You imagine me given over to you: I defy you! I hold myself against you! What I choose to be, you cannot change. I will not be what you think me—what you say I am!”

“I am sorry: you must suffer!”

“But be free!”

“She alone is free who would make free; she loves not freedom who would enslave: she is herself a slave. Every life, every will, every heart that came within your ken, you have sought to subdue: you are the slave of every slave you have made—such a slave that you do not know it!—See your own self!”

Mara took her hand from the head of the princess and went two backward paces from her.

A soundless presence as of roaring flame possessed the house—the same, I presume, that to the children had been a silent wind. Involuntarily I turned to the hearth: its fire was a still, small, moveless glow, as if it were keeping its breath.

But I saw the worm-thing come creeping out, white-hot, vivid as incandescent silver—the live heart of essential fire. Along the floor it crawled toward the settle, going very slow. Yet more slowly it climbed upon it and laid itself, as unwilling to go further, at the feet of the princess.

I rose and stole nearer. Mara stood motionless, as one waiting an event foreknown.

The shining thing crawled onto a bare bony foot. It showed no suffering; neither was the settle scorched where it had lain. Slowly—very slowly—it crept along her robe until it reached her bosom, where it disappeared among the folds.

The face of the princess lay stonily calm, eyelids closed as over dead eyes. For some minutes nothing followed.

Then, on the dry, parchment-like skin, drops appeared as of the finest dew—at first like the sheen on a cooled kettle, then swelling at once into seed-pearls. They ran together and began to pour in streams. It was as if her body had been made into a vessel and was sweating out what it could no longer contain.

I darted forward to snatch the worm from her withered bosom and crush it with my foot.

But Mara—Mother of Sorrow—stepped between and drew aside the closed edges of the robe. No serpent was there. No searing trail. The creature had passed in by the centre of the black spot, piercing through joints and marrow to the thoughts and intents of the heart.

The princess gave one writhing, contorted shudder, and I knew the worm was in her secret chamber.

“She is seeing herself,” said Mara; and laying her hand on my arm, she drew me three paces from the settle.

Of a sudden the princess bent her body upward in an arch, then sprang to the floor and stood erect.

The horror in her face made me tremble lest her eyes should open and the sight of them overwhelm me. Her bosom heaved and sank, but no breath issued. Her hair hung and dripped; then it stood out from her head and emitted sparks; again it hung down and poured the sweat of her torture onto the floor in darkening splashes that had no smell, no saltiness—nothing the senses could claim as familiar.

I would have thrown my arms about her, but Mara stopped me.

“You cannot go near her,” she said. “She is far away from us, afar in the hell of her self-consciousness. The central fire of the universe is radiating into her the knowledge of good and evil, the knowledge of what she is. She sees at last the good she is not, the evil she is. She knows that she is herself the fire in which she is burning, but she does not know that the Light of Life is the heart of that fire. Her torment is that she is what she is. Do not fear for her; she is not forsaken. No gentler way to help her was left. Wait and watch.”

It may have been five minutes or five years that she stood thus—I cannot tell. Time, in that room, had the circular feel of work done by hand in the same season every year: kneading, resting, kneading again, until the dough itself seems to remember.

At last she flung herself on her face.

Mara went to her and stood looking down. Large tears fell from Mara’s eyes onto the woman who had never wept and would not weep.

“Will you change your way?” Mara asked at length.

“Why did he make me such?” gasped Lilith. “I would have made myself—oh, so different! I am glad it was he that made me and not I myself! He alone is to blame for what I am! Never would I have made such a worthless thing! He meant me such that I might know it and be miserable! I will not be made any longer!”

“Unmake yourself, then,” said Mara.

“Alas, I cannot! You know it, and mock me! How often have I not agonised to cease, but the tyrant keeps me being! I curse him!—Now let him kill me!” The words came in jets, as from a dying fountain, spurting and failing.

“Had he not made you,” Mara answered, gently and slowly, “you could not even hate him. But he did not make you such. You have made yourself what you are.—Be of better cheer: he can remake you.”

“I will not be remade!”

“He will not change you; he will only restore you to what you were.”

“I will not be aught of his making.”

“Are you not willing to have that set right which you have set wrong?”

She lay silent. Her suffering seemed to ease, not as a sickness leaves, but as if some inner grip had slackened.

“If you are willing,” said Mara, “put yourself again on the settle.”

“I will not,” Lilith answered, forcing the words through clenched teeth.

Then a wind seemed to wake inside the house, blowing without sound or impact; and a water began to rise that had no lap in its ripples, no sob in its swell. It was cold, but did not benumb. Unseen and noiseless it came. It smote no sense in me, yet I knew it rising, the way you know a broth is nearing boil by the thickening of its surface before it speaks.

I saw it lift her at last and float her. Gently it bore her, unable to resist, and left rather than laid her on the settle. Then it sank swiftly away.

The strife of thought—accusing and excusing—began afresh and gathered fierceness. Lilith’s soul lay naked to the torture of pure inward light. She began to moan, then to sigh deep sighs, then to murmur as if holding colloquy with a dividual self: her queendom was no longer whole; it was divided against itself.

One moment she exulted as over her worst enemy, and wept; the next she writhed as in the embrace of a friend her soul hated, and laughed like a demon.

At length she began what seemed a tale about herself in a language so strange, and in forms so shadowy, that I could but here and there understand a little. Yet the language seemed the primeval shape of one I knew well, and the forms belonged to dreams that had once been mine but refused to be recalled. Now and then the tale touched things Adam had read from the disparted manuscript, and often it alluded to influences and forces—vices too, I could not help suspecting—with which I was unacquainted. It struck me with a pang like hearing an old recipe spoken in a lost dialect: you recognise the cadence, you smell the remembered kitchen, but the names of the herbs have fallen out of your mouth.

She ceased. Again came the horror in her hair—the sparkling and flowing alternate.

I sent a beseeching look to Mara.

“Those, alas, are not the tears of repentance,” she said. “The true tears gather in the eyes. Those are far more bitter, and not so good. Self-loathing is not sorrow. Yet it is good, for it marks a step in the way home, and in the father’s arms the prodigal forgets the self he abominates. Once with his father, he is to himself of no more account. It will be so with her.”

She went nearer and said, “Will you restore that which you have wrongfully taken?”
