“I have taken nothing,” Lilith answered, forcing out the words in spite of pain, “that I had not the right to take. My power to take manifested my right.”

Mara left her.

Gradually my soul grew aware of an invisible darkness, something more terrible than aught that had yet made itself felt. A horrible Nothingness, a Negation positive, infolded her. The border of its being—that was yet no being—touched me, and for one ghastly instant I seemed alone with Death Absolute.

It was not the absence of everything I felt, but the presence of Nothing.

The princess dashed herself from the settle to the floor with an exceeding great and bitter cry. It was the recoil of Being from Annihilation.

“For pity’s sake,” she shrieked, “tear my heart out, but let me live!”

With that there fell upon her—and upon us who watched—the perfect calm as of a summer night when the last machine has stopped and the fields can be heard again: an earthen hush, complete, as if the world had put away its harsh tools and returned to its older music. Suffering had all but reached the brim of her life’s cup, and a hand had emptied it.

She raised her head, half rose, and looked around. A moment more and she stood erect with the air of a conqueror, as if she had won a battle no one else could see. Dareful she had met her spiritual foes; they had withdrawn defeated. She raised her withered arm above her head, a paean of unholy triumph in her throat—

When suddenly her eyes fixed in a ghastly stare.

What was she seeing?

I looked, and saw: before her, cast from an unseen heavenly mirror, stood the reflection of herself, and beside it a form of splendour and beauty.

She trembled and sank again on the floor, helpless. She knew: the one what God had intended her to be, the other what she had made herself.

The rest of the night she lay altogether motionless.

With the gray dawn growing in the room, she rose, turned to Mara, and said in prideful humility, “You have conquered. Let me go into the wilderness and bewail myself.”

Mara looked at her a moment, and returned, “Begin, then, and set right in the place of wrong.”

“I know not how,” Lilith replied—with the look of one who foresees and fears the answer.

“Open thy hand,” said Mara, “and let that which is in it go.”

A fierce refusal struggled for passage, but she kept it prisoned.

“I cannot,” she said. “I have no longer the power. Open it for me.”

She held out the offending hand. It was more a paw than a hand; even in the dim it looked cramped by long clenching, as if the muscles had forgotten any other work.

Mara did not even look at it.

“You must open it yourself,” she said quietly.

“I have told you I cannot!”

“You can if you will—not indeed at once, but by persistent effort. What you have done, you do not yet wish undone—do not yet intend to undo!”

“You think so, I dare say,” the princess flared, insolence breaking through like bitterness in a sauce, “but I KNOW that I cannot open my hand!”

“I know you better than you know yourself, and I know you can. You have often opened it a little way. Without trouble and pain you cannot open it quite, but you CAN open it. At worst you could beat it open! I pray you, gather your strength, and open it wide.”

“I will not try what I know impossible. It would be the part of a fool!”

“Which you have been playing all your life! Oh, you are hard to teach!”

Defiance returned to Lilith’s face. She turned her back on Mara, saying, “I know what you have been tormenting me for! You have not succeeded, nor shall you succeed! You shall yet find me stronger than you think! I will yet be mistress of myself! I am still what I have always known myself—queen of Hell, and mistress of the worlds!”

Then came the most fearful thing of all.

I did not know what it was. I knew myself unable to imagine it. I knew only that if it came near me I should die of terror. I now know it was LIFE IN DEATH—life dead, yet existent. Lilith had had glimpses of it before, but it had never been with her until now.

She stood as she had turned. Mara went and sat down by the fire. Fearing to stand alone with the princess, I went also and sat again by the hearth.

Something began to depart from me. A sense of cold—yet not what we call cold—crept, not into, but out of my being and pervaded it. The lamp of life and the eternal fire seemed dying together, and I was about to be left with naught but the consciousness that I had been alive.

Mercifully, the bereavement did not go so far. My thought went back to Lilith.

Something was taking place in her which we did not know. We did not feel what she felt, but we felt something of the misery it caused her. The thing itself was in her, not in us; its reflex—her misery—reached us and was reflected in us: she was in the outer darkness, and we were present with her who was in it.

We were not in the outer darkness. Had we been, we could not have been with her; we should have been timelessly, spacelessly, absolutely apart. The darkness knows neither the light nor itself; only the light knows itself and the darkness also.

Something was gone from her which then first, by its absence, she knew to have been with her every moment of her wicked years. The source of life had withdrawn itself; all that was left her of conscious being was the dregs of her dead and corrupted life.

She stood rigid.

Mara buried her head in her hands.

I gazed on the face of one who knew existence but not love—knew neither life, nor joy, nor good. With my eyes I saw the face of a live death. She knew life only to know that it was dead, and that, in her, death lived.

It was not merely that life had ceased in her, but that she was consciously a dead thing. She had killed her life, and was dead—and knew it. She must death it for ever and ever. She had tried her hardest to unmake herself and could not. She was a dead life. She could not cease. She must be.

In her face I read beyond its misery—saw in its dismay that the dismay behind it was more than it could manifest. It sent out a livid gloom; the light that was in her was darkness, and after its kind it shone. She was what God could not have created. She had usurped beyond her share in self-creation, and her part had undone His. She saw now what she had made, and behold, it was not good. She was as a conscious corpse whose coffin would never come to pieces, never set her free. Her bodily eyes stood wide open, as if gazing into the heart of horror essential—her own indestructible evil. Her right hand also was now clenched—upon existent Nothing—her inheritance.

But with God all things are possible.

Without change of look, without sign of purpose, Lilith walked toward Mara. Mara felt her coming and rose to meet her.

“I yield,” said the princess. “I cannot hold out. I am defeated.—Not the less, I cannot open my hand.”

“Have you tried?” asked Mara.

“I am trying now with all my might.”

“I will take you to my father. You have wronged him worst of the created; therefore he best of the created can help you.”

“How can HE help me?”

“He will forgive you.”

“Ah, if he would but help me to cease! Not even that am I capable of! I have no power over myself; I am a slave! I acknowledge it. Let me die.”

“A slave thou art that shall one day be a child!” answered Mara. “Verily, thou shalt die, but not as thou thinkest. Thou shalt die out of death into life. Now is the Life for thee, that never was against thee!”

Like her mother, in whom lay the motherhood of all the world, Mara put her arms around Lilith and kissed her on the forehead.

The fiery-cold misery went out of Lilith’s eyes, and their fountains filled. She lifted her and bore her to her own bed in a corner of the room, laid her softly upon it, and closed her eyes with hands that moved like quiet mercy.

Lilith lay and wept.

The Lady of Sorrow went to the door and opened it.

Morn, with the Spring in her arms, waited outside.

Softly they stole in at the opened door, with a gentle wind in the skirts of their garments. It flowed and flowed about Lilith, rippling the unknown, upwaking sea of her life eternal—rippling it and rippling it, until at length she who had been as a weed cast on dry sand to wither should know herself an inlet of the everlasting ocean, henceforth to flow into her for ever and ebb no more.

She answered the morning wind with reviving breath, and began to listen.

For in the skirts of the wind had come the rain—the soft rain that heals the mown, the many-wounded grass—soothing it with the sweetness of all music, the hush that lives between music and silence. It bedewed the desert places around the cottage, and the sands of Lilith’s heart heard it and drank it in, as parched grain drinks its first wash, swelling toward bread again.

When Mara returned to sit by her bed, Lilith’s tears were flowing softer than the rain, and soon she was fast asleep.

The Mother of Sorrows rose without haste, as if she would not wake the grief too suddenly, drew her veil closer about her face, and went to call the Little Ones.

They slept like dough left to prove in a warm corner—still, deep, and whole; yet at her first word they started up at once, bright-eyed and ready, as if the night had been no more than the turning of a page. Down the stair they went after her in a little patter of bare feet, their sounds small and soft in the house’s hush, and she led them to where the princess lay.

Lilith slept, and in her sleep her tears had not ceased. They ran steadily, thread after thread, along the hollows of her cheeks, gathered at her jaw, and fell—clear drops with a faint salt tang that seemed to sharpen the air. The boys’ faces, that had come in laughing, stilled into wonder.

“The sky is falling!” whispered one, staring out at the rain beyond the window.

“The white juice is running out of the princess!” cried another, not mocking but awed, as though he had found a spring where no spring should be.

Odu leaned close, peering at the thin, glistening tracks. “Is it rivers?”

“Yes,” answered Mara, “the most wonderful of all rivers.”

Odu frowned, working at the word as if it were a crust too hard for his teeth. “I thought rivers was bigger, and rushed—like a lot of Little Ones, making loud noises!” He glanced toward me, for from me alone he had heard the thing described.

“Look at the rivers of the sky,” said Mara, and her voice seemed to tune itself to the rain’s steady drumming. “See how they come down to wake up the waters under the earth. Soon will the rivers be flowing everywhere—merry and loud, like thousands and thousands of happy children. Oh, how glad they will make you, Little Ones! You have never seen any, and do not know how lovely is the water.”

“That will be the glad of the ground that the princess is grown good,” said Odu. “See the glad of the sky!”

“Are the rivers the glad of the princess?” asked Luva. “They are not her juice, for they are not red!”

“They are the juice inside the juice,” said Mara.

Odu touched a finger to his own eye, tasted the wet on his skin by instinct though not with his tongue, and shook his head as if the matter would not come right in his understanding.

“Princess will not bite now!” said Luva, keeping her gaze fixed on Lilith’s face as though watching a beast that might wake.

“No; she will never do that again,” replied Mara. “But now we must take her nearer home.”

“Is that a nest?” asked Sozo.

“Yes; a very big nest. But we must take her to another place first.”

“What is that?”

“It is the biggest room in all this world.” Mara’s eyes went far away for a moment, as if she saw the room already crowded. “But I think it is going to be pulled down: it will soon be too full of little nests. Go and get your clumsies.”

“Please are there any cats in it?” asked a boy, anxious in the practical way of children.

“Not one. The nests are too full of lovely dreams for one cat to get in.”

“We shall be ready in a minute,” said Odu, and ran out, followed by all except Luva.

Lilith’s eyelids fluttered; she was awake now, listening. A sad smile lay on her mouth, as if she heard herself spoken of like a sick creature to be carried. Luva stood close beside her.

“But her rivers are running so fast!” Luva said, distressed, watching the wet darken the pillow cloth. “Her robe is all—I don’t know what. Clumsies won’t like it!”

“They won’t mind it,” answered Mara. “Those rivers are so clean that they make the whole world clean.”

I had fallen asleep by the fire; I had been awake for some time, hearing their voices and the rain’s steady cooking of the silence into something bearable. Now I rose.

“It is time to mount, Mr. Vane,” said our hostess.

“Tell me,” I said, “is there not a way by which to avoid the channels and the den of monsters?”

“There is an easy way across the river-bed, which I will show you,” she answered; “but you must pass once more through the monsters.”

“I fear for the children.”

“Fear will not once come nigh them,” she said, and there was something in her tone like a remembered lullaby—older than words, older than the fear itself.

We left the cottage. The beasts stood waiting about the door, patient as beasts that know their work is not hunting but bearing. Odu was already astride the neck of one of the two that were to carry the princess.

I mounted Lona’s horse. Mara brought her body and placed it in my arms—so light, so still, like bread cooled and gone stale before its time. When she came out again with Lilith, a cry of delight broke from the children: the princess was no longer muffled. Her face, uncovered, shone pale under the morning’s clean light, her lashes dark against her skin, her mouth set as if she feared even her own breath. The boys, entranced, forgot to help.

But the great elephants did not forget. With trunks gentle as hands that have kneaded dough and learned tenderness, one looped about her body, the other under her knees; with Mara’s guiding touch they laid her along between them as if setting a fragile platter between two steady boards.

“Why does the princess want to go?” asked a small boy. “She would keep good if she staid here!”

“She wants to go, and she does not want to go: we are helping her,” answered Mara. “She will not keep good here.”

“What are you helping her to do?”

“To go where she will get more help—help to open her hand, which has been closed for a thousand years.”

“So long?” The child pondered, like an elder weighing a seed against a season. “Then she has learned to do without it: why should she open it now?”

“Because it is shut upon something that is not hers.”

“Please, lady Mara, may we have some of your very dry bread before we go?” asked Luva suddenly, with that sharp turn children make from dread to hunger.

Mara smiled and went back. She returned with four loaves—so hard and brown they looked as if the oven had kissed them longer than kindness would advise—and a great jug of water. The loaves gave off a scent of scorched grain and honest heat; the water smelled of stone and depth.

“We will eat as we go,” they said; but the jug went from hand to hand at once. They drank with bright astonishment, as if water were a festival.

“I think,” said one, wiping his mouth, “it must be elephant-juice! It makes me so strong!”

We set out. The Lady of Sorrow walked with us—her beauty not loud like sunlight on metal, but steady like fire in a hearth—and the white leopardess followed, silent as a thought.

At first I thought our hostess meant only to show us the way across the channels and then return. Soon I knew she would go with us to the end. I would have dismounted that she might ride, but she would not.

“I have no burden to carry,” she said. “The children and I will walk together.”

It was the loveliest of mornings; the sun poured out his brightest, the wind brought his sweetest, yet the desert took no comfort, for it had no water to answer. The world sounded thirsty. Even the wind’s voice seemed to search for a creek-bed that would sing back.

We crossed the channels without difficulty. The Little Ones gambolled about Mara, their small talk and scuffing feet making a kind of domestic music in that bare place, as if they walked a kitchen floor rather than a waste. But we did not reach the ridge above the bad burrow until the sun was already slipping away.

Then I made the Little Ones mount their elephants, for the moon might be late, and I could not help my anxiety. The Lady of Sorrow came by my side and led the way; the elephants followed, the two bearing Lilith in the centre; the leopardess kept behind.

Just as we reached the frightful margin, the moon looked up and showed the shallow basin lying before us—smooth, quiet, deceitfully settled, like a pan of milk with a skin upon it.

Mara stepped into it. Nothing answered her tread, nor the hoofbeats of my horse: no crack, no sigh. But the moment the elephants carrying Lilith touched that ground, it woke like something sleeping ill. The seeming earth began to heave and boil, and the whole brood of the nest was commoved. It was not only sight; the place had its own brutal acoustics—the wet suck of shifting soil, the hard clap of something snapping shut, the hollow cough of buried cavities opening. Noise with no music in it, noise like a machine grinding bone.

Monsters uprose on all sides—every neck stretched, every beak and claw thrust out, every mouth gaping. Long-billed heads, horribly jawed faces, knotty tentacles without number reached toward Lilith. She lay rigid in an agony of fear; she dared not move so much as a finger.

Whether those hideous things even saw the children, I doubt. Not one touched a child. Not one foul member passed the living rampart of her body-guard to lay hold of her.

“Little Ones,” I cried, “keep your elephants close about the princess. Be brave; they will not touch you.”

“What will not touch us? We don’t know what to be brave at!” they called back, and I understood then: they were unaware. They could not see one of the deformities around them.

“Never mind then,” I returned. “Only keep close.”

They were panoplied in their blindness. Incapacity to see was their safety; what they could not be aware of could not hurt them. I, who saw, felt every shape like a taste in the mouth—a bitterness, a rancid taint—things that did not belong in the world’s stew, yet rose here like scum.

Mara kept a few paces in front of me when a solitary head—bodiless, obscene in its independence—bounced on the path between us. The leopardess came rushing under the elephants from behind and would have seized it, but with frightful contortions and a loathsome howl it twisted away, spun like a thrown pot, and buried itself in the ground.

The dead in my arms—Lona, dear weight of love and loss—seemed to absolve me from fear. I looked on unmoved, though never had I beheld such a crew accursed.

The leopardess shivered often now; the cold in that basin was not honest night-cold but something drawn from the absence of living breath. Then, to my left, the ground began to heave. A low wave of earth came slinking toward us; it rose, and out of it slouched a dreadful head with fleshy tubes for hair. It opened an oval mouth and snapped at me. The leopardess sprang—and fell baffled beyond it, finding nothing solid to seize, as if the thing were a lie given teeth.

Almost under our feet shot up the head of an enormous snake, eyes wallowing with a lamp-like glare. Again she rushed—again nothing. At a third horror she darted with the same fury and met the same emptiness; at last she ceased to heed the phantom-horde and paced on, sullen, wise, as if she had learned that some evils cannot be bitten.

I hastened the crossing, for the moon was carrying herself strangely. Even as she rose she seemed ready to sink back, and once I saw her drop her own breadth as if she had been struck. Her arc was low; now she began to descend with alarming speed, as if the sky itself were tired of holding light.

We were almost over when between us and the border of the basin rose a long neck, topped by what seemed the head of a corpse, its mouth half open and crowded with canine teeth like a butcher’s rack. I went on; it retreated, then drew aside.

The Lady stepped onto firm land. But the leopardess, between us, roused once more, turned, and flew at the throat of that terror.

I remained where I was until the elephants, with Lilith and the children, were safe upon the bank. Then I turned to look after the leopardess.

That moment the moon went down.

For an instant I saw leopardess and snake-thing convolved in a cloud of dust; then darkness swallowed them. My horse trembled, wheeled, and in three bounds overtook the elephants.

As we came up with them, a shapeless jelly dropped upon Lilith—cold and wet as fat scraped from a pan. Immediately a white dove fell upon it, stabbing with its beak. It made a squelching, sucking sound, and fell off, as if the foulness had weight even for a bird of peace.

Then I heard the voice of a woman speaking with Mara in the dark, and I knew the voice.

“I fear she is dead!” said Mara.

“I will send and find her,” answered the Mother. “But why, Mara, shouldst thou at all fear for her or for any one? Death cannot hurt her who dies doing the work given her to do.”

“I shall miss her sorely; she is good and wise. Yet I would not have her live beyond her hour!”

“She has gone down with the wicked; she will rise with the righteous. We shall see her again ere very long.”

“Mother,” I said—though I could not see her—“we come to you many, but most of us are Little Ones. Will you be able to receive us all?”

“You are welcome every one,” she answered. “Sooner or later all will be little ones, for all must sleep in my house! It is well with those that go to sleep young and willing! My husband is even now preparing her couch for Lilith. She is neither young nor quite willing, but it is well indeed that she is come.”

After that I heard no more. Mother and daughter moved away through the dark, their steps quiet as careful hands in a sleeping room.

But we saw a light in the distance, and toward it we went stumbling over the moor. Adam stood in the door holding a candle to guide us, talking with his wife; behind him she set bread and wine upon the table as if receiving travellers were as common as setting supper. The bread’s smell met us even outside—warm grain, crust browned; and the wine held a sweetness like fruit kept through winter, a memory preserved against forgetting.

“Happy children,” I heard Eve say, “to have looked already on the face of my daughter! Surely it is the loveliest in the great world!”

When we reached the door, Adam welcomed us almost merrily. He set the candle on the threshold and went to the elephants. He would have taken the princess to carry her in; but Lilith repulsed him. With a sudden pride she thrust the elephants apart and stood erect between them. They stepped away obediently, leaving her alone with him who had been her husband—gaunt now, ashamed of her uncomeliness, yet unsubmissive.

He stood with welcome in his eyes, shining through severity. “We have long waited for thee, Lilith!” he said.

She made no answer.

Eve and her daughter came to the door. “The mortal foe of my children!” murmured Eve, radiant in her beauty.

“Your children are no longer in her danger,” said Mara; “she has turned from evil.”

“Trust her not hastily, Mara,” answered her mother; “she has deceived a multitude!”

“But you will open to her the mirror of the Law of Liberty, mother, that she may go into it, and abide in it! She consents to open her hand and restore: will not the great Father restore her to inheritance with His other children?”

“I do not know Him!” murmured Lilith, and her voice shook as if the words were not in her native tongue, as if she spoke a name that would not fit her mouth.

“Therefore it is that thou art miserable,” said Adam.

“I will go back whence I came!” she cried, wringing her hands, and turned to depart.

“That is indeed what I would have thee do, where I would have thee go—to Him from whom thou camest! In thy agony didst thou not cry out for Him?”

“I cried out for Death—to escape Him and thee!”

“Death is even now on his way to lead thee to Him. Thou knowest neither Death nor the Life that dwells in Death! Both befriend thee. I am dead, and would see thee dead, for I live and love thee. Thou art weary and heavy-laden: art thou not ashamed? Is not the being thou hast corrupted become to thee at length an evil thing? Wouldst thou yet live on in disgrace eternal? Cease thou canst not: wilt thou not be restored and BE?”

Lilith stood silent, head bowed, as if the words pressed on her shoulders.

“Father,” said Mara, “take her in thine arms, and carry her to her couch. There she will open her hand, and die into life.”

“I will walk,” said the princess.

Adam turned and led the way. Lilith followed him into the cottage with a slow, stiff step, like someone moving through a room where every object remembers her crimes.

Then Eve came out to me where I sat with Lona in my bosom. She reached up her arms, took her from me, and carried her in with a tenderness that made the dead seem almost fed, almost warmed. I dismounted; the children also.

Outside, the horse and the elephants stood shivering. Mara patted and stroked every one. At her touch they lay down and fell asleep, as if she had found the hidden place behind their ears where peace lives.

She led us into the cottage. The Little Ones gathered at the table as naturally as chicks to scattered grain, and she gave them the bread and wine Eve had set. They took the bread in both hands, broke it with a crackle of crust, and chewed with the solemn delight of those who truly taste. The wine they sipped as if it were a story older than their words—sweet, dark, and warm in the throat, setting a quiet humming in their small bodies that steadied them after the basin’s harsh, unnatural noise.

Adam and Lilith stood together, both silent.

Eve came from the chamber of death where she had laid Lona down, and offered bread and wine to the princess.

“Thy beauty slays me! It is death I would have, not food!” Lilith said, and turned away.

“This food will help thee to die,” answered Eve.

But Lilith would not taste.

“If thou wilt nor eat nor drink, Lilith,” said Adam, “come and see the place where thou shalt lie in peace.”

He led the way through the door of death, and she followed submissive. But when her foot crossed the threshold she drew it back and pressed her hand to her bosom, struck through by that cold which is not of weather. A wild blast fell roaring on the roof and died away in a moan, as if some great creature had thrown itself against the house and slid away, thwarted.

Lilith stood ghastly with terror.

“It is he!” said her voiceless lips; I read their motion.

“Who, princess!” I whispered.

“The great Shadow,” she murmured.

“Here he cannot enter,” said Adam. “Here he can hurt no one. Over him also is power given me.”

“Are the children in the house?” asked Lilith, and at the word Eve’s heart began to move toward her, as if even such fear could be a doorway.

“He never dared touch a child,” said Eve. “Nor have you either ever hurt a child. Your own daughter you have but sent into the loveliest sleep, for she was already a long time dead when you slew her. And now Death shall be the atonemaker; you shall sleep together.”

“Wife,” said Adam, “let us first put the children to bed, that she may see them safe!”

He came back to fetch them.

As soon as he was gone, Lilith sank to her knees before Eve, clasped her knees, and spoke like one pleading in a language she barely commands. “Beautiful Eve, persuade your husband to kill me: to you he will listen! Indeed I would but cannot open my hand.”

“You cannot die without opening it. To kill you would not serve you,” answered Eve.

“But indeed he cannot! no one can kill you but the Shadow; and whom he kills never knows she is dead, but lives to do his will, and thinks she is doing her own.”

“Show me then to my grave; I am so weary I can live no longer. I must go to the Shadow—yet I would not!”

She did not understand; she could not. She struggled to rise and fell at Eve’s feet.

The Mother lifted her and carried her inward as one carries a child too exhausted to walk. I followed Adam and Mara and the Little Ones into the chamber of death.

We passed Eve with Lilith in her arms and went farther in.

“You shall not go to the Shadow,” I heard Eve say as we went by. “Even now is his head under my heel!”

Adam’s dim light glimmered on sleeping faces, and as he moved, the darkness closed over them again. The air itself seemed dead—perhaps because none of the sleepers breathed it in the way of the waking. Profound sleep filled the wide place. It was as if not one had stirred since last I was there: the forms I had then marked lay still. My father was as I had left him, only nearer to perfect peace; the woman beside him looked younger, as if the cold were not decay but a keeping, like fruit stored in a root-cellar against a future spring.

The darkness, the cold, the silence, the still air, the faces of the lovely dead made the children’s hearts beat softly, yet their tongues would talk—low, hushed, as if sound here mattered, as if a loud word would be a kind of violence.

“What a curious place to sleep in!” whispered one. “I would rather be in my nest!”

“It is SO cold!” said another.

“Yes, it is cold,” answered Adam; “but you will not be cold in your sleep.”

“Where are our nests?” asked more than one, looking about and seeing no couch unoccupied.

“Find places, and sleep where you choose,” replied Adam.

Instantly they scattered, fearless beyond the candle’s reach; yet we could still hear their gentle voices, and it was plain they saw where I could not.

“Oh,” cried one, “here is such a beautiful lady!—may I sleep beside her? I will creep in quietly, and not wake her.”

“Yes, you may,” answered Eve’s voice behind us.

We came to the couch while the little fellow was still creeping under the sheet, slow and careful as if sliding into a warm oven without disturbing the loaves. He laid his head beside the lady’s, looked up at us once, and was still. His eyelids fell; he slept.

We went a little farther. Another child had climbed onto the couch of a woman. “Mother! mother!” he cried, kneeling over her, face close to hers. Then, looking up at us: “She’s so cold she can’t speak—but I will soon make her warm!” He lay down, pressed close, and put his little arm over her. At once he too was asleep, smiling an absolute content, as if the warmth he promised had been given him.

We came to a third Little One: Luva. She stood on tiptoe, leaning over the edge of a couch.

“My own mother wouldn’t have me,” she said softly, as if ashamed of the words. “Will you?”

Receiving no reply, she looked up at Eve.

The great mother lifted her to the couch; Luva slipped under the snowy covering without more ceremony, like a tired kitten finding a place beside a hearth.

By this time each of the Little Ones—save three boys—had found at least an unobjecting bedfellow, and lay still and white beside a still, white woman. The little orphans had adopted mothers.

One tiny girl had chosen a father to sleep with—and that was mine. A boy lay beside the beautiful matron with the slow-healing hand.

On the middle one of the three couches hitherto unoccupied lay Lona. Eve set Lilith down beside it.

Adam pointed to the vacant couch on Lona’s right and said, “There, Lilith, is the bed I have prepared for you!”

Lilith glanced at her daughter—lying like a statue carved in semi-transparent alabaster—and shuddered from head to foot.

“How cold it is!” she murmured.

“You will soon begin to find comfort in the cold,” answered Adam.

“Promises to the dying are easy!” she said.

“But I know it: I too have slept. I am dead!”

“I believed you dead long ago; but I see you alive!”

“More alive than you know, or are able to understand. I was scarce alive when first you knew me. Now I have slept, and am awake; I am dead, and live indeed!”

“I fear that child,” she said, pointing to Lona. “She will rise and terrify me!”

“She is dreaming love to you.”

“But the Shadow!” Lilith moaned. “I fear the Shadow! He will be wroth with me!”

“He at sight of whom the horses of heaven start and rear, dares not disturb one dream in this quiet chamber!” said Adam. “When the Shadow comes here, it will be to lie down and sleep also. His hour will come, and he knows it will.”

“I shall dream then?”

“You will dream.”

“What dreams?”

“That I cannot tell, but none HE can enter into.”

“How long shall I sleep?”

“You and he will be the last to wake in the morning of the universe.”

Lilith lay down, drew the sheet over her, stretched herself straight, and lay still with open eyes.

Adam turned to Mara. She drew near.

“Lilith,” said Mara, “you will not sleep, if you lie there a thousand years, until you have opened your hand, and yielded that which is not yours to give or to withhold.”

“I cannot,” Lilith answered. “I would if I could, and gladly, for I am weary, and the shadows of death are gathering about me.”

“They will gather and gather, but they cannot infold you while yet your hand remains unopened. You may think you are dead, but it will be only a dream; you may think you have come awake, but it will still be only a dream. Open your hand, and you will sleep indeed—then wake indeed.”

“I am trying hard, but the fingers have grown together and into the palm.”

“I pray you put forth the strength of your will. For the love of life, draw together your forces and break its bonds!”

“I have struggled in vain; I can do no more. I am very weary, and sleep lies heavy upon my lids.”

“The moment you open your hand, you will sleep. Open it, and make an end.”

A tinge of colour rose in Lilith’s parchment face. The contorted hand trembled with agonised effort, as if the flesh remembered its own hardness and would not yield. Mara took it to help her.

“Hold, Mara!” cried Adam. “There is danger!”

Lilith turned her eyes upon Eve, beseeching. “There was a sword I once saw in your husband’s hands,” she whispered. “I fled when I saw it. I heard him who bore it say it would divide whatever was not one and indivisible!”

“I have the sword,” said Adam. “The angel gave it me when he left the gate.”

“Bring it,” pleaded Lilith, “and cut me off this hand that I may sleep.”

“I will,” he answered, and gave the candle to Eve. He went.

Lilith closed her eyes as if hiding from her own request.

In a few minutes Adam returned with an ancient weapon. The scabbard looked like vellum darkened by years; the hilt shone like gold that no handling could tarnish. He drew the blade. It flashed like a pale blue northern streamer—cold fire—and the light of it made Lilith open her eyes.

She saw the sword, shuddered, and held out her hand.

Adam took it. The sword gleamed once; there was a small gush of blood, startlingly red in that white place, and he laid the severed hand in Mara’s lap.

Lilith gave one moan and was already fast asleep.

Mara covered the arm with the sheet, and the three of them turned away.

“Will you not dress the wound?” I asked.

“A wound from that sword,” answered Adam, “needs no dressing. It is healing and not hurt.”

“Poor lady,” I said. “She will wake with but one hand!”

“Where the dead deformity clung,” replied Mara, and her voice was as certain as seedtime, “the true, lovely hand is already growing.”

We heard a childish voice behind us and turned again. Eve’s candle shone on Lilith’s sleeping face and the waking faces of three Little Ones grouped on the other side of her couch.

“How beautiful she is grown!” said one.

“Poor princess!” said another. “I will sleep with her. She will not bite any more!” He climbed into her bed, and sleep took him at once.

Eve drew the sheet over him with a tenderness that seemed to lay a quiet seal on the promise.

“I will go on her other side,” said the third. “She shall have two to kiss her when she wakes!”

“And I am left alone!” said the first, mournful as if loneliness were hunger.

“I will put you to bed,” said Eve. She gave the candle to her husband and led the child away.

We turned to go back toward the cottage. I was very sad, for no one had offered me a place in the house of the dead—no couch, no warm adoption into that hush where even fear is taught to speak softly.

Eve joined us and walked before with Adam. Mara walked beside me, carrying Lilith’s severed hand in the lap of her robe as one carries something that must not be dropped—not for horror, but for meaning.

As we stepped into the cottage we heard Eve’s voice: “Ah, you have found her! I sent them with the lantern,” she said to her husband, “to look for Mara’s leopardess: they have brought her.”

The door stood open. Two elephant-trunks came through it out of the night beyond, careful as servants bearing a dish too precious to jolt.

I followed Adam to the threshold. Between us we took the white creature from the elephants and carried her to the chamber we had just left, the women preceding us—Eve with the light, and Mara still with the hand.

There we laid the leopardess, beautiful and battered, across the feet of the princess: her fore-paws outstretched, her head couched between them, like a guardian laid down at the edge of a new sleep.

I turned from the stillness of Lona’s couch to where Eve sat, her presence like a hearth that had never once gone out.

“Mother,” I said, and the word tasted strange—older than any tongue I had spoken in my own world—“one couch beside Lona stands empty. I know well enough I have no claim; yet may I not sleep this night in your chamber with my dead? Pardon both my cowardice and my presumption. Take me in. I give myself up. I am sick of myself, and would fain sleep the sleep.”

“The couch next to Lona,” Eve answered, “is the one already made for you. But something waits to be done ere you sleep.”

“I am ready,” I said, though my body wanted nothing but to fold itself down like dough at last proved and spent.

She looked at me with a smile that had the gentleness of hands dusted in flour. “How do you know you can do it?”

“Because you require it.”

“What is it?”

She turned her head to Adam, as if the question belonged to him by right of long keeping, like a seed passed from palm to palm. “Is he forgiven, husband?”

“From my heart,” said Adam.

“Then tell him what he has to do.”

Adam turned not to me first, but to his daughter—Mara—who sat near, quiet as a well whose depth you cannot measure by sight. “Give me that hand, Mara, my child.”

She held it out in her lap. He took it as tenderly as one lifts a sprig of green from a bed after rain—no tearing, no haste, only the certainty of care.

“Let us go to the cottage,” he said to me. “There I will instruct you.”

As we went, the air itself seemed to remember an old violence: a sudden stormful blast rose, and with it a great flapping on the roof—like wings in panic, like torn canvas—then it sank again, not into peace but into a deep moan, as if the world, having cried out, laid its cheek back on the pillow of night.

When the door of the death-chamber was closed behind us, Adam sat, and I stood before him. The small room held a scent of earth and wood and something clean as water in a clay jar. It steadied me more than any reasoning could have done.

“You will remember,” Adam said, “how, after leaving my daughter’s house, you came to a dry rock, bearing the marks of an ancient cataract; you climbed that rock, and found a sandy desert.”

I remembered it as a mouth gone dry—an old river’s speech silenced, its grammar erased to a stony stutter. In my own land we spoke of dead languages as if they were books shut; here, the very land had lost its words.

“Go to that rock now,” he continued, “and from its summit walk deep into the desert. But go not many steps ere you lie down, and listen with your head on the sand. If you hear the murmur of water beneath, go a little farther, and listen again. If you still hear the sound, you are in the right direction. Every few yards you must stop, lie down, and hearken. If, listening thus, at any time you hear no sound of water, you are out of the way, and must hearken in every direction until you hear it again.”

He spoke as if teaching a child to bake by smell rather than by clock—to trust the quiet, the living signs, not the loud and false assurances. Not sight, not argument: ear to ground, cheek to the world.

“Keeping with the sound, and careful not to retrace your steps, you will soon hear it louder, and the growing sound will lead you to where it is loudest: that is the spot you seek. There dig with the spade I will give you, and dig until you come to moisture: in it lay the hand, cover it to the level of the desert, and come home.”

He paused, and his gaze sharpened, like a knife laid flat and true.

“But give good heed, and carry the hand with care. Never lay it down, in what place of seeming safety soever; let nothing touch it; stop nor turn aside for any attempt to bar your way; never look behind you; speak to no one, answer no one, walk straight on. It is yet dark, and the morning is far distant, but you must set out at once.”

He placed the hand in mine. It was Lilith’s hand—severed, light with a dreadful lightness, and yet heavy with the weight of what it meant. He brought me a spade, the handle smooth where years of use had polished it, the blade showing the honest wear of earth-work rather than battle.

“This is my gardening spade,” he said; “with it I have brought many a lovely thing to the sun.”

The words were simple, and they struck deeper than any oath. A tool that had opened ground for food and flower was now to open ground for judgment and mercy. The same iron for both: the difference lay in the hand that held it, and the intention that guided it.

I took it, and went out into the night.

It was very cold, and pitch-dark. The kind of dark that presses on the eyelids, that makes the world feel padded and uncertain, as if sound itself might be smothered before it reached you. To fall would be a dread thing, and the way I had to go was difficult even in broad sunlight.

But I had not set myself the task; and the minute I started I learned I was left to no chance. A pale light broke from the ground at every step, not shining far, only enough—like a cook’s lamp kept low so as not to wake a sleeping house—showing me exactly where next to set my foot. Through the heather and the low rocks I walked without once even stumbling. The night had its own economy: no waste, no glare—only what was necessary, given at the instant of need.

I found the bad burrow quite still. Not a wave arose, not a head appeared as I crossed it. The silence there was not health but a held breath. In my own world, noise had become a kind of tyranny—engines and shrieks and the constant hammered rhythm that leaves no room for a human mind to settle. Here, in this other place, there were silences that were also tyrannies: gaps where a river’s song ought to have been, emptiness where a natural choir had been silenced long ago.

A moon came, and herself showed me the easy way. Toward morning I was almost over the dry channels of the first branch of the river-bed, and not far, I judged, from Mara’s cottage.

The moon was very low and the sun not yet up when I saw, before me in the path—here narrowed by rocks—a figure covered from head to foot as with a veil of moonlit mist.

I kept on my way as if I saw nothing. I held Lilith’s hand close, and the spade in the other, the two burdens balancing like salt and bread: one for preservation, one for hunger.

The figure threw aside its veil.

“Have you forgotten me already?” said the princess—or what seemed she.

I neither hesitated nor answered; I walked straight on, jaw clenched on silence as on a bitter rind you must chew without spitting.

“You meant then to leave me in that horrible sepulchre! Do you not yet understand that where I please to be, there I am? Take my hand: I am alive as you!”

The words were sweetened like deceitful wine, meant to warm the will and loosen it. I was on the point of saying, Give me your left hand—but some memory not of thought but of touch restrained me. I held my peace, and steadily advanced.

“Give me my hand,” she suddenly shrieked, “or I will tear you in pieces: you are mine!”

She flung herself upon me.

I shuddered, but did not falter. Nothing touched me, and I saw her no more—as if the threat had been only a loudness, a blast of sound meant to drown out the quieter, truer murmur I had been sent to seek.

With measured tread along the path, filling it for some distance, came a body of armed men. The sound of their march struck the rocks with a hard, metallic certainty; the clash of their armour was like a brutal music, a parade meant to impress itself on the nerves and make obedience to fear feel like wisdom.

I walked through them—nor know whether they gave way to me, or were bodiless things. But they turned and followed. I heard and felt their march at my very heels, the iron rhythm trying to take possession of my breathing; but I cast no look behind, and, as if starved by my refusal, the sound of their steps and the clangour died away.

A little farther on, the moon now close to the horizon and the way in deep shadow, I descried, seated where the path was so narrow I could not pass her, a woman with muffled face.

“Ah,” she said, “you are come at last! I have waited here for you an hour or more! You have done well! Your trial is over. My father sent me to meet you that you might have a little rest on the way. Give me your charge, and lay your head in my lap; I will take good care of both until the sun is well risen. I am not bitterness always, neither to all men!”

Her voice had the cadence of a lullaby, the promise of a kitchen chair pulled out, a bowl of broth set down, a head allowed to drop into safe linen. And I was very weary. The temptation was terrible, not because it sounded false, but because it sounded plausible. What more likely, indeed, than that I was meant to meet the Lady of Sorrow on the road? What if, through slavish obedience to the letter of the command and lack of pure insight, I should trample under my feet the very person of the Lady?

My heart grew faint at the thought, then beat as if it would burst my bosom. I could almost smell rest—like warm bread, like water set by the bed.

Nevertheless my will hardened itself against my heart, and my step did not falter. I took my tongue between my teeth lest I should unawares answer, and kept on my way. If Adam had sent her, he could not complain that I would not heed her. Nor would the Lady of Sorrow love me the less that even she had not been able to turn me aside.

Just ere I reached the phantom, she pulled the covering from her face. Great indeed was her loveliness—but those were not Mara’s eyes. No lie could truly or for long imitate them: Mara’s gaze had the steadiness of a field known by generations, the quiet accuracy of one who measures by seasons rather than by hurry.

I advanced as if the thing were not there, and my foot found empty room. I had almost reached the other side when a Shadow—I think it was The Shadow—barred my way.

He seemed to have a helmet upon his head, but as I drew closer I perceived it was the head itself I saw—so distorted as to bear but a doubtful resemblance to the human. A cold wind smote me, dank and sickening—repulsive as the air of a charnel-house. It took hold of my breath and turned it sour; firmness forsook my joints, and my limbs trembled as if they would drop in a helpless heap.

For a moment the world was only that stench and that oppression, like being forced into a place where all the songs have been silenced and replaced by a single dead note—an industrial hum of despair.

I seemed to pass through him, but I think now that he passed through me: for an instant I was as one of the damned.

Then, as suddenly as a door opened onto morning, a soft wind like the first breath of a new-born spring greeted me, and before me arose the dawn.

My way now led me past the door of Mara’s cottage. It stood wide open, and upon the table I saw a loaf of bread and a pitcher of water. The loaf sat there like a vow made visible—crust browned, the cut lines on top opened in the bake, as if it had spoken in the oven and been heard. The pitcher held its plainness with dignity. In or around the cottage was neither howl nor wail. The absence of that noise was itself a mercy; it let the small domestic offerings be what they were: sustenance set out without barter, without spectacle.

I did not enter. I did not touch. My mouth ached with want, my throat with dryness, and yet Adam’s words lay on me like a binding cloth: let nothing touch it; stop not; turn not aside.

I came to the precipice that testified to the vanished river. The rock-face was scarred with the old water’s handwriting—channels, smoothings, hollows where the flood had once translated mountain into music. I climbed its worn face, and went on into the desert.

From its summit the sand lay like a page wiped clean—too clean, too uniform, the way a dominant tongue tries to make all other speech disappear. Yet beneath it, Adam had said, water murmured.

There at last, after much listening to and fro, I did as commanded. I went not many steps before I lay down and pressed my head to the sand. At first I heard nothing but my own blood in my ears, and the faint outer hush of dawn. Then—thin, uncertain, like a remembered word on the edge of forgetting—I caught a murmur far below, a living syllable.

I rose, went forward, lay down again. The sound was there. Again I moved, and again listened. Sometimes it faded, and I would lie with my cheek to the ground and turn my attention as one turns a cup to catch the last drops, until I found it again. I began to understand that this was not merely direction but discipline: to be guided by what is quiet and constant, not by what shouts.

At length the murmur grew fuller, nearer, almost eager—as if the hidden water strained toward being found. I determined the spot where it was loudest. Then I hung Lilith’s hand about my neck, as one might hang a bitter amulet that must not be set down, and began to dig.

It was a long labour. The sand would not hold an edge; it slid back upon itself, swallowing the space I made as quickly as I made it, so that I was forced to widen the hole, to work like one digging for a spring in a land that has forgotten how to keep water. The spade bit, lifted, shed; the grains rasped; my breath came in dry pulls. Yet Adam’s gardening spade was honest in my grip. Each thrust felt like an argument with the desert: you are not all there is; you are not the last word.

At length my blade threw up a damp spadeful. The smell rose—faint, exquisite, unmistakable: the scent of moisture in earth, the promise of life, like the first steam from a pot when water begins to wake. I flung the sexton-tool on the verge, and laid down the hand.

A little water was already oozing from under its fingers.

I sprang out, and made haste to fill the grave. Sand poured in, soft and relentless; I pressed it down, levelled it to the desert’s face, hiding what I had done as if to protect it from the loud and the cruel.

Then, utterly fatigued, I dropped beside it, and fell asleep.

When I came up out of the dark, the earth about my shoulders was wet as a cellar floor after rain, and the path I had clawed to the grave was already loosening—crumbing, sucking at my feet, turning to a living mire. The river in its ancient bed had swelled in the night like dough that will not be denied. It had begun to shoulder the stones and dead leaves and all the season’s refuse it carried, worrying its load as if the mountain itself had set a hand to its back. Soon it would be roaring over the precipice; and in its fall, split in two, one branch would hurry to resubmerge the orchard valley, the other to drown, perhaps, the horde of monsters; and between them it would cut the Evil Wood into an island.

I set out at once on my way back to those who had sent me.

At the precipice I took the narrow course between the two branches, for I would pass again by Mara’s cottage, lest she had returned. I hungered—not for bread alone, though my body had been long unkindly used—but for the plain comfort of her presence: the domestic surety of her open door, the homely order of her table, the clean taste of water in a world where even air could be bitter.

I reached the cottage. The door stood open still. The bread was still on the table, its crust dulled by the damp, as if the very silence had breathed upon it; the water still waited in its cup, holding a pale sky in its surface. No hearth snapped. No kettle muttered. No foot stirred the floor. The hush lay within and around the place like flour on a board after the hands have gone.

I stopped and called aloud at the door, but no voice answered. I went on.

A little farther, where the sand widened, I saw a grayheaded man sitting with his face in his hands, weeping, the tears dropping steadily as if he had been drawing water from an old well for too many years.

“What ails you, sir?” I asked. “Are you forsaken?”

He lifted his head. His eyes were red and bright. “I weep,” he said, “because they will not let me die. I have been to the house of death, and its mistress—though I am bent with years—refuses me. Intercede for me, sir, if you know her, I pray you.”

“Nay,” I answered, “that I cannot; for she refuses none whom it is lawful for her to receive.”

He stared as if I had spoken in a dialect he half remembered and resented. “How know you this of her? You have never sought death! You are much too young to desire it!”

“I fear your words may mean that, were you young again, neither would you desire it.”

“Indeed,” he said quickly, almost angrily, “young sir, I would not! And certain I am that you cannot.”

“I may not be old enough to desire to die,” I said, “but I am young enough to desire to live indeed. Therefore I go now to learn if she will at length take me in. You wish to die because you do not care to live: she will not open her door to you, for no one can die who does not long to live.”

“It ill becomes your youth to mock a friendless old man,” he returned. “Pray, cease your riddles!”

“Did not the Mother tell you something of the same sort?”

“In truth I believe she did,” he said, and his mouth twisted, “but I gave little heed to her excuses.”

“Ah then,” I replied, grieved for him as for a man starving beside a full table because he mistrusted the name of bread, “it is too plain you have not yet learned to die. Such had I too been, but for the Lady of Sorrow. I am indeed young, but I have wept many tears; pardon me if I presume to offer counsel: go to the Lady of Sorrow, and take with both hands what she will give you. Yonder lies her cottage. She is not in it now, but her door stands open, and there is bread and water on her table. Go in; sit down; eat of the bread; drink of the water; and wait there until she appear. Then ask counsel of her, for she is true, and her wisdom is great.”

He fell to weeping afresh, as if my words had only touched the rawness without reaching the root. I left him there, and the soft beat of his sobbing went on behind me like a small, steady drip in a cavern. What I said, I feared, he did not heed. But Mara would find him.

The sun was down, and the moon not risen, when I came to the abode of the monsters. It lay still as stone until I passed over its border, and then the world behind me changed. I heard a noise of many waters and a great cry, as if the swollen river had found a voice and the earth itself had answered it. The sound rose in a single rough chord—no bird-song, no leaf-whisper; it was the wild, untempered music of force. I did not turn my head. I would not let that roaring write its harsh words upon my heart.

Before I reached the house of death, the cold had sharpened into bitterness and the darkness had thickened until it seemed to have substance. Cold and darkness were one thing, and they entered my bones together, like a draught that burns and numbs in the same swallow. But from the window a small candle shone—the candle of Eve. Its light did not so much push the dark away as make a hearth-place in it, a little circle where the spirit could breathe. It guided me and kept frost and murk from my heart.

The door stood open, and the cottage lay empty.

I sat down, disconsolate. The clay floor held the chill like a stone platter holds cold meat, and the air had the faint, clean sterility of linen laid away too long. As I sat, a loneliness grew in me such as I had never yet known in all my wanderings. Thousands were near me, not one was with me. True, it was I who was dead, not they; yet whether by their life or by my death, we were divided. They were alive, but I was not dead enough even to know them alive: doubt would come, and doubt in that place was like a thin grit between tooth and tongue—small, but unendurable.

I took myself to task. The solitude was but seeming. I was awake, and they slept—that was all. It was only that they lay so still and did not speak. They were with me now, and soon, soon I should be with them. Yet the silence had its own sound, a pressure upon the ear as if the world were holding its breath.

I dropped Adam’s old spade. The dull thud on the clay floor went heavily through the house, and it seemed to reverberate from the chamber beyond. A childish terror seized me. I sat and stared at the coffin-door.

But father Adam, mother Eve, sister Mara would soon come to me, and then—welcome the cold world and the white neighbours! I forgot my fears, lived a little, and loved my dead.

Something moved in the chamber of the dead.

From it came what was like a dim, far-off sound—and yet it was not sound as I had known sound. There was no scrape of foot, no hinge, no breath; but my soul sprang into my ears. I knew without hearing, without feeling it. It was a tremor that belonged to no air: a message without a language I could name, the way one recognizes one’s own name called in a crowd before the syllables are caught.

It was coming. It drew nearer.

In the bosom of my desertion, an infant hope woke.

The noiseless thrill reached the coffin-door—became sound—and smote my ear. The door began to move with a low, soft creaking of hinges, like an old cupboard opened for bread in the dark. It was opening. I ceased to listen and stared, expectant.

It opened a little way, and a face came into the opening.

It was Lona’s.

Her eyes were closed, but her face was turned upon me and seemed to see me. It was white as Eve’s, white as Mara’s, but it did not shine as theirs did, as if the light in her had been drawn inward like a flame banked for sleep.

She spoke, and her voice was like a sleepy night-wind in the grass, the kind that barely bends the stalks yet changes the whole field’s whisper.

“Are you coming, king?” it said. “I cannot rest until you are with me, gliding down the river to the great sea, and the beautiful dream-land. The sleepiness is full of lovely things: come and see them.”

“Ah, my darling!” I cried. “Had I but known! I thought you were dead!”

She leaned upon my bosom—cold as ice frozen to marble. The cold struck through my clothes as if her very stillness had weight. She threw her arms, so white, feebly about me, and sighed as one who has tasted something sweet and is too tired to speak of it.

“Carry me back to my bed, king. I want to sleep.”

I bore her into the death-chamber, holding her close lest she should dissolve out of my arms like mist when the sun finds it. Unaware that I saw, I carried her straight to her couch, as if my body remembered the place for her better than my mind.

“Lay me down,” she said, “and cover me from the warm air; it hurts—a little. Your bed is there, next to mine. I shall see you when I wake.”

Already she was asleep. Her words faded the way the last scent of a cut herb fades when the blade is set down.

I threw myself upon my couch—blessed as never was man on the eve of his wedding.

“Come, sweet cold,” I said, “and still my heart speedily.”

But instead there came a glimmer of light in the chamber, and I saw the face of Adam approaching. He had not the candle, yet I saw him as one sees a familiar hand moving in dusk by knowing it. At the side of Lona’s couch he looked down on her with a questioning smile, then greeted me across it.

“We have been to the top of the hill to hear the waters on their way,” he said. “They will be in the den of the monsters to-night. But why did you not await our return?”

“My child could not sleep,” I answered.

“She is fast asleep,” he rejoined.

“Yes—now,” I said; “but she was awake when I laid her down.”

“She was asleep all the time,” he insisted, gently, as one correcting a man who misnames a thing because his tongue belongs to another country.

“She was perhaps dreaming about you—and came to you?”

“She did.”

“And did you not see that her eyes were closed?”

“Now I think of it, I did.”

“If you had looked ere you laid her down, you would have seen her asleep on the couch.”

“That would have been terrible!”

“You would only have found that she was no longer in your arms.”

“That would have been worse!”

“It is, perhaps, to think of,” he said, “but to see it would not have troubled you.”

“Dear father,” I said, and my voice sounded small in that wide hush, “how is it that I am not sleepy? I thought I should go to sleep like the Little Ones the moment I laid my head down.”

“Your hour is not quite come,” he answered. “You must have food ere you sleep.”

Food. The word, in that house, was not an abstract mercy; it called up texture and warmth, the act of taking into oneself what another has prepared—an exchange older than speech. Yet even that thought made me ache with my helplessness.

“Ah,” I said, “I ought not to have lain down without your leave, for I cannot sleep without your help! I will get up at once!”

But when I tried, I found my own weight more than I could move, as if the cold had turned my limbs to packed earth.

“There is no need,” he said. “We will serve you here. You do not feel cold, do you?”

“Not too cold to lie still,” I answered, “but perhaps too cold to eat.”

He came to the side of my couch, bent over me, and breathed upon my heart. At once warmth ran through me—not the feverish heat of struggle, but the steady comfort of a kitchen where the oven has been tended all day. My fingers remembered what it was to belong to life.

As he left me, I heard a voice and knew it was the Mother’s. She was singing. Her song was sweet and soft and low at first, as if she sat by my bed in the dark and would not wake the sleepers; it moved like a ladle in a pot, circling without clatter. But ere it ceased, it soared aloft, and seemed to come from the throat of a woman-angel, high above the region of larks, higher than man had ever yet lifted up his heart. The sound did not bruise the air like the river’s roar; it healed it. It made the silence hospitable. I heard every word she sang, but could keep only this, as if the rest belonged to a tongue older than mine, impossible to carry back into waking:

“Many a wrong, and its curing song;
Many a road, and many an inn;
Room to roam, but only one home
For all the world to win!”

And I thought I had heard the song before, not with my ears only but with something ancestral in me, as if it had survived in blood when the words of it had been forgotten.

Then the three came to my couch together, bringing me bread and wine, and I sat up to partake of it. Adam stood on one side of me, Eve and Mara on the other. The bread was plain, such as a careful hand bakes when flour is precious: a crust that yielded with a quiet crack, a crumb close and honest, smelling of grain and patient fire. The wine was dark, its scent rising like bruised fruit and sun stored in a jar. In that place, where even death had a domestic order, the act of eating felt like consent.

“You are good indeed, father Adam, mother Eve, sister Mara,” I said, and the words tasted of salt in my mouth. “To receive me! In my soul I am ashamed and sorry!”

“We knew you would come again,” answered Eve.

“How could you know it?” I asked.

“Because here was I,” said Mara with a smile—quick as the glint of a knife set right—“born to look after my brothers and sisters.”

“Every creature must one night yield himself and lie down,” said Adam. “He was made for liberty, and must not be left a slave.”

“It will be late, I fear, ere all have lain down,” I said.

“There is no early or late here,” he returned. “For him the true time then first begins who lays himself down. Men are not coming home fast; women are coming faster. A desert, wide and dreary, parts him who lies down to die from him who lies down to live. The former may well make haste, but here is no haste.”

“To our eyes,” said Eve, “you were coming all the time. We knew Mara would find you, and you must come.”

The bread strengthened me with a quiet insistence, as if each mouthful set a stone in place in a wall that had been falling. The wine made a small warmth behind my ribs, a reminder that even in cold there can be sweetness.

“How long is it since my father lay down?” I asked.

“I have told you that years are of no consequence in this house,” answered Adam. “We do not heed them. Your father will wake when his morning comes. Your mother, next to whom you are lying—”

“Ah, then it is my mother!” I cried.

“Yes,” he assented, “she with the wounded hand. She will be up and away long ere your morning is ripe.”

“I am sorry.”

“Rather be glad.”

“It must be a sight for God Himself to see such a woman come awake!”

“It is indeed a sight for God,” said Adam, “a sight that makes her Maker glad. He sees of the travail of His soul, and is satisfied. Look at her once more, and sleep.”

He let the rays of his candle fall upon her beautiful face. In the light she seemed not merely still, but gathered—as if all her life had been folded neatly within her, like linen pressed and laid by.

“She looks much younger,” I said.

“She is much younger,” he replied. “Even Lilith already begins to look younger.”

A drowsiness, blissful and heavy, began to settle upon me. It did not come like a blow, but like a clean cloth drawn gently over a loaf to let it rest.

“But when you see your mother again,” he continued, “you will not at first know her. She will go on steadily growing younger until she reaches the perfection of her womanhood—a splendour beyond foresight. Then she will open her eyes, behold on one side her husband, on the other her son—and rise and leave them to go to a father and a brother more to her than they.”

His words reached me as one in a dream, each phrase distinct, and yet belonging to a language I could not quite bring into my own. I was very cold, but already the cold caused no suffering. It was as if I had learned a new meaning for it, one my old vocabulary could not carry.

I felt them put upon me the white garment of the dead, the cloth cool and clean, like fresh-washed sheets shaken out in night air. Hands moved about me with the quiet competence of those who have set many tables and made many beds, who know that the body has its rites as surely as the soul.

Then I forgot everything.

The night about me was pale with sleeping faces, row on row, like a field under a thin moon; but I was asleep also, and did not know that I slept.

Existence came back to me as a taste comes back—first the bare fact of it on the tongue, then the memory of what it once meant. I knew myself alive, and with that knowing there lay upon me the profound, the infinite cold.

It was a blessing so intense my waking heart could never quite recover the shape of it. Warmth held no sweetness for me then. I knew I had once delighted in it—had leaned into it as into a hearth-scented room after rain—but I could not remember how. The cold had done what no counsel, no book, no brave resolution had ever done: it had quieted every care, dissolved every pain, smoothed the ragged edges of sorrow as frost smooths a churned field into one clean sheet.

And yet I must not call it mere comfort, as if it were a blanket laid upon misery. Sorrow itself was being swallowed up in the life drawing near—life that would restore every good and lovely thing a hundredfold, and not as an echo but as the thing itself, newly made and more itself than ever.

I lay at peace, full of the quietest expectation. The damp odours of the earth rose to me—loam and root and the dark sweetness of decay that is not corruption but pantry-work for spring. In that great stillness I became aware, not with sight but with some inward sense like smell turned to knowing, of the souls of primroses, daisies, and snowdrops waiting under the crusted ground. They waited as dough waits under cloth: patient, alive, already pledged to rise when the warmth comes.

How shall I tell the delight of that frozen, conscious sleep? I had no more to stand up. No more to choose and strain and carry myself. I had only to lie stretched out and still, as one laid on a table after long labour, hands at rest. How cold I was, words cannot reach it; yet colder and colder I grew—and welcomed it the more, as one welcomes the hush that follows when the last clanging machine is shut down and the field is given back its own small sounds.

By degrees I was less and less conscious of myself, and more and more conscious of bliss—unimaginable, yet felt. I had not made it. I had not prayed for it. It was mine in virtue of being; and being was mine because a Will dwelt in mine, as yeast lives hidden in the lump, making it more than flour and water.

Then the dreams began to arrive—and they came crowding, like generations entering a kitchen one after another, each with some inherited gesture, some old recipe of feeling.

I lay naked on a snowy peak. Below me white mist heaved like a billowy sea. The cold moon hung in the same air as I, and above the moon and me lay an even colder sky, in which both the moon and I seemed to dwell as small, pale embers in a vast ash.

I was Adam, waiting for God to breathe into my nostrils the breath of life.

I was not Adam, but a child in the bosom of a mother whose whiteness was radiant as new milk in a white bowl, light coming from her as warmth comes from bread when it is broken.

I was a youth on a white horse, leaping from cloud to cloud in a blue heaven, hasting calmly toward some blessed goal as if the very air were a road laid down for him.

For centuries I dreamed—or was it chiliads? or only one long night? Why ask? Time had nothing to do with me. I was in the land of thought—farther in, higher up than the seven dimensions, the ten senses. I think I was where I am—in the heart of God.

I dreamed away dim cycles in the centre of a melting glacier, the spectral moon drawing nearer and nearer. Wind and the welter of a torrent grew in my ears. I lay and heard them: wind and water and moon together sang, a peaceful waiting for a redemption drawing near. In that dream the world was made chiefly of sound—no harshness, no iron grinding, no invented clatter to sell itself as music. The wind had room to speak its own language; the water had room to answer; the moon, though it made no noise, seemed to press a rhythm upon both. Their speech was not my tongue, yet I understood it in the way one understands a mother’s humming before one knows words.

Cycles, I say—but perhaps it was the solemn, æonian march of a second, pregnant with eternity.

Then, suddenly—without once troubling the conscious bliss that held me—every wrong I had ever done, from far beyond my earthly memory down to the last, was with me.

Fully in every wrong lived the conscious I: confessing, abjuring, lamenting the dead, making atonement with each person I had injured, hurt, or offended. Every human soul to which I had caused a troubled thought was now unspeakably dear to me. I humbled myself before it, agonising to cast from between us the clinging offence—as one scrubs at a stain that has crept into the grain, until the very water runs clear again.

I wept at the feet of the mother whose commands I had slighted. With bitter shame I confessed to my father that I had told him two lies and long forgotten them. Now I had for long remembered them, and kept them like stones in my pocket, to crush at last at his feet.

I became the eager slave of all whom I had thus or anyhow wronged. Countless services I devised to render them. For this one I would build such a house as had never grown from the ground—stone laid true, beams scented of resin, hearth wide enough for a whole clan’s winter. For that one I would train such horses as had never yet been seen in any world—patient, swift, understanding, their hoofs falling with that good, quiet certainty that soothes the heart. For a third I would make such a garden as had never bloomed: haunted with still pools that held the sky like broth holds fragrance, alive with running waters whose music would cleanse the mind of every false note.

I would write songs to make their hearts swell, and tales to make them glow. I would turn the forces of the world into channels of invention to make them laugh with the joy of wonder. Love possessed me. Love was my life. Love was to me, as to Him that made me, all in all.

Suddenly I found myself in a solid blackness, upon which even that ghost of light that dwells in the caverns of the eyes could cast no fancied glimmer. Yet my heart, which feared nothing and hoped infinitely, was full of peace. I lay imagining what the light would be when it came, and what new creation it would bring with it.

Then, without conscious volition, I sat up and stared about me.

The moon was looking in at the lowest, horizontal, crypt-like windows of the death-chamber. Her long light slanted, I thought, across the fallen, still-ripening sheaves of the harvest of the great husbandman.

But no—those sheaves were gone.

Gathered in, or swept away by chaotic storm, not a sacred sheaf was there. My dead were gone. I was alone.

Desolation, dread—depths yet deeper than any I had known—opened under me. Had there never been any ripening dead? Had I but dreamed them and their loveliness? Why then these walls? Why the empty couches? No; they were all up—they were all abroad in the new eternal day and had forgotten me. They had left me behind, alone.

Tenfold more terrible was the tomb with its inhabitants away. The quiet ones had made me quiet with their presence, had pervaded my mind with their blissful peace. Now I had no friend; my lovers were far from me. For a moment I sat staring, horror-stricken. I had been alone with the moon on a mountain-top in the sky; now I was alone with her in a huge cenotaph, and even she seemed to be staring about, seeking her dead with a ghastly gaze.

I sprang to my feet and staggered from the fearful place.

The cottage was empty.

I ran out into the night. No moon was there. Even as I left the chamber, a cloudy rampart had risen and covered her. Yet a broad shimmer came from far over the heath, mingled with a ghostly murmuring music—as if the moon were raining a light that plashed as it fell, and the land caught it and answered.

I ran stumbling across the moor and found a lovely lake, margined with reeds and rushes. The moon behind the cloud was gazing upon the monsters’ den—water clear as glass and very still. But the musical murmur went on, filling the quiet air, drawing me after it as surely as the scent of baking draws one down a corridor.

I walked round the border of the mere and climbed the range of hills.

What rose to my eyes was like the return of a long-forgotten language to a people’s throat.

The whole expanse where, with hot aching feet, I had crossed and recrossed the deep-scored channels and ravines of the dry river-bed was alive with streams, with torrents, with still pools—a river deep and wide. The moon flashed on the water; the water answered the moon with flashes of its own—white splintering light breaking everywhere from its rock-encountered flow. From its bosom arose a great jubilant song, the song of new-born liberty.

I stood gazing, and my heart began to exult. My life was not all failure. I had helped to set this river free.

My dead were not lost. I had but to go after and find them. I would follow and follow until I came whither they had gone. Our meeting might be thousands of years away, but at last—at last—I should hold them. Wherefore else did the floods clap their hands?

I hurried down the hill: my pilgrimage was begun. In what direction to turn my steps I knew not, but I must go, and go, till I found my living dead.

A torrent ran swift and wide at the foot of the range. I rushed in: it laid no hold upon me. I waded through it. The next I sprang across; the third I swam; the next I waded again. Now and then I stopped to gaze on the ceaseless flash and flow, and to hearken to the multitudinous broken music. Every little fall had its own dialect; every eddy repeated a phrase differently; the whole wide speech of the river was one, and yet never the same twice. At times some incipient air seemed about to draw itself clear of the dulcet confusion, only to merge again in the consorted roar.

At moments the world of waters would invade as if to overwhelm me—not with the force of its seaward rush, nor the shouting of its liberated throng, but with the greatness of silence wandering into sound: that hush at the heart of singing, the space that makes music possible. I thought then of the dead house I had fled—its absence of any living noise more dreadful than a storm—and I understood how sound may be mercy, and how the wrong kind of sound, the forced and manufactured clang of it, can be tyranny over the spirit.

As I stood lost in delight, a hand was laid on my shoulder.

I turned and saw a man in the prime of strength, beautiful as if fresh from the heart of the glad Creator—young like Him who cannot grow old. He stood large and grand, clothed in a white robe, with the moon in his hair.

I looked: it was Adam.

“Father,” I cried, “where is she? Where are the dead? Is the great resurrection come and gone? The terror of my loneliness was upon me; I could not sleep without my dead; I ran from the desolate chamber. Whither shall I go to find them?”

“You mistake, my son,” he answered, and his voice, even in its breath, was consolation—as if one had been starving on dry crusts of fear and suddenly there came warm broth, plain, honest, strengthening. “You are still in the chamber of death, still upon your couch, asleep and dreaming, with the dead around you.”

“Alas! when I but dream, how am I to know it? The dream best dreamed is the likest to the waking truth!”

“When you are quite dead,” he said, “you will dream no false dream. The soul that is true can generate nothing that is not true; neither can the false enter it.”

“But, sir,” I faltered, “how am I to distinguish betwixt the true and the false, where both alike seem real?”

“Do you not understand?” he returned with a smile that might have slain all the sorrows of all his children. “You cannot perfectly distinguish between the true and the false while you are not yet quite dead; neither indeed will you when you are quite dead—that is, quite alive—for then the false will never present itself. At this moment, believe me, you are on your bed in the house of death.”

“I am trying hard to believe you, father. I do indeed believe you, although I can neither see nor feel the truth of what you say.”
