“You are not to blame that you cannot. And because even in a dream you believe me, I will help you. Put forth your left hand open, and close it gently: it will clasp the hand of your Lona, who lies asleep where you lie dreaming you are awake.”

I put forth my hand. It closed on the hand of Lona—firm and soft and deathless. It was like taking hold of a thing one has thought lost beyond all measuring: like finding, in a world stripped bare, the one familiar tool whose weight in the palm says, You can still make, you can still mend.

“But, father,” I cried, “she is warm!”

“Your hand is as warm to hers. Cold is a thing unknown in our country. Neither she nor you are yet in the fields of home, but each to each is alive and warm and healthful.”

My heart was glad. Yet immediately a sharp-stinging doubt rose, as if some bitter herb had been bruised under the sweetness.

“Father,” I said, “forgive me—but how am I to know surely that this also is not a part of the lovely dream in which I am now walking with thyself?”

“Thou doubtest because thou lovest the truth,” he answered. “Some would willingly believe life but a phantasm, if only it might for ever afford them a world of pleasant dreams: thou art not of such. Be content for a while not to know surely. The hour will come—and that ere long—when, being true, thou shalt behold the very truth, and doubt will be for ever dead. Scarce, then, wilt thou be able to recall the features of the phantom.

“Thou wilt then know that which thou canst not now dream. Thou hast not yet looked the Truth in the face, hast as yet at best but seen him through a cloud. That which thou seest not, and never didst see save in a glass darkly—that which, indeed, never can be known save by its innate splendour shining straight into pure eyes—that thou canst not but doubt, and art blameless in doubting until thou seest it face to face, when thou wilt no longer be able to doubt it.

“But to him who has once seen even a shadow only of the truth, and, even but hoping he has seen it when it is present no longer, tries to obey it—to him the real vision, the Truth himself, will come, and depart no more, but abide with him for ever.”

“I think I see, father,” I said. “I think I understand.”

“Then remember, and recall. Trials yet await thee—heavy, of a nature thou knowest not now. Remember the things thou hast seen. Truly thou knowest not those things, but thou knowest what they have seemed, what they have meant to thee. Remember also the things thou shalt yet see. Truth is all in all; and the truth of things lies, at once hid and revealed, in their seeming.”

“How can that be, father?” I said, and raised my eyes with the question—for I had been listening with downbent head, aware of nothing but the voice of Adam.

He was gone.

In my ears there was nought but the sounding silence of the swift-flowing waters. I stretched forth my hands to find him, but no answering touch met their seeking. I was alone—alone in the land of dreams.

To myself I seemed wide awake, yet I believed I was dreaming because he had told me so. Even in a dream, however, the dreamer must do something. He cannot sit down and refuse to stir until the dream grows weary and departs. I took up my wandering and went on.

Many channels I crossed, and came to a wider space of rock. There, dreaming I was weary, I laid myself down and longed to be awake. I was about to rise and resume my journey when I discovered I lay beside a pit in the rock, whose mouth was like that of a grave—deep and dark, no bottom visible.

In the dreams of my childhood I had found that a fall invariably woke me. Therefore, when I desired to discontinue a dream, I would seek some eminence whence to cast myself down that I might wake. With one glance at the peaceful heavens and one at the rushing waters, I rolled myself over the edge of the pit.

For a moment consciousness left me.

When it returned, I stood in the garret of my own house—in the little wooden chamber of the cowl and the mirror.

Unspeakable despair invaded me with the knowledge: between me and my Lona lay an abyss impassable, a distance no chain could measure. Space and Time and Mode of Being—like walls of adamant, unscalable, impenetrable—shut me in from that gulf. True, it might yet be in my power to pass again through the door of light and journey back to the chamber of the dead; and if so, I was parted from that chamber only by a wide heath and by the pale starry night betwixt me and the sun, which alone could open for me the mirror-door, and was now far away on the other side of the world.

But an immeasurably wider gulf sank between us in this: that she was asleep and I was awake. That I was no longer worthy to share with her that sleep, and could no longer hope to awake from it with her.

For truly I was much to blame. I had fled from my dream. The dream was not of my making any more than was my life: I ought to have seen it to the end. In fleeing from it, I had left the holy sleep itself behind me.

I would go back to Adam, tell him the truth, and bow to his decree.

I crept to my chamber, threw myself on my bed, and passed a dreamless night.

I rose and listlessly sought the library. On the way I met no one. The house seemed dead. I sat down with a book to await the noontide: not a sentence would yield itself to me. Words that once had been bread turned now to sawdust in the mouth. The mutilated manuscript offered itself from the masked door; the sight of it sickened me—what to me was the princess with her devilry? Language itself seemed to have lost its savour, as if some dominant, empty lexicon had swept through and flattened every living turn of phrase, leaving only bright labels without the thing they once named.

I rose and looked out of a window.

It was a brilliant morning. With a great rush the fountain shot high and fell roaring back. The sun sat in its feathery top. Not a bird sang; not a creature was to be seen. Raven nor librarian came near me. No step creaked; no door whispered; no domestic stir—no kettle beginning its low song, no broom on boards, no human breath of habit—came up from the house below. The world was dead about me, and the loudest thing in it was the fountain’s continual fall, a kind of splendour that could not console.

I took another book, sat down again, and went on waiting.

Noon drew near.

I went up the stairs to the dumb, shadowy roof. I closed behind me the door into the wooden chamber and turned to open the door out of a dreary world. I left the chamber with a heart of stone.

Do what I might, all was fruitless. I pulled the chains; adjusted and re-adjusted the hood; arranged and re-arranged the mirrors. No result followed. I waited and waited to give the vision time. It would not come. The mirror stood blank; nothing lay in its dim old depth but the mirror opposite and my own haggard face—eyes like emptied cups, cheeks without colour, the mouth set as if it had forgotten the taste of any true thing.

I went back to the library. There the books were hateful to me—for I had once loved them. Now they seemed like preserved herbs whose scent is gone, their labels still legible, their power dead.

That night I lay awake from down-lying to uprising, and the next day renewed my endeavours with the mystic door. But all was yet in vain. How the hours went I cannot tell. No one came nigh me. Not a sound from the house below entered my ears—not even the small, necessary noises by which a place confesses it is inhabited. I did not once feel weary—only desolate, drearily desolate, as if all the world’s living speech had withdrawn and left me among mere echoes.

I passed a second sleepless night.

In the morning I went for the last time to the chamber in the roof, and for the last time sought an open door. There was none.

My heart died within me.

I had lost my Lona. Was she anywhere? Had she ever been, save in the mouldering cells of my brain?

“I must die one day,” I thought, “and then, straight from my death-bed, I will set out to find her. If she is not, I will go to the Father and say—‘Even thou canst not help me: let me cease, I pray thee!’”

The fourth night I thought I fell asleep. It was not sleep. It was a kind of long, lightless simmering, like a pot left on dead embers that yet keeps its heat in the clay. And in that same night I woke indeed.

My eyes opened, and though the air lay black as a covered oven, I knew—by scent, by hush, by the very weight of stillness—that I was in the house of death; and I knew, as certainly as one knows the first clean crack of salt on the tongue, that every moment since I lay down there I had been dreaming, and only now was awake.

“At last!” I said inwardly, and my heart sprang like dough answered by yeast.

I turned my eyes; Lona stood by my couch, waiting for me.

I had never lost her—only for a little while lost the sight of her. All my lamentation, all my hunger of grief, had been like a famished man dreaming of famine while the loaf cooled under linen at his elbow.

It was dark, as I say, and yet I saw her: she was not dark. Her eyes held the same steady radiance I had seen in the Mother’s—light not flaring like a lamp fed with oil, but shining as if it were the substance of her being. The brightness issued from her face, and not from her face only: her death-dress, filled now with the light of her body tenfold awake in the power of its resurrection, was white as snow and glittered as if every thread had been washed in morning.

She had fallen asleep a girl; she awoke a woman—ripe, as fruit is ripe, not with show but with inward sweetness, the loveliness of life essential.

I folded her in my arms and knew, with a certainty more bodily than thought, that I lived indeed.

“I woke first!” she said, and the wonder in her smile was like the first taste of water after a long thirst.

“You did, my love—and woke me!”

“I only looked at you and waited,” she answered, as if waiting were a craft she had learned at a hearth, patient and exact.

The candle came floating toward us through the dark, a small obedient star moving without hand or chain. In a few moments Adam and Eve and Mara were with us. They greeted us with a quiet good-morning and a smile: they were used to such wakings, as old housefolk are used to bread rising and children being born.

“I hope you have had a pleasant darkness!” said the Mother.

“Not very,” I answered; “but the waking from it is heavenly.”

“It is but begun,” she returned; “you are hardly yet awake!”

“He is at least clothed-upon with Death, which is the radiant garment of Life,” said Adam.

He embraced Lona his child, put an arm around me, looked a moment or two inquiringly at the princess, and patted the head of the leopardess as one might lay a palm on a great, warm animal that belonged to peace.

“I think we shall meet you two again before long,” he said, looking first at Lona, then at me.

“Have we to die again?” I asked.

“No,” he answered, and his smile was like the Mother’s—quiet as dawn in a valley. “You have died into life, and will die no more; you have only to keep dead. Once dying as we die here, all the dying is over. Now you have only to live, and that you must, with all your blessed might. The more you live, the stronger you become to live.”

“But shall I not grow weary with living so strong?” I said. “What if I cease to live with all my might?”

“It needs but the will, and the strength is there!” said the Mother. “Pure life has no weakness to grow weary withal. The Life keeps generating ours.—Those who will not die, die many times, die constantly, keep dying deeper, never have done dying; here all is upwardness and love and gladness.”

She ceased with a look that said without words, We are mother and son; between us no farewell is possible.

Mara kissed me on the forehead and said, gaily, “I told you, brother, all would be well!—When next you would comfort, say, ‘What will be well, is even now well.’”

She gave a little sigh, the sort that carries its own history the way a remembered song carries a lost dialect—sweet, and half unheard by those who have forgotten the old cadence.

“—You know me now!” she ended, with a smile like her mother’s.

“I know you!” I answered. “You are the voice that cried in the wilderness before ever the Baptist came! you are the shepherd whose wolves hunt the wandering sheep home ere the shadow rise and the night grow dark!”

“My work will one day be over,” she said, “and then I shall be glad with the gladness of the great shepherd who sent me.”

“All the night long the morning is at hand,” said Adam.

A faint agitation moved in the soundless air—an unease like the beginning of a storm far out at sea, heard not by sight but by ear. “What is that flapping of wings I hear?” I asked.

“The Shadow is hovering,” replied Adam: “there is one here whom he counts his own! But ours once, never more can she be his!”

I turned to look on the faces of my father and mother, meaning to kiss them ere we went. Their couches were empty, save for the Little Ones who had, with love’s boldness, appropriated their hospitality and curled themselves where elders had lain. For an instant that awful dream of desolation shadowed me again, and my breath went thin.

“What is it, my heart?” said Lona.

“Their empty places frightened me,” I answered.

“They are up and away long ago,” said Adam. “They kissed you ere they went, and whispered, ‘Come soon.’”

“And I neither to feel nor hear them!” I murmured.

“How could you—far away in your dreary old house! You thought the dreadful place had you once more! Now go and find them.—Your parents, my child,” he added, turning to Lona, “must come and find you!”

The hour of our departure was at hand.

Lona went to the couch of the mother who had slain her and kissed her tenderly. It was the kind of kiss that neither bargains nor boasts—only gives, as bread gives itself when broken. Then she laid herself in her father’s arms.

“That kiss will draw her homeward, my Lona!” said Adam.

“Who were her parents?” asked Lona.

“My father,” answered Adam, “is her father also.”

Lona turned and laid her hand in mine, and the warmth of it was like the familiar grip of a wooden spoon—homely, true, carrying a lifetime of stirring.

I kneeled and humbly thanked the three for helping me to die. Lona knelt beside me, and they all breathed upon us. Their breath was not wind only, but a benediction with a scent in it—clean as rain on warm earth, and strangely intimate, like the smell of a childhood kitchen remembered in a language you can no longer speak aloud.

“Hark! I hear the sun,” said Adam.

I listened.

He was coming with the rush as of a thousand times ten thousand far-off wings, with the roar of a molten and flaming world millions upon millions of miles away. His approach was a great crescendo chord of a hundred harmonies—layer on layer, like the building of an old song in parts, each voice entering in its proper moment. In that sound there was no harsh clang of hammered metal, no grinding of machinery—nothing of the industrial shriek that tears a mind from itself. It was magnitude without violence, power without insult; the acoustic world made whole.

The three looked at each other and smiled, and that smile went floating heavenward—a three-petaled flower, the family’s morning thanksgiving. From their mouths and their faces it spread over their bodies and shone through their garments, as if joy were a fire behind thin paper.

Ere I could say, “Lo, they change!” Adam and Eve stood before me the angels of the resurrection, and Mara was the Magdalene with them at the sepulchre. The countenance of Adam was like lightning, and Eve held a napkin that flung flakes of splendour about the place like shaken flour in sunbeams.

A wind began to moan in pulsing gusts, a troubled rhythm, as if the house itself were breathing against a burden.

“You hear his wings now!” said Adam; and I knew he did not mean the wings of the morning.

“It is the great Shadow stirring to depart,” he went on. “Wretched creature, he has himself within him, and cannot rest!”

“But is there not in him something deeper yet?” I asked.

“Without a substance,” he answered, “a shadow cannot be—yea, or without a light behind the substance!”

He listened, head a little bent, as if catching a faint note under a louder one. Then he called out, with a glad smile, “Hark to the golden cock! Silent and motionless for millions of years has he stood on the clock of the universe; now at last he is flapping his wings! now will he begin to crow! and at intervals will men hear him until the dawn of the day eternal.”

I listened.

Far away—as in the heart of an æonian silence—I heard it: the clear, jubilant outcry of the golden throat. It cut clean through the darkness, not as noise cuts—brutal and meaningless—but as a true word cuts through a lie. It hurled defiance at death and the dark; it sang infinite hope, and coming calm. It was the expectation of the creature finding at last a voice; the cry of a chaos that would be a kingdom.

Then I heard a great flapping, nearer, heavier.

“The black bat is flown!” said Mara.

“Amen, golden cock, bird of God!” cried Adam, and his words rang through the house of silence and went up into the airy regions, as if the very beams had learned to echo.

At his AMEN—like doves arising on wings of silver from among the potsherds—up sprang the Little Ones to their knees on their beds, calling aloud, “Crow! crow again, golden cock!” as if they had both seen and heard him in their dreams.

Then each turned and looked at the sleeping bedfellow, gazed a moment with loving eyes, kissed the silent companion of the night, and sprang from the couch.

The Little Ones who had lain down beside my father and mother gazed blank and sad for a moment at their empty places, then slid slowly to the floor. There they fell each into the other’s arms, as if then first, by the witness of the other’s eyes, they were assured they were alive and awake. In their small voices I heard the fragile music of a people not yet trained into the loudness of dominion—speech still close to breath, and therefore close to truth.

Suddenly spying Lona, they came running, radiant with bliss, to embrace her.

Odu, catching sight of the leopardess at the feet of the princess, bounded to her next. Throwing an arm over the great sleeping head, he fondled and kissed it as if it were the dearest beast of his own hearth.

“Wake up, wake up, darling!” he cried; “it is time to wake!”

The leopardess did not move.

“She has slept herself cold!” he said to Mara, looking up with an appeal that broke the heart by its simplicity.

“She is waiting for the princess to wake, my child,” said Mara.

Odu looked at the princess and saw beside her, still asleep, two of his companions. He flew at them.

“Wake up! wake up!” he cried, and pushed and pulled, now this one, now that. But soon his hands paused, and his face changed. He began to look troubled and turned to me with misty eyes.

“They will not wake!” he said. “And why are they so cold?”

“They too are waiting for the princess,” I answered.

He stretched across and laid his hand on her face. “She is cold too! What is it?” he cried, and looked round in wondering dismay, as if the world had used a word on him that he could not translate.

Adam went to him.

“Her wake is not ripe yet,” he said. “She is busy forgetting. When she has forgotten enough to remember enough, then she will soon be ripe, and wake.”

“And remember?”

“Yes—but not too much at once though.”

“But the golden cock has crown!” argued the child, and fell again upon his companions.

“Peter! Peter! Crispy!” he cried. “Wake up, Peter! wake up, Crispy! We are all awake but you two! The gold cock has crown so loud! The sun is awake and coming! Oh, why won’t you wake?”

But Peter would not wake, neither would Crispy; and Odu wept outright at last—tears new to his own cheeks, as if some ancient spring had opened in him and found a channel.

“Let them sleep, darling!” said Adam. “You would not like the princess to wake and find nobody? They are quite happy. So is the leopardess.”

He was comforted and wiped his eyes as if he had been all his life used to weeping and wiping, though now first he had tears wherewith to weep—soon to be wiped altogether away.

We followed Eve to the cottage. There she offered us neither bread nor wine; there was no breaking of crust, no warmth of cup—only her radiant stillness, desiring our departure as a mother desires the grown child to go and live his own life, not clinging with the greed of loneliness.

So, with never a word of farewell, we went out.

The horse and the elephants were at the door, waiting for us. We were too happy to mount them, and they followed us.

Dark had loosened its hold, not into day but into that kitchen-hour of the world when the hearth is raked and the first loaf is only beginning to remember it has yeast in its heart. We walked in a dim twilight, and the air had the smell of spring turned inside out—earth damp as washed roots, sap rising, crushed thyme where our feet went, and somewhere far off the bruised sweetness of gorse. It was as if the land had set a pot on the fire in the night and now lifted the lid for us alone.

Or perhaps the marvel was in us. With no honest light in the sky to explain anything, everything was nevertheless clear: every heather-bush with its purple bells, each small shrub, every blade of grass. They did not merely catch light; they seemed to trade it, as neighbors trade starter—each giving a little of what it had, each receiving a little more than it expected. Nothing threw a shadow. The very act of looking was like tasting: my sight had become a tongue, and the world offered itself to it.

Each growing thing showed me its inward recipe—its shape and color declaring the thought that held it together, the way a grandmother’s bread announces its method the moment you break it: not by lecture, but by crumb. The springing green did not say, I am grass; it said, I am the idea of grass made edible to the sun. I stepped barefoot, and my feet—so long acquainted with stone and thorn—loved every plant they trod upon as if each were a familiar herb returned to the pot after long exile. The world’s life and mine were one broth: the macrocosm simmering, the microcosm fed, and at last both tasting of the same hidden fire. To be aware of a thing was to know its life and mine at once; to know whence we came and where we were at home; to know we are what we are because Another is what He is.

Sense after sense woke in me like old dialects remembered at the table—words for tastes you had forgotten could be named, and then discover no common language holds them. I had new doors in my being and everything came in: fragrance like warm grain, texture like cool water over wrist, and other senses that had no human term, because there had never been, in our thin lexicons, room enough for such plenty. I understood then how a language can die—not with a bang but with a shrug—simply because it cannot carry what its people no longer dare to feel.

A little breeze brushed a heather-bush, and its purple bells rang—no harsh clink of metal, but a sweet, quick tin-tinning like tiny spoons tapping the rim of a glass while someone laughs in the next room. The sound wasn’t merely heard; it entered the body as nourishment. I was in the joy of the bells, in the joy of the breeze, in the joy of the hearing that received them. The world’s music was not entertainment sold back to us in bright packets; it was an ecology—each living thing keeping time for the others, each note making a shelter for the mind. No engine roared, no iron shriek cut across the living weave. In that clean soundscape, my thoughts stood up straighter, as if relieved of a constant assault.

I felt like a quiet ocean with a ground-swell of joy continually lifting new waves—always new forms, the same deep sweetness. Life was a holiday, but not a careless one: a feast prepared through long labor, through night work, through hidden kneading. I knew then that life and truth are one; that life, mere and pure, is bliss; and that where being is not bliss, it is not life but life-in-death. Every breath I drew was like steam rising from a pot you have tended for hours: gratitude with a scent. At last I was. I lived, and nothing could touch my life.

My darling walked beside me, and we were on our way home to the Father. So much was ours before even the first sun rose upon our freedom—what then must the eternal day bring with it?

We came to the fearful hollow where once the monsters of the earth had wallowed. It was as I had beheld it in my dream—a lovely lake. The air above it was cool and clean, edged with the mineral tang of deep water. I leaned and gazed into its pellucid depths as into a clear bowl set on a table.

A whirlpool had scoured out the soil in which the abortions once burrowed, and at the bottom lay visible the whole horrid brood. A dim greenish light pervaded the crystalline water and showed every hideous form beneath, as if the lake itself kept a lantern to expose what must not be forgotten. Coiled in spires, folded in layers, knotted on themselves or stretched long and large, they lay in motionless heaps—fantasies of ghoulish dismay, more obscene than any fevered mind could brew. Tentacles and tumid bulges, glaring sepian orbs and wicked heads—each head a flower of wrongness bursting from an abominable stalk—made a garden no one would harvest.

Not one of them moved as we passed. But they were not dead. So long as there exist men and women of unwholesome mind, so long as a dominant tongue can name cruelty “order” and call the stripping of memory “progress,” that lake will be peopled with loathsomenesses. Some evils wait in silence the way rot waits in a bin: it does not need motion; it needs only neglect.

But listen—the herald of the sun: the auroral wind softly trumpeting his approach. The first light came not as glare but as sound and warmth together, like a distant oven-door opening. Heaping before his prow a huge ripple-fretted wave of crimson and gold, the sun rushed aloft as if newly launched into the upper sea, paused, and looked down on the world. White-raving storm of molten metals, he was yet but a coal from the altar of the Father’s never-ending sacrifice to his children.

Every little flower straightened its stalk, lifted up its neck, and stood expectant. Something more than the sun, greater than the light, was coming—coming—none the less surely because long upon the road. What are to-day, to-morrow, ten thousand years to Life himself, to Love himself? He is coming, and the necks of all humanity are stretched out to see Him. Every morning they will thus outstretch themselves; every evening they will droop and wait—until He comes. And I wondered, with a shiver that was half joy and half fear of my own forgetting: is this but a vision drawn in air? When He comes, will He indeed find them watching thus, or will they have traded their longing for some jangling imitation?

It was a resurrection-morning. The night had been spent in preparing it, as a cook spends the dark hours making ready the feast that will look effortless at noon. The children went gamboling before, and the beasts came after us. Butterflies fluttered like colored petals blown from a shaken sieve; dragon-flies darted and hung, blue-green sparks above the damp. They came down upon us like a snow-storm of rainbow flakes, then rose again into the humid air like a rolling vapor of embodied odors—honeysuckle, wet bark, sun-warmed leaf.

It was a summer-day more like itself—more ideal—than ever man who had not died found in any world. I walked on the new earth under the new heaven and found them the same as the old, save that now they opened their minds to me and I saw into them. The soul of everything I met came out to greet me and make friends with me, telling me we came from the same and meant the same. You are going to Him, they said, with whom we always are and whom we always mean; we are lightnings that take shape as we flash from Him to His. Even the dark rocks drank like sponges the rays showered upon them, and then gave back a living warmth, as stones by a fire give out what they have received long after the flame has lowered.

Lona and I were two joy-fires walking side by side. Earth breathed heavenward her sweet-savored smoke—green scent, blossom scent, the clean exhalation of water—and we breathed homeward our longing. For thanksgiving, our very consciousness was that.

We came to the channels that once had been dry and wearyful. Now they ran and flashed and foamed with living water that shouted in its gladness. Far as the eye could see, it was a rushing, roaring, dashing river, made vocal by its rocks. The sound was not noise; it was nourishment. Each plunge and eddy had its own note, each stone a different drumhead, each bank a different hollow for resonance. I thought of the old world where people build cages of clangor, then sell themselves silence in little expensive portions. Here, the river gave freely, and the mind grew quiet under its honest music.

We did not cross it, but walked in glory and in joy up its right bank until we reached the great cataract at the foot of the sandy desert—roaring and swirling and dropping sheer—where the river divided into its two branches. There we climbed the height—and found no desert. Through grassy plains, between grassy banks, flowed the deep, wide, silent river full to the brim, as if the land had been set to proof and found able at last to hold its own abundance.

Then first to the Little Ones was revealed the glory of God in the limpid flow of water. Instinctively they plunged and swam, and the beasts followed them—great bodies entering with the ease of creatures who have never been taught to mistrust their own joy. The desert rejoiced and blossomed as the rose. Wide forests had sprung up; their undergrowth was flowering shrubs peopled with song-birds. Every thicket gave birth to a rivulet, and every rivulet to its water-song—small bright dialects of sound, each untranslatable into the other, yet all belonging to one speech of life. To hear them together was to understand how meaning can be carried without conquest, how difference can be harmony without being flattened into sameness.

The place of the buried hand gave no sign.

Beyond and still beyond, the river came in full volume from afar. Up and up we went, now along grassy margin, now through forest of gracious trees. The grass grew sweeter underfoot, and its flowers more lovely and various as we went—as if the land were laying out course after course, each more delicate, each more itself. The trees grew larger, and the wind fuller of messages—not slogans, not jingles, but the plain speaking of leaves rubbing leaf, bough answering bough, birds stitching their calls into the open air.

At length we came to a forest whose trees were greater, grander, more beautiful than any we had yet seen. Their live pillars upheaved a thick embowed roof, and betwixt their leaves and blossoms hardly a sunbeam filtered—only a green-gold dimness like light passing through a bottle of oil. The air smelled of resin and bruised petals; you could almost taste it on the back of your tongue.

Into the rafters of this aerial vault the children climbed, scrambling and leaping in a land of bloom, shouting to the unseen elephants below and hearing them trumpet their replies. The calls rolled through the timbered hush with a depth that made the chest answer, as if the whole forest were an instrument and the elephants its low strings. The conversations between them Lona understood while I only guessed at them, blunderingly, like a stranger trying to catch the sense of a beloved dialect by rhythm alone.

The Little Ones chased the squirrels, and the squirrels, frolicking, drew them on—always at length allowing themselves to be caught and petted. Often a bird, lovely in plumage and form, would light upon one of them, sing a song of what was coming, and fly away, leaving the air trembling as if it had been touched by a spoon and would keep ringing for a while.

Not one monkey of any sort could they see.

Lona and I kept the lower path where the grass still held the river’s breath, and the Little Ones had gone up into the green rafters of the taller trees as if the boughs were ladders made for their feet. For a while there was only the steady hush of water and leaf—sound so clean it seemed to rinse the inside of the ear—until a shout broke overhead, not harsh but round and young, like a call across a farmyard at supper-time.

They began dropping out of the foliage, one after another, as if the branches were shaking them loose with laughter. Their cheeks were bright with wind and sap-smell.

“We saw it!” they cried. “We climbed the tallest tree—taller than all—and far across the plain, on the side of the lonely mountain, there’s a curious something!”

“The mountain rises and rises,” one added, squinting as though the sky were still in his eyes, “until the sky gathers thick to keep it down, and knocks its top off!”

“It may be a city,” said another, “but it’s not at all like Bulika.”

The word “city” came to me with a taste, as if I had bitten an old name and found it had kept its spice. I went up to see for myself. From that height the plain spread like a table laid in pale greens and browns, and beyond it the solitary mountain lifted its shoulder into a blue that did not know where rock ended and air began. Upon its flank, climbing into the clouds, stood indeed a city—ascending, dissolving, reappearing—so that mountain and sky and palace and precipice mingled in broken shine and shadow, as if the place were being continually built by weather.

I came down. The Little Ones were already on the move, and Lona and I with them. They sped as those do who have never learned to look back for loss; their merriment ran ahead like a bright instrument in an orchestra, and we followed the tune of their feet.

The river, too, grew lovelier as we went, until I understood with a kind of shameful wonder that I had never seen real water before. What I had called water in my own world had been only its likeness—useful, measured, often bruised by pipes and stones and noise. This was water that held silence inside it. It carried the sound of itself—lap, glide, lift—not a clatter, not a harshness. Even its scent had a body: cold sweetness, green-rooted, with the faintest mineral edge as of clean flint struck in the dark.

By and by we could see the city from the plain, lodged among blue clouds. But other clouds had begun to gather around a lofty tower—or was it a rock?—that stood above the city nearer the crest. They were gray, then dark gray, then purple, writhing in cross-grained motions, tossing up a vaporous foam; and in places the cloud thickened into whorls that spun like water in a basin.

Then a flash broke from it—dazzling, swift—and for one instant it seemed to play about the Little Ones in front of us like a blessing that could not keep still. After it came a blinding darkness, but no thunder—no brutal clap such as shakes a city’s metal roofs. Only a deepened hush, as if the air listened.

In that hush I heard the children, their voices lowered with delight, like people speaking in a kitchen when something rare is on the table and no one wants to frighten the fragrance away.

“Did you see?”

“I saw.”

“What did you see?”

“The beautifullest man.”

“I heard him speak!”

“I didn’t—what did he say?”

And Luva, the smallest voice, baby-rough with joy, answered as one repeating a phrase in a home-dialect that holds more than the dictionary can carry:

“He said, ‘’Ou’s all mine’s, ‘ickle ones: come along!’”

I had seen the lightning; I had heard no words. Lona, walking close beside me, had seen and heard with them, as if her soul were tuned to that register of sound where meaning rides the breath. It struck me—sharp as the scent of crushed leaf—that what is spoken may be the same and yet not the same, depending on whose ear receives it; and that the Little Ones’ clipped, dear grammar held a truth no polished sentence could improve. Their speech was not broken; it was rooted.

A second flash came, and my eyes, though not my ears, were opened in a way I cannot translate without loss. The great quivering light was compact of angel-faces—faces within faces, each briefly distinct as a flame seen through rain—lighting themselves visible and vanishing as if their very being were a kind of music too fine to stay.

A third flash came; its substance and radiance were human.

“I see my mother!” I cried, and the cry rose out of me like a hunger suddenly given its name.

“I see lots o’ mothers!” said Luva, as if mothers were a plentiful harvest here, and grief had been only a misunderstanding of the seasons.

Once more the cloud flashed, and in that single bright draught all kinds of creatures were there: horses and elephants, lions and dogs—beasts more themselves than any beast I had known; and birds—oh, such birds! Great ones whose wings gleamed singly every colour gathered in sunset or rainbow; little ones whose feathers sparked as if the earth’s hoarded stones had taken flight—silvery cranes, red flamingoes, opal pigeons, peacocks gorgeous in gold and green and blue, jewelled humming-birds. There were great-winged butterflies; and lithe, volumed creeping things, bright as wet skins under starlight, all together in one heavenly flash.

“I see that serpents grow birds here,” said Lona, “as caterpillars used to grow butterflies!”

“I saw my white pony,” I said—my throat thick with the remembered warmth of its neck under my palm—“the pony that died when I was a child. I needn’t have been so sorry; I should just have waited!”

Still no thunder. Instead there came a sweet rain, and the whole air changed its taste: coolness that caressed, a freshness that soothed the heated places of the mind. We breathed deep. Each breath felt like drinking without swallowing. The drops fell thickening, and as they fell they flashed colours as if every gem of the earth had wakened and turned its face upward. A mighty rainbow sprang from the rain and spanned the city.

The blue clouds gathered thicker; the rain fell in torrents; the children exulted and ran until it was all we could do to keep them in sight. Yet even in that rush the soundscape stayed gentle—no harsh drumming, only the soft multitudinous tapping of water on leaf and skin, the river’s larger voice running beneath like a bass line. It made me think of my own world’s incessant mechanical roar—wheels and engines and hollow music sold too loudly—and I felt, with sudden anger and sorrow, how noise can steal a people’s inward language, until even their prayers come out in borrowed tones.

With silent, radiant roll, the river swept onward, filling to the margin its smooth, yielding channel. It did not scrape over rock or shingle or sand; it flowed over grass, and in the grass grew primroses and daisies, crocuses and narcissi, pimpernels and anemones—a starry multitude, large and bright under the brilliant water. The water did not cloud with the rain; it took no yellow, no brown, no muddied memory. It shone with a pale berylline gleam that rose from its deep, dainty bed; and the scent of it was like sliced cucumber and cold stone and something faintly floral, as if the flowers beneath were steeping the current like herbs in a clear broth.

As we drew nearer to the mountain, we saw the river came from its very peak and rushed in full volume through the main street of the city. It descended to the gate by a stair of deep and wide steps, mingled of porphyry and serpentine, which continued down to the foot of the mountain. There, on both banks, shallower steps rose to the gate and along the ascending street.

Without the briefest halt the Little Ones ran straight up the stair to the open gate. Outside, on the landing, sat the portress—a woman-angel of dark visage—leaning her shadowed brow on her idle hand, as if she had been listening for a sound that had not yet arrived. The children rushed upon her and covered her with caresses; before she could gather herself to understand, they had taken heaven by surprise and were already in the city, still mounting beside the descending torrent.

A great angel, attended by a company of shining ones, came down to meet and receive them. But the Little Ones, merry as sparks, evaded them all and ran on. Then a group of woman-angels descended in a kind of dance—movement so ordered it made no noise at all—and in a moment the children were fettered in heavenly arms, laughing as if capture were merely another game with a sweeter end. The radiants carried them away, and I saw them no more.

“Ah!” said the mighty angel, continuing his descent to meet us, who were now almost at the gate and within hearing. “This is well! These are soldiers to take heaven itself by storm! I hear of a horde of black bats on the frontiers: these will make short work with such!”

Then he saw the horse and the elephants clambering up behind us, their hides wet with rain, their breath steaming faintly in the cool air.

“Take those animals to the royal stables,” he added; “there tend them; then turn them into the king’s forest.”

He turned to us and bent low with the sweetest smile.

“Welcome home,” he said.

The words struck like bread placed warm into the hands—simple, sustaining, impossible to argue with. Immediately he turned again and led the way higher. The scales of his armour flashed like flakes of lightning, but without glare; their brightness belonged to the air as naturally as the river’s voice. Thought cannot form itself to tell what I felt, thus received by the officers of heaven. It was as if every hunger I had carried unnamed—every appetite of heart and sense I had dulled with habit—had been recognized and answered.

We stood for a moment at the gate whence issued roaring the radiant river. I know not whence came the stones that fashioned it, but among them I saw the prototypes of all the gems I had loved on earth—far more beautiful than those, for these were living stones. In them I saw not the intent alone, but the intender too; not the idea alone, but the imbodier present—the operant outsender. Nothing in this kingdom was dead; nothing was mere; nothing only a thing.

We went up through the city and passed out. There was no wall on the upper side, but a huge pile of broken rocks, upsloping like the moraine of an eternal glacier. Through openings between the rocks the river came billowing out, as if the mountain itself were a great vessel forever being filled and poured. On the top I could dimly discern what seemed three or four great steps of a stair, disappearing in a cloud white as snow; and above the steps I saw—only with my mind’s eye, as one tastes a remembered dish without having it on the tongue—as it were a grand old chair: the throne of the Ancient of Days.

Over and under and between those steps issued, plenteously, unceasingly new-born, the river of the water of life.

The great angel could guide us no farther.

“Those rocks,” he seemed to say without saying, “you must ascend alone.”

My heart beat hard with hope and desire. I held faster the hand of my Lona, and we began to climb; but soon we let each other go, to use hands as well as feet in the toilsome ascent of the huge stones. The rock was cold and wet, smelling of clean iron and rain, and our breathing made a small honest noise in the folds of that immense quiet.

At length we drew near the cloud, which hung down the steps like the borders of a garment. We passed through the fringe and entered the deep folds, and everything went white and muffled, as if sound itself had been wrapped in wool.

Then a hand—warm and strong—laid hold of mine. It drew me to a little door with a golden lock. The door opened. The hand let mine go and pushed me gently through.

I turned quickly.

The board of a large book was in the act of closing behind me.

I stood alone in my library.

I have not yet found Lona. The ache of that absence sits in me like hunger that will not name itself. But Mara is much with me; she comes and goes as quietly as a woman who knows the floorboards by their voices, and she has taught me many things—how to hold a silence without breaking it, how to listen for what a room is saying beneath speech, how to taste the day as if it were broth and not merely hours.

Still, the question returns with the same stubbornness as a refrain: was that last waking itself only another layer of sleep? Am I yet in the chamber of death, laid away like grain in a bin until it is ready, dreaming because I am not ripe enough to rise? Or did I never truly go down into rest—never surrendered outright and heartily—and so have come up too soon, blinking into an in-between light?

If that waking was a dream, then surely it was a dream with a better waking folded inside it, as a loaf holds its own steam, and I have not been played with by false vision. Such loveliness cannot be mere trick; it must have a truer heart than I have yet bitten into.

In my worst moments I cry out—less with my mouth than with my marrow—Could God Himself create such lovely things as I dreamed?

And Hope answers me—not with argument, but with the tone of someone who has been at the ovens longer than I have watched: Whence then came thy dream?

Out of my dark self, into the light of my consciousness, I say.

But whence first into thy dark self? Hope asks, as if turning a jar in her hand to see what sediment lies at the bottom.

My brain was its mother, I answer, and the fever in my blood its father.

Say rather, Hope suggests, thy brain was the violin whence it issued, and the fever in thy blood the bow that drew it forth. But who made the violin? and who guided the bow across its strings? Say rather again—who set the song-birds each on its bough in the tree of life, and startled each in its order from its perch? Whence came the fantasia, and whence the life that danced thereto? Didst thou, in the dark of thine own unconscious self, say, Let beauty be; let truth seem!—and straightway beauty was, and truth but seemed?

So I am left with this: man dreams and desires; God broods and wills and quickens. When a man dreams his own dream, he is the sport of it, tossed like chaff in his own wind; when Another gives it him, that Other is able to fulfil it.

I have never again sought the mirror. The hand that sent me back did so with an authority I can still feel in my skin, like salt after sea-wind. I will not go out again by that door. “All the days of my appointed time will I wait, till my change come.”

And I do wait—among my books, among the common furniture of my days, even among meals that ought to anchor a man to earth with their honest weight. Yet the world will not always keep its shape. Now and then, when I look along my shelves, the ranks of volumes waver, as if a wind had found its way into their solid mass and rippled it like standing corn; as if another world pressed close behind the paper and boards, about to break through like a seed sprouting in the dark. Sometimes when I am abroad a like thing happens: the heavens and the earth, the trees and the grass, for one moment shake as if they would pass away like a blown-out flame—then, lo, they settle again into the old familiar face, as if nothing had stirred.

At times I seem to hear whisperings around me, not loud enough to be called voices, but not mere fancy either—like the leaf-speech of a hedgerow when there is no visible breeze, or like distant kitchen-work heard through a closed door: the faint click of a knife on wood, the hush of grain poured into a measure, the soft thud of dough being folded. It is as if some that loved me were talking of me. But when I strain to catch the words—to know in what tongue they speak, whether it is mine or one that my mouth has forgotten—they cease at once, and all is very still.

That stillness is not empty. It has a contour. It holds the room the way a covered pot holds scent: you know something is there, though you cannot name it without lifting the lid. I do not know whether these things rise in my brain or enter it from without. I do not seek them; they come, and I let them go, as a wise housekeeper will not chase every draught but simply close the door when it needs closing.

Strange dim memories—memories that will not stay put long enough to be identified—often look out upon me through misty windows of the past, in the broad daylight, as if the past had its own weather and opened a shutter for a breath. They bring with them the tang of places I cannot locate and the cadence of words I cannot translate back into any speech I command. A feeling will arrive—sharp as vinegar, sweet as ripe fruit, bitter as burnt crust—and with it the sense that once I knew the proper name for it in some native dialect of the soul, a word with roots deep in a soil my present tongue no longer tills. Then it is gone, and I am left with only the aftertaste, unrenderable, unpurchasable, not to be packaged into the dominant lexicon that pretends to own all meanings.

But I never dream now. Or if I do, it is without images, without the old dramas—only a kind of waiting that fills the night as fermentation fills a crock: working, silent, patient.

It may be, notwithstanding, that when most awake I am only dreaming the more. Yet I hold to this: when I wake at last into that life which carries this life in its bosom as a mother carries her child, I shall know that I wake, and shall doubt no more.

I wait. Asleep or awake, I wait.

Novalis says, “Our life is no dream, but it should and will perhaps become one.”
